Chapter 01 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the first canto, the Thirumalai Charukkam, of Sekkizhar Swamigal’s Periya Puranam, Sekkizhar Swamigal described the glories of Thiru-Kailayamalai and the events that take place there, under the heading “Thirumalai Sirappu.” Next, in the section titled “Thirunattu Sirappu,” he marvels at the glories of the Chola country.

Following this, in the section “Thirunagara Sirappu,” he describes the glory of Thiruvarur city and recounts the history of Manu Needhi Chola.

Then, in “Thirukkoota Sirappu,” he describes the general characteristics of Shiva’s devotees. The next topic Sekkizhar presents is called “Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam.” The meaning of this title is that the Lord prevented Sundaramoorthy Swamigal from being bound. This section narrates the story of how the Lord took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal into His grace. Sekkizhar has composed in this Puranam, specifically the Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam, the historical events from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred incarnation in Tamil Nadu, up to the point where he sang the Thiruththonda Thogai in Thiruvarur.

Following this, as an elaboration of the Thiruththonda Thogai, he begins to narrate the stories of the sixty-three Nayanmars in the subsequent canto, starting with the Thillai Vazh Andhanar Charukkam.

Therefore, this also serves as an introduction to the Periya Puranam.

Now, let us examine the summary of this Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam. This section recounts the events where the Lord prevented Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and took him into His grace; hence, it is called the Thaduthu Atkonda section, the Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam. Where did the Lord prevent Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and take him into His grace? Why did He do so? This is the essence of this Puranam. In the first part of the first canto, the Thirumalai Charukkam, in the section “Thirumalai Sirappu,” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, as Aalala Sundarar, served the Lord as a close devotee at Thiru-Kailayamalai, carrying the sacred ash casket and flower garlands, performing Shiva’s service.

On one occasion, while going to the sacred garden to pluck flowers, his mind was slightly drawn to Kamalini and Anindithai, two companions of Goddess Parvati who were present there. Consequently, the Lord commanded him, “You shall be born in the southern land, where great penance was performed, marry Kamalini and Anindithai, and then return to Kailash.” Upon hearing this, Aalala Sundarar obtained a promise from the Lord: if, during his birth in this earthly realm and before returning to Kailash after marrying them, he were to accumulate any further karmic actions, the Lord Himself should personally intervene and take him into His grace. Having received this promise, Aalala Sundarar, who was Aalala Sundarar in Kailash, incarnated as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in Tamil Nadu. Kamalini, the companion of Goddess Parvati, incarnated as Paravai Nachiyar in Thiruvarur, and Anindithai incarnated as Sangili Nachiyar in the sthalam called Gnayiru.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was born in Thirunavalur, and when he reached the age of sixteen and attained marriageable age, there, arrangements were made for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to marry the daughter of the nearby Shadangavi Sivachariyar. This daughter of Shadangavi Sivachariyar was neither Kamalini nor Anindithai, to whom Sundarar’s mind was drawn in Kailash.

Therefore, at the event of marrying the daughter of Shadangavi Sivachariyar, the Lord intervened and took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal into His grace. Hence, this is named Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam.

Furthermore, the Lord created the circumstances for the Thiruththonda Thogai to be composed, where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went to Thiruvarur and sang praises to the Shiva devotees gathered in the Devasiriya Mandapam. This Puranam narrates this very event. This is the section known as Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam. The gross reason for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s birth in this earthly world, after his mind was drawn to Kamalini and Anindithai as Aalala Sundarar in Thiru-Kailash, is thus.

However, the Lord’s compassion in doing so was for a subtle reason. That subtle reason was that the southern land, where great penance was performed, should flourish, and the Thiruththonda Thogai, which extols the greatness of devotees, should emerge. Based on this Thiruththonda Thogai, the Thiruththondar Thiruvandhadhi and this Periya Puranam, which is the Thiruththondar Puranam, should arise, and Shiva devotion should prosper everywhere. This was the Lord’s compassionate intent.

Therefore, the events in this Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam are structured to fulfill both the subtle and gross reasons simultaneously. Aalala Sundarar, by the Lord’s command, is to live in the southern land. He incarnates to live in the southern land. He takes birth in the village of Thirunavalur in Tamil Nadu. This Thirunavalur is currently located in the Villupuram district. In the era when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal incarnated, it was known as Thirumunaippadi Nadu.

In that glorious land of Thirumunaippadi Nadu, in the sacred sthalam of Thirunavalur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal incarnated as the blessed son of Sadaiya Nayanar and Isaignani Ammaiyar, who belonged to the Adisaivar lineage. Incarnation, or Avatharam, means descending from above.

Therefore, it is appropriate to say that Aalala Sundarar, who was in a superior state in Thiru-Kailash as Aalala Sundarar, descended to this earthly realm in Tamil Nadu to redeem us all.

Therefore, it is appropriate to say that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal took sacred birth. The lineage in which Sundaramoorthy Swamigal took birth is the Adisaivar lineage. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that he incarnated by divine grace to uphold the profound Vedic Shaivism within that Adisaiva lineage.

Here, “Arumarai Saivam” refers to Vaidika Shaivism, which is celebrated as Saiva Siddhanta. Sekkizhar describes the event of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s incarnation, which occurred to foster Vaidika Saiva Siddhanta in the South, through the following verse: “Mathoru Paaganarkku vazhi vazhi adimai seyyum Vedhiyar kulaththul thondri Mempadu Sadaiyanarkku Edham” Karpin vazhkai manai Isaignaniyarpaal Theedhu agandru ulagam uyya Thiru avatharam seithaar. That is, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is born into the lineage of Sivachariyars who have served Lord Shiva generation after generation. Thirunavalur is a village where many Brahmins lived.

There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is born to Sadaiya Nayanar, who possessed great spiritual merit in the Adisaiva lineage, and Isaignani Ammaiyar. Sekkizhar refers to this lineage as “Mathoru Paaganarkku vazhi vazhi adimai seyyum marabu” (the lineage that serves the Lord with a female half, generation after generation), which is the Adisaiva lineage. They are called Sivabrahmanas. They do not engage in any profession other than the sacred service of performing puja in the temple. They do not engage in any other profession than the sacred service of performing puja within the temple. These individuals are celebrated in literature, history, and society by various names such as Sivamaraiyor, Siva Andhanar, Adisaivar, and Sivabrahmana.

Unlike other Vedic scholars who are said to have originated from the face of Brahma, these individuals are descendants of the five Rishis who originated from the five sacred faces of Lord Shiva’s Sadashivamurthi. According to the Saiva Agamas, they are the ones who perform Atmartha Puja and Parartha Puja, serving the Lord generation after generation. Parartha Puja, in Shaivism, is exclusively reserved for them according to the Saiva Agamas. Parartha Puja is the right to enter the sanctum sanctorum and perform sacred service to the Lord’s form enshrined in the temples. This is their right. They belong to the gotras of the five sages who emerged from the five faces of Sadashiva: Kausikar, Kashyapar, Agasthiyar, Bharadwajar, and Gautamar.

These five sages are not the same as the five Brahma-born sages with similar names; these are the five sages who directly emerged from the face of Lord Shiva.

Now, by “vazhi vazhi adimai” (hereditary servitude), Sekkizhar refers to both the maternal and paternal lineages. That is, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal incarnates in a lineage that has served the Lord generation after generation through both maternal and paternal lines.

Thus, our Lord, who incarnated in this manner, was named Nambi Arurar by the elders.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s My sacred father’s name is Sadaiya Nayanar. Sadaiyanar is one of the sacred names specifically attributed to Lord Shiva. Naming children after deities and elders has been a custom in our country since ancient times. The fact that his father was known by the name Arurar, and that he gave this name to his son, supports this idea. The mother of our Nambi Arurar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, is named Isaignaniyar.

Isaignani Ammaiyar is the sacred daughter born into the family of Sivagothi Gnana Sivachariyar in Thiruvarur.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal incarnates here, born to parents from two great, spiritually accomplished Adisaiva lineages, so that the world may be redeemed and evil may be removed.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal incarnates.

Here, “theedhu agandru” (evil removed) should be understood as the removal of bonds (pasam), and “ulagam uyya” (the world may be redeemed) should be understood as attaining Shiva’s grace.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s incarnation takes place for these two purposes: for us, as living beings, to be freed from bonds and attain Shiva’s grace.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was named Nambi Arurar, and then, adorned with anklets and a beautiful forehead ornament inlaid with gems, and with a sacred thread of pure gold around his waist, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal grew up. The “Aimbadai” mentioned here, the Aimbadai ornament, is a type of ornament made in the form of the five weapons of Lord Vishnu, the guardian deity—the conch, discus, mace, sword, and bow—to protect children from evil spirits. It is a type of ornament made in the form of the five weapons of Lord Vishnu, the guardian deity—the conch, discus, mace, sword, and bow—and strung around the chests of children to protect them. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was playing, rolling a small chariot in that street, Narasimha Munaiyar, the king of Thirunavalur, saw the beauty of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal playing in the street one day. His heart was captivated, and he went to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s father, Sadaiya Nayanar, and requested the privilege of raising this greatly blessed child. It is noteworthy that Sadaiya Nayanar and Narasimha Munaiyar were already friends.

Therefore, with their consent, King Narasimha Munaiyar, took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to his palace at the age of three and raised him with the characteristics befitting a prince, yet without deviating from the traditions of the Siva Vedic lineage. As he grew, he adhered to the conduct appropriate for his Saiva Brahmin lineage, and at the proper age, the ceremonies of wearing the sacred thread (Poonool) and enrolling him in school were performed. Although Sundaramoorthy Swamigal grew up as the beloved child of the king of Thirunavalur, his parents ensured that his Upanayanam was performed, maintaining the traditions of the Siva Brahmin lineage. Wealth, position, and material possessions depart with the body, but what accompanies the soul is only the family’s conduct.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also adhered to that family conduct without forgetting it.

Thus, having undergone the sacred thread ceremony and commenced his education, he learned all the arts. Upanayanam, when split as “Upa-nayanam,” means an additional, great eye. This refers to the coming of a third eye, superior to the two natural eyes. This third eye, located between the eyebrows, is opened through meditation. This upward-gazing third eye belongs exclusively to Lord Shiva. Since Sundaramoorthy Swamigal underwent Upanayanam and commenced his education, he too appears in this auspicious Shiva-like form.

The topknot, sacred ash, triple-stranded thread, deer skin, munji grass twisted into a waist cord, loincloth, and pavithram (sacred ring) are the components of this attire. Sekkizhar refers to this as the Mangalam Sai Kolam (auspicious ascetic’s attire).

Furthermore, he calls the triple-stranded thread “Marai Munnool” (Vedic sacred thread), meaning a thread worn according to Vedic injunctions, with the initiation of the Gayatri mantra, which is the heart of the Vedas. Elders explain that Gayatri means removing the karmas of those who attain it and freeing them from rebirth. Saiva scholars state that Lord Shiva is the one indicated by Gayatri. The truth that Lord Shiva is the one to whom the word “Bhargum” in the Gayatri mantra refers is found in the authoritative statement of Haradatta Sivachariyar’s Sruti Sukshma Malai, as it pertains to the meaning of the higher Gayatri. This is the truth found in the authoritative statement of Haradatta Sivachariyar’s Sruti Sukshma Malai.

Thus, with the Vedic tradition, the Adisaiva tradition, and the characteristics of a king all combined, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal grew up in the palace of King Narasimha Munaiyar from the age of three to sixteen. According to Tamil grammatical structure, sixteen years is the age of marriage for men. The story of our Arurar Nambi, from the age of three when he played by rolling a small chariot, to the age of sixteen, his marriageable age, growing up as the beloved child in the king’s palace, is narrated here by Sekkizhar.

Let us return to reflecting upon the Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 02 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thaduthu Atkonda Puranam, Aalala Sundarar, who had been serving the Lord in Kailash, incarnated as the son of Sadayanar Peruman and Isaignani Ammaiyar in Thirunavalur, in the Thirumunaippadi country of Tamil Nadu. Subsequently, he grew up in the palace of Narasimha Munaiyar, the king of Thirunavalur, from the age of three to sixteen. Although he grew up in the royal palace, our Sundaramoorthy Peruman adhered to the conduct proper to Saiva Brahmins, undergoing the Upanayanam and Vidyarambam ceremonies, and mastering all the arts. He is now sixteen years old. He has reached the age of sixteen and is now of marriageable age.

Therefore, Sadaiya Nayanar, Nambi Arurar’s sacred father, is searching for a blessed daughter from his own Adisaiva lineage who would be suitable for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

At this time, Sadaiyanar learned that a daughter was growing up in the home of a certain Shadangavi Sivachariyar in Puthur, a village near Thirunavalur. Shadangavi Sivachariyar also belonged to the Adisaiva lineage.

Therefore, Sadaiya Nayanar sent elders and wise persons as emissaries to arrange the marriage of Shadangavi Sivachariyar’s daughter to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. It is a custom of ancestors, a rule of dharma, and a proper procedure to inquire about the lineage, tradition, and gotra, among other things, when arranging a marriage for a girl. It is a custom of ancestors, a rule of dharma, and a proper procedure to inquire about the lineage, tradition, and gotra, among other things, when arranging a marriage for a girl.

Here, understanding “kulam” (lineage) means inquiring about the inherent qualities that make one excel in conduct, education, wealth, and so forth. The gotra system of the Adisaivars is said to be fivefold. The five types of gotras belonging to the Adisaivars are those descended from the five Rishis who emerged from the five sacred faces of Sadashiva. Subsequently, these are also the branch gotras that emerged from each of those gotras.

Therefore, understanding the gotra here means following the rule that the bride and groom should not belong to the same gotra. It is customary to ascertain the bride’s gotra, meaning that those belonging to the same gotra should not engage in marriage.

Therefore, the bride or groom must be chosen from a different gotra. Even if they are from different gotras, it is customary to inquire and clarify whether they are close relatives.

Furthermore, it is necessary to inquire and ascertain compatibility, omens, and other factors.

Thus, it was the practice of that era to inform learned elders, who would investigate all aspects such as lineage, tradition, gotra, compatibility, and omens, and then send them to negotiate the marriage. It was the practice of that era for the groom’s family to send learned elders, well-versed in lineage, gotra, and other matters, to the bride’s family to negotiate the marriage. Even in the Kanda Puranam, it is clear from the Puranas, including the Kanda Puranam, that seven Rishis went to negotiate the marriage of Parvati Ammaiyar as the bride for Lord Shiva.

Thus, these elders, skilled in lineage, family, wisdom, and other qualities, received the message from Sadaiya Nayanar and conveyed these events and details to Shadangavi Sivachariyar in Puthur, expecting his consent to marry Shadangavi Sivachariyar’s daughter to Nambi Arurar. Shadangavi Sivachariyar also gave his joyful approval.

The elders, happily accepting this approval, They arrive at Thiru-Navalur and inform Sadaiya Nayanar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s father, of Sadangavi Sivachariyar’s consent. He is overjoyed. Sadaiya Nayanar, thus delighted, writes a wedding invitation, specifying the auspicious date and time, and sends it to the bride’s family. A ‘Naal Olai’ refers to a palm-leaf invitation detailing the wedding date and auspicious hour, sent by the groom’s family to the bride’s.

Thus, the wedding invitation written by Sadaiya Nayanar is carried by auspicious women and men, bearing auspicious items, from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s house, the groom’s residence. They proceed to Putthur.

There, the people of Putthur receive these visitors from the groom’s house, welcoming them with auspicious women and men carrying auspicious items, and lead them with great joy to Sadangavi Sivachariyar’s house. The women of Sadangavi Sivachariyar’s household, along with everyone else, accept the wedding invitation with great joy and celebrate it. They convey this happy news to all relatives. Subsequently, they eagerly begin all the auspicious ceremonies that must precede the wedding. They erect a grand canopy and adorn it with flower garlands. Decorating with flower garlands hung profusely from festoons and other ornaments.

This was the custom for decorating canopies in ancient times.

Then, in that canopy, all the elder Brahmins of the Adi Shaiva tradition, sumangalis (married women whose husbands are alive), and everyone else gather together and perform the ‘Mulaippalikai Saathuthal’ ceremony. This is called ‘Anguraarpanam,’ an auspicious ritual performed seven days before the wedding. This Anguraarpanam or Mulaippalikai Saathuthal involves placing navadhanyams (nine grains) mixed with milk near a kumbha (pot) dedicated to the moon god, chanting mantras, and nurturing them in small pots filled with soil. Sri Arumugathambiranar states that twelve palikais are prepared for the twelve Adityas, filled with sacred earth taken with mantras, and grains are sown in them to sprout. Sowing grains to sprout is called Mulaippalikai Saathuthal. ‘Saathuthal’ means to adorn or to combine earth and water appropriately; this is a traditional name. The growth of these sprouts is considered an auspicious sign for good fortune, making it a joyous celebration.

This Anguraarpanam is the first ritual performed in all auspicious events such as festivals and Kumbhabhishekams.

Thus, while all auspicious ceremonies began joyfully at the bride’s home, at the same time, auspicious ceremonies for the groom’s side also began joyfully at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s residence in Thiru-Navalur. On the day before the wedding, in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s house in Thiru-Navalur, auspicious instruments like drums sounded. They tied a golden protective thread (Kaappu) on Nambiyaarurar, who was adorned with flower garlands. This ‘Kaappu Saerththal’ is a ritual where, on the day before the wedding, the bride and groom, each in their own home, perform auspicious ceremonies and have a protective thread tied. This is called ‘Rakshabandhanam.’ It takes place on the afternoon of the day before the wedding. Tying a thread on the groom’s right hand for protection and on the bride’s left hand for protection is called ‘Kaappu Saerththal.’ From that moment until the wedding is concluded and the Kaappu is untied, it protects the couple from all harm, hence its name ‘Kaappu.’ Thus, at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s residence, the wedding On the first night, all other rituals are being performed according to Vedic injunctions.

Auspicious musical instruments are playing. As if to witness the beautiful wedding attire of our Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the sun rises that morning. On the morning of the wedding day, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, before the auspicious time designated by the astrologers, entered the Thiru-Manjana Saalai (sacred bathing chamber) to don the ceremonial wedding attire. He, a superior being adorned with the sacred thread gracing his chest, possessing knowledge and wisdom, and wearing gem and flower garlands, entered the chamber. In a fragrant, ghee-infused vessel, filled with sacred water containing appropriate flowers and aromatic substances, They filled it with sacred water, adding appropriate flowers and fragrant substances. Near it, on a golden throne, they seated Nambi Aaroorar, the groom.

They performed a sacred bath for Nambi Aaroorar, who is dear to the Lord, and adorned his beautiful Thiru-Meni (sacred body) with fragrant powders, enhancing its beauty. Sekkizhar describes this water, with which Sundaramoorthy Swamigal performed his sacred bath, as holy and pure. It is our tradition to meditate on bathing water as the Ganges, Yamuna, Shiva’s foot-water, Shanmukha’s foot-water, etc.

Therefore, the bathing water is pure and holy water. After Sundaramoorthy Swamigal completed his sacred bath, they draped a beautiful silk garment, fragrant with agar smoke, around his waist. A skilled decorator A skilled decorator took a white cloth, dried the moisture from Nambigal’s hair, and then styled it.

Afterwards, they applied a sandalwood paste, made by mixing pure, fragrant green camphor paste with dew collected from beautiful flowers, onto Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred body. They also applied a musk-infused blended paste.

Following this, they adorned his chest with the sacred thread, worn according to tradition, and placed the ‘Pavithram’ on his hand.

Thus, our Lord Nambi Aaroorar shone with the Pavithram on his hand. ‘Pavithram’ means purity. All wedding rituals performed after the ‘Kaappu Saerththal’ are done with a ‘Sankalpam’ (sacred vow).

Therefore, it is customary that they must be performed while wearing the Pavithram. It is a rule that it must be done wearing the Pavithram. A Pavithram is a type of knot made of darbha grass or gold, worn on the finger.

Hence, that finger is also called the Pavithra விரல் (Pavithra finger). Thus adorned, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with the beautiful appearance of a king and the beautiful appearance of his traditional Vedic attire, shone brightly, appearing as a beautiful Sundarar, captivating all who saw him.

Afterwards, Nambi Aaroorar meditated on the Lord’s sacred feet in his mind and applied sacred ash. While all the other adornments mentioned above were done by others, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal himself desired to apply the sacred ash, meditating on Lord Shiva’s. He meditated on the sacred feet and chanted His names before applying it. This is an important point we must note here.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, adorned in his wedding attire, prepared to go to Putthur, mounted on a ‘Yoga Puravi,’ that is, a ‘Yoga Horse.’ Here, Sekkizhar refers to the horse carrying the groom, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, as a ‘Yoga Horse.’ A ‘Yoga Horse’ means a horse endowed with all the complete characteristics of a horse. That is, it is a horse possessing all the good qualities, such as auspicious swirls. It should be understood as a horse with auspicious swirls that bring good fortune.

Furthermore, in the four paths of Saiva Siddhanta – Chariya, Kriya, Yoga, and Gnana – Our Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came to explain the path of Yoga among the four paths.

Therefore, Sekkizhar might have referred to it as ‘Yoga Puravi,’ the ‘Yoga Horse.’ Let us proceed to contemplate the wedding procession that is about to unfold.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 03 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the first canto of the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thiru-Malai-Saru-Kam, we are examining the Puranam of Thaduthaatkonda. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was Aalala Sundarar on Thiru-Kailasa mountain, as he went to pluck flowers to weave garlands for Lord Shiva in the sacred garden, he saw Kamalini and Anindithai, two companions of Goddess Parvati, who had already come there to pluck flowers for weaving garlands for Goddess Parvati. He looked at them with a loving heart. Knowing this, Lord Shiva commanded him on Kailasa, “Because you looked at these two women with a loving heart, you shall be born in the southern region of the earth, marry those two women, and then return to Kailasa.”

The Lord commands. This is the apparent reason, but the true reason is that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal should incarnate here to bless this southern land, which performed great penance, and bestow upon us the Thiru-Thonda Thogai, a text extolling the greatness of the devotees. This is the subtle reason. When Lord Shiva commanded him to be born on earth, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal heard it and pleaded, “Lord, since I looked at these two women here, you command me to go to earth, marry them, and return. If, while this happens, I incur any further karma, you yourself must come and stop me and take me as your own.” Lord Shiva accepted this and granted His grace.

Accordingly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal In the country of Thiru-Munaippadi Nadu, within Tamil Nadu, in the town of Thiru-Navalur, he was born as the son of Sadaiya Nayanar and Isaignani Ammaiyar, of the Adi Shaiva Brahmin lineage. He was adopted by Narasimha Munaiyar, the king of Thiru-Navalur, and grew up splendidly in the palace.

Now, having reached the age of sixteen and the age of marriage, his father, Sadaiya Nayanar, arranges for him, Nambiyaarurar, also known as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, to marry the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar, who lives near Thiru-Navalur. Auspicious ceremonies are taking place in both the groom’s and bride’s homes for the wedding.

Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is also adorned in beautiful wedding attire.

Then, mounted on a Yoga Puravi, a Yoga horse, this wedding procession begins towards Putthur. In this procession, various auspicious musical instruments resound. Excellent singers perform music everywhere, singing various songs. Women also sing ‘Pallandu’ (long live). The four Vedas are chanted there.

Thus, all the relatives of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also mounted vehicles such as elephants, horses, and palanquins. Countless young men and women also gathered closely and joined the procession. This procession approaches Putthur.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, adorned in wedding attire, As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at Putthur in his wedding attire, this place became known as ‘Manam Vandha Putthur’ (Putthur where the fragrance came). As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s wedding procession approached Putthur, Brahmin women, holding full kalasams (pots), incense and lamps, and Mulaippalikai (sprouting pots) in their hands, and carrying fragrant flowers, darbha grass, sandal powder, and auspicious puffed rice, came forth to greet Sundaramoorthy’s wedding procession. Some say that countless eyes are needed to behold the beauty of this groom. Some whisper that Sadangavi Sivachariyar’s daughter has performed greater penance than other women. Some, seeing this wedding attire, say they have attained great fortune. Some refer to this groom, adorned with various ornaments, as the embodiment of Purna.

Some refer to him as the embodiment of Purna.

Thus, our Lord of Grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who arrived in such beautiful wedding attire, dismounts from the white horse he was riding and reaches the place where the marriage is to take place. I shall now begin to narrate the event that transpired at that auspicious moment of the wedding. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that he has received immense grace and attained great fortune by singing this history.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came here in wedding attire to marry the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar. However, on Thiru-Kailasa, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had looked with love at two women named Kamalini and Anindithai. They are now born and growing up as Paravai Nachiyar in Thiru-Arur and Sangili Nachiyar in Gnayiru, respectively.

However, this daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar is not Kamalini or Anindithai Ammaiyar, whom Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was destined to marry.

Therefore, Lord Shiva, as He had promised Aalala Sundarar earlier, descends from Kailasa at this moment, when he is about to marry someone other than those two women, thereby incurring further karma, and intervenes to take him as His own. This event is now described by Sekkizhar.

Thus, Lord Shiva, who had promised Aalala Sundarar, departed from Thiru-Kailasa mountain, where the Vedas always resound, and manifested Himself in a divine form at the wedding venue. How did Lord Shiva manifest here? He appeared in the guise of an elderly Brahmin. Always He concealed his third eye, which never leaves Lord Shiva, and adorned himself with white sacred ash. Not only that, but just as he concealed his third eye and wore white sacred ash, his white, grey hair, like the moon always adorning his matted locks, hung from his head down to his neck. Rudraksha earrings dangled from his sacred ears.

A sacred thread adorned his beautiful chest. A white cloth, worn as an upper garment, swayed on his shoulder. In the Lord’s sacred hand, a parasol, shielding him from the sun, shone. Around his sacred waist, He wore a loincloth below, and over it, a swaying garment. We mentioned that he held a parasol in one hand, didn’t we?

Now, in his other hand, he held a bamboo staff. At the tip of this bamboo staff, a white cloth and darbha grass were tied. Our Lord, in this guise of an elderly Brahmin, walked slowly, his legs faltering, leaning on the bamboo staff. Sivakavimani describes this appearance as the ‘Vanaprastha Kolam’ (hermit’s attire). Sivakavimani further states that this particular form suggests a ‘Vanaprastha Kolam’ without a wife. There are two types of Vanaprastha.

Among these two, the form the Lord has now taken is called ‘Vibathneeka Vanaprastha Ashramam.’ They call the form the Lord has taken ‘Vibathneeka Vanaprastha Ashramam.’ Didn’t they say that at the tip of the bamboo staff the Lord carried, there was a white cloth and darbha grass tied? Elders explain that this white cloth is a change of clothes for bathing, and darbha grass is essential for performing ‘tharpanam’ (ancestral rites), hence it was tied to the tip of the bamboo staff. This beautiful elderly disguise was so exquisitely beautiful that one might suspect it was a form that had gradually matured from youth. The profound beauty of maturity and wisdom, born from meticulously observing Vedic rituals, was evident. That beauty was outwardly manifest as the Lord entered the wedding canopy. It is natural for the beauty of even the most beautiful individuals to diminish with age.

However, here, the beauty seemed to have increased, appearing as if it were an aged form, causing everyone to wonder as the Lord entered. Lord Shiva, whose sacred mouth had, in ancient times, graciously expounded countless Vedas, now entered the wedding canopy in this elderly guise. He stood before the elders, the assembly seated before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was seated as the groom, and said, “Oh members of the assembly, listen to these words I am about to speak.” Upon hearing this, the numerous Brahmins and other elders in the assembly, along with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, looked at the elderly Brahmin and said, All the countless Brahmins and other elders in the assembly, along with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, looked at the elderly Brahmin and said, “May your arrival be a good arrival. Your presence here is indeed the result of our past penance.” They then requested, “Please, tell us what you have come to say.” What does Lord Shiva, in the guise of the elderly Brahmin, say?

Let us now move forward in our contemplation.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 04 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam,’ Lord Shiva, in the guise of an aged Brahmin, has arrived at the wedding hall where Sundaramurthi Swamigal and the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariar are to be wed. He has come to claim Sundarar as his own, as per the promise he had previously made to Sundarar in Kailash. Before the wedding stage, looking at Sundarar and the other assembly members, Lord Shiva declares, “All you members of the assembly, listen to what I am about to say.” Hearing this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and the other assembly members welcome him and request, “Please state what you have come here to say.” Lord Shiva, who adorns his matted locks with the crescent moon, the Ganga, snakes, and the flowers of the Thumbai and Konrai, has concealed these divine attributes. He has appeared as an aged Brahmin, a vanaprastha in the garb of a householder, to claim Sundarar. Lord Shiva, in this guise, addresses Sundarar, saying, “Sundara, there is a prior agreement between you and me.

“Therefore, resolve that great dispute, and only then proceed with this wedding sacrifice.” The Brahmin, who had concealed his third eye, spoke thus. Hearing this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal turned to the aged Brahmin and said, “Elder, if there is a dispute between you and me, I will only marry after that dispute is settled.”

“Therefore, state the full details of that dispute here,” he said.

“Then, the aged Brahmin addressed the assembly, saying, “O assembly members, this Sundaramurthi standing here is my slave. This is the dispute I have come to speak of.” Hearing the Lord speak thus, all the people gathered under the wedding canopy expressed various emotions. Some were shocked, wondering who would speak in such a manner. Some became angry and tried to approach him. Some mocked and laughed at his words. Even Sundaramurthi Swamigal stood before the Brahmin and, finding the Brahmin’s words amusing, laughed derisively in front of him.

“Thus, with derision, Sundaramurthi Swamigal looked at the aged man, and laughed. Trembling and faltering, the old man picked up his fallen upper garment, draped it over himself, and approached Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “Sundara, this is the deed of servitude written and given to me by your father’s father. It contains a pledge that your entire lineage will live as my servants. Not knowing this truth, why did you mock and laugh at what I said?” Thus, when the Lord spoke directly to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, struck by the Lord’s gracious gaze, felt his heart soften due to the continuation of the love he already held for the Lord.

“Then, the smile vanished from Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s face.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal then began to speak, saying, “Elder, I am born into the unique lineage of Adisaivas, who are dedicated to Parartha Shiva Puja. We are Brahmins of an unblemished Adisaiva lineage, whose traditional occupation is performing Shiva Puja to remove the bonds of souls, and who engage in no other profession. Today, for the first time, I hear from you alone that Brahmins of such a lineage can be slaves to another Brahmin. Are you mad, O Brahmin?” He said, “Masila marabil vanda vallal vediyanai nokki nesam mun kidanda sindai nigazhchiyal siripu neengi asil anthanargal veror anthanarkku adimaiyathal pesa.” “Indru unnai kettom pithano maraiyon endrar.” This is a verse by Sekkizhar. Hearing this, the Lord, the Brahmin, addressed Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “Sundara, even if I am mad, as you say, or even if I am a demon, and no matter how many more harsh words you speak, I will not be ashamed by any of them.

“You still do not know my true nature.

“Therefore, do not mock me. Do the work I command; that servitude is what is proper for you,” he said. The term ‘pithan’ refers to one who is afflicted by madness, losing their intellect and wandering in confusion due to an excess of bile.

“Furthermore, ‘peyan’ refers to one possessed by a demon, losing their self-awareness. It also means that ‘pithu’ is an internal illness, a mental confusion that arises when the bile within our body increases. ‘Peyan’ refers to being possessed by a demon, which is an external affliction.

“Furthermore, ‘peyan’ also refers to Lord Shiva, who has demons as his retinue.

“The Lord also states that no matter how many harsh words you speak, or how much you scold or abuse me, I will not be ashamed. This is because Lord Shiva is accustomed to being scolded and beaten by his devotees, and therefore, he says, ‘I have no shame.’ Hearing these words from the Brahmin, two great emotions arose in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart. Looking at the Brahmin’s beautiful, mature, and sacred form, and being captivated by its beauty, the love he had previously felt for him intensified.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart melted with love and affection. However, in contrast, the word ‘slave’ that he uttered provoked anger.

“Thus, these two great emotions arose in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart. But even though these two contradictory emotions arose, instead of being carried away by them, he remembered that the elder had mentioned a deed of servitude. He clearly thought, “We must investigate the truth of that deed.” So, he turned to the elder, his exalted companion, and asked, “Show me that deed of servitude you mentioned.” Sekkizhar states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart rejoiced and melted with love upon seeing the sacred form of the aged Brahmin, adorned with sacred ash on his forehead and rudrakshas on his body. This means that upon seeing the sacred form of Shiva, his heart was filled with love and melted. Saiva Siddhanta scriptures speak highly of this state of being, where one’s heart melts with love upon seeing the sacred form, placing it among the qualities of those who have attained spiritual maturity. That is, such love and desire do not arise in the mind upon seeing the sacred form unless the souls are already mature.

“Sekkizhar explains this very point.

“Thus, even though love and anger arose simultaneously, Sundaramurthi Swamigal set them aside and asked for the deed to investigate the truth. According to the maxim, “Kaythal upathal akatri oru porulkan aythal arivudaiyar kannathe,” which means, “To investigate a matter by removing both hatred and attachment is the mark of the wise,” it is the nature of truly wise people to investigate and understand from a neutral standpoint, without being swayed by love or anger.

“Thus, “Show the deed,”

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal requested. Hearing this, the Brahmin said, “Sundara, you are involved in this case, so you are not qualified to examine the deed. I will show it before this assembly.

“After they have made their decision, you are only fit to serve me according to their judgment.” Hearing these words, Sundaramurthi Swamigal became angry and pursued the Lord, who is unattainable and whom Brahma and Vishnu could not find even after searching. As Sundaramurthi Swamigal approached the Lord to snatch the deed, the Brahmin Lord ran into the wedding hall.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal swiftly pursued the Lord, who was running with his beautiful form, and caught him.

“Once before, when all the Devas stood and worshipped Lord Shiva to destroy the three cities (Tripura Samharam), Lord Shiva commanded them to prepare the necessary instruments. The earth became the chariot, the sun and moon became the wheels, the Vedas became the horses, Brahma became the charioteer, Mount Meru became the bow, Adisesha became the bowstring, Agni, Vayu, and Vishnu became the arrow, and all other Devas became other instruments. When Lord Shiva ascended that chariot, all the Devas became arrogant, thinking that Lord Shiva had come to burn the three cities because of them. Knowing this, Lord Shiva smiled slightly. With that very smile, the three cities were reduced to ashes. Sekkizhar Swamigal wonders, who else but Sundaramurthi Swamigal could have pursued and caught Lord Shiva, who possesses such greatness?

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, in his anger, runs to catch the Lord, does he not? This is the Lord’s Tirodhana Shakti, which instills desire and aversion in souls to remove their flaws and bonds. Just as mangoes and other fruits first become unripe, then sour, then ripe and sweet, so too, here, aversion and anger first arise, and then they transform into joy and the grace of the Lord, taking hold of us.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having pursued and caught the aged Brahmin, snatched the deed from his hand. He tore the deed, saying, “How can one Brahmin be a slave to another Brahmin? This is not the custom.” As Sundaramurthi Swamigal tore the deed, the Lord, who is unknowable even by the Vedas, in the guise of an aged Brahmin, complained, “Is it proper for this Sundaramurthi, also known as Nambiyarurar, to snatch and tear the deed?” The assembly members, including the relatives present, separated the two and addressed the Brahmin, saying, “O Brahmin, you are presenting a novel case, unheard of in this world until now. Let that be.

“First, tell us, where have you come from? What is your native place?” Sekkizhar Swamigal states here that the Lord is unknowable even by the Vedas. If so, the hymns, Vedic verses, and Upanishads of the Vedas describe the form and qualities of the Lord. How so? The Vedas speak of the characteristics of the Lord’s second state, the Tadastha state, among His two forms: Svarupa state and Tadastha state. Sekkizhar states here that the Lord is unknowable even by the Vedas, referring to the Lord’s Svarupa state.

“Furthermore, the Vedas originate from Pranava, and Pranava originates from Nada. Nada is the first of the thirty-six Tattvas.

“But the Lord is Tattvatita, beyond all these thirty-six Tattvas.

“Therefore, Sekkizhar states here that the Lord is unknowable even by the Vedas. This is what the Sivagnana Siddhiyar, one of the fourteen Saiva Siddhanta scriptures, states: “Vedam sathiram miruthi puranam kalai gnanam virumbu asabai vaigari athirangal melam nadamudivana ellam pasa gnanam endrum pasa gnanathalum pasu gnanathalum parpariya paramparanai pathi gnanathale nesamodu ullathe nadi endrum.” Thus, when everyone asked the Lord where he was from, the Lord replied, “I am from here. My dwelling place is not far away. Thiruvannainallur, which is nearby, is my native place.

“Moreover, by snatching and tearing the deed from my hand, this Sundaramurthi has himself confirmed the truth that he is my slave,” said the Lord. Seeing the Lord who spoke thus, Nambiyarurar, also known as Sundaramurthi Swamigal, thought, “He must be an old litigant.” The love he had previously felt for the Lord intensified and flowed within his heart, making it melt and his desire grow. “Sir, if you are from Thiruvannainallur, then let us discuss this false case there,” Sundaramurthi Swamigal said to the Brahmin. To this, the Brahmin said to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, “It is good if you come to Vennainallur. Before the pure Brahmins of that town, I will show the original deed that I wrote and prove that you are my slave.” Saying this, he walked ahead.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal followed him like iron drawn to a magnet. Everyone else followed, wondering what would happen next.

“Let us continue to contemplate the events that are to unfold in Thiruvannainallur.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 05 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam,’ at the wedding hall where Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s marriage is to take place, the Lord appears as an aged Brahmin and presents a deed claiming Sundaramurthi Swamigal as his slave.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal snatches the deed from the Lord and tears it up.

The Lord then states that this deed is merely a copy, and that he possesses the original deed, which he will use to prove Sundarar’s servitude. Seeing the Lord’s aged Brahmin appearance, everyone asks, “O Brahmin, where are you from?” The Lord replies, “Thiruvannainallur is my native place.” Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal also says, “Let us go to Thiruvannainallur and plead this case.”

The Lord agreed and, as an aged Brahmin, walked ahead.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal followed him like iron drawn by a magnet, and all the villagers followed them, filled with doubt and uncertainty about what would happen next.

Then, everyone reached Thiruvannainallur and presented their case to the court of Brahmins, the assembly of Thiruvannainallur. It is the law today to conduct a case and inquiry at the place where the crime occurred or where the plaintiff resides. We can infer from this that such a system existed in those times as well.

Thus, the assembly to which Sundarar and the Brahmin presented their case in Thiruvannainallur was a village assembly. During the Chola period, this village assembly system was a very strong institution. Its members were Brahmins who had studied the Vedas and possessed pure conduct. We learn from inscriptions and historical records that these assemblies had the right to administer justice, conduct inquiries, and perform other public duties.

Thus, the Lord, in the guise of the aged Brahmin, addressed the Thiruvannainallur judicial assembly of the village assembly, saying, “O assembly members, this Sundaramurthi, also known as Nambi Aruran, from Thirunavalur, is my slave. His grandfather wrote a deed stating that his entire lineage would be my slaves, signed it, and gave it to me.

“Therefore, I presented a copy of the deed there, claiming him as my slave.” “But he tore up that deed.

“Then, knowing that my village is Thiruvannainallur, he himself brought me here to argue the case,” he said. Hearing this, the Brahmins in the assembly asked, “Elder, O Brahmin, it is not customary in this world for one Brahmin to be a slave to another Brahmin. Why do you speak of such a novel thing?” To this, the Brahmin replied, “Sirs, this case is not one that follows worldly customs. This case arises from an agreement made between me and Sundaramurthi, the grandfather of Thirunavalur, with mutual consent and willingness. It is a deed written by his grandfather, Aruran, who willingly and gladly agreed that he and his entire lineage would be my slaves.”

“Therefore, this is not a case that follows worldly custom or tradition. It is a case that arises from one party violating an agreement made with mutual consent,” he explained. Hearing this, the assembly members looked at Sundaramurthi Swamigal and asked, “Is it right for you to tear up the deed that he presented, stating that your grandfather and your lineage are his slaves? Will snatching and tearing it bring you victory? This elder has come here to file a case against you and is accusing you.

“Nambi Arurar, what is your opinion on this?” Hearing this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal addressed the assembly members, saying, “O elders who have mastered all scriptures, you know that I am an Adisaiva. You also know that I belong to the Adisaiva lineage. Yet, this Brahmin claims that I am his slave. This is a mystery beyond my comprehension; it is something I cannot understand,” he said.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, addressing the assembly members, refers to the Lord, who appeared as the aged Brahmin, as “Dasaiyellam odunga muththan” (one whose flesh has withered away due to old age), as stated by Sekkizhar Swamigal. The subtlety here is that, on the surface, “Dasaiyellam odunga muththan” means that this elder has become so old that all the flesh on his body has wasted away, leaving him with only bones and skin. The inner meaning is that he is the ancient one, the elder, who, with his cosmic form, causes this entire world, created by Maya, to dissolve within himself. This is the inner meaning of ‘muththan’ (elder).

“Furthermore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal argues that Adisaiva Sivabrahmanas are those who are dedicated to Lord Shiva alone. Adisaivas are those who are subservient to the eternal Shiva, and not to anyone else.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that what this elder says is a mystery beyond his comprehension. The accomplishment that the Lord achieves is a mystery that cannot be grasped by anyone’s mind. This act, that of Lord Shiva appearing as an aged Brahmin, is the act of the one who is known as ‘Ulagaellam unarnthu odarku ariyavan’ (the one who is difficult to know and speak of, though known by all the world).

“Therefore, just like Lord Shiva himself, his actions are beyond our mental comprehension. The mind stands above the sense organs and organs of action. That mind which directs the individual soul, that soul-knowledge which illuminates illusion, and the possessor of that illusion, Mahesan, has come here as an elder Brahmin.

“Therefore, his actions, too, are an illusion beyond the grasp of the mind, as explained by Sivakavimani. This elder Brahmin performed a wonder that no one could even conceive of in their minds: he spoke through his mouth, called many to listen, showed a palm-leaf as proof, and then argued before this assembly. Everyone witnessed this event with their own eyes, yet it remained an illusion beyond their mental comprehension.

“Thus, the assembly, having heard the argument of Nambi Aruran, also known as Sundaramurthi Swamigal, looked at the elder Brahmin, who was the Lord, and said, “You claim that Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who belongs to Thiru-Navalur, is your slave.

“Therefore, you must prove that claim before us.” The assembly not only said this but also outlined three ways to prove the case. Sekkilar’s verse states: “Show one of the three: in practice, in document, or in the sight of others.” This means that to prove a case, one must employ three methods: practice, document, or the testimony of others. The assembly states that the case must be proven through one of these three types of evidence. In any case, the plaintiff or the accuser, must prove the accusation or the case; this is the underlying meaning.

“Furthermore, the assembly must declare and demonstrate the methods by which a case should be proven, establishing a legal framework. This is one of the duties of the assembly that dispenses justice.

“Therefore, they order the proof to be established by following one of the three methods: in practice, in document, or in sight. Among these three, ‘practice’ refers to common custom. This common custom is a practice that has been continuously followed by the wise from time immemorial. The second type of evidence is a ‘document,’ which refers to palm-leaf manuscripts or records that convey the details related to the case. The third method of proof, ‘sight,’ refers to the testimony of others. Sight refers to the direct testimony of someone who personally witnessed the details of the case with their own eyes.

“Thus, the assembly tells the elder Brahmin that the case must be proven by following one of these three methods. In response, the Brahmin states that he intends to prove it through a document, as the first method of proof, ‘practice,’ refers to common custom.

“Here, the idea of a Brahmin becoming a slave, or an Adisaiva Brahmin becoming a slave to another Brahmin, or one human becoming a slave to another, is neither in scriptural tradition nor in common custom. Based on this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal rejects the case brought by the elder.

“Therefore, the elder also states that this case is not based on common custom, and thus he will not prove it by the first method, which is ‘practice.’ Now, regarding the third method, ‘sight,’ which refers to the direct testimony of someone who witnessed the details of the case, if there were any such person, their testimony would be considered. However, all the events mentioned by the elder took place during the time of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather.

“Therefore, there is no one present now who witnessed them directly.

“Thus, it cannot be proven by the third method either. So, the elder states that he will prove it by the second method, which is a ‘document.’ He says, “I have the palm-leaf document that Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather wrote and gave to me. The palm-leaf I showed at the marriage pandal was a copy.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal tore and burned that one. He has arrived.

“But I possess the original palm-leaf, the authentic one. I am ready to show it here.

“However, if you promise to protect it from Nambi Aruran snatching and tearing it, as happened before, then I will show the original palm-leaf, the authentic one,” says the elder.

“Thus, the elder states that he will prove it through the second type of evidence, a document, among the three types: practice, document, and sight. Hearing this, the assembly members assured him, “We will provide protection for that palm-leaf; we will not allow any harm to come to it.” Upon this assurance, the elder Brahmin went to a secluded place, retrieved the original palm-leaf, and brought it to the assembly. The fact that he did not mention this original, authentic palm-leaf earlier, To prevent the thought from arising that this elder is now fabricating something new, Sekkilar, anticipating this thought in us, had already composed the scene where, when Nambi Arurar tore and burned the palm-leaf shown at the marriage pandal, the elder immediately declared that he would enslave him by showing the original palm-leaf. Sivakavimani states here that it is no wonder that the Lord, who creates all that is necessary for the salvation of beings from illusion and from all worlds—creating bodies, instruments, worlds, and experiences—would engage in such a play of original and copy palm-leaves. Sekkilar portrays the Lord as ‘Mayai Vallan’ (Master of Illusion), implying that He causes everyone to be enveloped in illusion while remaining untouched by it, shining as one beyond illusion. That ‘Mayai Vallan’, Sivakavimani explains the word thus.

“As the Lord showed the original palm-leaf, the village assembly appointed an official named Kanakkan to receive, examine, and read it. As the assembly ordered, Kanakkan respectfully received the document, the palm-leaf, from the elder, bowed, took it, removed the rolled cover, unrolled the scroll, and first examined and ascertained its antiquity.

“Then, for all the wise members of the assembly to hear, he began to read the text contained within. How does Sekkilar Swamigal describe Kanakkan as he reads the palm-leaf in his poem? He describes him as ‘Arul Peru Karanathan’ (one whose instruments are filled with grace). ‘Karanas’ refer to the internal and external instruments of our body. Sekkilar Swamigal states that all the instruments of Kanakkan, who reads the palm-leaf, are instruments filled with grace. This implies that he possesses internal and external instruments that have received divine grace to hold, see, and then read the contents of this palm-leaf, which the Lord Himself gave with His own hands. That is, without His showing, beings cannot know Him.

“Therefore, for someone to read that palm-leaf, which received the Lord’s grace, and the letters and words, which are the Lord’s grace, their instruments—that is, those Karanas— The mind, intellect, consciousness, ego, and the external instruments such as the organs of action and perception—all these instruments must be overflowing with the Lord’s divine grace; otherwise, one cannot read the Lord’s words.

“Therefore, Sekkilar Swamigal refers to him as ‘Arul Peru Karanathan’ (one whose instruments are filled with grace). What were the words on the palm-leaf shown by the Lord?

“Let us continue to ponder.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 06 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
Before the village assembly court of Thiru-Venney-Nallur, the Lord, disguised as an elder Brahmin, presented the original palm-leaf to the judges of the court, claiming Nambi Aruran as his slave. The assembly ordered an official to receive and read it. He respectfully received the palm-leaf from the elder Brahmin, bowed, unrolled the scroll, removed its cover, examined its antiquity, and, satisfied with its condition, began to read its contents. The message on the palm-leaf was as follows: “I, Aruran, of the Adisaiva lineage in Thiru-Navalur, where Brahmins reside, hereby declare for all to know: Arumarai Naval Adisaivan Aruran Seigai Perumuni Venney Nallur Pittanukku Yaanum Enbal Varumurai Marabulurum Vazhi Thondu Seidarku Olai Irumaiyal Ezhuthi Nernthen Itharku Ivai En Ezhuthu. This verse means: “I, Aruran, of the Adisaiva lineage in Thiru-Navalur, have, with full consent of mind and action, written and given this palm-leaf, agreeing that I and my descendants, generation after generation, shall serve Thiru-Venney-Nallur Pittan, the great sage.

“This is my signature.” Thus, the official read out the contents of the palm-leaf. Sekkilar describes this in his poem. This palm-leaf, in which two individuals enter into an agreement before the villagers and the assembly, The names, lineage, and places of residence of the two individuals entering into the agreement are detailed, followed by the terms of the agreement, signatures, and witnesses. This indicates the ancient practice of writing legal documents. The ‘Aruran’ mentioned here is the name of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather, that is, the father of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s father. We have already seen that Sadaiya Nayanar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s father, named his son Sundaramurthi Swamigal, or Nambi Arurar, after his own father, Aruran.

“After hearing the contents of the palm-leaf read by the official, the assembly members examined the signatures of the witnesses following the signatory’s signature and found them to be correct.

“Then, the assembly members addressed Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “If the signature on this palm-leaf is truly your grandfather’s, then examine it thoroughly and ascertain its authenticity.”

“Thus, after carefully examining the oral testimony of the elder Brahmin, who was the plaintiff in this case, and the palm-leaf document as evidence, they then turned to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the defendant, to hear his oral testimony and evidence. This clearly illustrates how meticulously and thoughtfully the judges of the village assembly in small villages administered justice in those days.

“Furthermore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal had already stated to the assembly that the elder Brahmin’s claim of servitude was an illusion beyond his comprehension.

“Therefore, the assembly members addressed Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “Understand this additional information as well.” That is, the assembly members tell Sundaramurthi Swamigal to look at this palm-leaf and the signatures within it with his own eyes, read it carefully with his mind, understand it clearly, and then comprehend it. When the assembly members asked Sundaramurthi Swamigal this, the elder Brahmin stated that he (Nambi Arurar) is not qualified or suitable to understand this deed of servitude. That is, Nambi Arurar had already expressed his inability by saying that it was incomprehensible to him.

“Moreover, he is a young man who does not know the ancient script.

“Therefore, the elder Brahmin states that it is not proper to question him (Nambi Arurar) in this manner.

“Furthermore, the Lord, the benevolent one who came to take him into His fold, preemptively suggested that if another document with the handwriting of Nambi Arurar, Sundaramurthi’s grandfather, could be found, it should be brought and compared with the signature on this palm-leaf to see if the two signatures match, and then a judgment should be given based on what seems just to their hearts. That is, the question is whether this palm-leaf was written by Nambi Arurar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather, himself, or if someone else wrote it imitating his handwriting.

“Therefore, to ascertain this, the Lord suggests bringing another palm-leaf with the handwriting of Arur Nambi’s

“The Lord suggests bringing another palm-leaf with the grandfather’s handwriting and comparing it. It is noteworthy that handwriting verification is still used in modern judicial investigations. In this poem, Sekkilar refers to the Lord as the benevolent one who forcefully takes souls into His fold.

“The Lord takes Nambi Arurar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, into His fold through this case. By initiating this case, regulating it, and guiding it, the Lord also takes this assembly into His fold.

“Moreover, by taking Nambi Arurar into His fold, He bestowed upon the world the Thiru-Thondar Thogai and other Thiru-Varams, and the Periya Puranam which arose from the Thiru-Thondar Thogai, thereby providing for all beings in this world, whether they desire it or not. Sivakavimani explains the phrase ‘valiya aatkollum vallal’ (the benevolent one who forcefully takes souls into His fold) by stating that Sekkilar Swamigal here implies that the Lord is a benevolent one whose profession is to save all these souls, whether they desire it or not, by forcefully taking them into His fold. Nambigal’s Thiru-Varam states, ‘It is your duty to remove the sorrows of those who worship you.’ Therefore, in accordance with the elder Brahmin’s request, the assembly members also decided to do so. To dispel Nambi Arurar’s confusion, they retrieved another palm-leaf written by Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather himself from the treasury where it was kept safely. They compared its handwriting with the handwriting on the palm-leaf shown by the elder Brahmin, and both signatures were found to be identical. They conclude that both are identical.

“Thus, it is a surprising fact that in those times, there was a system of preserving documents and records containing the signatures of citizens in public forums and storing them in treasuries. Even today, we see government registration officials preserving the signatures of their citizens and safeguarding documents by photographing and copying them.

“Similarly, it is known from such historical accounts that a more advanced system than this existed among the Tamils in those days. Having concluded the inquiry, the assembly members addressed Nambi Arurar, saying, “Nambi Arurar, the palm-leaf written by your grandfather and the palm-leaf shown by this elderly Brahmin here, The signatures on both are identical.

“Therefore, there is nothing more we can do. Hence, Nambi Arurar, Sundaramurthi, you have lost this case to this sage. It is your duty to accept his command and serve him, doing whatever tasks he assigns.

“Therefore, you are his slave.” Hearing this verdict, Nambi Arurar said, “If this is my fated judgment, how can I not accept it?” Sekkilar Swamigal refers to the assembly members who delivered this fair judgment as ‘men of great virtue.’ Sivakavimani explains this by saying that these assembly members, the judges of the court, were men of great virtue because they were righteous judges who delivered justice impartially and excellently. That is, they did not reject the case outright, nor did they deliver a one-sided judgment, but thoroughly investigated and sought the truth, even though the case was brought on the premise of whether it was proper for a Brahmin to be enslaved by another.

“Furthermore, the members of this assembly were also Brahmins. Even though they were Brahmins, they set aside their lineage and other considerations, stood apart from them, and administered justice fairly.

“Therefore, Sekkilar states that they were ‘men of great virtue.’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal, hearing this verdict, submitted to it. In this era, Just as there are hierarchical court systems, with lower courts, then intermediate courts, then state-level courts, and finally the supreme court at the national level, such systems also existed in ancient times. Despite all this, our Sundaramurthi Swamigal submits and bows to the verdict of this village assembly court. In this era, people, driven by arrogance, do not abide by the verdicts given by lower courts and instead go to higher courts, where they continue to boast and fight. This is common among people of this age.

“However, despite having similar opportunities, Sundaramurthi Swamigal abandoned all considerations of lineage, well-being, youth, and time, completely suppressed his arrogance, and with great With great humility, he submits to the verdict of the assembly members. Sivakavimani points out that there is a Thiru-Vasagam verse that perfectly describes this state: “Sathi kulam pirappu ennum suzhippattu thadumarum aathamili naayenai allal aruthu aatkondhu pedhai gunam piraruruvam yaan enathu endru uraimaithu kodhil amuthu aananai kulaavuthillai kandene.” This Thiru-Vasagam verse, Sivakavimani says, now perfectly portrays the state of Nambi Arurar. Distinctions such as caste and lineage are necessary only for this worldly life. When the state of being enslaved to others disappears and one becomes enslaved only to the Lord, that name, Once that state arrives, all worldly matters such as caste, lineage, education, and wealth are pushed aside, overcome, and disappear. At that very moment, arrogance completely vanishes, its nature fades and subsides, and then a state of bliss will come. This is what we can understand from these events.

“Then, the court members addressed the elder Brahmin standing before them, saying, “Oh, noble sage, in the palm-leaf you showed, you mentioned our glorious Thiru-Venney-Nallur as your village. If that is true, then show us your ancient house in this village and the life you have lived here from time immemorial.” Nambi Arurar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, is submitting to the verdict of the assembly, isn’t he?

“Now, that verdict It is important to bring into practice, not only to deliver the verdict, but also to bring that verdict into practice, which is also the responsibility of the courts.

“Therefore, if Sundaramurthi Swamigal is to serve this elderly Brahmin and perform duties for him, then that elderly Brahmin must have a home and a life, right? Only then can Sundaramurthi Swamigal perform slave service to that elderly Brahmin, and that elderly Brahmin can rule over Sundaramurthi Swamigal. If that home and life do not exist for that elderly Brahmin in this village, then the verdict given would become meaningless.

“Therefore, the assembly members request to show the home of that elderly Brahmin and the life he lives there. Why did the assembly members ask? He said, “Show that ancient home and the ancient life that has been passed down through generations.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather had written that “I and those who come in my lineage will serve you.” Therefore, from the time of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s grandfather, that ancient home and life must have existed for this elderly Brahmin.

“So, they ask to show the ancient home and way of life. That virtuous sage, who spoke and won this unparalleled and novel case, looked at the assembly members and said, “If none of you know me, then come with me; I will show you my home and my life.” Having said this, the elderly Brahmin walked ahead, and the assembly members, the Brahmins who came from Thiru-Navalur, and Nambi Aaroorar, walked behind. That elderly Brahmin entered the Thiru-Arulthurai temple within the Thiru-Vennainallur sthalam. After he entered the Thiru-Arulthurai temple, he suddenly disappeared. Those who had come with him, the others, could not find that elderly man. Everyone stood bewildered.

“Let us continue to ponder what happens next.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 07 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In Thiru-Vennainallur, in the court, Lord Shiva, who came as an elderly Brahmin, showed the palm-leaf document and proved Sundaramurthi Swamigal to be his slave, thereby winning the case.

Therefore, as requested by the assembly, to show his home and life in Thiru-Vennainallur, Lord Shiva, the elderly Brahmin, walks ahead. Behind him walks Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the Nambiyaroorar. Others follow behind. That Brahmin enters the Thiru-Arulthurai temple in the village of Thiru-Vennainallur. Those who had been seeing him until he entered the temple are bewildered when they find him missing after he enters the temple. In this song, Sekkizhar Swamigal calls that elderly Brahmin a virtuous sage.

This means he is the fruit of all virtues and one whose form is virtue itself.

Furthermore, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that the crowd and Nambi followed that elderly Brahmin. The crowd refers to the gathering of others, and Nambi refers to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Why does Sekkizhar mention the crowd and Nambi separately? It is to indicate that the Lord has come to separate Sundaramurthi Swamigal from this world and take possession of him.

Therefore, leaving the others, only Nambi, only Sundaramurthi Swamigal, will follow the Lord and live in servitude. This elderly Brahmin, in the village of Thiru-Vennainallur, enters the Thiru-Arulthurai temple.

Here, Thiru-Arulthurai is the specific name of that temple. That is, generally in Shaivite Thiru-Sthalams, the Thiru-Sthalam has one name, and the temples within those Thiru-Sthalams have a separate name. Thiru-Vennainallur is the name of the village, and Thiru-Arulthurai is the name of the temple in that village.

Similarly, Thiru-Pennagadam is the name of the village, and Thiru-Thoongaanai Maadam is the unique name given to the temple in that village.

In this way, in all other Sthalams, the Thiru-Sthalams have one name, and the temples within these Thiru-Sthalams have a separate name, which is a specialty of Shaivism. Sekkizhar describes the scene where the elderly Brahmin enters, saying, “Arulthuraiye Pukkar” (He entered Thiru-Arulthurai). This means that the Lord is present in the realm of grace. This indicates that the Lord is in the realm of grace. It is common practice to call the bank of a river or sea a ‘thurai’ (shore/port). The name of this temple is Thiru-Arulthurai, meaning that the Lord, from this shore of grace, rescues those who have fallen into the ocean of worldly existence, the ocean of samsara.

He stands at Arulthurai and brings them to shore.

The Lord himself is the boat, the raft, the vessel that brings those in the ocean of samsara to shore. And for those souls who reach the shore, the Lord grants grace as the shore itself. It means that the Lord, from this Thiru-Arulthurai temple, to rescue the people of the world, rescued Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the Samayacharyar, and Meykanda Swamigal, the Santhanaacharyar. At another Thiru-Thurai, Thiru-Thuraiyur, he rescued Arunanathi Sivacharyar, a Santhanaacharyar. Among all these Thuraigal, at the great Thiru-Perunthurai, he rescued Manikkavasagar Swamigal.

Thus, as soon as the elderly Brahmin entered the Thiru-Arulthurai temple, he disappeared from the sight of everyone else. No one else could see him. Unless the Lord reveals himself by his grace, these souls cannot see him. It is the nature of these souls to see him when he reveals himself by his grace, and to be bewildered when he conceals himself. “Who would not see if He reveals?” is the sacred utterance of that Lord. Until now, the Lord, in the guise of an elderly Brahmin, appeared to everyone, but no one knew he was the Lord.

But now, when he disappeared, everyone realized that he was indeed the Lord. As he disappeared from their sight, they stood bewildered. For the Lord, that incomprehensible being, who until now had stood before everyone as an elderly Brahmin, visible to their eyes, tangible to their touch, a subject of their speech and scorn, and a party to their lawsuit, suddenly disappeared, causing bewilderment to all.

Thus, that Lord, who came as an old Brahmin, entered the Thiru-Arulthurai temple. Since Sundaramurthi Swamigal was judged to be a slave to that elderly man, he was obligated to follow that elderly man.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal also follows that elderly man into the temple.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal follows with confusion and doubt as to why that elderly Brahmin is entering the temple. Why is he confused? Because the Lord enshrined in Thiru-Arulthurai in this village of Thiru-Vennainallur is the master to whom the Adisaivars have been slaves generation after generation. Since Sundarar belongs to the Adisaiva lineage, the Lord in the form of the Lingam enshrined within is his master.

But the elderly man who came today is one who took possession of Sundaramurthi Swamigal from his father’s father’s time.

Therefore, he is a newly acquired master. This elderly Brahmin is inside the temple in the form of a Lingam, who has been taking possession of Sundarar, who belongs to the Adisaiva lineage, from time immemorial, generation after generation.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal follows with doubt as to why this newly acquired master entered the temple of our old master, the Lord. While the rest of the group stood bewildered outside, only Sundaramurthi Swamigal followed alone. As he went inside and called out to his new master, the old Brahmin, Lord Shiva, along with Goddess Parvati, appeared to Sundarar in the sky, seated on his bull, Rishabha. Here are some subtleties given by Sivakavimani for this song: Sekkizhar Swamigal shows in the song that Sundaramurthi Swamigal called out to the elderly Brahmin who went inside with great desire. Sivakavimani says that this word “great desire” (peruviruppu) is the love that Sundaramurthi Swamigal had for the Lord from before, that is, from a very long time ago, and the penance he performed. The love and wisdom that were hidden until now, now shone as mature love and mature wisdom, and the power of concealment was removed, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal called out with great desire.

That is why the Lord appeared there, says Sivakavimani. And now, Lord Shiva appears with Uma Devi, seated on Rishabha. Before this, the Lord came as an elderly Brahmin to the wedding pandal, which was the form of a Vibhatnika Vanaprastha Ashram, that is, a form where he came alone without his wife, Uma Devi. That was the Lord’s Thirodhanam, the power of concealment.

Thus, by the power of concealment, Thirodhanam, the Lord now comes to bestow grace upon the souls that have been ripened by the power of concealment.

Therefore, now, with Uma Devi, the power of grace, he manifests and shines in that Thiru-Arulthurai temple.

Thus, the Lord, who appeared with Parvati, began to reveal the truth to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “Sundara, you were formerly serving me on Mount Kailash by offering flower garlands and sacred ash caskets.

“At that time, you set your heart on Kamalini and Anindithai, the companions of Goddess Parvati on Mount Kailash.

“Therefore, by my command, you attained this birth. Even though you attained this birth, so that this sorrowful life would not pursue you, as per the promise I had already given you in Kailash, I followed you and, in the presence of the Brahmins of Thiru-Vennainallur, in that assembly, I showed the palm-leaf document and took possession of you, thus revealing the truth.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s intended married life is called a sorrowful life by the Lord. Regarding this, Sivakavimani explains: Generally, married life is a joyful life, and slave life is a sorrowful life. This is a common understanding in the world that we all perceive. The Lord, coming as a Brahmin, stopped Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s married life and took him as a slave. From an ordinary worldly perspective, it would seem to us that the Lord stopped Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s joyful married life and gave him a sorrowful slave life.

“But what did the Lord actually do? He made Sundaramurthi Swamigal realize that married life brings sorrow. If Sundaramurthi Swamigal had accepted that marriage, it would have paved the way for accumulating more and more karma and pushed him further into the cycle of rebirth.

“But because the Lord came as a Brahmin and took possession of him, Sundaramurthi Swamigal was blessed with the great fortune of serving only the Lord, and instead of accumulating more karma, he had the opportunity to perform only Shiva Punya and Shiva Puja, which granted him permanent liberation and restored him to his life on Mount Kailash. This truth of grace will become clear to us.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, hearing the words of the Lord, like a young calf hearing the lowing of its mother cow, leaped, jumped, and began to cry out loud. His entire body, from head to toe, was filled with goosebumps. Placing his hands on his head, he prayed with tears in his eyes, “Lord, I denied you before, saying I was not your slave. Yet, you came and forcibly took possession of me! What a gracious act of yours!” As the Lord performed this divine grace, five celestial drums sounded in the sky. The Devas showered a rain of Kalpaka flowers and rejoiced.

“The Vedas resonated. Sivakavimani explains what Sekkizhar Swamigal said about the events that occur when the Lord’s divine grace manifests, as follows: The Lord’s divine Whenever divine manifestations and other miracles occur, five celestial drums automatically sound, which is the tradition. These five celestial drums, or five divine musical instruments, are: Thorkaruvi (percussion instruments made of hide), Thulaikkaruvi (wind instruments), Narambukkaruvi (string instruments), Kanjakkaruvi (cymbals and gongs), and Midatrukaruvi (vocal music). Among these, Thorkaruvi refers to instruments like the drum (perigai). Thulaikkaruvi refers to instruments like the flute (pullangulal). Narambukkaruvi refers to instruments like the veena.

“Kanjakkaruvi refers to instruments like cymbals (thalam). Midatrukaruvi refers to singing with the throat to produce the seven musical notes.

“Next, the Devas shower a rain of Kalpaka flowers from the sky.

“Then, the Vedas themselves resound. By the command of the Lord, the sounds of the Vedas, without anyone uttering them, emanated from the primal sound itself and resonated. What does the Lord say in response to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who stood weeping and worshipping?

“Let us continue to ponder.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 08 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thaduthaatkonda Puranam, at the Thiru-Vennainallur sthalam, in the Thiru-Arulthurai temple, the Lord appears to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, seated on Rishabha, accompanied by Parvati. Seeing Sundarar weeping and worshipping, the Lord begins to speak thus. We can understand this from the following song by Sekkizhar.

The Lord says: “And you, by speaking harshly, have gained the name Vanthondan. To us, the most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is song.

“Therefore, on this earth, sing Tamil songs to us,” said the Lord with the mouth that sings the sacred Vedas. The meaning of this is that Lord Shiva addresses Sundara Lord Shiva addresses Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, “Because you vehemently spoke against me, from today onwards you shall be known as Vanthondan (the harsh devotee). To me, the most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is songs of praise.

“Therefore, on this earth, sing Tamil songs in praise of me,” thus spoke the Lord, whose sacred mouth sings the Vedas, to Nambi.

“The Lord says to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, “You spoke harshly to me, you reviled me, did you not?
