“Therefore, from today onwards, you shall be called Vanthondan.” This harsh speaking refers to when Lord Shiva came as a Brahmin at the wedding pandal, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal spoke and reviled him, saying, “Is he a madman or a Brahmin?” For that reason, the Lord bestows the name Vanthondar upon Sundaramurthi Swamigal here. Since the Lord himself desired and bestowed this name, Sundaramurthi Swamigal has used this name Vanthondar in many places in his Thevarams.

“Similarly, we should note here that the Lord bestowed the name Navukkarasu upon Thiru-Navukkarasar and also graced Manikkavasagar Peruman. These are called Diksha Namams (initiation names). Aaroorar Nambi was the name given to Sundaramurthi Swamigal by his parents.

“Now, the name given by the Lord is Vanthondar, which is a Diksha Namam. Sivakavimani explains various subtleties for this song.

“Lord Shiva says, “To us, the most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is song.” He says that the Lord Shiva is one who has no desires or aversions for himself. The service that Lord Shiva, who has no desires or aversions, gladly accepts is praise through songs. Why does he gladly accept these praises and songs? It is to grant salvation to those souls who sing these praises with melting hearts. It is to bestow his grace upon the souls who sing these praises and songs.

“That is why the Lord gladly accepts them.

“Furthermore, here, “archanai” (worship) stands as a general term encompassing various forms of service and worship.

“The Lord says, “To us, the most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is song,” does he not? The word “song” here should be understood as a song that arises from the love for the Lord, overflowing from that love, and expressed as a manifestation of that love.

“Thus, it is such a song, spontaneously flowing from an abundance of love, that the Lord will delight in and bless.

“Furthermore, in response to Sundarar’s request, “You have taken me as your slave; what should this slave do now?” the Lord reveals Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s duty, saying, “Now, go to all the Thiru-Sthalams on this earth where I have graciously manifested, and sing Tamil songs in my praise.” The Lord who spoke thus is the one who, to enable souls to know and attain Him, himself composed and sang the Vedas with his sacred mouth.

“Therefore, the Lord addressed Sundarar, saying, “Sundara, I have sung the Vedas with My holy mouth. You shall compose Tamil hymns equal to those Vedas, as My utterance from within you, for the salvation of this world.” The Lord, whom Thiru-Mal and Thiru-Mugan sought in vain and later found by praising with the sacred five-letter mantra, thus commanded him to sing. With a heart contemplating the method of singing according to this command, Nambiyaroorar, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, stood with folded hands, reverently meditating on the holy feet of the Lord who performs the sacred dance in the Thillai Chithrambalam, in the Chitsabhai. “You came as an old Brahmin, a Vedic scholar, and won Me in a dispute. That was Your act of grace,” I stood, not understanding. Even so, O flawless ambrosia who dispelled my ignorance and saved me, what shall I know and what shall I say to sing in the great ocean of Your qualities?

“Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal appealed to the Lord.

“The Lord transcends word and meaning, and is difficult for all beings in this world to comprehend and praise.

“Therefore, to know Him and to praise Him, He Himself must inspire from within.

“Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, seeking the words and meaning to sing of the Lord, appealed to the Lord Himself. Just like the Lord, His devotees also possess the same nature.

“Therefore, He To sing of the devotees, that is, later in Thiru-Arur, when the Lord commanded him to sing of the Lord’s devotees, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also asked there that the Lord Himself should provide the opening line. And there too, the Lord Himself provided the opening line.

“Thus, as Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stood seeking words and meaning to sing of the Lord, Sekkizhar’s verse states that the Lord spoke as follows: “Looking upon the devotee with grace, the Lord with the crescent moon said, ‘Since you once called Me a madman, sing of Me as ‘Madman’ (Pithan).’” The great devotee, who stood there, began to sing of the Lord who enslaved him. The meaning of this is that the loving Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the staunch devotee, was graced by the Lord’s holy gaze.

“The Lord said, “Sundara, in the marriage pandal, you once called Me a madman or a Brahmin.

“Therefore, sing of Me by My name, ‘Madman’ (Pithan).” Regarding this verse, Sivakavimani offers these subtle interpretations: “Anbanai arulin nokki Anganar arulichcheyvar” (Looking upon the devotee with grace, the Lord with the crescent moon said) – the first line of Sekkizhar Swamigal’s verse, “arulin nokki,” means a gaze of grace. That is, the gracious gaze of the Lord, the Guru, completely removes our ignorance and bestows complete knowledge; this is a scriptural truth.

“Thus, with a gaze of grace, He looked upon Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and then bestowed grace, that is, spoke words of grace. That is, the latter part of the verse, “Sing of Me as ‘Madman’ (Pithan),” is what the Lord says, is it not? That itself is a word of grace. “Anbanai arulin nokki” means a gaze of grace. “Anganar arulichcheyvar” means an act of grace, that is, a gracious teaching. This is a method of initiation (Diksha).

“The Lord previously commanded, “Sing in sweet Tamil and perform Shiva worship,” did He not?

“Therefore, the corresponding Shiva Diksha must also be performed.

“Thus, following the Agamic rule that such Shiva Diksha is necessary, the Lord cast His gracious gaze upon Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and imparted the gracious teaching as follows: “You previously called Me a madman, therefore, sing of Me as ‘Madman’.” Whoever thinks of the Lord in whatever way, Shiva will stand and bestow grace upon them in that very manner. Sivakavimani explains that this truth is conveyed here. In the Thiru-Vilayadal Puranam, when piglets, having lost their mother sow, were crying without milk, the Lord transformed Himself into their mother sow and gave them milk. This is a gracious historical event.

“Here, since Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asked the Lord, “Are you a madman or a Brahmin?”, the Lord commands him to sing using that very term, “madman.” Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stood, requesting words and meaning from the Lord, did he not? The Lord, with His gracious gaze, revealed the meaning for singing.

“Now, by saying, “Sing of Me as ‘madman’,” He revealed the word for singing. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, likewise, began to sing of the Lord. He began to sing of the Lord. Sekkizhar describes it in the following verse: “Koththar malar kuzhalaal oru kooraay adiyavar paal mey thaayinum iniyaanai avviyan naavalar perumaan Piththa piraisoodi ena perithaan thiruppathigam itthaarani muthalaam ulagellaam uyya eduththaar.” (He who has a part of the Goddess with clustered flowers in her hair, and is sweeter than a mother to His devotees, the great poet, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, began to sing the great Thiru-Pathigam, “Piththa Piraisoodi,” for the salvation of this earth and all worlds.) The meaning is that Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, as commanded by Lord Shiva, who holds Uma Devi in one part of His body and is therefore sweeter than a mother to His devotees, began to sing this glorious Thiru-Pathigam, starting with “Piththa Piraisoodi,” for the salvation of this earthly world and all other worlds.

“Now, the Pathigam, the Sthalam: Thiru-Vennainallur. Indalam.

““Piththa pirai soodi perumaaney arulaalaa Eththaan maravaadhe ninaikkindren manaththunnai Vaiththaay pennai thenpaal vinnai nalloor arulthuraiyul Aththaa un kaalaayini allel enalaame.” (O Madman, wearer of the crescent moon, O Lord, O gracious one, How can I forget You? I keep You in my mind. You have placed me in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode. O Father, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Naayen pala naalum ninaippinri mana thunnai Peyaayth thirindheydhen peralaaga arul petren Veyaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arulthuraiyul Aayaa un kaalaayini allel enalaame.” (Like a dog, for many days, without thinking of You, I wandered like a ghost and attained You; I received Your grace. O Lord, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Manne maravaadhe ninaikkindren,”

“Manaththunnai ponne manithaane vayiramme poruthunthi Minnaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thooraiyul Annne unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (O King, I never forget You, I keep You in my mind. O Gold, O Gem, O Diamond, O Lord who resides in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Mudiyen ini piraven peren mooven petra moorththi Kodiyen pala poyye urai penai koorikkolli Sediyar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thooraiyul Adikeel unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (I cannot end, I will not be born again, I will not grow old. O Lord who has received these three. I am a cruel one, speaking many lies, a backbiter. O Lord, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Paatham panivaargal peru pandam madhu pani yaathan porulaanen arivillen arulaal.” “Thaathaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thooraiyul Aathi unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (O Primordial One, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Thannaar mathisoodi thazhal polum thirumeni Ennaar pura moondrum eriyunna nagai seythaay Mannaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thuraiyul Annaa unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (You wear the cool moon, Your holy body is like fire.

“You smiled and consumed the three cities. O Lord, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Oonaay uyir aanaay udal aanaay ulagaanaay Vaanaay nilanaanaay kadalaanaay malaiyaanaay Thennaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thuraiyul.” “Aanaay unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (You became flesh, You became life, You became body, You became the world. You became sky, You became earth, You became ocean, You became mountain. O Lord, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Ettraar pura moondrum eriyunna silai thottaay Thetraathan sollith thiriveno sekkar vaaneer Ettraay pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thooraiyul Aattraay unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (You drew Your bow and burned the three cities of the enemies. Shall I wander speaking things I do not understand, O Lord of the red sky, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Mazhuvaal valan endhi maraiyothi mangai pangaa Thozhuvaar avar thuyaraayina theerthallun thozhile Sezhuvvaar pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thooraiyul Azhagaa unakkaalaayini allel enalaame.” (You hold the battle-axe in Your right hand, You chant the Vedas, You are the partner of the Goddess.

“It is Your duty to remove the sorrows of those who worship You. O Beautiful One, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, I am Your servant; how can I say otherwise?) “Kaarur punalai.” “Seythik karai kalliththirai kaiyaal Paarur pugazhai thiththigal panmaamaniyunthi Seeroor pennai thenpaal vennai nalloor arul thuraiyul Aaruranam perumaarkalaame Thiruchitrambalam.” (With hands that break the banks of the dark waters, You scatter fame throughout the world, O Lord who bears many gems, in Thiru-Vennainallur, south of the Pennai, in the sacred abode, we are Your servants.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 09 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled “Thaduthatkonda Puranam,” we have contemplated up to the point where Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Pathigam “Piththa Piraisoodi” by the grace of Lord Shiva in Thiru-Vennainallur. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how this Pathigam originated in a verse, which is as follows: “Koththar malar kuzhalaal oru kooraay adiyavar paal mey thaayinum iniyaanai avviyan naavalar perumaan Piththa piraisoodi ena perithaan thiruppathigam itthaarani muthalaam ulagellaam uyya eduththaar.” The meaning of this verse is that our Lord Shiva, who holds Uma Devi in one part of His body and is therefore sweeter than a mother to His devotees, As commanded by Him, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Nambigal of Navalur, sings the great Thiru-Pathigam beginning with “Piththa Piraisoodi.” How does he sing it? He sings it for the salvation of this earthly world and all other worlds.

Now, let us look at the explanation given by Sivakavimani for this verse.

Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Uma Devi as “Koththar malar kuzhalaal” (she with hair adorned with clustered flowers). Uma Devi always has hair adorned with flowers. This signifies that Uma Devi is eternally auspicious (Nithya Kalyani). The “Koththar malar” mentioned here refers to the Konrai flowers that bloom in clusters.

Thus, Uma Devi is described as having hair like the fruit that emerges from these clustered Konrai flowers. The meaning is that Uma Devi has hair like the fruit that emerges from the clustered Konrai flowers.

The Lord wears these Konrai flowers. The right side of the Lord’s body is His part, and the left side is the Goddess’s part.

Therefore, Sivakavimani states that the dark hair of the Goddess is like the fruits that result from the blossoming of the Konrai flowers worn by our Lord on His matted locks on His right side.

Thus, the Lord has Uma Devi, who is so specially adorned, on His left side. Uma Devi is the Lord’s gracious divine form.

Therefore, whenever the Lord comes to bestow grace upon beings, He comes with Uma Devi, the Goddess.

Thus, the Lord is sweet to His devotees, sweeter than a mother, only when He manifests with the Goddess as one part, as one half. This is Sivakavimani’s explanation.

The Lord is thus sweet to His devotees.

Therefore, Sivakavimani states that He is not sweet to those who are not His devotees. As evidence for this, he quotes the Thiru-Vasagam line, “Allathaarku allatha vediyanai” (the Brahmin who is not for those who are not). Although the Lord bestows grace according to all beings, to His devotees, He is sweeter than a mother. In worldly life, an ordinary mother thinks only of what is sweet and pleasant for her children.

But she does not have the power to accomplish all that she thinks, nor does she possess the knowledge for it. Unlike this worldly mother, the Lord, for His children, the devotees, possesses full power and full knowledge—that is, omniscience and omnipotence—to provide what is sweet and beneficial, according to the spiritual maturity of each devotee.

Therefore, our Lord is far superior to the ordinary mothers we see in the world; He is the one who does the sweetest things for His devotees.

Furthermore, a mother can only provide what is necessary, sweet, and beneficial for her child in this one birth. But the Lord, who is sweeter than a mother, Is the one who possesses the power and love to provide what is necessary and beneficial for beings in every birth they take. Not only that, but that Lord alone has the power to prevent that birth itself. A mother does good for a being by causing a body to come into existence in one birth.

But the Lord first gives a body to beings who are eternally bound in the state of ignorance (Anava) in their primal state. In every birth, He provides the necessary body, instruments, worlds, and experiences (Thanu, Karana, Bhuvana, Bhoga).

Thus, whether viewed scripturally or philosophically, the Lord appears to us as sweeter than a mother. Manikkavasagar also says, “Thaayodu” “Than padum thukka saagara thuyaridai pizhaiththum” (and escaped the ocean of sorrow suffered with the mother).

Thus, Sivakavimani offers this subtle interpretation for the lines “Koththar malar kuzhalaal oru kooraay adiyavar paan mey thaayinum iniyaan” in Sekkizhar Swamigal’s verse.

Now, let us see how Sivakavimani offers subtle interpretations for the lines “Avviyan naavalar perumaan Piththa piraisoodi ena peritham thiruppathigam.” Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stood before the Lord, asking, “From the great ocean of Your qualities, what shall I take and sing?” To this, the Lord commanded, “When I came as a Brahmin in the marriage pandal, you called Me a madman, did you not?

“Therefore, begin your song with that very term, ‘Piththa’.” Therefore, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also begins the Pathigam with “Piththa Piraisoodi.” Previously, at the moment he was about to marry the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar in Puthur, the Lord came in the form of an old Brahmin and prevented the marriage. But at that time, Sundarar did not know it was the Lord.

“Therefore, he unknowingly called the old man a madman (Piththa). But now, even after the Lord has appeared as Rishabarudar, he begins to sing “Piththa Piraisoodi.” If the question arises whether he can sing “Piththa” even after knowing the Lord, he sings it now because the Lord Himself commanded him to sing “Piththa.” Now, the second meaning for singing “Piththa” is that “Piththa Piraisoodi” are the first two terms. Sivakavimani offers this second explanation: “I am not a madman as you previously said; you are the one who wears the crescent moon.” This is the second explanation given by Sivakavimani.

“Now, the third explanation for the phrase “Piththa Piraisoodi”: The Lord is the one who wears the crescent moon.

“The Lord wearing the crescent moon is an act that demonstrates His compassion. Out of compassion for the moon, the Lord takes it and wears it on His head.

“Thus, it is because of the compassion the Lord has for beings that He is called “Piththan” (madman). That is, the Lord, out of compassion for beings and for His devotees, wanders as if mad in continually enslaving His devotees. In this sense, he sings “Piththa.”

“The Lord behaves as if mad, or is mad, towards devotees who are mad about Him. This is the inner meaning.

“Thus, Sivakavimani provides explanations for the phrases “Piththa Piraisoodi.” Not only that, but for every action performed by devotees, there is always a precedent. Devotees of Shiva are accustomed to following what their predecessors and Gurus have done.

“So, if one asks if there is a precedent for Sundarar singing “Piththa Piraisoodi,” Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman has sung “Piththa Piraisoodi” in Thiru-Erukaththampuliyur Thevaram. Thiru-Navukkarasar Peruman has sung “Peypaada nadamaadum Piththan thannai” (the madman who dances while ghosts sing) in Thiru-Naraiyur Thiruthandagam.

“Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to this Pathigam, “Piththa Piraisoodi,” as a “Peritham Thiru-Pathigam” (great sacred hymn).

“First, what is a Pathigam? A Pathigam is a collection of ten verses. Sometimes, there can be eleven or even twelve verses. This Pathigam, which begins with “Piththa Piraisoodi,” also consists of ten verses.

“Therefore, it is a Pathigam.

“Furthermore, it is a Thiru-Pathigam (sacred hymn) because it praises Lord Shiva. Not only that, Sekkizhar Swamigal calls it a “Peritham Thiru-Pathigam.” “Peritham” means that which makes us great. That is, because this Pathigam grants the path to liberation (Mukthi), which is the way to salvation, to all who sing it with devotion, this Pathigam is a great sacred hymn (Peritham Thiru-Pathigam).

“Furthermore, Lord Shiva, who is the subject of this Pathigam, is the greatest.

“Therefore, by singing of this greatest one, this Pathigam also becomes a great sacred hymn. Not only that, but previously, when the Lord came as an old man, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal called Him a madman (Piththa). Even though a devotee called Him a madman, the Lord accepted that fault as a virtue and bestowed grace upon beings, and upon Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

“Therefore, this Pathigam is a great sacred hymn. That is why, when describing this moment of grace when Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this Pathigam, Sekkizhar Swamigal says in the final line, “Itthaarani muthalaam ulagellaam uyya” (for the salvation of this earth and all worlds). That is, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings “Piththa Piraisoodi.” Do you know why this is? Sekkilār Swamigal states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings this Thiru-pathikam so that all living beings in all worlds, starting from this earthly world, may attain salvation and liberation. Sekkilār Swamigal says, “Iththāraṇi muthalām ulagu ellām uyya” (so that this earth and all other worlds may attain salvation). That is, this earthly world, this land, and all other worlds may attain salvation.

“There are countless worlds in the upper realms and countless universes in the cosmos. While there are innumerable such exalted worlds, Sekkilār Swamigal places this earthly world, the world we live in, first, saying “Iththāraṇi” (this earth), including this world and others. Sekkilār Swamigal states that this is for the salvation of all, placing this earthly world, where we live, first. Why? Because other worlds are places where living beings can only experience the results of their abundant merits or sins.

“However, this earthly world, Bhuloka, is a place where one can perform Shiva puja and be freed from the cycle of births.

“Therefore, Sekkilār Swamigal places this earth first. The great fortune of performing Shiva puja, accumulating Shiva’s blessings, and being liberated from the cycle of birth to attain moksha is a great blessing bestowed only upon the living beings of this earthly world. Other There are no such opportunities in the lower worlds.

“Therefore, this earthly world is supreme. Sekkilār Swamigal describes the divine event of this Thiru-pathikam’s composition, stating that Sundaramurthi Swamigal sang this Thiru-pathikam, “Piththā Piraisūdi,” for the salvation of all living beings in this earthly world and all other worlds. In the next verse, he elaborates on the unique melodic structure of this Thiru-pathikam.

“We shall proceed with our reflection. ”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 10 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, under the section “Thaduththāṭkoṇḍa Purāṇam,” we reflected on the circumstances surrounding the composition of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s Thiru-pathikam, “Piththā Piraisūdi,” and the commentary provided by Sivakavimani on the words of “Piththā Piraisūdi.” Now, Sekkilār Peruman, in the next verse, elaborates extensively on the melodic structure of this Thiru-pathikam, “Piththā Piraisūdi.” Just as we now speak of “music” for songs, there was formerly a tradition of setting Tamil songs to a system called “paṇ.” Scholars state that 23 to 25 paṇs are used in the Thevaram Thiru-pathikams. This Thiru-pathikam, which begins with “Piththā Piraisūdi,” is set in the paṇ “Indalam.” It is set in the paṇ called Indalam.

Therefore, Sekkilār Swamigal explains the melodic structure of this Thiru-pathikam in great detail here. In the Periya Puranam, in the histories of Thiru-nāvukkarasu Swamigal, Thiru-gnānasambandha Peruman, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal, Sekkilār Peruman includes details of the Thiru-sthalams they visited, the circumstances under which the Thiru-pathikams were composed there, and the specific Thiru-pathikams they sang.

In this context, since this is the first instance where the circumstances of a Thevaram Thiru-pathikam’s composition are explained, at this initial point—that is, where details about Thevaram Thiru-pathikams appear—he elaborates extensively on this paṇ structure. Sivakavimani states that we should apply this same criterion and formula when interpreting all subsequent Thiru-pathikams mentioned by him. Sivakavimani states that we should apply this same criterion and formula when interpreting all subsequent Thiru-pathikams mentioned by him.

Now, the verse: “Muzhalil kuraiyā nilai mummaippaḍi kūḍum kiḻamaiyināl niṟaipāṇiyin isai kōḷ puṇar nīḍum pukaḻ vakaiyāl iṟaiyāṉ makiḻ isai pāḍinaṉ nellā nikarilāṉ.” Its meaning: Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings the Tamil musical composition that delights the Lord, that is, this Thiru-pathikam called “Piththā Piraisūdi.” The paṇ of this Thiru-pathikam is Indalam. This paṇ called Indalam falls into which category? Scholars generally state that there are eleven characteristics of a paṇ. These eleven characteristics are fully present in The four major paṇs that fully embody these characteristics. Indalam is a subdivision of Marutham, one of these four major paṇs. It is characterized by its musical classification and its ability to present itself purely in four states.

Furthermore, it aligns with the musical classification of rhythmic states such as strong, weak, and even.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, understanding the nature of the paṇ and ensuring that its characteristics were fully met, sang this Tamil musical composition, which belongs to a great musical tradition, to delight the Lord. This is the direct meaning of this verse. Sivakavimani offers the following commentary on this: First, Sivakavimani defines what a paṇ is. He states that paṇ is when the sound, originating from the Muladhara, rises as an alapana (vocalization) of a letter, which is called music.

Moreover, it is called paṇ because it is formed by eight places where music originates and eight ways in which music originates. That is, there are eight places of origin and eight ways of origin. The eight places where paṇ originates are the chest, throat, tongue, nose, palate, lips, teeth, and head. The eight ways or actions by which paṇ originates are taking, placing, softening, These include Kampitham, Kudilam, Oli, Uruttu, Thākku.

Thus, a paṇ is that which is well-formed by eight places of origin and eight ways of origin. Our ancient Tamil texts state that this paṇ has eleven characteristics. These eleven characteristics are as follows: Muthal, Murai, Muḍivu, Niṟai, Kuṟai, Kiḻamai, Valivu, Melivu, Samaṉ, Varaiyaṟai, Nīrmai. Their detailed explanation can be found in the commentary of Aḍiyārkkunallār in the Arangēṟṟu Kāthai of Silappathikaram.

Now, the four major paṇs that fully embody these eleven characteristics are Marutham, Pālai, Kuṟiñji, Sevvaḻi. These four One of the four major paṇs is Marutha-paṇ. This Marutha-paṇ belongs to the agricultural land, Marutha nilam, and is suitable for singing in the morning. There are four major divisions within Marutha-paṇ: Navir, Vaḍugu, Vañji, Seythiram. Among these, Indala-paṇ is the seventy-third paṇ, an inner paṇ, in the second division, Vaḍugu. The second Thiru-pathikam in the eleventh Thiru-murai, composed by Karaikkal Ammaiyar, is set in the paṇ Indalam.

Indala-paṇ is one of ten paṇs suitable for singing during the day. Indala-paṇ is suitable for singing between ten and twelve nāḷigai (a measure of time) in the morning. Scholars believe that even the time when the Lord took Sundaramurthi Swamigal into His service can be calculated from this. Scholars believe that even the time when the Lord took Sundaramurthi Swamigal into His service can be calculated from this. This Indala-paṇ is a paṇ that expresses the emotions of pride, wonder, and anger. Its janyam (derivative) is in the 36th melam, Chalanattai.

Its corresponding rāgam is sometimes called Lalitha Panchami or Nāthanāmakriya. Vāriyār Swamigal explains that since it was sung using the names of Lord Shiva, the Lord, such as Piththā Piraisūdi, it is set in the rāgam Nāthanāmakriya.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings the first Thiru-pathikam in this highly esteemed Indala-paṇ. Sekkilār states that this Thiru-pathikam is a “Kuraiyā nilai” (flawless state) Thiru-pathikam in Indala-paṇ. What does “Kuraiyā nilai” mean? It refers to the qualities that establish and purely present the four aspects: vaṇṇa-kūṟupāḍu (color/tone), thāḷa-kūṟupāḍu (rhythm), pālai-nilai (mode), and paṇṇu-nilai (melody). The explanation for “Kuraiyā nilai” is the qualities that establish and purely present the four aspects: vaṇṇa-kūṟupāḍu (color/tone), thāḷa-kūṟupāḍu (rhythm), pālai-nilai (mode), and paṇṇu-nilai (melody).

Furthermore, another meaning states that it is the method of singing where the swaras (notes) belonging to the paṇ are kept without reduction in their respective māthras (time units).

Moreover, this paṇ is a “mummaippaḍi” paṇ, meaning that the three musical variations—thuritham (fast), viḷambitham (slow), and mathiyamam (medium)—are present without reduction. The musical components of sthāna-nilai (positional state) such as valivu (strength), melivu (softness), and saman (evenness) are also present without reduction.

Additionally, the three musical variations of eḍuththal (raising), paḍuththal (lowering), and naḷithal (softening) are present without reduction, and the three musical variations of thāram (high), mantharam (low), and mathiyamam (middle) are present without reduction, resulting in a highly excellent and complete grammatical structure. This is the meaning. Not only that, but it also follows the method of “niṟai pāṇi,” meaning that the paṇ comes with an unreduced thāḷam (rhythm). This Thiru-pathikam, this Thiru-pathikam, the thāḷam consists of koṭṭu (strike), asai (movement), thūkku (lift), and aḷavu (measure), as stated in Silappathikaram. These four are names of māthras (time units). That is, koṭṭu means half a māthra, asai means one māthra, thūkku means two māthras, and aḷavu means three māthras.

The functions of these four thāḷams are as follows: koṭṭu is pressing, asai is striking and rising, thūkku is striking and lifting, and aḷavu is striking, and the sound directly receives three māthras and comes to a measure.

Furthermore, here, pāṇi Sivakavimani also gives another meaning for “pāṇi.” That is, Sivakavimani states that “pāṇi” refers to the forty-one thāḷams mentioned, ranging from ēka thāḷam, which has half a māthra, to Pārvathi Lōchanam, which has sixteen māthras.

Furthermore, he states that another meaning for “pāṇi” includes the five thāḷams such as Kachchapuḍa.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, setting this sacred Thiru-pathikam in this paṇ, sings it as a musical Thiru-pathikam, a Tamil musical composition, with not even a slight deviation from the musical grammar, making it a fully complete Thiru-pathikam.

The Lord listens to it and rejoices. It shines as music that delights the Lord.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings this Thiru-pathikam as Tamil music, a blend of Tamil and music. Because, The Lord said, didn’t He? “The most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is song.” So, He speaks of song here. In the second line, He says, “Therefore, sing in pure Tamil.” So, He says, “Sing pure Tamil with music.” Here, Sivakavimani states that “music” refers to the primordial music, numbering eleven thousand nine hundred and ninety-one, expressed through the arrangement of strings.

Furthermore, Sivakavimani quotes from Aḍiyārkkunallār’s commentary, stating that the “Tamil” here, that is, “Tamil song,” refers to sentences constructed with Tamil letters that have come without Northern (Sanskrit) letters, adhering to their grammatical rules. It means a Tamil Thiru-pathikam constructed without changing the Sanskrit letters to Tamil, but keeping them with their respective grammatical rules. “Āriyam Thamizhoḍu isaiyāṉavaṉ” (He who is one with Sanskrit and Tamil) is from Thevaram. Such an incomparable Thiru-pathikam, with an incomparable paṇ, is sung by our incomparable Nambi Ārūrar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, about the incomparable Lord. Through this verse, Sekkilār Swamigal explains everything: the paṇ for this Thiru-pathikam, its musical grammar, its meaning, and the benefit derived from that meaning. Since this is the first place where details about Thevaram Thiru-pathikams are mentioned, Sekkilār Swamigal explains it in such great detail. He implicitly suggests that we should interpret all subsequent Thevaram Thiru-murai Thiru-pathikams in the same way.

What was mentioned here was vocal music.

Similarly, Sekkilār elaborates on the grammar of vocal music in the Puranam of Āṇāya Nāyanār, and on the special characteristics of the yāḻ (lute) in the Puranam of Thiru-gnānasambandha Peruman and Thiru-nīlakaṇḍa Yāḻppāṇar. Let us take up again our reflection on the Periya Puranam. 
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 11 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called “Thaduththāṭkoṇḍa Purāṇam,” we saw how the Lord came as an elderly Brahmin to Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr and took Sundaramurthi Swamigal into His service.

Then, the Lord appeared to Sundaramurthi Swamigal as Rishabhārūḍar with Pārvathi and commanded him to sing the Thiru-pathikam “Piththā Piraisūdi.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal then bestowed this sacred Thiru-pathikam upon us. We have seen how Sekkilār extensively explained its special features and melodic structure.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal begins this Thiru-pathikam by singing, “Piththā Piraisūdi Perumāṉē Aruḷāḷā Eththāṉ maṟavāthē niṉaikkiṉṟēṉ maṉaththuṇṇai vaiththāy Peṇṇai theṉpāl Veṇṇai Nallūr Aruḷ thuṟaiyuḷ Aththā uṉakkāḷā iṉi allēl eṉalāmē” (O Madman, wearing the crescent moon, O Lord, O Gracious One! How can I forget You? I keep You in my mind. You have placed me in Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr, on the southern bank of the Peṇṇai, in the sacred Aruḷ thuṟai. O Father, I am now Your servant. How can I say otherwise?).

Regarding the specific meaning of this Thiru-pathikam, Sivakavimani states the following: “O Madman, wearing the crescent moon, O Lord, O Gracious One! I, who remember You without forgetting You in any way, have placed You within my mind. You are enshrined in the Thiru-aruḷthuṟai temple in Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr, on the southern bank of the Peṇṇai river. O my Father, my Elder, my Lord, my Beginning, my Elder, my Power, my Beauty! I am now Your servant. Earlier, I denied being Your servant.

“Can I deny it now? No, I cannot.” Now, let us briefly reflect on the special features of this sacred Thiru-stalam, Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr. This Thiru-stalam, Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr, is located in the Villupuram district of Tamil Nadu.

“Here, the Lord’s names are Kirupāpurīswarar, Aruḷkoṇḍanāthar, Āṭkoṇḍa Veṇupurīswarar. The Goddess is Maṅgaḷāmbikai, Vēṟkaṇṇiyammaṉ. The sacred waters of this sthalam are Thaṇḍu Thīrtham, Sivaṉārkēṇi, Peṇṇai Nathi, Nīli Thīrtham, Sivagaṅgai Thīrtham, Kāma Thīrtham, Aruḷthuṟai Thīrtham, Pāṇḍava Thīrtham, Vaikuṇḍa Thīrtham, Vēda Thīrtham. The name of the village is Veṇṇai Nallūr, Thiru-veṇṇai Nallūr. The name of the temple is Thiru-aruḷthuṟai. Lord Shiva, who performed the sacrifice, destroys their arrogance.

“The arrogance of the sages of Thārukāvaṉam was destroyed, and they performed penance in this sthalam.

“The Lord forgave their mistake and bestowed His grace.

“Therefore, this temple was named Thiru-aruḷthuṟai. Remembering the mistake of the sages, Having forgiven and blessed them, the Lord here is named Kirupāpurīswarar.

“Here, Lord Shiva bestows grace as a Swayambhu Murthi (self-manifested idol). The Goddess appears in a standing posture with four arms. This is the 225th Thevaram Sthalam among the 274 Shiva temples sung in Thevaram, located in Nadu Nadu. Those who worshipped here include the Vedas, Thirumāl, Shanmukhar, Vijayan, Sundaramurthi Nāyanār, Meykaṇḍa Deva Nāyanār. This temple has a large Rājagōpuram (main tower) and two Prākārams (circumambulatory paths). After passing through the gopuram and entering, there is a mandapam where Sundarar’s legal case was settled.

“Next are the flagstaff, the flagstaff Vinayagar, and the Balipeetam (sacrificial altar). Directly above, at a height, is the Vimana temple where the Lord appeared to Sundarar as Rishabhārūḍar. Opposite it, below, is the shrine of Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

“Here, Sundaramurthi Swamigal is seen holding a palm-leaf manuscript. The shrines of the Lord and the Goddess face east. The Pillaiyar here is named Pollā Pillaiyar. “Polluthal” means to carve with a chisel. Since this Vinayagar appeared here as a self-manifested idol, not carved by a chisel, He is named Pollā Pillaiyar. It is noteworthy that this Pillaiyar imparted spiritual wisdom to Meykaṇḍa Deva Nāyanār at the age of five.

“The presence of the Goddess here with Saṅganithi, Padumanithi, and a lion vehicle is special. This is the sthalam where Umadeviyar attained the grace of Lord Shiva by performing penance within a fort made of fresh butter. The Thiru-pathikam said to have been sung by Thiru-nāvukkarasu Swamigal is not available to us. It was in this sthalam that Meykaṇḍa Swamigal, the first of the four Saiva Samaya Santhānāchāriyārs, received initiation from Paranjothi Munivar, who was traveling through the sky from Thiru-kailayam, and composed the “Sivagnāna Botham,” a Saiva Siddhanta philosophical text. The Vijayaliṅgam, which bestowed progeny upon Arjuna, is here. The Sundaraliṅgam, worshipped by Devendran, is here.

“The Saṅkaraliṅgam, worshipped by Mahavishnu, is here. Arjuna, one of the Pāṇḍavas, absolved himself of the sin of witnessing his elder brother Dharma and Pāñchāli alone by worshipping the Lord here.

“Moreover, he received the boon of progeny by praying to the Lord.

“Thus, the Lord blessed Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who had praised Him, saying, “Henceforth, you shall continue to sing Our praises in many ways in this world.” Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal narrates what the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar did when the marriage was thus obstructed. On that day, when the marriage began, it was obstructed. The daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar, who lived in Puthoor, always meditated on the exalted Nambi Arurar as her incomparable Lord and husband in her heart, and through that path, she attained rest. It is difficult to obtain She attained the life in Sivaloka by meditating on Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Because she always meditated on Sundaramurthi Swamigal in her heart, she attained the life in Sivaloka, inseparable from Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, as he had said, “I remember Lord Shiva without forgetting,” attained the Shiva life by remembering Him. But this lady, by remembering Sundaramurthi Swamigal in the same way without forgetting, attained the life in Sivaloka. Sekkizhar Swamigal narrates this story of this lady because she did not consider Sundaramurthi Swamigal, her Lord, as a husband for her body like other women, but rather as the life-Lord, an object of meditation. She attained the difficult-to-obtain life in Sivaloka.

“Therefore, Sekkizhar narrates her subsequent history thus: Sekkizhar’s message to us here is that we can attain Sivaloka by meditating on Lord Sundaramurthigal. After Lord Shiva came as a Brahmin and took possession of Nambi Arurar in Thiruvennainallur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal visits Thiru Navalur, the place of his birth. He worshipped the Lord there and sang a Thevaram Thiru-pathigam. The Thevaram sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal at that time is not available to us.

“Now, let us briefly consider the special features of this Thiru Navalur sthalam. This is the sacred birthplace of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Sadaiyanayanar and Isaignani Ammaiyar led a marital life and worshipped the Lord, and were blessed to have Sundaramurthi Swamigal as their son. This is the eighth sthalam of Nadu Nadu.

“The Lord here is Naval Eswarar, Bhakthajan Eswarar. The name of the Goddess is Manonmani Navalambikai Sundaranayaki. The sthala-viruksham here is the Naval tree. Theertham is Gomuki Theertham, Garuda Nadi. It is currently known as Thiru Namallur. This is the birthplace of four Nayanmars: Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s father Sadaiyanayanar, mother Isaignani Ammaiyar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s foster father Narsinga Munaiyaraiyar, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal himself.

“Lord Shiva requested Sundaramurthi Swamigal to go to all the Thiru-sthalams where He resides on this earth and sing His praises in musical Tamil, did He not? In accordance with that, Sundaramurthi Swamigal now departs from Thiru Navalur and reaches the Shiva sthalam called Thiru Thuraiyur.

“There, he stands before the Lord and sings pathigams. We shall continue our reflection.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 12 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thaduthatkonda Puranam, after the Lord took possession of Sundaramurthi Swamigal in Thiruvennainallur, he sang the pathigam beginning with ‘Piththa Piraisoodi’.

Afterwards, Sundaramurthi Swamigal went to Thiru Navalur, his birthplace, and sang a Thevaram pathigam about the Lord there.

Then, Sundaramurthi Swamigal arrived at the sthalam called Thiru Thuraiyur. Standing before the Lord enshrined in Thiru Thuraiyur, he prayed, “O Lord, in Thiruvennainallur, You stopped me and took possession of me, preventing me from going on the path of evil deeds. That is, You stopped me from going on the wrong path and took possession of me.

“Now, please grant me the path of penance.” He prayed and sang the Thiru-pathigam. He sang the Thiru-pathigam. Sekkizhar’s verse states that he reached Thiru Thuraiyur, where Shiva resides, and standing before the Lord, he sang a Thiru-pathigam that illuminates the path of worldly existence, praying, “You stopped me from going astray due to evil deeds; please grant me the path of penance.” The Thiru-pathigam that Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to here as illuminating the path of worldly existence is the Thiru-pathigam that begins with ‘Malaiyar Aruvi Thiral’. This Thiru-pathigam is indeed the Thiru-pathigam that illuminates the path of worldly existence and grants the path of penance. That is, this Thiru-pathigam is a lamp that illuminates us from the ocean of worldly existence, the path of birth and death, that we have traversed so far. The path of worldly existence refers to repeatedly being born and dying and taking births here.

“That path is called the path of worldly existence.

“Therefore, this Thiru-pathigam has the power to prevent us from that cycle of birth. Not only does it prevent us from the path of worldly existence we have been on so far, but it also has the power to protect us from the suffering of birth, which is the path of worldly existence, in the future.

“Moreover, Sundaramurthi Swamigal prays for the path of penance by singing these pathigams.

“Therefore, this pathigam is also one that grants us the path of penance. In every verse of this pathigam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal prays to the Lord, saying, “I will pray to You for the path of penance.” He prays for the path of penance.

“The Lord enshrined in Thiru Thuraiyur also grants the path of penance to Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

“Similarly, for everyone who sings this pathigam, the path of penance will unfailingly be granted, as Sundaramurthi Swamigal assures us in the eleventh verse of this Thiru-pathigam.

“Now, what is the path of penance? Penance, as described in the Shiva Agamas, refers to the four stages: Chariyai, Kiriyai, Yogam, and Gnanam. In short, the path that leads to Shiva Puja is the path of penance. It is the path of penance to prevent the mind from following the senses, to unite the mind, and to constantly remember and worship Lord Shiva. This Thiru-pathigam, which grants the path of penance, bestows upon us the blessing of performing both Atmarttha worship and Pararttha worship, as described in the Shiva Agamas.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, sang to the Lord, saying, “I will pray to You for the path of penance” in every verse. At the end of this pathigam, the Lord grants that path of penance to Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

“Thus, this pathigam not only gave the path of penance to Sundaramurthi Swamigal but also, by removing attachments, grants the path of penance to all people who learn and practice it. This is known from Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s Thiru-kadaikappu in the final verse of this pathigam: “Those who sing these words of the truthful Tamilian of Ooran will truly attain the path of penance.” Now, Sivakavimani offers another subtle point.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal prays to the Lord, saying, “Please grant me the path of penance,” does he not? But Sekkizhar Swamigal, in the section on the glory of the holy assembly, sings about the devotees as those who shine without desiring even liberation. He states there that the characteristic of devotees is not to desire even the bliss of liberation. If so, would praying for the path of penance here contradict that characteristic of devotees? Sivakavimani says it would not contradict it. He explains how.

“Our actions are the cause of our birth.

“Therefore, we have taken birth here to experience and exhaust the Prarabdha karmas we have brought with us. If we are to experience and exhaust the Prarabdha karmas, we must perform actions here.

“But when performing such actions, only the Prarabdha should be exhausted, and no further Agamya should accumulate. That is, all the actions we perform here should only serve to exhaust the karmas we have already performed, and we should perform actions in such a way that no further Agamya accumulates.

“At the same time, we can also exist without performing actions. If we can attain it, then if we live by constantly remembering and worshipping the Lord alone through the three instruments He has given us – mind, speech, and body – the Prarabdha we have already brought will be experienced and exhausted through our body. When our instruments, such as the mind, are filled with the grace of Shiva by remembering the Lord through the mind, praising the Lord through speech, and bowing to the Lord through this body, and serving the devotees, no further Agamya will accrue. This is called the path of penance.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal prays for and receives this path of penance.

“Therefore, praying for the path of penance in this way does not contradict the characteristic of not desiring even liberation. When one desires, one should desire the birth of others. The Thirukkural states that not desiring other things will lead to their attainment.

“Similarly, Sivakavimani explains that in the subsequent narratives, all the pathigams where Sundaramurthi Swamigal asks the Lord for gold and wealth are meant to signify that the instruments of action should be used to exhaust Prarabdha, and not for worldly pleasures. We must understand this. Throughout this pathigam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal prays to the Lord, saying, “I will pray to You for the path of penance, I will pray to You for the path of penance.” Now, the pathigam, sthalam Thiru Thuraiyur, pann Indampalam. “Malaiyar aruvi thiral ma maniyundhi kulaiyarag konarndhethri or pennai vadapal kalaiyar algul kanniyar aadum thuraiyur tha Alai vaa unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Maththam matha yanaiyin ven maruppundhi muththam konarndhethri or pennai vadapal paththar payin reththi paravun thuraiyur aththa unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Kandhangamil karagil santhanamundhi sen than punal vanthizhi pennai vadapal mandhi palamanam aadum thuraiyur enthay unai vendik kolven thavaneriye.

“Arumparnthana malligai shanbaganjadi surumparag konarndhethri or pennai vadapal karumpar mozhik kanniyar aadunthuraiyur verumpa unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Padarnthanamavum palakkalunjadi nadaravan thethri or pennai vadapa Madarnthana maligai suzhum thuraiyur veda unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Mattar malar kondraiyum vanniyum sadi mottarag konarndhethri or pennai vadapal kottattodu pattoli ova thuraiyur sitta unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Madhar mayirpeeliyum ven nuraiyundhi thadharag konarndhethri or pennai vadapal potharnthana poigaigal suzhum thuraiyur natha unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Koyyar malarkongodu vengaiyunjadi seyyarag konarndhethri or pennai vadapal maiyar thadankanniyar aadum thuraiyur aiya unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Vinnarnthana megangal nindru pozhiya mannara Konarndhethrivor pennai vadapal pannar mozhi pavaiyar aadunthuraiyur anna unai vendik kolven thavaneriye.

“Mavay pilanthanum malarmisaiyanum ava avar thedi thirinthalamanthaar poovarnthana poigaigal sulun thuraiyur deva unai vendik kolven thavaneriye. Seyyar kamalam malar navalur mannan kaiyal thozhutheththa padunthuraiyur mel poyya tamil uran uraiththanavallar meyyee peruvargal thavanerithane.

“Thus, as Sundaramurthi Swamigal prayed, the Lord granted him the path of uninterrupted penance, where the senses do not stray but remain focused. Accordingly, Sundaramurthi Swamigal adopted this path, and worshipped the Lord enshrined in this Thiru Thuraiyur sthalam, surrounded by flower-filled groves, by holding the Lord’s lotus feet in his mind, praising Him with his words, bowing with his hands, and performing Shiva Puja.

“Now, let us consider the special features of this Thiru Thuraiyur sthalam. The name of the sthalam is Thiru Thuraiyur, currently known as Thiru Thalur. The names of the Lord are Sishta Gurunathar, Pasupatheeswarar, Thavaneriyappar. The names of the Goddess are Poongothai Nayaki, Sivaloka Nayaki. This is the fifteenth sthalam among the Nadu Nadu sthalams mentioned in Thevaram. Arunanathi Sivachariyar, who composed the Saiva Siddhanta philosophical text called Sivagnana Siddhiyar, was born and lived in this sthalam.

“The sthala-viruksham is the Kondrai tree. Theertham is Surya Theertham, Pennaiyaru. The Pennaiyaru river, which flowed south of the village during Sundarar’s time, now flows north. The old Pennaiyaru river exists on the south side under the name Malattaru.

“The Lord appears in a west-facing shrine. The Goddess appears in a north-facing shrine.

“Thus, after Sundaramurthi Swamigal duly worshipped the Lord in Thiru Thuraiyur, he desired to behold and worship the sacred dance of Koothaperuman in Chidambaram, also known as Thiru Perum Patra Puliyur. As this desire arose in his mind, he set out to go to Chidambaram. On the way, he visited many sthalams.

“We will continue to reflect on that sacred journey.””
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 13 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
We are examining the section of the Periya Puranam called Thaduthatkonda Puranam.

Lord Shiva takes possession of Sundaramurthi Swamigal in the sthalam of Thiruvennainallur. Afterwards, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches Thiru Navalur.

There, he sings a Thevaram pathigam and then reaches the sthalam of Thiru Thuraiyur. There, he prays to Lord Shiva, receives the path of penance, and performs Shiva Puja.

Afterwards, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart melts remembering the sacred feet of Koothaperuman, who is enshrined in Thillai (Chidambaram).

Therefore, he prepared to set out for Chidambaram. On the way, he visits the sthalams of Thiru Adigai and Thiru Manikkuzhi. The Periya Puranam not only narrates the stories of the devotees but also sets forth various rules and regulations on how Shiva devotees should be. It is a sacred text that expounds various principles and disciplines.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who was preparing to set out for Chidambaram, visits the sthalams on the way. Sekkizhar subtly demonstrates here that if we embark on a pilgrimage to any sacred place, we should also visit the sthalams on the way.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal demonstrates this here. From Thiru Thuraiyur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal crosses the Pennaiyaru river to proceed to Chidambaram. Sekkizhar describes the Pennaiyaru river here. This Pennaiyaru river is a river that flows with cool waves and a great volume of water. In this flowing river, agarwood trees and sandalwood trees float, and peacock feathers abound. Various kinds of flowers float on the river, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes.

Agarwood trees, sandalwood trees, etc., grow in mountainous regions. This Pennaiyaru river originates in a mountainous region and carries agarwood trees, sandalwood trees, and various other types of trees from there.

Similarly, it carries various types of flowers. It carries flowers from the mountainous regions and then, after coming down from the mountains to the plains, it carries various types of flowers from the groves and forests there.

Thus, this Pennaiyaru river, from its place of origin, carries various treasures and bestows them upon the people along its path, providing benefit. After crossing this Pennaiyaru river, Sundaramurthi Swamigal prepares to disembark and proceed to Thiru Adigai.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal also shines like this Pennaiyaru river. Sekkizhar Swamigal, by describing the Pennaiyaru river, states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal bestows the spiritual wealth he received in Thiruvennainallur and the path of penance he received in Thiru Thuraiyur to all the places he visits.

Moreover, the Pennaiyaru river only bestows these treasures upon the people in the villages it passes through, but Sundaramurthi Swamigal bestows them upon all living beings in the Thiru-sthalams he is about to visit. Not only does he bestow them, but he also grants the Thevaram Thiru-Pathigams for the benefit of all beings in all worlds. He sings the Thevaram Thiru-Pathigams so that all beings in all worlds may attain the wealth of Shiva’s grace, and he sings them so that all beings in all ages may attain that same wealth of Shiva’s grace.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal now crosses the Pennai River and reaches the outer boundary of the sacred sthalam of Thiru-Adhigai in the evening. Do you know how glorious this Thiru-Adhigai sthalam is? It was in this Thiru-Adhigai sthalam that Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal, celebrated by the world as ‘Aludaiya Arasu,’ performed the Uzhavara service with the tool called Uzhavarappadai, which he held in his sacred hands. The Uzhavarappadai is a tool for clearing stones. Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal, who always held this in his sacred hands, used the Uzhavarappadai to clear stones, grass, thorns, and other debris from in front of the sanctum, in the circumambulatory paths, and in the streets surrounding the temple of this Thiru-Adhigai sthalam, thus performing sacred service with his own hands. This was Thiru-Adhigai.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, fearing to tread with his feet upon the earth of Thiru-Adhigai where Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal had performed the Uzhavara service with his sacred hands touching the ground, stood outside Thiru-Adhigai without entering the town. Outside Thiru-Adhigai is the Siddhavadamadam which is a public monastery suitable for Shiva devotees on pilgrimage to stay.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stayed there with his retinue. In that evening, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, staying in that grove-encircled Siddhavadamadam, continuously meditated upon the sacred feet of Thiru-Veerattaneeswarar, who is enshrined within Thiru-Adhigai, and lay down to sleep in that monastery with his retinue. Thiru-Veerattaneeswarar, the Lord Shiva within Thiru-Adhigai, seeing Sundaramoorthy Swamigal meditating on His sacred feet and sleeping, came out of the Thiru-Adhigai temple in the guise of an elderly Brahmin to bestow His grace upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and entered the Siddha- Vadamadam. Unbeknownst to anyone in that Siddhavadamadam, Lord Shiva, disguised as an elderly Brahmin, entered and came to the place where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was sleeping. He placed His lotus-like sacred feet upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred head and appeared as if He were sleeping there.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, feeling someone’s feet upon his sacred head, awoke and, looking at the elderly Brahmin, said, “Swami, you have placed your feet upon my head.” To this, the elderly man, who was the Lord, replied, “My old age has made me lose my way and do this.” Accepting this reply, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Tamil, moved his head to another spot and lay down to sleep. Even as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal attempted to sleep with his head in another place, the Lord again placed His sacred feet upon his head in the same spot.

Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, again feeling the sacred feet upon his head, awoke and angrily asked the Lord, “You have stepped on me many times like this; who are you?” Immediately, the elderly man said, “Do you still not know who I am?” and disappeared. As the Brahmin who had spoken thus suddenly disappeared, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal realized that it was Lord Shiva who had come to bestow grace. Thinking thus, he lamented, “Because of my arrogance, I failed to recognize my Lord, who came in the guise of an old man and suddenly disappeared.” He then bowed at the sacred feet of Thiru-Adhigai Veerattaneeswarar and sang a Thevaram Pathigam beginning with “Thammaanai Ariyaadha Saathiyaar Ulare.” Now, the Thiru-Pathigam: Sthalam: Thiru-Adhigai Veerattanam. Pann: Kolli Kouvvanam.

Song One: “Thammaanai Ariyaadha Saathiyaar Ulare, Sadai Mel Kol Piraiyaanai, Vidai Mel Kol Vihirthan, Kaimmaavin Uriyaanai, Karikaattil Aadal Udaiyaanai, Vidaiyaanai, Karai Konda Kandathu Ammaanthan Adikkondhu En Mudimel Vai” “Thidumenum aasaiyaal vaazhgindra arivilaa naayen Emmanai Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Two: “Munney Em Perumaanai Marandhu Enkol Maravaadhu Olindhu Enkol Maravaadha Sindhaiyaal Vaazhven Ponney Nanmaniyey Venmuththay Sempavazhak Kundramey Eesan Endru Unniyey Pugazhven Anney En Aththa Endru Amararaal Amarappaduvaanai Adhigai Maa Nagarul Vaazhbavanai Enney En Ezhikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Three: “Virumbinorkku En” “Adhullam Vidagilaa Vidhiyey Vinnvar Tham Perumaanai Mannavar Nindru Eththum Karumbey En Katti Endru Ullaththaal Ulgi Kaadhal Ser Maadharaal Gangaiyaal Nangai Varum Punalum Sadaikkanindhu Valaaraadha Piraiyum Variyaravum Udan Thuyil Vaiththarulum Endhai Irumbunal Vandhu Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Four: “Nattraanathoruvanaai Naanaaya Paranaai Nallaattru Nambiyai Vellaattru Vidhiyay Aattraanai Theeyaanai Kadalaanai Malaiyin Thalaiyaanai Kadungalozhi Kankai Neer Vella Aattraanai Piraiyaanai” “Ammaanai Emmaan Thammaanai Yaavarkkum Arivariya Sengal Ettraanai Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Five: “Sendhar Thaai Malai Mangai Thiruniramum Parivum Udaiyaanai Adhigai Maa Nagarul Vaazhbavanai Koondhal Thaazh Punal Mangai Kuyilanna Mozhiyaal Sadaiyidaiyil Kayalinangal Kuthigolla Kulaayivaaindha Neervara Undhi Maraamarangal Vanakki Marikadalai Idangolvaan Malai Aaram Vaari Endhu Neer Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Six: “Kaimaan Mani Neela Kanda” “Emperumaan Vallena Kompanindha Maadhavanai Vaanor Thammaanai Thalaimaganai Thanmadhiyum Paambum Thadumaarunjadaiyaanai Thaazh Varai Kaivendra Vemmaana Madhakariyin Uriyaanai Vedhavidhiyaanai Venniru Sanniththa Meniyemmanai Eri Kedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Seven: “Veydhaaya Vinai Kadalil Thadumaarum Uyirkku Miga Irangi Arul Porindhu Veedu Perru Aakkam Peydhaanai Pinaganai Maing Avizhum Kandathu Enthol Emperumaanai Pen Paagam Orupaal Seidhaanai Sekkarvaan Oliyaanai Theevaai Aravadu Sadaiyaanai Thiri”

“Purangal Veva Eydhaanai Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Eight: “Ponnaanai Mayil Oorthi Murugavel Thaadhai Podiyaadu Thirumeni Nedumaaran Mudimel Thennaanai Kudapaalin Vadapaalin Gunapaal Seraadha Sindhaiyaan Sekkarvaan Andhi Annaanai Amarargal Tham Perumaanai Karumaan Uriyaanai Adhigai Maa Nagarul Vaazhbavanai Ennaanai Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Nine: “Thirundhaadha Vaazh Avunar Puram Moonrum Veva Silai Valaiviththu Oru Kanaiyaal Thozhil Poonda Sivanai Karu” “Thaala Madakkalittrin Uriyaanai Periya Kan Moonrum Udaiyaanai Karudhaadha Arakkan Perunthozhgal Naalaaindhum Iraaindhu Mudiyum Udaiyaanai Peruvuruva Moonru Muramalai Mel Irundhaanai Erikedila Vada Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” Song Ten: “Enbinaiye Kalanaaga Anindhaanai Engal Erudherum Perumaanai Isaignaani Siruvan Vanbanaiya Valarpozil Soozh Vayal Naavaloorkon Vanthondan Arooran Madhiyaadhu Sonna Anbanai Yaavarkkum Arivariya Aththar Perumaanai Adhigai Maa Nagarul Vaazhbavanai En Ponnai Erikedila Vada” “Veerattaanathu Uraivaanai Irai Podhum Ighazhvan Pol Yaaney.” 
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 14 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduththaatkonda Puranam,’ Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches the outskirts of the sthalam called Thiru-Adhigai Veerattanam. As it was a sthalam where Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman had performed sacred service with his own sacred hands, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, fearing to tread upon it with his feet, did not enter the sthalam but remained outside. While he was sleeping in the Siddhavadamadam, the Lord of Thiru-Adhigai Veerattanam came in the form of an elderly Brahmin and bestowed ‘Thiruvadi Deekshai’ (initiation by touch of the sacred feet) upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal twice.

Now, Sivakavimani elaborates on the subtleties of this history.

Therefore, let us now contemplate the subtleties of this section. When Sekkizhar Swamigal narrates the verse where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal enters the Siddhavadamadam, Sekkizhar Swamigal, in this context of describing Thiru-Adhigai as the sthalam where Thiru-Navukkarasar performed the Uzhavara service, is presenting the first instance in the Periya Puranam where information about Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal is introduced.

Therefore, as if he too were worshipping him along with the rest of the world, he states, “Aludaiya Arasu Ulagetthum Uzhavarappadaiyali Vidaiyavarkku Kai Thondu Virumbu Perumpathi.” Sekkizhar Swamigal thus praises it as a great sthalam where Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman, the ‘Aludaiya Arasu’ (Lord of the World), performed sacred services with his hands, holding the Uzhavarappadai, which is praised by all these worlds.

Here, Sivakavimani offers another subtle interpretation for the Uzhavarappadai. Uzhavaram is a tool for removing stones, grass, thorns, etc., from the sacred streets. Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman, the ‘Aludaiya Arasu,’ always held it in his hand. Sivakavimani explains that this signifies that the Uzhavarappadai serves as a tool to remove obstacles encountered by those who follow the path of Shiva, and thus Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman guides the people of the world by displaying this tool.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, fearing to tread with his feet upon the sthalam where Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal had performed the Uzhavara service, stood outside and meditated on the Lord. This very nature is the quality of standing as a servant to the devotees and worshipping them.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who came to sing the glories of the devotees, that is, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who came to sing the Thiru-Pathigam called ‘Thiruththondathogai’ which speaks of the greatness of the devotees, demonstrates here how a Shiva devotee acts as a servant to other devotees.

Similarly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal later did not enter the sthalam of Seerkazhi (the sthalam where Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman was born) but stood outside and worshipped the Lord, and the Lord appeared there.

In the same way, when Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman visited Thiru-Alangadu, that sthalam was where Karaikkal Ammaiyar had walked on her head.

Therefore, fearing to tread upon that sthalam, he remained outside and sang a Pathigam.

Thus, we must praise this great Shiva quality of our great saints and Shaivite elders, who shone as servants to devotees.

Now, the Siddhavadamadam: This is an ancient public monastery located about three ‘naligai’ (a measure of distance) northwest of Thiru-Adhigai, outside the town of Thiru-Adhigai. It is now known as Kottaalambakkam. There is also a Shiva temple here now.

Furthermore, Sivakavimani explains the phrase “Kai Thondu Virumbum” (desiring service with hands) as follows: Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman was one who always shed tears from his eyes. He was one whose appearance was characterized by the continuous shedding of tears from his eyes, thinking of the Lord. From the words of Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal, sweet Tamil garlands of songs would always emerge. The mind of Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal would always meditate upon and contemplate the sacred feet of the Lord. Despite all this, Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal greatly desired to perform the sacred service of Uzhavara work in addition to these. This is how Sivakavimani explains the phrase “Kai Thondu Virumbum.” Furthermore, the Siddhavadamadam where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stayed was a monastery surrounded by groves, where devotees would stay.

This grove-encircled place is suitable for Shiva Puja, penance, and meditation.

Therefore, monasteries are always situated amidst flower gardens. From this monastery, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal continuously and incessantly meditated upon the sacred feet of Veerattaneeswarar in Thiru-Adhigai.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal always wished that the Lord would place His feet upon his head.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal meditated upon the Lord’s sacred feet and lay down to sleep.

Therefore, Lord Shiva, who is by nature one who bestows upon those who desire, assumed the form of an elderly Brahmin and entered the Siddhavadamadam, unbeknownst to anyone, to place His sacred feet upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. This guise of an elderly Brahmin is an ancient form that Lord Shiva always prefers to adopt.

Lord Shiva is the foremost of all; therefore, He naturally and willingly appears in an ancient form. When Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Sundaramoorthy Swamigal here, he describes him as ‘Poonthaaran,’ meaning Sundaramoorthy Swamigal adorned with a lotus garland.

Thus, for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was adorned with a lotus garland, what was placed upon his head should also be a lotus.

Therefore, the Lord placed His lotus-like sacred feet upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred head. Sivakavimani further explains that the Lord’s sacred feet are lotus-like. The Lord’s sacred feet are those that possess all the complete characteristics, including the ‘Padmarekhai’ (lotus lines) and other auspicious marks. Sekkizhar will elaborate on this later in the biography of Manakkanjara Nayanar.

Thus, the Lord here bestows ‘Thiruvadi Deekshai’ by placing His lotus-like feet upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred head. Earlier, in Thiru-Thuraiyur, the Lord had bestowed upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal the path of penance. As a continuation of that, He bestows ‘Thiruvadi Deekshai’ here.

Furthermore, Sekkizhar states here that Lord Shiva, who came in the guise of an old man, pretended to be sleeping while placing His sacred feet upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred head.

The Lord is always the one who performs the eternal five actions: creation, preservation, destruction, concealment, and bestowal of grace. He is always performing these five actions (Pancha Krityas) as His eternal dance.

Therefore, He never sleeps or knows sleep. That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal states here that the elderly Brahmin was in the posture of sleeping.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, upon feeling feet on his sacred head, awoke and asked the elderly Brahmin, “Sir, your feet are on me.” To this, the elderly Lord replied, “My old age has made me lose my way,” as Sekkizhar composes. Sivakavimani explains the subtlety of this as follows: The literal meaning is that the Lord, as the elderly Brahmin, said to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, “My old age has made me unable to perceive the direction of your head.” This old age is an external, visible thing. This is the superficial meaning of “My old age has made me lose my way.” The inner meaning is that the Lord is one who cannot be known by anyone in any direction, nor can His nature be understood. The reason why the Lord appears thus to those in all directions, as one whose nature cannot be known, is His ‘Mooppu,’ meaning His antiquity. He is the ancient of ancient things.

Thus, Sivakavimani explains this as the inner, implicit meaning.

Then, when the elderly Brahmin placed his feet on Sundarar’s head a second time, Sundarar became angry and asked, “You” When asked, “Don’t you know who I am?” Lord Shiva disappeared.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, realizing the truth, sang the Thiru-pathikam beginning with “Are there any castes that do not know their master?” This refers to caste in the sense of a division, a class, or a group. The meaning is, “Are there any groups in this world that do not know their own master? No, there are not.

“But I have remained without knowing my master.” He sings with the anguish of having failed to recognize the Lord who bestows good upon him, whereas everyone else knows their own masters.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal sang this Thiru-pathikam. In this sacred hymn, Sundaramurthi Swamigal expresses his longing for the Lord’s sacred feet (Thiruvadi Dikshai) and then laments that he failed to recognize the Lord even after He graciously came twice to bestow this initiation.

“Now, let us reflect briefly on the special features of this Thiru-vathikai sthalam. This Thiru-vathikai sthalam is known as Thiru-vathikai Veerattanam. It is one of the eight Veerattanams, and history records that it is the place where the heroic act of burning the three cities (Thiripuram) occurred. This sthalam is also where Thiru-navukkarasu Swamigal was graced by the Lord. It is also the place where Thilagavathi Ammaiyar, the elder sister of Thiru-navukkarasu Swamigal, performed sacred service.

“Therefore, their sacred forms are installed and worshipped here. The linga-form of the Lord in this Thiru-vathikai Veerattaneswarar temple shines with sixteen supreme kalas (aspects). The Theertham (sacred water) is the Gedilam River. The Lord’s name is Thirupurantakeswarar, Veerattaneswarar. The Goddess’s name is Thirupurasundari. To commemorate Shiva’s grace in ascending the chariot made by all the Devas to burn the three cities, the sanctum sanctorum of this sacred sthalam is built like a chariot.

“Sivakavimani states that its shadow does not fall on the ground below. This is the seventh sthalam among the Nadunattu sthalams, located on the northern bank of the Gedilam River. This sthalam has a total of eight Thevara Thiru-pathikams. This Thiru-vathikai sthalam is sung by all three: Thiru-navukkarasu Swamigal, Thiru-gnanasambandha Peruman, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal. The sthala-vriksham (sacred tree) here is the Sarakkonnai. The Theerthams here include the Gedilam River, known as the Southern Ganga, along with Alaganga and Chakra Theertham.

“Those who worshipped in this temple include Thilagavathiyar, Sambandar, Appar, Sundarar, Kapila Deva Nayanar, Pattinathu Pillaiyar, Nambiyandar Nambi, Thirumoolar, and Sekkizhar. This is the sthalam where the Lord displayed His sacred dance to Thiru-gnanasambandha Peruman. It is also the birthplace of Manavasagam Kadandhar, who authored ‘Unmai Vilakkam,’ one of the fourteen Saiva Siddhanta Sastras. The Lord, Thirupura Thaga The festival of burning the three cities takes place during the Chithirai Peruvizha (grand festival) on the day of the chariot procession. It is a tradition that King Raja Raja Cholan built the Brihadeeswarar Temple in Thanjavur taking the vimana (tower) of the sanctum sanctorum of this temple as an example. Such are the glories of this Thiru-vathikai sthalam.

“Sekkizhar Swamigal praises the Gedilam River flowing in this Thiru-vathikai sthalam as the Southern Ganga. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how gold dust, gem clusters, elephant tusks, luminous pearls, flowers, and fragrant sandalwood logs float in this Gedilam River, thus calling it the Southern Ganga. Bringing many such items, Sivakavimani subtly explains why Sekkizhar calls this Gedilam River, which collects these items, the Southern Ganga. Gold and gems float in this Gedilam River, pearls arrive, and various flowers come. All these are items suitable for the Lord’s Shiva Puja. It is an Agamic rule to offer these gold, gems, pearls, and flowers in the water, perform Thirumanjanam (sacred bath) for Lord Shiva, and offer Arghya, Padya, and Achamanam.

“Furthermore, the elephant tusks floating in this river are useful for making palanquins and other conveyances for the Lord to ascend.

“Thus, this Gedilam River provides for the Lord’s worship. The Gedilam River brings gold, pearls, gems, flowers, and elephant tusks needed for worship, performs Thirumanjanam for the Lord, and worships the Thiru-vathikai sthalam. The Ganga River also brings various such items from the Himalayas, making them available for everyone’s use.

“Moreover, because it flows from Lord Shiva’s matted locks, it performs Thirumanjanam for the Lord. That Ganga River is therefore like this Gedilam, he says. The Ganga is in the north, and the Gedilam is here in the south.

“Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal praises this Gedilam River as the Ganga of the South. Let us journey onward with Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s sacred pilgrimage.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 15 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam,’ the Lord commanded Sundaramurthi Swamigal in Thiru-vennainallur, saying, “Go and sing in all the sacred sthalams where I am enshrined on earth.” Thus, he sang the first pathikam in Thiru-vennainallur, then came to Thiru-navalur and sang a pathikam there.

After that, he came to Thiru-thuraiyur, sang a Thevara pathikam there, and received the desired path of penance from the Lord.

Then he came to Thiru-vathikai, where, without entering the town, he stayed in the Siddhavadamadam outside. There, he received Thiruvadi Dikshai from the Lord and sang a Thevara Thiru-pathikam.

Afterwards, he bathed joyfully in the Thiru-kedilam River, praised by Sekkizhar as the Ganga of the South, which flows in the city of Thiru-vathikai. After joyfully bathing in the Thiru-kedilam River, he embarks on a journey along the southern bank of that sacred river. He then reaches the sacred place called Thiru-manikkuzhi. This sacred sthalam of Thiru-manikkuzhi is where Vishnu, in his Vamana avatar, begged three feet of land from Emperor Mahabali, pushed him into the netherworld, and then worshipped Lord Shiva in that Vamana form. Mahabali is referred to as Mavali in Tamil. Mavali means one who possesses great strength in battle.

In Tamil Nadu, there is a sacred sthalam called Thiru-maraikkadu. The sthalam known as Thiru-maraikkadu is also called Vedaranyam. In that sacred temple, a mouse, to satisfy its hunger, attempted to eat the ghee from the sacred lamp in the Shiva shrine within the Thiru-maraikkadu temple. As it tried to eat the ghee from the sacred lamp in the temple to appease its hunger, the flame of the sacred lamp singed the mouse’s nose. Unknowingly, the mouse then stirred the wick. By stirring the wick, the sacred lamp shone even brighter.

Thus, the mouse unknowingly became the cause for the light of the sacred lamp in Lord Shiva’s temple to shine even brighter. Due to this meritorious act, in its next birth, the mouse was born as Mahabali, the emperor of the three worlds, possessing authority and power, as recounted in the Puranas. Thiru-navukkarasu Swamigal mentions this in one of his Thevarams: “In the full Maraikkadu, the mouse, its nose singed by the bright lamp, stirred it, and the lamp shone brightly.

“The Lord of Kurukkai Veerattanam, it seems, bestows the full earth, sky, and the vast heavens without any lack.” This is Appar’s Thevaram.

“Thus, to atone for the sin of punishing Mahabali, a devotee of Shiva endowed with excellent qualities, at the request of the Devas, Thirumal (Vishnu) worshipped Lord Shiva here in his Vamana form. Thiru-manikkuzhi is the name of this sacred sthalam.

“Here, ‘Mani’ refers to a Brahmachari (celibate student), and ‘Kuzhi’ means a place. It is a Shiva sthalam where Thirumal, with red eyes, in the form of a small Vamana (dwarf) Brahmachari, worshipped.

“Therefore, this sthalam is known as Thiru-manikkuzhi. This The sthalam known as Thiru-manikkuzhi is the seventeenth sthalam among the Nadunattu sthalams, out of the two hundred and seventy-four sthalams sung in Thevara Pathikams. It is located near Thiru-pathiripuliyur, on the southern bank of the Garuda River. In this sacred sthalam of Thiru-manikkuzhi, the Lord’s sacred name is Vamanapurishwarar, Uthavi Nayagar (Helper Lord), Manikkavaradar. The Goddess’s sacred name is Ambujakshi, Uthavi Nayagi (Helper Goddess), Manikkavalli. The Sthala Vriksham here is the Konrai tree.

“The Theertham is Shveta Theertham and the Gedilam River. When a merchant from the North, named Athri, was passing through this region, thieves attempted to rob him.

“The Lord protected the merchant from the thieves and helped him.

“Therefore, this sacred sthalam is called Uthavi (Help), and the Lord is called The Lord is called Uthavi Nayagar (Helper Lord) and the Goddess Uthavi Nayagi (Helper Goddess). In the Periya Puranam, it is stated that Sundarar bathed in the Gedilam River and visited Thiru-manikkuzhi.

“However, the Thiru-pathikam sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for this sthalam is not available to us. In this sacred sthalam, the Lord is eternally united with Goddess Parvati to impart wisdom to the Devas and remove their ignorance.

“Therefore, we cannot directly behold the Lord here. Since the sanctum sanctorum itself serves as the bedchamber, there is no separate bedchamber. Generally, in all Shiva temples, we can continuously behold the Shiva Lingam during puja times.

“However, in this sthalam, the Lord grants darshan for only two or three seconds.

“The Lord grants darshan and then disappears behind a curtain.

“Therefore, the curtain is always drawn in front of the Moolavar (presiding deity) shrine. On the upper part of this curtain, the form of Bhimarudra, one of the Ekadasarudras, is painted. It is believed that since the Lord and Goddess are united here, Bhimarudra, one of the Ekadasarudras, is present in the form of the curtain to guard them.

“Therefore, this sacred sthalam also has the name Bhimasankara Kshetram. In the worship of this temple, Archana, Deeparadhana, Puja, etc., are first performed for this Bhimarudra, and then for the Lord. After visiting this Thiru-manikkuzhi sthalam, which possesses such glories, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the sacred sthalam called Thiru-thinainagar.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the sacred sthalam of Thiru-thinainagar. Thiru-thinainagar is currently known as Theerthanagiri. The sacred name of the Lord enshrined in Thiru-thinainagar is Sivakkozhundeesar. The sacred names of the Goddess are Neelayathakshi, Oppila Nayagi, Karunthadankanni, Ilankombu. The Sthala Vriksham is the Konrai tree. The Theerthams are as follows: Gowri Theertham, which is in the form of a well in the prakaram (circumambulatory path), created by the Goddess for Shiva Puja.

“Next is Sambuva Thadagam or Jambavan Theertham, created by Jambavan, currently a lotus pond on the northern side of the temple.

“Thirdly, Garuda Theertham, which is in the form of a lake, this Near this Theertham is the Garuda Linga temple.

“Fourthly, Deva Theertham; the sea near this temple is called Deva Theertham.

“Fifthly, Chakra Theertham.

“Thus, there are five Theerthams in this sacred sthalam. This is a sthalam worshipped by Sundaramurthi Swamigal and by a king named Veerasena. There is a story associated with this sthalam. A long time ago, a farming couple, Periyan, a Pallan, and his wife, lived here with great devotion to Shiva. They made it a practice to feed a Shiva devotee every day before eating themselves.

“Once, while they were farming, the Lord came as a devotee and asked for food. The farmer told the devotee, “Please stay here; I will go home and bring food.” The Lord, who came in the guise of a devotee, said, “I will not eat anything without working.

“So, give me some work in your field. I will eat as wages if you wish.” The farmer told him to plough his field and went home to fetch food. By then, all the millet crops sown in the field had grown well, the ears were ripe, and they were ready for harvest.
