While the farmer went home from the field to fetch food, Lord Shiva, who had come as a devotee, made the millet crops, which were ready for harvest, ripen and appear.

“Then, the Lord appeared to Periyan, the Pallan, right there. When he requested the Lord to be enshrined there, the Lord manifested as a Swayambhu Lingam here.

“Thus, a miracle in a single day Because the millet crops ripened, this sthalam was named Thiru-thinainagar.

“Now, the Thiru-pathikam sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for this sacred sthalam. Sthalam: Thiru-thinainagar. Pan: Thakkesi.

“Song One: Neeru thaangiya thirunithalanaai netrik kannanai niraivalai madandhai kooru thaangiya kolkaiyinaanai kutramilliyai katraiyanjadaiyael aaru thaangiya azhaganai amararkku ariya sothiyai varivaraal ugazhum seru thaangiya thiruthinainagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu (Flame of Shiva) in Thiru-thinainagar, which is filled with fertile fields praised by the beautiful-eyed women. He is the one who bears the sacred ash on his forehead, has a third eye, shares a part of his body with the full-bangle-wearing Goddess, is faultless, carries the river on his matted locks, is beautiful, and is a light unknown to the Devas. Song Two: Pinikol aakkai pirappu irappu ennum idhanai neekki eesan thiruvadi inaikkaal thuniyavendidi solluvan ke. Ezh nee anjal nenjamae vanjar vaazh madhil moondru anikol venjilaiyaal ugachcheerum aiyan vaiyagam paravi nindretthum thiniyum vaar pozhil thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae.

“Gloss: O mind, do not fear! The Lord, who destroyed the three fortified cities of the deceitful with his fierce bow, is praised by the world. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, which has dense, extensive groves. Song Three: Vadikol kanninai madandhaiyar thambaal mayalathu utru vanjanaikku idamaagi mudiyumaakarudhael eruthaerum moorthiyai mudhalaaya piranaai adigal endradiyaar thozhuthetthum appan oppilaa mulai umai konai sedikol kaanmali thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, do not be deluded by women with sharp eyes, becoming a target for deceit and perishing. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, which is filled with dense forests.

“He is the Lord who rides the bull, the primordial Lord, the Father worshipped by devotees as ‘Adigal,’ and the Lord of Uma with incomparable breasts. Song Four: Paavamae purindhu agalidandhil pala pagardhu alamandhu uyir vaazhkai kaavendru. Uzhaindhu ayarndhu veezhaadhae annal than thiram arivinaar karuthi maavin eeruri udai punaindhaanai maniyai maindhanai vaanuvarkku amudhai devadevanai thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, do not commit sins, speak many things in this wide world, become distressed, and fall weary, crying out for life to be saved. Consider the greatness of the Lord, who wears a garment of elephant hide, is a gem, a strong one, nectar to the celestials, the God of gods. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar.

“Song Five: Ondralaa uyir vaazhkaiyai ninaindhittu udal thalarndhu aru maanidhiyatrri endrum vaazhalaam emakkena paesum idhuvum poiyenavae ninai ulamae kundrulaaviya puyamudaiyaanai koothanai kulaavi kuvalaiyaththoor senralam payil thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, do not think of this transient life, become weary, lose great wealth, and say, ‘We can live forever.’ Consider this to be false. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, where people of the world gather and worship. He is the one with mountain-like shoulders, the dancer. Song Six: Vendharaai ulagaandu aramburindhu veetrirundha ivvudali idhu thannai theindhirandhu vendhuyaru. Thidum ippokka vaazhvinai vittidu nenjae paanthalangaiyil aattugandhaanaai paramanai kadarchoor thadindhitta sendhar thaadhaiyai thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae.

“Gloss: O mind, abandon this transient life that will decay and die, causing great sorrow. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar. He is the one who delights in dancing with a snake in his hand, the Supreme Being, the father of Murugan who destroyed the demon Surapadman in the sea. Song Seven: Thannil aasaru chiththamum indri thavam muyandru avamaayina paesi pinnulaar sadai katti en panindhaal peridhum neendhuvadarithathu nirka munnelaam muzhu mudhalendru vaanore moorthiyaagiya mudhalvan thannai sennalaara vayal thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, without pure devotion in oneself, attempting penance and speaking vain words, even if one ties matted locks and bows, it is difficult to achieve great liberation. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, which has fields of red paddy.

“He is the primordial Lord, the first and foremost form worshipped by the celestials. Song Eight: Parindha sutramum matra van thunaiyum palarum kandu azhudhu ezha uyir udalai pirindhu pom idhu nichayam. Arindhaar paedhai vaazhvenum pinakkinai thavirndhu karunthadang kanni panganai uyirai kaala kaalanai kadavulai virumbich cherundhi pon malar thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, knowing that life will certainly depart from the body, with grieving relatives and other strong companions watching and weeping, abandon the entanglement of foolish life. Desire the Lord, who shares a part with the dark-eyed Goddess, who is life itself, the destroyer of time, the God. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, which has golden Cherundhi flowers.

“Song Nine: Namaiyalaam palar igazhndhuraippadhan mun nanmai ondilaa thaerar pun samanaam samayamaagiya thavathinaar avaththanmai vittrozhi nanmaiyai vendil umaiyor kooranai aerugandhaanaai umbar aadhiyai emperumaanai simayamarp pozhil thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai senradai mananae. Gloss: O mind, before many others scorn us, if you desire goodness, abandon the useless practices of the Therar and the base Samanar religions, which have no merit. Go and reach the Sivakkozhundhu in Thiru-thinainagar, which has dense groves on its peaks. He is the one who shares a part with Uma, who delights in riding the bull, the primordial Lord of the celestials, our great Lord. Song Ten: Needu pokkaiyir piraviyai pazhiththu neengalaamendru manathinai therutti sedulaam pozhil thiruthinai nagarul sivakkozhundhinai thiruvadiyai thaan naadalaam. Pugazh Navaloor Aali Nambi Van Thondan Ooran uraitha paadal aan Tamil pathu ivai vallar mutthiyaavadhu paragathi payanae Thiruchitrambalam.

“Gloss: These ten Tamil songs, spoken by Ooran, the renowned Nambi Van Thondan of Navaloor, if mastered, will lead to liberation, the supreme state.

“As the benefit of this pathikam, in the tenth song, Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that those who can sing these ten Tamil songs, sung by Nambiyaruran, will attain the highest state of liberation. That supreme state is the benefit of this pathikam.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 16 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
We are contemplating the Thiru-sthalam pilgrimage of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam’. Having visited Thiru-vennainallur, Thiru-navalur, Thiru-thuraiyur, Thiru-vathigai, Thiru-manikkuzhi, and Thiru-thinaingar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal now reaches the boundary of Thillai. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approaches the boundary of Chidambaram, Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed many verses describing the beauty of Chidambaram.

Let us now ponder them.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Chidambaram, approaching its boundary. What kind of place is this Chidambaram? The sound of the veena with strings, the sound of the drum, the sound of the Vedas, and the singing of the celestial nymphs ...the sound of the music continuously resonates in that sacred place, which is Thillai, also known as Chidambaram. Sounds are generally categorized into two types: ‘osai’ (noise) and ‘oli’ (sound with meaning). ‘Osai’ refers to sounds that arise without any specific meaning, such as the sound of ocean waves or the sound of wind. There is sound, but no inherent meaning.

However, the sound in human speech and songs carries meaning, and this is referred to as ‘oli’.

Therefore, sounds without meaning are called ‘osai’, and sounds with meaning are called ‘oli’.

Here, Sekkizhar Peruman states that in Chidambaram, the ‘oli’ of music and the ‘oli’ of the Vedas are heard continuously. The ‘Veda oli’ is the sound of Vedic mantras produced by continuous ...recitation. This ‘oli’ arises encompassing Lord Shiva, who is the essence of the Vedas.

Similarly, the ‘geetha oli’ (sound of music), that is, the ‘oli’ of women singing with music, is heard continuously.

Therefore, both of these are ‘olis’. However, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to the sound from instruments like the veena and the sound from percussion instruments like the drum as ‘oli’. In reality, the sounds emanating from the veena and from percussion instruments like the mridangam are ‘osai’ (mere noise), meaning they are sounds without inherent meaning.

But here, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to them as ‘yaazh oli’ (sound of the veena) and ‘muzhavu oli’ (sound of the drum) because devotees ...in Chidambaram play the veena while singing the praises of the Lord. Since these hymns praising the Lord are referred to as ‘oli’ (meaningful sound), and the musical sound accompanying them is also called ‘geetha oli’, the sound of the veena that resonates with it is also praised as ‘oli’ here.

Similarly, since the Lord in Chidambaram is always performing His sacred dance, and the drum sound (muzhavoli) is played in accordance with that dance, this drum sound also attains the greatness of ‘oli’ because it is associated with Lord Shiva, the ultimate reality. This explanation is given by Sivakavimani.

Furthermore, in Chidambaram, the Lord is continuously performing the five cosmic functions (pancha krithyangal) of creation (srishti), sustenance (sthithi), dissolution (samharam), concealment (thirodhanam), and grace (anugraham) in the form of His dance. Among these five functions, the act of creation (srishti thozhil) ...is the primary function. The meaningful sound that the Lord produces for this act of creation is the sound of the veena. Why is the sound of the veena associated with the act of creation? Because in creation, the first thing to emerge is ‘natham’ (primordial sound). For that reason, the sound of the veena is associated with the act of creation here.

Thus, the ‘natham’ that first arises in the act of creation, and the Vedas, which are the sound-form spoken by the Lord, continuously resonate without interruption. Since the Vedas continuously resonate in Chidambaram without interruption, and sacrifices are performed according to the Vedas, the Devas (celestial beings) are always present in Chidambaram to receive the oblations. Since the Devas are always present in Chidambaram, the Deva Mathas (celestial mothers) are also present in Chidambaram ...and continuously sing praises to the Lord with music.

Similarly, devotees continuously sing devotional hymns like Thevaram, praising the Lord, set to proper melodies. In this Chidambaram, the sound of the veena, the sound of the drum, the sound of the Vedas, and the sound of music continuously occur without interruption. This is heard up to the boundary of Thillai.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who has reached the boundary of Thillai, also hears this.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches the boundary of Chidambaram. This Sundaramoorthy Swamigal always has a sacred chest adorned with a garland of lotus flowers and an excellent ruby necklace.

Here, the lotus garland ...refers to the garland worn by Brahmins, and the ruby necklace is an ornament worn by kings.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having grown up in the palace of King Narasimha Munaiyaraiyar, who belonged to the Adisaiva lineage, possesses both the wealth of kings and the wealth of Brahmins. On the boundary of this sacred Thillai, Chidambaram, there are many tanks and water bodies. In these water bodies, all aquatic flowers, including lotuses, bloom luxuriantly. As these blooming lotus flowers sway, beautiful carp fish leap against them. As these carp fish leap, the lotus flowers, along with all other flowers, sway. The bees, which are feeding on the pollen within these flowers, rise up and ...sway.

As the lotus flowers and other blossoms, along with the bees, sway, the water birds associated with them also make sounds and move, leaping and flying. There are many such tanks and water bodies. In these water bodies, waves are always rippling, and they are filled with white conches. Not only these water bodies, but also many paddy fields are nearby. These paddy fields are also filled with water, and fish leap in them.

Thus, Chidambaram, which has beautiful water bodies and paddy fields on its boundary, is a most excellent sacred place that removes the three impurities of the devotees who reach and worship it, bestowing liberation.

Now, let us look at the inner meaning and interpretation of this verse as explained by Sivakavimani. In the water bodies filled with lotus flowers, carp fish leap and play against each other. Leaping against is the nature of carp fish. As these carp fish leap against the lotus flowers, many aquatic flowers, including the lotus flowers, sway. As the flowers blooming in the water sway, the bees collecting pollen within those flowers and the water birds also sway and make sounds.

Thus, as the carp fish leap, the lotus flowers sway, and as a result, the bees and small water birds within the flowers also sway. This creates waves in the tanks. As waves are created in the tanks ...the eels in those tanks leap into the paddy fields near the tanks. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thillai, these carp fish leap in the direction opposite to the direction from which Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is coming. The eels leap into the paddy fields, indicating the direction in which Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is entering Chidambaram. The carp fish leap against the lotus flowers blooming in the lotus tanks, that is, in the direction opposite to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s arrival, leaping towards Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

This is because the lotuses in the tanks are inanimate lotuses.

Therefore, leaving them aside, the carp fish leap towards the lotuses on Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s sacred chest, which are the lotuses of wisdom ...and leap towards them. That is, when spiritual masters like Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrive, the souls wallowing in the mire of samsara (worldly existence) should abandon this mire and leap towards the enlightened spiritual masters. This is what the carp fish signify, leaping away from the lotuses in the mire and towards the lotus of wisdom, which is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s heart-lotus.

Furthermore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came to explain ‘Yoga’ within ‘Gnanam’, which is a subdivision of the path of ‘Gnanam’ among the four paths of Saivism: Chariyai, Kiriyai, Yogam, and Gnanam. In this ‘Adhara Yoga’, the ‘Jeevan’ (soul) travels upwards, transcending the six lotus centers (Adharas) of Mooladhara, Swadhishtana, Manipooraka, Anahatha, Visuddhi, and Agnya, possessing a quest for wisdom.

Therefore, the carp fish in the tanks outside Chidambaram leaping past the lotus flowers indicates the state where the ‘Jeevan’ leaps into wisdom, transcending the six lotus centers, in the path of ‘Gnana Yoga’ that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came to bestow upon us.

Thus, when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrives in Thillai, some explain that this natural scene observed in the outer world reminded the devotees with a quest for wisdom of the inner vision of ‘Gnana Yoga’.

Thus, for those who reach the boundary of Thillai, this sacred place, Chidambaram, removes the three impurities. If merely standing at the boundary of Chidambaram removes the three impurities, then ...if one enters and worships Chidambaram, liberation is certain. This is the inference drawn here. The three impurities are Anavam (ego), Kanmam (karma), and Mayai (illusion). These three are strong bonds. How are these three impurities removed?

Among the Kanma malams, Sanchitha Kanmam is removed by Athvasukthi, Prarabdham is exhausted by experience, and Agamiyam is completely destroyed by Sivagnanam.

Thus, when the three divisions of Kanmam – Sanchitham, Agamiyam, and Prarabdham – are destroyed, the Maya malam becomes like a small lamp before the sun, absorbed in the great light of Siva Sakthi. The Anava malam becomes like darkness before the sun, losing its power before the presence of Shiva.

Therefore, since these three impurities are removed even at the boundary of Chidambaram ...if one enters and worships Chidambaram, the bliss of liberation is certainly attained. This is the explanation of Sivakavimani.

Therefore, it is a tradition for devotees going to Thillai to worship at that boundary in that direction before entering. This applies not only to Chidambaram but to every Thiru-sthalam. When undertaking a pilgrimage to any Thiru-sthalam, it is customary to first circumambulate and worship that Thiru-sthalam. This is an aspect of temple worship.

Thus, the ‘pasu-thannmai’ (animalistic nature) that souls possessed before coming to Chidambaram, upon arriving here, transforms and attains the nature of Shiva.

Now, there are many groves on the boundary of Chidambaram. In these groves, trees like Naval (Jamun), Ma (Mango), Magizham (Mimusops elengi), Thennai (Coconut), Narathai (Citrus), Paakku (Areca nut) ...trees, Kumkumam (Saffron) trees, Iluppai (Mahua) trees, cool Vaazhai (Banana) trees, and dense Vanji (Calamus) trees, along with all other trees, grow tall as if touching the clouds. In these groves, cuckoos sing. Since there are water bodies, paddy fields, and groves, the outer boundary of Chidambaram appears like a ‘Bhoga Bhoomi’ (land of enjoyment). Sekkizhar describes it thus: just as outside Bharatha Khandam, which is a ‘Gnana Bhoomi’ (land of wisdom), there is Kimpurusha Khandam, which is a ‘Bhoga Bhoomi’, similarly, outside Chidambaram, which is a ‘Gnana Bhoomi’, there are groves, water bodies, and paddy fields, making it appear like a ‘Bhoga Bhoomi’. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that the many types of trees mentioned in this verse are useful for the Lord’s worship, providing ...flowers, offerings, betel leaves, mouth fresheners, sacred ablutions, and sacred lamps.

This is noteworthy. From these trees in the groves, flocks of cuckoos sing sweetly. Do you know how glorious these cuckoos are? Manikkavasaga Peruman has addressed these cuckoos, singing, ‘Geetham Iniya Kuyile’ (O sweet-singing cuckoo). Manikkavasaga Swamigal has commanded these cuckoos, singing, ‘Geetham Iniya Kuyile, Aathi Gunam Ondrum Illaan, Antham Illaan Varakoovuvai’ (O sweet-singing cuckoo, call Him who has no beginning, no end, to come). These cuckoos are descendants in that lineage.

That is why the author later states that when Thiru-navukkarasu Swamigal arose to worship Thillai, these cuckoos called out to all the people, inviting them to worship him and attain salvation. continues to describe the beauty of Chidambaram. Let us enjoy and worship it through Sekkizhar’s words and attain the grace of Shiva.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 17 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam’, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches the boundary of Thillai. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes the natural scenery there. Sivakavimani explains the meaning and inner meaning of those scenes.

Let us proceed with our contemplation. On the outer boundary of Chidambaram, there are many sacred groves. In these groves, there are Vanni trees, Kondrai trees, Surapunai trees, Shanbhaga trees, Sandalwood or Aathi trees, Palasu trees with flowers, Cherunthi trees with yellow flowers, Kongu trees, Kura trees, Karpaga trees, Pathiri trees, Vilva trees, and many other trees. In these sacred groves, these ...trees all provide flowers and leaves suitable for the Lord’s worship. Sandalwood helps in anointing the Lord’s sacred body. The leaves of Vilvam, Vanni, and Aaram are especially suitable for Shiva Puja.

The Cherunthi tree is a small tree that blooms with yellow flowers suitable for Shiva Puja. Kura flowers are also dear to the Lord. Sivakavimani states that devotees who undertake garden renovation work in this era, seeing this verse, would greatly benefit by planting and nurturing these types of trees, as it would be a great act of Shiva Punya. Flowers suitable for the Lord’s worship are of four types: Kottuppoo (tree flowers), Kodi-ppoo (creeper flowers), Nila-ppoo (ground flowers), and Neer-ppoo (water flowers). Kottuppoo are flowers that bloom on trees. Kodi-ppoo are flowers that bloom on creepers.

Nila-ppoo are flowers that come from small plants on the ground. Neer-ppoo are flowers like lotuses found in tanks and water bodies. In the previously mentioned verse, Sekkizhar Swamigal had already described water flowers by describing lotus flowers. In this verse, he describes trees that yield Vanni, Kondrai, Pathiri, and other flowers. These are Kottuppookkal (tree flowers). That is, Pathiri flowers or Kondrai flowers belong to the category of Kottuppoo, which bloom from trees.

Furthermore, in these groves, many creeper flowers like Malligai (Jasmine) and Mullai (Jasmine) are also found. Not only that, but also Nila-ppoo varieties like Nandiyavattam (Crape Jasmine) and Alari (Oleander), which are ground flowers that grow as small plants from the ground ...are also present.

Therefore, on the outer boundary of Chidambaram, water flowers like lotuses are in the tanks. The second type, Kottuppoo, meaning flowers from trees like Pathiri and Kondrai, are also present. Creeper flowers like Malligai and Mullai, which are Kodi-ppoo, and their flowers are also present. Nila-ppoo varieties like Nandiyavattam and Alari are also in those sacred groves.

Thus, all four types of flowers suitable for the Lord’s worship are luxuriantly growing in the sacred groves surrounding Chidambaram. Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to these groves as sacred groves. Since they provide flowers, leaves, roots, etc., suitable for the Lord’s worship, they are sacred groves.

Thus, by providing flowers suitable for the Lord’s worship, the trees, creepers, and plants in this sacred grove are blessed with the great fortune of performing Shiva Punya.

Furthermore, these are single-sensed beings, and therefore, they are blessed with the fortune of performing only Shiva Punyas, without performing any other karmas.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to this grove, filled with single-sensed beings that perform only Shiva Punyas, as a sacred grove. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed to this sacred grove and proceeded. That is, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships these sacred groves and proceeds, because the trees and other elements in these sacred groves are Shiva Punya materials, and therefore ...they are worthy of worship.

Furthermore, these pure beings that perform Shiva Punyas are here as trees, plants, and creepers suitable for Shiva Puja due to the special power of their penance, meaning the merit of penance performed in many previous births.

Therefore, it is a rule of temple worship that we should worship these beings who have performed Shiva penance and proceed.

Thus, it is understood that those who perform temple worship should worship the sacred groves as soon as they see them and proceed. There is a story in Sivarahasyam that a sage once performed severe penance to be born as a tree that performs Shiva Puja and received that blessing and boon.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal enters, bowing to the outer boundary of Chidambaram and worshipping the sacred groves there.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal enters, bowing to the outer boundary of Chidambaram and worshipping the sacred groves there. The outer perimeter of this Chidambaram is encircled by moats. These moats are exceedingly wide, deep, and long, encompassing Chidambaram. They are so vast that it appears as if the ocean itself has entered to form these moats, with the intention that we too should worship the Lord who performs the Thiru-Dance in Chidambaram.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal is greatly delighted upon seeing these immense moats. Even today, the Kollidam river and its canals surround Chidambaram on three sides: east, south, and north. Sivakavimani writes that the moat on the west side has since silted up. This Thillai-paththi is a ‘Naan Marai-paththi,’ meaning a place praised by the four Vedas, and a place that fosters the virtues and sacrifices prescribed by the Vedas.

Thus, in Chidambaram, the four Vedas resonate ceaselessly. These four Vedas extol the sacred anklet sound (Thiru-Silamboli). This anklet sound, praised by the Vedas, is the sound that arises from the Lord’s dance in this sacred place.

Here, the Lord performs the dance for the salvation of all beings, witnessed by Sivakami Ammaiyar.

Thus, the Vedas praise this anklet sound that arises from the Lord’s dance. Why does the Lord dance here? He dances for the salvation of the seven great worlds. The ‘seven great worlds’ refers to all worlds, grouped as seven by seven. He dances to grant salvation to the seven types of births: single-sensed plants, two-sensed aquatic creatures, three-sensed reptiles, four-sensed birds, five-sensed animals, six-sensed humans, and celestials.

Thus, the Lord performs the sacred dance here for the salvation of all beings born in the seven types of existences across the seven times seven worlds. He performs the sacred dance for Sivakami Ammaiyar to witness. It is by Sivakami Ammaiyar witnessing this sacred dance that all these beings attain salvation. Why does our Mother Uma Devi, Sivakami Ammaiyar, witness this sacred dance of the Lord? Because souls are unable to directly behold the Lord’s sacred dance with their physical eyes; they are incapable of seeing it directly.

Therefore, these souls, with the Lord’s consent, Our Mother Uma Devi, the Goddess, witnesses the sacred dance performed by the Lord with her divine eyes and bestows the benefit of that vision upon us, the souls. As Kumaraguruparar Swamigal explains, “Like a mother who, when her milk-fed infant cannot tolerate the medicine for an illness, consumes the medicine herself so that it reaches the child through her milk and cures the ailment.” Just as a mother consumes medicine for her infant who cannot take it directly, so too, beings lack the direct qualification or power to witness the Lord’s sacred dance.

Therefore, our Mother Sivakami Devi, who bore us, beholds the Lord’s sacred dance with her own eyes and bestows its benefit upon all beings.

Furthermore, the Lord lifts His Kunchitha Padam (raised foot) and dances there, and the anklet on that raised sacred foot resonates. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this as “eduthu nadam navindru arul silamboli” (the anklet sound that arises from the gracious dance performed by lifting the foot). Lifting the foot refers to one of the Lord’s sacred feet, the raised foot.

Moreover, it can also be interpreted as the sacred foot that lifts beings so they do not become entangled in this cycle of birth. This is the beginning of creation (Srishti). The beginning of Srishti is Nada (primordial sound).

Therefore, that Nada is the anklet sound. The Lord, taking a small portion from the Moolaprakriti (primordial matter), initiates the act of creation for the sake of beings, bringing forth Nada as the first of the thirty-six Tattvas (principles).

Therefore, Nada is the first manifestation of creation. The two sacred feet of the Lord are Jnana Shakti (power of knowledge) and Kriya Shakti (power of action). Between these two sacred feet of the Lord, Jnana Shakti and Kriya Shakti, the Shuddha Maya (pure illusion) remains contained.

Thus, Shuddha Maya, contained between the Lord’s sacred feet of Jnana Shakti and Kriya Shakti, brings forth Nada as the first Tattva from itself.

Therefore, the wise explain that Shuddha Maya is the anklet worn by the Lord, and the sound emanating from that anklet is Nada, the first Tattva arising from Shuddha Maya. This is what Thiru-Undhiyar states: “Thiru-Silambosai oli vazhiye sendru niruththanai kumbidu endru Undhi Para” (Go forth by the path of the sacred anklet’s sound, halt, and worship Him, Undhi Para). The four Vedas in Thillai praise this anklet sound of such great glory.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal observes the long moats surrounding Thillai. Lotus flowers bloom in these moats. The bees in these lotus flowers fly up and enter the Thazhai (screw pine) flowers. After entering the Thazhai flowers, they collect the pollen there and emerge. The pollen in the Thazhai flowers is white.

Therefore, when Sundaramurthi Swamigal sees these white-colored bees emerging from the Thazhai flowers, they remind him of devotees who have applied sacred ash (Thiru-Neeru). But what is the difference? The pollen that these bees have applied is a product of Maya (illusion), whereas the sacred ash applied by the devotees is pure Thiru-Neeru. It is natural for any object to evoke memories according to the observer’s mindset.

Therefore, to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who is always contemplating devotees, these bees with white pollen on their bodies remind him of devotees who have applied Thiru-Neeru. They are always joyful, humming with delight.

Similarly, devotees also apply Thiru-Neeru. They are always joyful, remembering the Lord. They are always singing the Lord’s praises.

Thus, applying Thiru-Neeru, being joyful, and singing the Lord’s praises are common characteristics shared by devotees and bees.

Here, bees fly from inside the lotus flowers and collect pollen from the Thazhai flowers. The lotus is a flower of the Marutham land, that is, the land rich in agricultural fields. The Thazhai is a flower of the Neythal land, which is the coastal region and the land adjacent to the sea. The fact that these two flowers, the lotus of the Marutham land and the Thazhai of the Neythal land, are found on the outer perimeter of Chidambaram indicates that this land of Chidambaram is a blend of Marutham and Neythal lands.

Thus, these bees, that is, the bees in the lotus flowers in the moat on the outer perimeter of Chidambaram, why do they rise up, fly, and hum? Because inside Chidambaram, within the city of Chidambaram, there is a wonderful honey, the Sabhanayagar, who is always performing the Ananda Thandavam (dance of bliss) in Thiruchitrambalam. To seek and obtain that honey, the Vedas, like bees, hum inside Chidambaram. Why are the Vedas referred to as bees? Just as bees have six legs, the Vedas also have six Vedangas (limbs of the Vedas): Shiksha, Vyakarana, Chandas, Nirukta, Jyotisha, and Kalpa.

Therefore, bees with six legs are compared to the Vedas which have six Angas (limbs).

Thus, just as the Vedas, like bees, sing praises to the inexhaustible honey, the Sabhanayagar, within the city of Chidambaram, so too do the bees in the moat outside Chidambaram, like devotees, strive to apply sacred ash and hum, as described by Sekkizhar Swamigal.

Furthermore, the bees in the flowers in the moat on the outskirts of Chidambaram visit many different flowers, consuming small drops of honey. Even though they consume it little by little, they have no real interest in it. It only provides temporary sweetness to the tongue.

Moreover, the honey from ordinary flowers, if consumed excessively, can even cause illness.

Therefore, these bees are tired of consuming this honey. In contrast, what kind of honey is found in Thiruchitrambalam within the city of Thillai? It is honey that is always sweet to all senses and to life itself. It is honey that never cloys, no matter how much is consumed. It is great honey that always bestows good upon those who consume it.

Therefore, the bees in the moat on the outer perimeter of Chidambaram, desiring to consume that great honey, fly up and hum.

Thus, Sekkizhar beautifully describes the natural scenery of Chidambaram. Let us resume our reflection.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 18 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthaatkonda Puranam,’ Sekkizhar Swamigal explains Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s darshan of Chidambaram.

Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes and praises this sacred place of Chidambaram, comparing it to Lord Brahma. Chidambaram is a golden sacred place. Lord Brahma is also golden-hued; He is revered as Hiranyagarbha. Just as Lord Brahma has four sacred faces, Chidambaram also has four sacred gateways, one for each direction. White flags flutter and wave at these gateways. On Lord Brahma’s tongue, the Goddess of Learning, draped in white silk, shines.

Lord Brahma always recites the Vedas with His tongue.

Similarly, in this Chidambaram, the Vedas always resonate.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal enters through the northern sacred gateway of this highly esteemed Chidambaram.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships the city of Thiru-Thinnai Nagar and then comes to Chidambaram.

Therefore, he arrives from the north and enters through the northern sacred gateway.

Similarly, Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman enters Chidambaram from the southern sacred gateway. Appar Peruman enters through the western sacred gateway.

Thus, each of our Samayacharyas (religious preceptors) reaches Chidambaram through a different direction. Hearing of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s arrival, the devotees of Thillai, spurred by their love for him, gathered before the northern sacred gateway to welcome him.

Thus, as Sundaramurthi Swamigal approached the northern sacred gateway, the devotees and Nambis (priests) who had already gathered there, upon seeing each other, simultaneously bowed to one another. It was impossible to discern who bowed first, as both bowed upon seeing each other. This is the proper way to greet devotees.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds to enter Chidambaram to worship the Lord, but before that, he bows to the devotees at the gateway.

Therefore, the veneration of devotees should occur first, and the veneration of the Lord should follow. This is the proper order. Inside Chidambaram, Lord Nataraja is enshrined in the Ponnambalam, which is a gold-roofed edifice.

Similarly, the beautiful and important mansions of the other Thillai Vaazh Andhanars (Brahmins of Thillai) are also gold-roofed and shine with a golden hue.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal enters through the northern sacred gateway and reaches the northern sacred street. In Chidambaram, in this sacred street where Lord Nataraja delights to reside, the four Vedas always resonate. The dances, songs, and sounds of celestial maidens continuously take place. In the sky, always enveloped by clouds, five divine drums continuously resound. In the fragrant new garlands of flowers, the humming of bees continuously resounds. The sound of devotees, whose eyes overflow with tears of surging love, continuously chanting “Pottri!

“Pottri!” (Praise! Praise!) resounds everywhere. From the eyes of the devotees, tears flow due to the love overflowing in their hearts. This is one of the characteristics of a devotee. Tears are the outward sign that reveals the unseen love within the heart, as expressed in the Thirukkural: “Is there a lock for love? The tears of the loving will proclaim it.” Each mansion in this Chidambaram appears like a city of Kubera (god of wealth), rising high enough to touch the clouds.

“The smoke from the homas (sacred fires) nurtured by the yogic contemplation of the Brahmins residing in these unparalleled mansions ascends to the sky, mingling with the clouds and forming a cloud-like mass. Within these cloud masses, that is, as the sacrificial smoke from the Brahmins’ rituals rises and appears like clouds, the white flags hoisted above those mansions shine like lightning within those cloud masses. On the gopurams (temple towers) of Chidambaram, everywhere on the ‘nasipakkangal’ (the decorative projections above the levels of the gopuram), peacocks are dancing. The ‘nasi’ is the facade placed above the levels of the gopuram, and peacocks dance there; it is a special place for peacocks to dance. Peacocks naturally roost and build nests on the gopuram entrances during the twilight hours when they are often entranced, and during the rainy seasons. Peacocks are known to dance upon seeing clouds.

“In Chidambaram, since the Brahmins always perform sacrifices during the daytime, the resulting homa smoke appears like clouds. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes that the peacocks are always dancing upon seeing these clouds.

“Thus, the homa smokes that cause the peacocks to dance originate from the homas performed by the Brahmins. In those sacrifices, fire kindled by rubbing Arani sticks (sacred wood) always burns. It was customary in those times to use only fire kindled by Arani for yagnas.

“Furthermore, the Vedas and Agamas conclude that fire obtained from Arani sticks is the purest fire. One might ask if there is a distinction between pure and impure fire. Indeed, such a distinction exists.

“Therefore, it is ignorance to think that all fire is the same fire. The qualities of fire vary due to its place of origin, the wood it burns, its dwelling place, and various other factors. The fire produced by modern matches, electricity, etc., has different qualities. There is a difference between a tamarind tree and a neem tree.

“Similarly, medical texts state that there is a difference between the fire from tamarind wood and the fire from neem wood. Sivakavimani explains that the quality of fire varies due to various factors. He mentioned three main reasons: one, the place where the fire originates; two, the wood or other fuel it burns; and three, the place where the fire resides. He illustrates the difference in the first factor, the place where fire originates, by pointing out the different qualities of fire and heat produced by matches, electricity, etc. He explains the difference in the second factor, the wood or other fuel that the fire burns, by citing medical texts that describe the difference between tamarind wood fire and neem wood fire.

“Thirdly, fire yields different qualities depending on where it resides. For example, there is a difference between the fire that arises in a yagna and the fire that arises during funeral rites.

“Thus, the elders state that the qualities of fire vary due to different factors such as its place of origin, the materials it burns, and its dwelling place. The practice of kindling fire with Arani sticks is gradually diminishing from common use. We should note that in Shiva Yagnas, the custom of kindling fire from the sun’s rays using a Suryakantha (sunstone) to begin the sacrifice is still in practice.

“Thus, in the Brahmin mansions along the streets of Chidambaram, sacrifices are always being performed. The homa smoke generated by these sacrifices always gathers in the sky like clouds. The hosts of Devas (celestial beings) are always present in Chidambaram to receive the oblations from these ongoing yagnas. Flower garlands are always hanging everywhere in the streets of Chidambaram. At the entrance of every home, a Poorana Kumbam (auspicious pot) always shines. Chariots and other vehicles are always moving through the streets of Chidambaram, and their bells continuously chime.

“In the alms-houses throughout Chidambaram, mountains of rice, prepared from red paddy, are displayed. Why does Sekkizhar mention that the food is made from red paddy? Sekkizhar Swamigal specifies red paddy because of its special qualities and characteristics. Black paddy, known as Kar Nel, is considered inferior, while red paddy is highly esteemed. The details of this will be extensively explained later in the biography of Arivattaya Nayanar.

“Thus, alms-houses that prepare and offer food like mountains are always present in Thillai. This is why Thillai is praised by Navukkarasar Swamigal as “Annam Palikkum Thillai Chitrambalam” (Thillai Chitrambalam, which bestows food). Because Navukkarasar Peruman thus bestowed the epithet “Annam Palikkum Thillai Chitrambalam,” as a result, even today, alms-houses are abundant throughout Thillai, and food distribution continues.

“Furthermore, water pandals (stalls offering water) are plentiful throughout Chidambaram. ‘Thanneer’ (water) here refers to cool water. Alms-houses that alleviate hunger and water pandals that quench thirst are abundant everywhere in Chidambaram.

“Thus, by Sekkizhar Swamigal mentioning ‘Thanneer pandal,’ meaning cool water, he implies that cool water is always suitable for drinking and is the best.

“Therefore, Sivakavimani explains that the hot beverages currently in common use are not suitable for drinking. Hunger and thirst come one after another.

“Therefore, alms-houses are everywhere throughout Chidambaram to alleviate hunger, and water pandals are everywhere to quench thirst. The beauty of all worlds, as if gathered into one, shines forth in Chidambaram, which is filled with auspicious and abundant blessings.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, entering through the northern Thiru-vaayil (sacred entrance) of this Chidambaram, circumambulates the northern Thiru-veethi (sacred street) and proceeds inward, revering the inner street that surrounds the sacred hall where the Lord dances. In front of this Chidambaram temple, great deities like Thirumal, Naanmugan, and Indra, along with other devas, wait outside, observing the time when they can enter and behold the Lord.

“However, devotees filled with merit, purity, and love enter without hindrance, behold the Lord, and exit. This inner Thiru-veethi is the sacred street that encircles the Ambalam.

“Therefore, upon seeing it, the memory of the great devotees who came and sang here arises. The events of Nayanmars like Thirumooladeva Nayanar, Manikkavasagar, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, and Aiyadigal Kadavarkon worshipping here arise in the heart of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. The nature of the sacred texts like the Vedas, Upanishads, Sivaragasyam, Thirumoolar’s Thirumandiram, Thiruvasagam, and Thevaram, all singing praises of this sacred place and the Lord, also arises in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal thus circumambulated and worshipped the great walled golden mansion and the great Ambalam, Meru, according to tradition.

“Here, Sekkizhar mentions the golden mansion is the Ponnambalam, the Thiruchitrambalam. Devas and Hiranyavarman, among many others, have, until this day, at various times, roofed the Thiru-chitrambalam mansion with gold, as known from historical accounts.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal circumambulates and worships the Ponnambalam, surrounded by great walls, and the Perambalam, which is Meru, according to tradition. Perambalam is the hall where Utsava Nayakas, including Somaskandar, are always enshrined. Chitrambalam is the golden hall where Koothudaiyan Sabhanayagar is enshrined. Both are surrounded by sacred walls. Both, that is, Perambalam and Ponnambalam, are south of the Thirumoolattanathar temple. Why is this Perambalam described as Meru? has a name called Sushumna or Suzhumunai.

“Lanka is the Ida-kalai, and the Himalayas are the Pingalai, and this Thillai, known as Chidambaram, shines as the Sushumna or Suzhumunai.

“Therefore, this Perambalam is called Meru. Above the Sushumna, shining brightly, is the light of Shiva-gnana.

“Therefore, circumambulating this Meru, Sundaramurthi Swamigal always entered through the Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil in front of the Thiruchitrambalam, which is surrounded by the ever-growing light. This Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil is very close to the place where the Lord dances. Beyond this, there are no other entrances. To behold the Lord, we have to pass through many entrances, both inside and outside. Among the countless entrances leading to the Lord, this Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil is the last sacred entrance. Beyond this, there are no other sacred entrances.

“For the devoted servants who enter from outside, this is the last sacred entrance. But for the Lord who comes from within to bestow grace upon devotees, this is the first sacred entrance. This Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil serves as the last sacred entrance for devotees proceeding towards the Lord and the first sacred entrance for the Lord coming towards the devotees. Only devoted servants can enter through this sacred entrance, hence it is named Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil.

“Let us continue to behold the Thiruchitrambalam.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 19 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
We are now contemplating Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s darshan of Chidambaram in the section of Periya Puranam called Thaduthatkonda Puranam.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal visits the Thiru-thina Nagar sthalam and from there enters Chidambaram through the northern gate of Thillai. He beholds the northern boundary, then the northern Thiru-vaayil, then circumambulates the northern street, enters the Chidambaram temple, circumambulates the inner street, circumambulates the Thiru-maligais of Ponnambalam and Perambalam where Thiruchitrambalam is located, enters the Thiru-anukkan Thiru-vaayil, and stands before Thiru-chitrambalam. In front of Thiru-chitrambalam are the steps called Thiru-kazhitruppadi. The Thiru-kazhitruppadi consists of five steps. On both sides of these steps are two elephant-trunk-shaped balustrades. ‘Kalitru’ means elephant. Since there are elephant-trunk-shaped balustrades on both sides and five steps in between, this staircase is named Thiru-kazhitruppadi.

The elephant-trunk shape signifies Pranava, and the five steps signify Sri Panchakshara. If one crosses these Sri Panchakshara steps, one can behold the great dancer who performs the Panchakrityas. There are five sabhas in the Chidambaram temple: the Raja Sabha, also known as the Thousand-Pillared Hall; the Deva Sabha, which is the Perambalam; the Nritta Sabha; the Kanaka Sabha, and the Chit Sabha, which is the Chitrambalam.

Thus, five sabhas are located within the Thillai temple. Among these, the Raja Sabha, the Thousand-Pillared Hall, is the Annamaya Kosha. The Deva Sabha, the Perambalam, is the Pranamaya Kosha. The Nritta Sabha, that sacred hall, is the Manomaya Kosha. The Kanaka Sabha is the Vijnanamaya Kosha. The Chit Sabha, the Thiruchitrambalam, is the Anandamaya Kosha.

Within this Anandamaya Kosha, the Chit Sabha, the Ananda Nataraja Peruman shines, dancing. We saw in the first work that this Chit Sabha shines as the Hridaya Sthana of the Virat Purusha. In the Thousand-Pillared Hall, also called the Annamaya Kosha and Raja Sabha, Sri Gnana Maha Nataraja Peruman will have the Ani Uthiram Thirumanjanam.

Similarly, he will grant the Margazhi Thiruvathirai Darshanam. Six ablutions for Nataraja Peruman occur every year: Chithirai Thiruvonam Abhishekam, Ani Uthiram Thirumanjanam, Avani Chaturdashi Abhishekam, Purattasi Chaturdashi Abhishekam, Margazhi Thiruvathirai Darshanam, Masi Chaturdashi Abhishekam. Since our one year is one day for the Devas, it is believed that Sri Nataraja Peruman bestows grace through six ablutions and darshan in Chidambaram, representing the six daily pujas of the Devas. Among these, Ani Uthiram and Margazhi Thiruvathirai take place in this Thousand-Pillared Hall. This is the Annamaya Kosha, also called the Rajya Sabha. It was in this hall that our Sekkizhar Peruman’s Periya Puranam was inaugurated.

This Thousand-Pillared Hall is a thousand-petaled lotus, called Sahasrara Padma. The Sivaganga nearby is called Chandra Pushkarani, the Amrita Vapi. The Perambalam, also known as Deva Sabha, near Thiruchitrambalam, is the Pranamaya Kosha.

Here, Utsava Nayakas, including Somaskandar, are enshrined. Anapaya Chozhan, who patronized Sekkizhar Peruman, roofed this Perambalam with gold, making it golden, according to history.

Next, the Manomaya Kosha is the Nritta Sabha. It is located south of the flagstaff, in front of Nataraja Peruman. The Urdhva Tandava Murthy is enshrined here. The entire hall resembles a chariot drawn by horses, and is adorned with wheels. This Nritta Sabha is a hall supported by fifty-six pillars, featuring unique sculptural work found nowhere else.

Next, the Kanaka Sabha, which shines as the Vijnanamaya Kosha.

Here, Nataraja Peruman receives Thirumanjanam. This Kanaka Sabha is also called Ponnambalam.

Similarly, scholars praise Thiruvalangadu as Ratna Sabha, Madurai as Rajata Sabha (Velliyambalam), Thirunelveli as Tamira Sabha, and Thirukutralam as Chitra Sabha. Next is the Thiruchitrambalam, which is the Anandamaya Kosha. Here lies the Chidambara Rahasyam. The Vimana above this Thiru-chitrambalam temple, which is the Chit Sabha, has nine kalasams. These nine kalasams represent the nine Shaktis: Vama, Jyeshta, Raudri, Kali, Kalavikarani, Balavikarani, Balapramathani, Sarvabhootadamani, and Manonmani. The sixty-four wooden beams in this Vimana represent the sixty-four arts.

The golden roof above this sacred Vimana is made of gold and is covered with twenty-one thousand six hundred gold plates. This signifies that a human being breathes, on average, twenty-one thousand six hundred times a day. Seventy-two thousand nails were used to connect these twenty-one thousand six hundred gold plates. These seventy-two thousand nails represent the seventy-two thousand nadis that support the twenty-one thousand six hundred breaths a human takes. In the lower part of this Thiruchitrambalam, in the eastern direction, at the Chandeswarasvasthanam, is Brahma Devar; in the southern direction, Vishnu Murthy; and in the northern direction, Vairava Murthy, also known as Shankara Rudra. In this great hall of Thiru-chitrambalam, there are five pedestals for Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, Maheswara, and Sadashiva.

The five sacred letters, Sri Panchakshara, form the five steps. On both sides of these five steps are the elephant trunks, representing Pranava, as balustrades. The door beyond the Sri Panchakshara steps is called Avidya. There are ninety-six windows in this Thiruchitrambalam, which represent the ninety-six Tattvas and Tattvikas. In the center of this Chit Sabha are four golden pillars representing the four Vedas. The surrounding twenty-eight wooden pillars represent the twenty-eight Agamas.

The space contained within these is called Suddha Vidya. The pedestal from Chidambara Rahasyam up to the place where Sri Nataraja Murthy is enshrined is called Pranava Peetam. Upon that Pranava Peetam, the Srimath Ananda Nataraja Murthy, with a complete form, is enshrined. Near it, in the Vayu Bhaga, the Vyaktavyakta Lingam, which is Sakala Nishkala, is enshrined. Behind it, the Aranas and Agamas, which are known and recited, and which transcend the limits of qualities, and the imperishable supreme light of Shiva, in the form of Chidakasha, praised by the exalted as Thiruchitrambalam, shine forth. This form of Chidakasha is what those with physical eyes, who have seen it, call the secret.

Here, the Thiru-ambalachakram is installed in the form of a mantra.

Once a year, civet paste is applied, and a golden bilva garland is placed upon it. The curtain hanging over it is black on the outside and red on the inside. This signifies that the Lord’s concealing power, Tirodhana Shakti, transforms into the power of grace and benefits us. In this sanctum sanctorum, along with Shivakami Sundari Ammaiyar, the universal mother, who is Para Shakti, there are also the Sthoola Nataraja Murthy, the Ratnamaya Sukshma Nataraja Murthy, and another Sukshmatara Lingam. Sages describe the Chidambara Rahasyam as Nishkala, the Vyaktavyakta Lingam as Sakala Nishkala, and the Srimath Ananda Nataraja Murthy as Sakala form.

Thus, the Para Shakti in the form of Akasha within this Chit Sabha is called Thiruchitrambalam, and the Para Brahma Shiva performing the dance in that Thiru-chitrambalam is called Ati Sukshma Chith. This Akasha is not inert like the elemental Akasha; it is Chith itself.

Therefore, it is called Chidambaram, meaning Chith Ambaram, Chidambaram. Since this Chidambaram shines here without cessation, this Thillai forest also gets the name Chidambaram.

Thus, the form of Shiva-Shakti, which is unobstructed knowledge, is the Ambalam, and therefore it is called Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to this Thiru-chitrambalam as the Thiru-chitrambalam that blossomed at the beginning, middle, and end of the Vedas, and as the Thiru-chitrambalam that blossomed in the minds of devotees. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Thiru-chitrambalam blossomed at the beginning, middle, and end of the Vedas. Pranava is said to be the seat for the Lord, Ishvara, and the origin for all other beings. The Chit-Ambalam, called Thiru-chitrambalam, acts as the life force for Pranava, activating it. Pranava is activated by Thiru-chitrambalam.

This Pranava manifests as ‘Om’ in its collective state and as ‘A’, ‘U’, ‘M’ in its individual state. These ‘A’, ‘U’, ‘M’ are found at the beginning, middle, and end of the Vedas. How so? In the first Veda, Rig Veda, the first letter of the first word of the first mantra is ‘A’. In the middle Veda, Yajur Veda, the middle letter of the mantra located in the middle is ‘U’. And in the third Veda, Sama Veda, the last letter of the last word of the last mantra is ‘M’.

Therefore, the Vedas are the expanded form of Pranava. Hence, the rule is that the Vedas should begin with Pranava and end with Pranava. Since Pranava is thus located at the beginning, middle, and end of the Vedas, Thiruchitrambalam, which is the life force of Pranava, is also located and blossomed at the beginning, middle, and end of the Vedas, taking Pranava as its abode.

Similarly, when reciting the twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas, we begin with ‘Thiruchitrambalam’ and conclude with ‘Thiru-chitrambalam’. Just as the Vedas begin and end with Pranava, so too the twelve Thirumurais, the Tamil Vedas, begin with ‘Thodu’ in the first hymn ‘Thodudaiya Seviyan’, with the letter ‘O’ on ‘Tha’, and end with ‘Ulagellam’ in the final verse of the twelfth Thirumurai, Periya Puranam, concluding with ‘Ma’, thus manifesting as an expansion of Pranava. Just as the Vedas begin and end with Pranava, it is customary to begin reciting the twelve Thirumurais, the Tamil Vedas, with ‘Thiru-chitrambalam’ and conclude with ‘Thiru-chitrambalam’ after recitation. Within this Thiru-chitrambalam, the Lord, Srimath Ananda Nataraja Peruman, continuously performs the sacred dance. The purpose of this sacred dance performed by the Lord is to remove the inherent impurities from souls, purify them, ripen them, and enable them to attain liberation, which is eternal bliss. The souls are countless, eternal, and possess the capacity to understand when enlightened.

For such souls, like rust on copper or husk on paddy, Anava, Karma, and Maya have inherently concealed the Ichcha, Gnana, and Kriya Shaktis of the souls. If these impurities are removed, souls will attain bliss.

However, these impurities do not disappear on their own.

Moreover, these impurities are not intelligent.

Similarly, souls cannot remove these impurities on their own because souls do not have independence.

Therefore, the Lord must remove the impurity. That impurity is called Malam, and Malam is explained as that which conceals the Chith Shakti of the soul. To remove such Malam and grant the blissful abode of liberation, the Lord performs the five functions. These five functions are creation, preservation, destruction, concealment, and bestowal of grace. Among these, creation is the manifestation of Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas in accordance with the karmic fruits of souls. Preservation is the sustenance of these granted Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas until the time they are experienced according to karma.

Destruction is the temporary rest or repose given to souls to alleviate their weariness from experiencing these Bhogas.

Now, the fourth function, concealment, or Tirodhana, is to mature these impurities and bring about ripeness in souls. By the ripeness thus attained, grace is registered, and thereby, the bestowal of supreme bliss upon pure souls is Anugraha, the act of grace. This is the fifth function. The simultaneous performance of these five functions is the five-fold dance, which is the sacred dance performed by Gnana Maha Nataraja Peruman here in Thiruchitrambalam. From the Udukkai (drum) held in one of the Lord’s sacred hands, creation, or Srishti, takes place. In another sacred hand, held in a gesture of protection, preservation, or Sthiti, takes place.

The Agni (fire) held in another sacred hand of the Lord performs destruction, or Samhara. The function of concealment, or Tirodhana, takes place in the Lord’s planted foot. The function of grace, or Anugraha, which is liberation, takes place in the Lord’s raised sacred foot.

Thus, the five functions occur simultaneously in the sacred dance of our Lord.

Furthermore, the Lord is in the form of Panchakshara. The letter ‘Na’ is in the Lord’s sacred feet, ‘Ma’ in the abdomen, ‘Shi’ in the shoulders, ‘Va’ is the face, and ‘Ya’ is the sacred crown.

Thus, when counted from foot to head, the Lord’s form shines as the sacred five letters ‘Na Ma Shi Va Ya’.

Now, the Lord’s Udukkai (drum) is ‘Shi’, the extended sacred hand is ‘Va’, the Abhaya Karana (hand of protection) is ‘Ya’, the fire is ‘Na’, and the dwarf (Muyalagan) below is ‘Ma’. When viewed this way, the Lord’s right side is seen as ‘Shi Va Ya’ and the left side as ‘Na Ma’, making the Lord’s form the five letters ‘Shi Va Ya Na Ma’. The ‘Na Ma Shi Va Ya’ mentioned above is the Sthoola Panchakshara. The ‘Shi Va Ya Na Ma’ just mentioned is the Sukshma Panchakshara.

Furthermore, ‘Shi’ is Shiva, ‘Va’ is Shakti, and ‘Ya’ is the soul.

Therefore, placing the soul, ‘Ya’, between Shiva, ‘Shi’, and Shakti, ‘Va’, in the protective Abhaya Hasta, demonstrates the immense compassion of our Lord.

Now, the Lord’s Thiru-vasi (sacred arch) is Pranava. The fifty-one flames within it are the fifty-one Aksharas. The Lord, with his extended sacred hand, casts away Maya from us. He illuminates the illusion, then with the fire held in another Thiru-hand, He burns away our strong karmas. With His firm Thiru-foot, He suppresses the mala (impurity). With His raised Thiru-foot, which is grace, He immerses us, the souls, in the ocean of bliss.

This is the goal of the Thiru-kuthu (sacred dance) of the Lord, as stated by the Saiva Siddhanta scripture, “Unmai Vilakkam” (The Exposition of Truth). This dance of bliss is called the Nadanta Natakam (Dance of the End of Sound). The one who witnesses and reveals this dance is Sivakami Thayar. The gentle smile of Ananda Nataraja Peruman removes the three types of suffering of souls. The Thiru-chadai (sacred matted hair) of Peruman is the very form of gnana (wisdom). His dancing with it spread on both sides signifies the boundless bestowal of gnana.

It signifies the generosity of the benefactor. On Peruman’s sacred body, the sacred ash that removes all our bonds, and the sacred thread in the form of the Kundalini, and the heroic anklet in the form of Para-nada that cuts off the karmas of devotees and grants liberation, all shine forth. The Muyalagan (Apasmara) beneath Peruman’s firmly planted Thiru-foot is referred to as the ignorant Muyalagan, and the snake in the Muyalagan’s hand is referred to as the fierce snake by Sundaramurthi Swamigal. How does Sundaramurthi Swamigal worship Ananda Nataraja Peruman, who, out of compassion for us, is always performing the Thiru-kuthu?

Let us continue our contemplation.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 20 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
Sundaramurthi Swamigal stands before Thiruchitrambalam in Chidambaram and beholds the vision of Sri Ananda Nataraja Peruman, the Lord. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this scene of Sundaramurthi Swamigal receiving the vision in two rare verses. In Thiru-chitrambalam, in the Chitsabhai, the Thiru-padam (sacred foot) resting upon Muyalagan, and the raised red Thiru-padam, and the Thiru-chilambu (sacred anklet) adorning the feet, and the great radiance on the sacred body, and the excellent Thiru-thodai (sacred thigh), and the tiger skin garment worn over the Thiru-thodai, and the folds fitting the sacred waist, and the beauty of the sash worn around the sacred waist, and the Udarabandhan (waistband) adorning the beautiful Thiru-undhi (sacred navel). The joining of the sacred thread, the chest adorned with the sacred thread, the waving red sacred hand, the radiance of the sacred hand with the snake as a bracelet, the resounding Damarukam (drum), the fire pot, the well-formed shoulders, the Thiru-neelakantam (sacred blue throat), the ears adorned with Thiru-thodu (sacad earring), the captivating charm of the sacred smile emanating from the flawless coral-like sacred mouth, the blossoming of the sacred face with the beauty and radiance of a supreme red lotus flower, the movement of the sacred eyes lovingly gazing at Uma Devi with her beautifully tied hair, the beauty of the eyebrows, the beautiful forehead, the frontal eye, the long flowing matted locks behind, the serpent ornaments, the garland of Konrai flowers, And the pure Ganga, and the crescent moon, and the sacred ash shining on the sacred body, thus Shiva performs the dance, to the astonishment of Sakthi, Sivakami Ammaiyar. As Thirumal and Brahma search, and as the entire world flourishes, and as the Vedic anklets resound, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who performs the Thiru-kuthu outside the Ambalam, with hands opened and closed, with folded hands, with eyes full of bliss, and with the inner faculties immersed in these, driven by love, and possessing great ascetic merit, went near the Thiru-kazhitruppadi (sacred steps) and bowed down and rose. The following verse is a very rare verse composed by Sekkizhar Swamigal.

It explains how Sundaramurthi Swamigal received the vision. It is a verse that contains a wealth of philosophical meanings. Only the grace of Shiva and the grace of the Guru can explain the philosophical meaning of this verse to us. The verse is as follows: “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla alapparunkaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga gunam oru moonrum thirundhu sathuvikame yaga Indhu vazh sadaiyan aadum anandha ellaiyil thanipperum koothin vandha perinbellathul thilaithu maarilaa magizhchiyin malarndhar.” This is the verse. Its concise explanation is that Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with all five great senses taken over by his eyes, with all the actions of the immeasurable four inner faculties becoming one with the mind, and with the three gunas becoming pure sattva, immersed himself in the ocean of supreme bliss that came from the unique great dance of the Lord with the moon-dwelling matted hair, and blossomed in unchanging joy.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal became one who blossomed in unparalleled great joy, immersed in the ocean of supreme bliss that occurred within him due to the great dance of the Lord with the moon-dwelling matted hair, a dance that is immeasurable and incomparable, with all five types of knowledge becoming one with the eyes, and all the actions of the immeasurable four inner faculties becoming one with the mind, and the three gunas becoming pure sattva. The explanation given by Sivakavimani for this is: “Aindhu arivum kankale kolla” (Let the five knowledges be taken by the eyes). The five great knowledges are the knowledge that flows in five ways, in five paths. What is this five knowledge? Knowledge is one.

However, it goes through five different senses and perceives five different objects through five different organs.

Therefore, it is five knowledges, five great knowledges. The mind, that is, the mind, which is one of the inner faculties, perceives five subtle elements through the five sense organs of our body, using the five sensory powers. These five subtle elements are the cause of the five elements. How? The mind uses the sense organ of the eye to perceive the subtle element of light or form.

Similarly, it perceives the sense of sound through the ear. It perceives the subtle element of smell or fragrance through the nose. It perceives the subtle element of taste or flavor through the sense organ of the tongue. It perceives the subtle element of touch or tactile sensation through the sense organ of the skin.

Thus, the mind perceives five types of subtle elements using five types of organs. That power is called indriya.

Here, Sekkizhar states in this verse, “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla” (Let the five great knowledges be taken by the eyes), referring to the vision that Sundaramurthi Swamigal sees.

Thus, when Sundaramurthi Swamigal beholds the Lord, these five powers, which grasp the five subtle elements through the five sense organs, all converge in the eyes. Only the eyes remain, seeing, while the other four senses – nose, tongue, body, and ears – do not direct their knowledge to their respective objects, and thus do not function. This is the meaning of the line “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla” for Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Now, the next line, “Alapparungaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga” (Let the four immeasurable inner faculties become the mind). This should also be understood in the same way as the previous line. That is, he first said that all the functions of the five senses remained in the eyes, did he not? “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla” (Let the five great knowledges be taken by the eyes).

Similarly, here he says, “Alapparungaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga” (Let the four immeasurable inner faculties become the mind). “Alapparungaranangal” means the inner faculties. There are four such inner faculties: Manam (mind), Buddhi (intellect), Chittam (consciousness), and Ahankaram (ego).

Thus, these four inner faculties became Chittam alone. That is the meaning. Among these four inner faculties, Ahankaram, Manam, and Buddhi ceased to function, and only Chittam functioned.

Here, Manam, through the power of the five senses, perceives the five subtle elements, which are the five sense objects, through the five sense organs, and first sees a Nirvikalpa perception. Nirvikalpa perception means seeing generally.

Thus, the mind that has received the Nirvikalpa perception will have many doubts about the things it has seen, heard, tasted, smelled, or felt. It will have many doubts, wondering if this is that, or that is this. This act of doubting is the function of the mind.

Now, the knowledge of the soul, that is, the soul, through Buddhi, another inner faculty, understands the name, species, quality, and action of the object of the Nirvikalpa perception received by the mind, and determines, “This is indeed this.” This act of determining is the function of Buddhi. While the soul uses the mind to perceive Nirvikalpa perception and Buddhi determines it to be such, the third inner faculty, Ahankaram, constantly creates excitement in the soul. That is, the soul must rise with the ego, saying, “I will experience this.” Only when it rises with ego and excitement does the mind grasp the nirvikalpa perception, and then the intellect determines it. This is the third inner faculty, ego. The soul, having thus known, immersing itself in that experience, is the fourth state, the inner faculty called chittam.

When the soul repeatedly contemplates the qualities of the object it has determined, a state of immersion in that object, in that matter, arises. Chittam is the instrument for such repeated immersion and contemplation.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, here, directly beholding the Lord dancing blissfully in the golden hall, became absorbed in that sacred form, lost in ecstasy, and his mind did not grasp any other object. His intellect did not turn to any other object. His ego did not rise to know any other object. Only his Chittam was active, and these immeasurable inner faculties of Manam, Buddhi, and Ahankaram became one with Chittam. Sekkizhar subtly shows in this second line, “Alapparungaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga” (Let the four immeasurable inner faculties become the mind), that state of experience where he was repeatedly immersed and absorbed in the thought of the Lord.

Now, thirdly, Sekkizhar Swamigal says, “Gunam oru moonrum sathuvikame aga” (Let the three gunas become sattvic). We must understand this in the context of the same contemplation we have already made. There are three gunas: Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas. These three gunas, Remain as Sattva alone.

Here, when Sundaramurthi Swamigal has the vision, these three gunas – Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas – stimulate the four inner faculties, and these three gunas and four inner faculties function, creating twelve states. These twelve states, through the five sense organs, further mix and create countless actions. Without all this, all these disappear, and the three gunas become Sattva alone, absorbed in pure Sattva. When Sundaramurthi Swamigal sees the vision, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that the vision seen by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is the “Aadum Ananda Ellaiyil Thanipperum Koothu” (the unique great dance of bliss without limits). This phrase should be understood by combining the words individually in various ways: “Aadum Koothu” (dancing dance), “Ananda Koothu” (dance of bliss), “Ellaiyillatha Koothu” (limitless dance), “Thani Koothu” (unique dance), “Perum Koothu” (great dance). Because the eyes beheld the Lord who performs that dance, the Lord who dances outside, it is a “dancing dance.” The result of this seeing is bliss, therefore it is a “dance of bliss.” Since this sacred dance is immeasurable and limitless, it is a “limitless dance.” Now, next, “Thani Koothu” (unique dance).

The meaning of this unique dance is that it is not like the “Oona Natana” (fleshly dance) which is performed in the bound state, by propitiating the deities Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, Maheswara, and Sadashiva, who are the presiding deities of the five kalas: Pratishta Kala, Nivritti Kala, Vidya Kala, Shanti Kala, and Shantyatita Kala. But it is a “Gnana Natana” (dance of wisdom) performed in the state of liberation, which has released the three impurities for the great silent sages.

Therefore, it is a unique dance.

Now, there is nothing beyond this sacred dance.

Therefore, Sekkizhar next says that this sacred dance is a great dance. Shiva is the embodiment of bliss.

Therefore, this sacred dance of His also bestows bliss. It is an action that immerses living beings in bliss.

Therefore, it is the sacred dance of bliss. Like the Lord, it has no beginning, middle, or end.

Therefore, it is a limitless sacred dance. Just as dancers show many mudras, the Lord also shows us that He is capable of performing the five cosmic functions of creation, sustenance, dissolution, concealment, and grace, by holding a drum and fire in His sacred hands, by showing the Abhaya and Varada mudras, by trampling Muyalagan with one sacred foot, And by raising the other, He reveals to us that He is capable of performing the five cosmic functions of creation, sustenance, dissolution, concealment, and grace.

Furthermore, He is the one who performs the Tandava (cosmic dance) with one foot, not with two, in this sacred dance. The Lord, unlike others, does not dance on the ground but in the open sky. Generally, for dancers, a Nattuvan (dance master) is indispensable. A dance cannot be performed without a Nattuvan.

But the Lord Himself has the power to make living beings dance. There is no one else to make Him dance.

Therefore, the Lord dances by Himself, without a Nattuvan.

Therefore, this is also a unique dance. This sacred dance, When Sundaramurthi Swamigal beholds this sacred dance, his five great senses are taken over by his eyes. His four immeasurable inner faculties become one with his mind. His three gunas become pure sattva. Having become thus, he is immersed in a flood of supreme bliss and attains unchanging joy.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal beholds the unique, limitless dance of the great Lord with great knowledge and great bliss. Knowledge is classified as great knowledge and small knowledge according to the greatness or smallness of the objects known. The knowledge of the five senses that perceives the Shivabhoga, which is the knowledge of the sacred feet, becomes great knowledge for Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s five senses. It is because of this that this knowledge becomes supreme knowledge. By beholding the great dance of the Lord with this supreme knowledge, a flood of supreme bliss overflows in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart, and he attains unchanging joy.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who beheld the great dance, the limitless dance, the unique dance of the Lord, immersed himself in that dance, became absorbed in its nature, and experienced it as non-dual. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that the joy that arises from this is supreme bliss, and that Sundaramurthi Swamigal blossomed in unchanging joy. In the previous verse, Sekkizhar Swamigal used the word “malarthi” (blooming) when Sundaramurthi Swamigal bowed. Sekkizhar Swamigal here says “malardhal” (blossoming), that is, “marilaa magizhchiyin malarndhar” (blossomed in unchanging joy). “Malarthuthal” (to make bloom) is the Turiya state where devotion to the Lord stands firm. “Malardhal” (to blossom) is the Turiyatita state beyond the state of knowledge.

Thus, in this verse, “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla alapparungaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga gunam oru moonrum thirundhu sathuvikame yaga Indhu vazh sadaiyan aadum anandha ellaiyil thanipperum koothin vandha perinbellathul thilaithu maarilaa magizhchiyin malarndhar,” Sekkizhar Swamigal uses “malardhal” to mean the Turiyatita state beyond the state of knowledge. Sivakavimani explained its literal meaning in this way. Elders say that its ultimate meaning, its philosophical truth, must be understood through the Guru.

Therefore, Sivakavimani, with a note, writes the commentary for this verse, stating that explaining its meaning is possible only for those who have received gnana-upadesha (initiation into wisdom) and the grace of the Guru according to the rules, and that it is not possible for simple people like him.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 21 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In Periya Puranam, in the Thaduthatkondapuranam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, beholding the wondrous vision of Peruman performing the Ananda-kuthu in Thiruchitrambalam in Chidambaram, “Aindhu perarivum kankale kolla alapparungaranangal nankum sindhaiye aga gunam oru moonrum thirundhu sathuvikame yaga Indhu vazh sadaiyan aadum anandha ellaiyil thanipperum koothin vandha perinbellathul thilaithu maarilaa magizhchiyin malarndhar.” Thus, from the eyes of the joyful Sundaramurthi Swamigal, tears of bliss gushed forth like a waterfall. As this rain of tears of bliss poured down, he clasped his hands above his head and, facing Shiva, offered a crescent moon. O Lord with the matted locks, though birth is generally fraught with suffering, the life I have received by witnessing Your sacred dance, having come from Kailash to this earthly realm, has now become a source of great joy for me.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Peruman praised and prostrated, singing a Thevaram Thiruppathigam, rich in melody and difficult to comprehend. The Thevaram pathigam sung here is not available to us now. Since Sekkizhar Swamigal himself stated that it is a pathigam very difficult for all to understand, it seems this pathigam is not available to us. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus prostrated before the Peruman, the Peruman who had previously stopped and graced Sundaramoorthy Swamigal at his wedding, This Peruman, who is performing the incomparable grand dance here, this Peruman with the uplifted sacred foot, by His divine grace, a sound arose in the sky. That sound spoke thus: “Come to our abode in Thiruvarur, surrounded by beautiful fields, made fertile by the Kaveri with its pearl-bearing waves.” Hearing that sound, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal understood it and arose. Sekkizhar Swamigal’s mention of a sound arising in the sky here refers to the Chidakasam (the sky of consciousness).

The sound that arose in that Chidakasam manifested as a resonance from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s heart-lotus, thus proclaiming: “Come to Thiruvarur, made fertile by the Kaveri.” Hearing the Peruman’s command to come to the illustrious Thiruvarur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arose. Taking this command, bestowed by the grace of the Dancer in the hall, upon his head, he clasped his hands above his head in reverence, bowed, took leave, circumambulated the Thiruchitrambalam reverently, and exited.

Then, he reached the southern gopuram, which shone like a golden mountain reaching the sky. Standing outside the southern gopuram entrance of Chidambaram, he prostrated before the gopuram, then bowed to the long street and rose.

After that, he passed through the southern gateway of this eternal Chidambaram city.

Then he reached the southern boundary outside the city. Reaching that southern boundary, he stood there, bowing to it. Accepting the Lord’s command, “Come to us in Thiruvarur,” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal slowly began to depart from Chidambaram and walk.

Thus, meditating on the grace bestowed by the Lord, he slowly set out from the southern boundary of Chidambaram and walked southwards. He recalled the grace that stopped and claimed him in Thiruvannainallur.

Then he recalled the grace that bestowed upon him the path of penance in Thiru-Thuraiyur. He recalled the grace that gave him the sacred foot initiation at the Siddhavadam Math outside Thiru-Adhigai. He recalled the grace he witnessed in Chidambaram.

Then he recalled the grace of Ananda Nataraja Peruman of Chidambaram, who commanded him to come to Thiruvarur. Meditating thus on the divine grace of the Lord, he reached the northern bank of the sacred Kollidam River, the southern boundary of Chidambaram.

Then, he crossed the Kollidam River and ascended its southern bank.

Thus, leaving the boundaries of Thillai, he journeyed southwards towards Thiruvarur. The four boundaries surrounding Thillai are: the sea to the east, Nagacheri to the west, Thirumanimutharu to the north, and the sacred Kollidam River to the south.

Thus, crossing the sacred Kollidam River, the southern boundary of Chidambaram, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, whose chest was adorned with the sacred thread, proceeded southwards, followed by his retinue of devotees. He reached Thiru-Kazhumalam, also known as Sirkazhi, the sacred sthalam where Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman, who grew by partaking of the sacred milk from the breast of Uma Devi, who exemplifies all virtues, incarnated. The sacred sthalam of Sirkazhi has twelve sacred names. Among them is the name Thiru-Kazhumalam. This Thiru-Kazhumalam is a significant name. It is called Thiru-Kazhumalam because people who reach this sacred sthalam attain wisdom, cleanse themselves of their three impurities (mummalangal), and are saved.

Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman commanded that the twelve sacred names of Sirkazhi, starting with Brahmapuram and ending with this name Kazhumalam, should be recited in proper order. He sings in this same order in pathigams such as Thiru-Viragam, Thiru-Ekapadam, Thiru-Malaimatru, Thiru-Gomuthiri, and Thiru-Ezhukootrirukkai.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having reached Sirkazhi, the sacred place where Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman incarnated, thought, “This is the sthalam where Gnanasambandha Peruman’s sacred incarnation occurred, therefore it is improper to tread upon this sacred sthalam.” With this thought, he circumambulated the sthalam and sang a Thiruppathigam. This sacred sthalam possesses the great fortune of the Lord’s presence and the great fortune of Gnanasambandha Peruman’s birth and grace. The incarnation of Gnanasambandha Peruman was for the purpose of removing the impurities of all beings in the world.
