The reason for the removal of these impurities of beings is The wisdom of Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman. Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman attained such Shiva-gnanam because he partook of the sacred milk from the Mother’s breast. The Mother was the one who bestowed that sacred milk.

Therefore, to signify all this, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this sacred sthalam as having the great fortune of being the birthplace of one who grew by partaking of the sacred milk from the Mother who gave birth to dharma.

Thus, with the intention of not setting foot within the boundaries of this Sirkazhi sthalam, also known as Pukazhi, which was blessed with the great fortune of the incarnation of Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman, the child-saint, he stood outside its boundaries and worshipped. As he circumambulated this sthalam, the Lord, with Uma Devi, on the Rishabha vahana, Appeared before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, bestowing His divine vision. This sthalam, known as Sirkazhi or Pukazhi, has the nature of protecting those who enter it.

However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s resolute stance, declaring, “Even if I do not receive that blessing, I will not tread upon this sthalam where Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman, the child-saint, incarnated,” illustrates to us the profound love he held for Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman. This divine act is akin to how Thiru-Navukkarasar Peruman, at the Thiru-Adhigai Veerattanam sthalam, chose not to tread upon the sacred ground where he had performed sacred service with his own hands, but instead sang from outside.

Similarly, at this Sirkazhi sthalam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal circumambulated the sthalam from outside without entering. The Divine Mother and Father, residing with Periya Nayaki in Thiru-Thonipuram within Sirkazhi, appear to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on the Rishabha vahana.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal did not enter, so the Divine Mother and Father, who are within this sthalam, came out of the sthalam to grant Sundaramoorthy Swamigal their divine vision.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal did not enter due to his immeasurable love for Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman.

Therefore, just as the Lord appeared to Thiru-Gnanai, He also appeared to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as the one with the earring in His ear, mounted on the Rishabha.

Thus, the love Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had for Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman was a mature, profound love.

Therefore, due to that profound love, even though he did not enter to worship the Lord and Goddess, The Lord and Goddess came out and granted him their divine vision.

Thus, immersed in that divine grace where the Lord and Goddess granted their divine vision, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a pathigam here. In every concluding line of this pathigam, he describes the vision he saw, stating, “I beheld the prosperous city of Kazhumalam.” Now, the pathigam. Sthalam: Thiru-Kazhumalam. Pan: Thakkēsi.

Song number one: “Sādhalum pirathalum thavirthu ena vaguththu thannarul thantha em thalaivanai malaiyin māthinai madhitthangōr pāl koṇḍa maṇiyai vaṟu punal saḍaiyiḍai vaiththa emmānai ēthilan manaththukkōr eṟumbuṇḍa nīr” En vagai oruvanai engal pirānai kāthil veṇkuḻaiyanai kaḍal koḷ mithantha kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song two: Maṟṟoru thuṇai ini maṟumaikkum kāṇēn varunthalutṟēn maṟavāvaram peṟṟēn suṟṟiya suṟṟamum thuṇaiyenṟu karuthēn thuṇaiyenṟu nāṉ thoḻappaṭṭa oṇ suḍarai mutthiyum gnānamum vāṉavar aṟiyā muṟai muṟai pala pala neṟigaḷum kāṭṭi kaṟpaṉai kaṟpiththa kaḍavuḷai aḍiyēn kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song three: Thirutthinai nagar urai sēnthanaappan en seyviṉai aṟuththiḍum sempoṉai ampoṉ orutthaṉai allathu ingu āṟaiyum uṇarēn uṇarvu peṟṟēn Uyyum kāraṇam thannāl virutthanai bālanai kaṉaviḍai viravi viḻiththu engum kāṇamāṭṭāthu viṭṭirunthēn karutthaṉai niṟutthañjai kālaṉai vēlai kaḻu mala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song four: Maḻaikkarumpum malarkkoṉṟaiṉāṉai vaḷaikkaḻuṟṟēn maṟavāmaṇam peṟṟēn piḻaiththu oru kāl iṉi pōypāmai perumai peṟṟēn peṟṟathāra perugilpārkuḻaikkaruṅ kaṇḍaṉai kaṇḍukoḷvāṉē pāḍugiṉṟēn seṉṟu kūḍavum vallēn kaḷaikkarumpum kathalippala sōlai kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē.

Song five: Kuṇḍalaṅkuḻai thigaḻkāthaṉē enṟum koḍumaḻuvāṭpaḍai kuḻagaṉēyenṟum Vaṇḍalampum malarkkoṉṟaiyaṉē enṟum vāy veṟuviththoḻuthēn vithiyālē paṇḍai nam pala maṇamuṅ kaḷainthu oṉṟāy pasupathi pathiviṉap palanāḷum kaṇḍa alaṅ kaḻukkarai yōtham vanthuḷavum kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song six: Varum peruvalviṉai enṟirunthu eṇṇi varunthalutṟēn maṟavāmaṇam peṟṟēn virumbi en maṉaththiḍai meykuḷirpeythī vēṇḍinaṉṟē thoḻuthēn vithiyālē arumpiṉai alaṟiṇai amuthinai thēṉai aiyaṉai aṟavaṉ en piṟavi vēr aṟukkum karumpiṉai peruñcheṉṉal neruṅgiya kaḻaṉik kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song seven: Ayalavar paravavum aḍiyavar thoḻavum anparkaḷ sāyaluḷ aḍaiyalutṟirunthēn muyal pavar piṉseṉṟu muyal valai yāṉai paḍumeṉa moḻinthavar vaḻi muḻuthu eṇṇi puyaliṉai thiruviṉai poṉṉiṉathu oḷiyai miṉṉiṉ athu uruviṉai eṇṇiḍai poruḷai kayaliṉam sēloḍu vayal viḷaiyāḍum kaḻumalavaḷanagar kaṇḍukoṇḍēnē. Song eight: Niṉaitharu pāvaṅgaḷ nāsaṅgaḷāga niṉainthu muṉ thoḻuthu eḻappaṭṭa oṇ suḍarai maṉai tharu malaimagaḷ kaṇavaṉai vāṉōr māmaṇi māṇikkaththai maṟaipporuḷai puṉaitharu pugaḻiṉai eṅgaḷathu oḷiyai iruvarum Oruvan enṟu uṇarvu aṟiyavaṉai kaṉaitharu karuṅkaḍal ōtham vanthu ulavum kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song nine: Maṟaiyiḍai thuṇinthavar maṉaiyiḍaiyirupp vañjaṉai seythavar poygaiyuḷ māya thuṟaiyuṟak kuḷiththuḷathāga vaiththuyththa thuṉmaiyeṉum thagaviṉmaiyai ōrēn piṟaiyuḍaichchaḍaiyaṉai eṅgaḷ pirāṉai pēraruḷāḷaṉai kāriruḷ pōṉṟa karaiyaṇi miḍaruḍai aḍigaḷai aḍiyēn kaḻumala vaḷanagar kaṇḍu koṇḍēnē. Song ten: Seḻumalar koṉṟaiyum kūviḻa malarum viraviya saḍaimuḍiyaḍigaḷai niṉainthittu aḻumalar kaṇṇiṉai aḍiyavarkku allāl aṟivaṟithavaṉ thiru Iruvaḍi iṇai iraṇḍum kaḻu mala vaḷa nagar kaṇḍu koṇḍu ūran saḍaiyaṉ than kāthalan pāḍiya patthum thoḻu malareḍuththa kai aḍiyavar thammai thuṉpamum iḍumpaiyum sūḻugilāvē.

As a benefit of this pathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal states that sorrow and distress will not approach those devotees who read, recite, listen to, and learn this pathigam. We can contemplate the special features of this Sirkazhi sacred sthalam in the puranam of Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 22 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, the Thaduthatkonda Puranam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having completed his darshan in Chidambaram, where Lord Shiva commanded him to come to Thiruvarur, set out from Chidambaram and proceeded southwards. On his way, he first reached the sthalam of Sirkazhi. Since it was the sthalam where Gnanasambandha Peruman incarnated, he did not enter but circumambulated the sthalam.

There, the Lord, mounted on the Rishabha, with an earring in His ear, appeared to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus beheld the Kailash vision outside Sirkazhi, lost in ecstasy, that vision disappeared, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was startled.

Then, the gracious words previously spoken by Koothaperuman came to his memory, And desiring to go to Thiruvarur, he continued his journey.

Thus, he worshipped that sacred place, known as Puravam and Sirkazhi, from outside, and then reached Thiru-Kolakka, located to the west of Sirkazhi. This sthalam of Thiru-Kolakka is the place where Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman arrived for his first pilgrimage the day after attaining wisdom.

Here, at Thiru-Kolakka, the Lord bestowed upon Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman golden cymbals inscribed with the five sacred letters. The Goddess here gave sound to them.

Thus, because the Lord bestowed the sacred cymbals upon Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman, He is known here as Sapthapureeswarar, Thalapureeswarar, and Thiruthaalamudaiyar. And because she gave sound to those golden cymbals, the Ambāl here is known as Dhvani Prathāmbāl and Ōsai Koḍuttha Nāyaki. This sthalam is also known as Thiruthaalamudaiyār Kōyil. Its special features can be seen later in the Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman Puranam. The Thevaram pathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, praising the Lord and Goddess enshrined in Thiru-Kolakka, is not available to us now.

However, Sekkizhar informs us that the Thevaram by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for this sthalam, beginning with “Puṟṟil vāḻ aṟavārththa pirāṉai” in the Thakkēsi pan, was sung later when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was going to Thiru-Kurukāvūr. Having completed his darshan at Thiru-Kolakka, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Reached the sacred sthalam of Thiru-Punkur, filled with groves.

There, with great devotion, he worshipped Sivalokanathar and sang a Thiruppathigam. In this sthalam of Thiru-Punkur, Thiru-Nalaipovar Nayanar stood outside the temple, worshipped, and then excavated the sacred tank to the west of the temple as a sacred service. Athanur, where he incarnated, is to the southeast of this Thiru-Punkur sthalam. This is the revered sthalam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, who was a close friend of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Thiru-Perumangalam, the sthalam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, is to the north of this Thiru-Punkur sthalam.

Here, the Pungu tree is the sthala-viruksham, hence it is called Thiru-Punkur.

The Lord here is Here, the Lord is Sivalokanathar, and the Goddesses are Soundarya Nayaki and Sokka Nayaki. The sthala-viruksham of this sthalam, the Pungu tree, is what we commonly call the Pungam tree today. The Thevaram pathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for this Thiru-Punkur sthalam is also not available to us.

However, the available Thevaram of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for this sthalam, beginning with “Anthanāḷan” and set to the Thakkēsi pan, was sung by Nambigal when he visited this sthalam with Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar after their friendship was established. After visiting this sthalam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal continued his journey to Thiruvarur. On the way, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bathed in the sacred Kaveri River. This Kaveri River, along its course, Spreads golden particles on both its banks and carries various flowers on its surface.

Thus, after bathing in the Kaveri, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped Thiru-Mayiladuthurai, where the Lord is enshrined. This Thiru-Mayiladuthurai is the sthalam where the Mother worshipped the Lord in the form of a peacock. It is now commonly known as Mayavaram. It is also called Mayuram Gauri Mayuram. Its special features will be seen later.

Thus, after worshipping Thiru-Mayiladuthurai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then reached the sthalam called Ambar Mahāṇam. The pathigams sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for this sthalam of Ambar Mahāṇam are not available. This sthalam of Ambar Mahāṇam is the sthalam where Kali Devi worshipped. It is named Ambar Mahāṇam because Kali Devi worshipped here to be absolved of the sin of killing the two demons, Amban and Ambasuran. It is also known as Koyil Mahāṇam. This is the sacred place where Somasi Mara Nayanar performed yagna and attained blessings.

Here, the sage Mahakalar also worshipped and attained blessings. It is located on the banks of the Arasalaru River. This Arasalaru is named Ari Sol Aru. It is a branch river that separated from the Kaveri at the command of Vishnu (Hari) for His worship. This Ari Sol Aru is now known as Arasalaru. To its east is the grand temple of Thiru-Ambar Perunthirukoyil, a Madakoyil.

The special features of this Thiru-sthalam can be seen in detail in the Somasi Maranayanar Puranam. After visiting this Thalam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches Thiru-Pukalur, a Thalam where the Lord lovingly resides. This Thiru-Pukalur is a Thalam where the Lord willingly manifests and resides to shower grace upon all beings. This Thiru-Pukalur is the Thalam where Appar Peruman attained the sacred feet of the Lord by taking refuge in Him. Souls that fear the suffering of birth seek refuge in the Lord here, hence it is called Thiru-Pukalur. It is in this Thalam, also known as Saranyapuram, that the Lord transformed bricks into gold for Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

This history will be seen later. This Thalam is also where Muruganayanar attained blessings through sacred service. We do not currently have the Thevaram Pathikam sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for this Thiru-Pukalur Thalam. The Pathikam beginning with “Thammaiye Pukazhndhu Ichchai Pesidanum” which we now have, sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for this Thalam, was composed later. That history is found later in the Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam.

Thus, after worshipping Thiru-Pukalur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches Thiru-Arur. Many devotees reside in Thiru-Arur, which is adorned with beautiful chariot-like streets. As Sundaramurthi Swamigal approaches Thiru-Arur, the Lord informs the devotees residing in Thiru-Arur as follows: The Lord informs them as follows: “Our Nambiyaruran, who is filled with great love, is coming here at Our invitation. Go forth joyfully to welcome him.” Thus, Lord Shiva conveys this message to the heart of every devotee residing in Thiru-Arur. Regarding this statement by the Lord, Sivakavimani offers the following interpretation: Lord Shiva refers to Sundaramurthi Swamigal as a devotee with boundless love for Him.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal possesses an eternal and unceasing desire for the Lord, hence Lord Shiva Himself speaks of “boundless love.” Mahalingaiyar interprets this as unwavering devotion.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s arrival in Thiru-Arur is at the Lord’s invitation. It is upon that basis that he comes. In Thillai, Lord Shiva invited him, saying, “Nambal Varuga to Thiru-Arur,” and in obedience to that command, Nambi Aruran, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, arrives here in Thiru-Arur. Since Lord Shiva, the Supreme Being, has thus invited Sundaramurthi Swamigal to come here, the Lord, in return, conveys to the heart of every devotee residing in Thiru-Arur the proper manner of going forth to welcome Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Furthermore, the Lord graciously informed the devotees residing in Thiru-Arur about the necessity for Sundaramurthi Swamigal to reveal the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, a Pathikam praising the glory and fame of the devotees, right here in Thiru-Arur. Let us continue our reflection.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 23 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled “Thaduththaatkonda Puranam,” Sundaramurthi Swamigal beholds the darshan of Koothaperuman in Chidambaram. Subsequently, Koothaperuman commands him, “Come to Us in Thiru-Arur.” Accepting this command, Sundaramurthi Swamigal departs from Chidambaram and, after visiting Thalam-s such as Sirkazhi, Thiru-Kolakka, Thiru-Punkur, Mayiladuthurai, Ambar Maganam, and Thiru-Pukalur, reaches the boundary of Thiru-Arur. As he thus reaches the boundary of Thiru-Arur, the Lord enters the heart of every Shiva devotee in Thiru-Arur and commands them to go forth and welcome Sundaramurthi Swamigal. As Thiyagaraja Peruman of Thiru-Arur thus commands in the hearts of all devotees, those devotees who heard the command Each one shares their experience with others. Knowing that Thiru-Arur Thiyagaraja Peruman has thus commanded in everyone’s heart, they are greatly delighted.

If our Lord has thus revealed through His divine grace, then is not this Sundarar, who is arriving, also our Lord?

Thus, they rejoice, immersed in divine feeling, and prepare to welcome Sundaramurthi Swamigal. They adorn the entirety of Thiru-Arur. Thiru-Arur Thiyagaraja Peruman is the Lord who has graced Devalokam for a long time. The way in which devotees adorn this Thiru-Arur is considered even more exalted than Devalokam, to the extent that Sundarar, in his estimation of Thiyagaraja Peruman, Devotees throughout Thiru-Arur prepare to welcome Murthi Swamigal. In every mansion and hall of Thiru-Arur, long, tall banners are hoisted. Beneath these banners, long floral garlands are hung.

Thick garlands, woven from the leaves of dense areca nut trees and mango leaves, are hung everywhere. The “Thalaikkamugu” mentioned here refers to the areca nut tree, not just for its fruit, but specifically a variety grown for ornamental purposes, with abundant leaves and foliage. Such areca nut tree fronds are woven into garlands and hung throughout the streets.

Similarly, garlands are densely arranged to form archways throughout the streets. They place auspicious golden pots filled with holy water at the entrances of mansions throughout the streets of Thiru-Arur.

Thus, with full kumbhas shining everywhere, they arrange golden lamps studded with navaratnas in rows at all entrances, illuminating them with light. They meticulously clean and adorn the verandas of every house with pure, fragrant sandalwood paste. They take golden powders, pounded from turmeric and other ingredients used for the Lord’s sacred ablutions, mix them with puffed rice, and create kolams everywhere at the entrances. For the beauty of these kolams, they adorn them with pearls and other gems. Large floral garlands are strung and used to decorate pandals everywhere.

Thus, welcoming with cooling items such as sandalwood paste, cool pearls, pandals, and cool water is a custom of our hot country. This is called Desacharam. Conversely, in cold countries, there is also a custom of welcoming with warming items. Women skilled in music sing auspicious songs. Women skilled in dance perform dances to welcome. Drums, roaring like the thunder of clouds, resound everywhere.

Devotees are making such grand and special arrangements both inside and outside the city of Thiru-Arur. Having made such special arrangements, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s arrival in Thiru-Arur Even before his arrival, all the devotees gather at the outer entrance of Thiru-Arur. Seeing all the devotees gathered at the entrance of Thiru-Arur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, before those great and special devotees of Thiru-Arur could prostrate to him, himself clasped his hands in reverence and paid his respects.

The devotees also bowed first. Even before they bowed, Sundaramurthi Swamigal offered his salutations. This illustrates the principle that every devotee should be the first to bow to another devotee. It was impossible to distinguish whether the devotees bowed first or Sundaramurthi Swamigal bowed first, as both bowed to each other the moment they saw each other. A similar incident occurred in Chidambaram as well. We have already seen Sekkizhar’s account of a similar event.

Thus, when one devotee meets another, it is the nature of devotees to bow in such a way that it is impossible to discern who bowed first. As both thus bowed to each other, with hearts overflowing with joy, the devotees of Thiru-Arur and Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceed along the streets of Thiru-Arur, which are adorned as previously described.

Thus, with the devotees who came to welcome him at the Lord’s command at the forefront, Sundaramurthi Swamigal now graciously sings a Thevaram Thiru-Pathikam. Addressing these devotees of Thiru-Arur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal asks, “Is it this Thiru-Arur where our Lord lovingly resides? Will that Lord also accept us as His own?” He sings a Thiru-Pathikam in the Kantharam pan, conveying the sentiment, “Ask and tell me.” As he sings this Thiru-Pathikam, he reaches the sacred entrance of the Lord.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, addressing the devotees of Thiru-Arur, humbles himself before them and sings a Pathikam implying, “Will the Lord of Thiru-Arur accept me, who am so lowly and unworthy of being taken as His own, and those like me? Please ask and tell me.” The inner meaning of this is that the devotees of Thiru-Arur are ancient devotees of Thiyagaraja Peruman, blessed with the merit of being born in Thiru-Arur.

Thus, he pleads, “Will your Lord, disregarding my lack of merit and unworthiness, accept me? Will he accept me? Please make a request on my behalf. Will the Lord, acceding to that request, accept me as well? You, who are familiar with the Lord, please ask Him and tell me.” Similarly, when Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman came to behold Thiru-Arur, he addressed the devotees who came to welcome him, asking, “When Thiru-Aruran, who is the Lord, is distressed, why do you not grieve?” and sang a Thiru-Pathikam. Appar Peruman also similarly addressed the devotees in Thiru-Arur, asking, “Do we have the merit to be servants of the servant of Arur’s Lord with the flowing matted locks?” and sang.

Thiru-Arur is a Thalam distinguished by the excellence of the devotees’ servitude, for the Devasiriya Mandapam, where all the Devas gather and worship, The Mandapam where devotees gather and reside is prominent in Thiru-Arur. It is in this Thiru-Arur that the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, a Pathikam praising the glory of the devotees, is destined to emerge.

Thus, Thiru-Arur is a Thalam distinguished by the excellence of the devotees’ servitude. The reason why the greatness of the devotees residing in Thiru-Arur is so immense is that in many previous births, they fully performed Shiva-tapas, Shiva-puja, and Shiva-punyam, and are souls already accepted by the Lord. These perfected souls are deemed worthy of being born in Thiru-Arur and are born here.

Thus, the beings born in Thiru-Arur are already perfected souls, souls worthy of liberation, and therefore, the concept of rebirth Such perfected souls, for whom there is no further birth, attain the qualification to be born in Thiru-Arur and live here as devotees.

Thus, by being born and living here in Thiru-Arur, the Lord grants them liberation. This is why in the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, those born in Thiru-Arur are praised as a collective group of devotees, saying, “I am a servant to all those born in Thiru-Arur.” The proverb “Birth in Thiru-Arur grants liberation” also originated for this reason. Kalladanar refers to this Thiru-Arur Thalam as “Piranthavar Pirava Perumpathi,” meaning that those born here will not be born anywhere else again.

Thus, these divine devotees, born and living in Thiru-Arur, are liberated souls already accepted by the Lord.

Therefore, Thiru-Sundaramurthi, Swamigal, Thiru-Navukkarasar Swamigal, and Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman, all three, worshipped these devotees, inquired of them, and sang Pathikams before entering the Thalam and singing praises to the Lord.

Thus, those born in Thiru-Arur are liberated souls, distinguished as ancient devotees. We can see this same idea expressed by Manikkavasagar in Thiruvasagam, where he sings, “Pattudaiyeer Eesan Pazha Adiyeer Pangudaiya Butthadiyom Punmai Theerthu Aatkondal Pollatho,” highlighting this special quality.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, addressing the devotees who stood to welcome him in Thiru-Arur, asks in the final line of each verse of this Pathikam, “Is it Arur where the Lord resides? Will our Lord, who resides in Arur, also accept me? Please ask and tell me.” He sang the Thiru-Arur Pathikam.

Now, the Pathikam: Thalam Thiru-Arur, Pan Kantharam.

“Karaiyum kadalum malaiyum kalaiyum malaiyum ellam Uraiyil viravi varuvan oruvan Uruththiralokan Varaiyin madamagalkezhvan vanavar thanavarkkellam Araiyan iruppathum Arur avar emmaiyum azhvaro kelir.” (The one who pervades all shores, seas, mountains, mornings, and evenings, the unique Rudraloka, the consort of the mountain maiden, the king of Devas and Asuras, resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Two: “Ariyēn thannai perithum ugappan munipavar thammai munivan mugam pala pēsi mozhiyēn Kanigal palavudai sōlai kāykkulai īndra kamukin Iniyan iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr.” (I do not know myself, I greatly love Him, I am angry with those who are angry, I do not speak many words. He, who is sweet like the areca nut that bears fruit in the grove with many fruits, resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us?

Please ask.) Song One: “Sollirkkulāvanri sollēn thodarnthavarkkum thunai allēn Kallil valiya manaththēn katra perum pulavānar Allal perithum arruppān arumarai Arangam pōthum Ellai iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr.” (I speak nothing but words, I am not a help to those who follow, I have a heart as hard as stone, I am a great scholar. He who greatly removes suffering, whose sacred Arangam is sufficient, resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Four: “Neriyum arivum serivum nīthiyum nān mikap pollēn Miraiyum thariyum ugappēn vēndittru cheythu thirivēn Piraiyum arravum punalum pirangiya senjadai vaiththa Iraivan iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr.” (I am very bad in conduct, knowledge, density, and justice. I love to wander and do as I please.

The Lord who holds the crescent moon, the snake, and the flowing water in His bright red matted locks resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Five: “Nīthiyil onrum vazhuvēn nirkkandakkeythu vāzhvēn Vēthiyar thammai vegulēn vegunda” Varkkundhunaiyākēn Sōthiyar sōthiyammanai sunna vennīraninthitta Āthi iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr. (I do not err in justice, I live by what I see. I do not get angry with Brahmins, nor do I support those who are angry. The Primal One, the light of lights, who is adorned with sacred white ash, resides in Arur.

Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Six: Aruththam perithum ugappan alavalaiyēn alanthārkal Oruththarkkum uthaviyēn allēn utravarkkum thunai allēn Poruththamēl onrum izhāthēn putreduththidangonda Aruththan iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr. (I greatly love wealth, I am not a chatterer, I am not helpful to anyone who is distressed, nor am I a support to my relatives. I have lost nothing in terms of suitability.

The Lord who took the anthill as His abode resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Seven: Santham pala arukillēn sārnthavar tham adichchārēn Munthipporuvidai ēri mū ulakum thirivānē Kanthangkamal konrai mālai kanniyan vinnavaar ēththum Enthai iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr. (I do not cut off many desires, I do not approach the feet of those who are attached. He who rides the foremost bull and wanders through the three worlds, who wears a fragrant Konrai garland, and is praised by the celestials, my Father, resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.)

Nindikkondēyumkilāyppen nichchayamē ithu thinnam Mindarkku mindalār pēsēn meipporulinri unarēn Pandangu ilangaiyarkōnai paruvarai kīzh adarththitta Andan iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr. (I will not speak ill of others, this is certain. I will not speak to the quarrelsome, I do not understand without true meaning.

The Lord who once crushed the king of Lanka beneath a great mountain resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask.) Song Nine: Namar pirar enpathariyēn nān kandathē kandu vāzhvēn Thamam perithum ugappen thakkavāronrum izhāthēn Kumaran thirumāl piraman kūdiya thēvar vanangum Amaran iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō kēḷīr. (I do not know who is mine and who is another, I live by what I see. I greatly love darkness, I have lost nothing suitable. The Immortal One, worshipped by Kumaran, Thirumal, Piraman, and the assembled Devas, resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us?

Please ask.) Song Ten: Āsai pala arukillēn āraiyum anri uraippēn Pēsiṟ salakkalāl pēsēn pizhaippudaiyēn mananthannāl Ōsai perithum ugappēn. Why, the Lord who consumed the poison of the roaring ocean resides in Arur. Will He also rule over us? Please ask. Song Eleven: “Enthai iruppathum Ārūr avar emmaiyum āzhvarō” endru Sinthai seyyum thiram vallān thirumaruvandiral thōlān Manthamuzhavam iyampum vala vayal nāval Ārūran Santham isaiyodum vallār thām pukazh eythuvar thāmē.

As a result of this Pathikam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that those who read, listen to, and learn this Pathikam will attain great fame.

Let us follow Sundaramurthi Swamigal further in the darshan of Thiru-Arur.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 24 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled “Thaduththaatkonda Puranam,” Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches Thiru-Arur, accepts the welcome of the devotees, bows to them, and, appealing to the devotees with the question, “Will this Lord residing in Arur also accept me? Please tell me,” he circumambulates the streets of Thiru-Arur and reaches the sacred temple. The greatness of this Thiru-Arur sacred Thalam is immeasurable.

Let us ponder, for a little while, the greatness of this Thiru-Arur sacred Thalam. This Thiru-Arur sacred Thalam holds a unique eminence among the many Shiva Thalam-s in the South. It is capable of bestowing both worldly pleasures necessary for this birth and the bliss of liberation. It grants liberation upon birth, and among the five great elemental Thalam-s, it is the Prithvi Sthalam. This Thiruvarur is the Mooladhara Sthalam for Virat Purusha. All the Shiva-kalas present in all Shiva temples in the world, along with the evening protection known as Sayarakshai, are manifested here in Thiruvarur.

Its antiquity is proclaimed by Appar Adigal, who states, “The day Thiruvarur was taken as a temple, when He stood as one in the world, or when He became a tall form on earth.” This Thiruvarur sthalam is renowned for its beautiful chariots, as in “Thiruvarur Therazhagu.” It serves as a haven for arts like painting and sculpture. To the north of this sthalam are Sukkanar, and to the south, Odambokkiyar, adding to its immense beauty. This sthalam has many Shiva Theerthams.

Moreover, small canals also flow within the city. This Thiru-temple is one of the largest temples in South India, renowned for its historical antiquity, and serving as a boundary for the Shaiva religion. It possesses four very tall outer Thiru-madils (enclosure walls). In the middle of the eastern wall stands a large Gopuram, which is immense. This massive Gopuram, a sthoola-lingam, grants darshan to devotees coming from afar. To the east of the southern wall is the Southern Gopuram, adorned with beautiful sculptures.

The Western Gopuram and Northern Gopuram are larger than the Southern Gopuram. Along with these four Gopurams, there is also the Vitta Vaayil Gopuram in the Ishanya (northeast) part of the outer wall of the Thiru-temple, the Ariyan Gopuram in the second prakaram, and the Alagiyan Gopuram in front of the Vanmeeganathar shrine, making a total of seven Gopurams. The three prakarams within this Thiru-temple, along with the monastery complex and the Thiru-veethi (sacred street), form five prakarams. During the reign of the Cholas, this Thiru-temple was at the center, surrounded by twenty-one streets, according to history. The width of this Thiru-temple is six hundred and sixty-six feet from south to north, and eight hundred and forty-six feet from east to west. In ancient texts, the temple’s sacred expanse is described as “Koyil five veli, kulam five veli, Sengazhuneer Odai five veli.” The antiquity of this Thiru-temple is unfathomable.

Here, Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, manifested as a Swayambhu and blessed the Devas. The Thiyagaraja Peruman here was first worshipped by Vishnu in the Milk Ocean, then by Indra in Devaloka. He is the divine deity brought here from Devaloka by the Chola Emperor Musukunda. This Thiyagaraja Peruman’s shrine, known as Paravaiyul Mandali, located on the eastern street of this Thiru-temple, is a Thiru-temple praised in Thevaram hymns. At the entrance of the Eastern Gopuram, on the left side, is a Vinayagar temple, and on the right side, a Murugan temple. After worshipping them and entering, one can see the Vinayagar shrine.

His sacred name is Veethi Vidanga Vinayagar. Behind him is Brahmma Nandi. Proceeding clockwise from there, one enters the Periya Prakaram. Near the Southern Gopuram in that prakaram are the Bhakta Kaatchi Mandapam and the Oonjal Mandapam. In the Bhakta Kaatchi Mandapam, Thiyagaraja Peruman, after concluding the Panguni Uthiram festival, performs his sacred dance, and grants darshan to devotees. In the Oonjal Mandapam, Chandrasekarar, on the Thiru-athirai festival day in Margazhi, performs the Oonjalattu Utsavam (swing festival).

After passing these mandapams and proceeding clockwise, one finds a flower garden in the Southern Prakaram. Here is the Akasa Vinayagar shrine. At the beginning of the Western Prakaram, in the southern corner, is the Thulabharam Mandapam, and a short distance from it is the Saraswati Theertham. Following that is a large mandapam, known as Chithirasabha Mandapam or Sabapathi Mandapam. During the Panguni Uthiram festival, if the Thiru-ther (sacred chariot) reaches the Utharapathathul Nilai, Thiyagaraja Peruman will proceed to that mandapam and offer Uthira Theertham. The Uthira Maha Abhishekam will also take place there.

The small mandapam near this is called Purana Mandapam. Behind this Sabapathi Mandapam, is the shrine of Kamalambal. This is a separate Amman temple.

Here, the Goddess is enshrined in a meditative posture. This is also one of the Parasakthi Peedams. It is a unique feature that the Amman’s temple faces the Ishanya (northeast) direction. In this separate Amman temple, the Aadi Pooram festival is celebrated. For this, there is a separate flagstaff. To the south of the Amman temple, Uchchishta Vinayagar is enshrined.

After worshipping him, and then bowing to Nandi Bhagavan and receiving permission, upon entering, one will see two beautiful stucco images in the Maha Mandapam. These are the images of Anindithai and Kamalini, who were companions of the Goddess in Thiru-Kailasam. After seeing them and entering further, one will find Kamalambikai, showering a flood of blissful knowledge, with a serene hand and a noose, with hands holding an Akshamala, and a hand holding a lotus, thus with four hands, having folded her sacred feet one over the other and letting them hang, enshrined in a yogic posture. After receiving the grace of the Goddess and circumambulating, one can see the Akshara Peedam in the southwest corner of the Amman temple’s southern prakaram. In it, only the peetam and the Thiru-vaasi (arch) are present. Fifty-one Aksharams are inscribed on the Thiru-vaasi.

Following this, Shanmuga Peruman is enshrined with Valli and Deivanai. Next to him is Bala Subramanya, and next to him, Kalaimagal (Saraswati) bestows grace. In the northern prakaram, there are Shiva Lingams established by great Rishis like Viswamithrar and Kashyapar, as well as Nagas (serpents). Opposite these is the Chandeswari temple. After receiving the grace of the Devi and coming out, opposite is the temple called Parpatheeswaram, with its shrine facing west. It has separate structures like Nandi and Ardhamandapam.

The Puranic history states that the Shiva Lingam in this Thiru-temple was established and worshipped by Kamalambikai. In front of this Thiru-temple, that is, between the Kamalambal temple and Parpatheeswaram, there is a well. It is called Mukthi Kinaru (Well of Liberation). The Devasiriyan, a thousand-pillared mandapam, is located beyond this in the northeast corner. Sekkizhar states, “Thiruvarur Perumai Thigazhgindra Devasiriyam.” Here, Shiva devotees are always gathered. Shiva devotees, with clear minds, partake of the Shiva medicine and immerse themselves in the great ocean of Shiva-ananda.

The Selva Thiyagaraja Peruman’s Aazhi Ther festival, after its completion, He proceeds here and performs the Maha Abhishekam called Peruneerattu, wielding the Shiva Sceptre.

Therefore, this mandapam is also known as Rajadhani Mandapam. To its west is a small pond, called Sanga Theertham. The Theertha festival takes place here on the full moon day of the Chithirai month.

Then, one must cross the Thiru-madil of the second prakaram. Connecting the second prakaram and the first prakaram is a Gopuram, named Ariyan Gopuram. On either side of this Gopuram are enshrined Vinayagar and Subramanya. After worshipping them and entering, on the left side of the Gopuram entrance, facing north, one can see Viranmeendar Nayanar enshrined. It was he who, thinking that Thambiran Thozhar Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had come without worshipping the devotees in the Devasiriya Mandapam, sternly rebuked him, saying, “He who does not worship the devotees as his refuge, and the gracious Thiyagaraja who takes him as his own, are both outside.” He was blessed to be one of the Gananathas.

Therefore, those who come here for worship must first consider the Devasiriya Mandapam as containing devotees, worship them, then worship Viranmeendar Nayanar, and receive his permission before proceeding further. This is one of the worship practices of this Thiru-temple. Next to be seen is the Rajanarayana Mandapam with Nandi and Balipeedam.

There, Thiyagaraja Peruman, at the end of the Panguni Uthiram festival, proceeds and receives the Ennaikappu (oil anointing). Hence, it is also called Ennaikappu Mandapam. He also proceeds there for the Thiru-athirai festival. On the right side of the facade of this mandapam is the Vinayagar shrine, and on the left side is the Durga shrine.

Then, facing south, is the Juvarahareswarar shrine. After worshipping him and proceeding south, in the mandapam on the left side, are the Ekadasa Shiva Lingams worshipped by Rudras. After worshipping them, one must pass the Abhisheka Kattalai Arai Vaasal, Swami Madappalli, Vahana Arai, Rajan Kattalai Arai Vaasal.

Then, one should have darshan of Ganapathi, Musukunda Devar, and Udaiyarth Devar, and also the Lingam worshipped by Thulasi Maharaja, and the Lingam worshipped by Devendran. After having darshan of these deities, one should stand on the square stone below the Mani Mandapam and have darshan of the seven Gopurams, which are sthoola-lingams, and attain the Araneri of Thiruvarur. Araneri is a separate sthalam, praised in the songs of Thiru-navukkarasu Nayanar. After worshipping the Nandi and Balipeedam in front of the Thiru-mun, and entering, one can see Aranerinathar.

Here, the Lord’s sacred name is Achaleswarar, and the Goddess is named Vandarkuzhali. It was in this sthalam that Naminandhi Adigal Nayanar, due to the cruelty of the Jains, when there was no oil to light the sacred lamp, performed the service of lighting the sacred lamp with water from Deva Theertham. It is here that the Thiruppani Kattum Mandapam (Mandapam for construction work) is located. This mandapam signifies the valor of Kalarichinga Nayanar and Seruthunai Nayanar. We will see this in detail later. This sthalam is referred to as Achalesam in the Sthala Puranam.

A king named Samarkaran performed severe penance for four hundred years and had a vision of Shiva Peruman. Shiva Peruman asked him what he desired. He requested that Shiva Peruman be enshrined in the Lingam he had established and worshipped at the base of this sthalam.

The Lord graciously agreed to remain here immovably, hence the name Achalesam. This Shiva Lingam has a unique glory: the shadow of Achaleswarar does not fall in any direction other than the east. Those who are to die within six months, if they look, that shadow will not be visible. These truths can be learned from the Thiruvarur Puranam sections, which state, “The good shadow of that Lingam does not fall in any direction other than the east, and if those who are to die in six months look, that shadow will not be seen.” Thus, after worshipping this sthalam, one can successively worship Dhaksheswarar, Manuchakreswarar, Bhageeratheswarar, Kailasa Thiyagar, Pandiyanathar, Sapthamathar, Saptharishiswarar, Cheranathar in the second southern prakaram, and then reach Adakeswaram. Adakeswaram is an Appu Sthalam (water element sthalam).

Here, there is a Naga Bilam (serpent hole). Shiva Peruman is enshrined with the Goddess. After worshipping this and proceeding clockwise, there is a Pathiri tree nearby, which is the sacred tree of this sthalam. After worshipping that as well, one can successively worship Viswamithreswarar, Puroor Vasakareswarar, Mahabaleeswarar, Rowthra Durgai, Arunachaleswarar, Varuneswara Vinayagar, and Varuneswarar.

Next, on the western side of the second prakaram, one can worship deities like Thamneswarar, Kaikootu Vinayagar, Lakshmeswarar, Agneeswarar, and then reach Anandheswaram. Anandheswaram is a separate temple within this Thiru-temple.

Here, one can see Anandheswarar after worshipping Vinayagar, Moothal Munivar, Konappillaiyar, and Theshtalingiyam. This was established by a sage named Manganan. That sage performed worship according to Agamic rules and attained Siddhi. Unable to contain his joy, he danced.

Hence, this Lingam came to be known as Anandalingam. A Chetti (merchant) named Pattinasami earned his livelihood by selling lotus flowers.

One day, the lotus flowers did not sell. He brought those flowers and, by chance, offered them in worship to Anandheswarar. That day was Vaikasi Visakam. As a result, in his next birth, he ruled as a king, with Kanchipuram as his capital.

Then, after having darshan of the seven Gopurams in the northern second prakaram and circumambulating, one can successively worship deities like Puraneswarar, Hanumanthaswarar, Vibheeshaneswarar, Balasubramanyar, Garbheswarar.

Then, one can reach the temple called Viswakarmeswaram. This is a beautiful temple filled with exquisite stone carvings. In this Thiru-temple, deities like Dhakshinamurthy, Ganapathi, Durga, and Brahma are beautifully arranged. The Lord’s sacred name is Viswakarmeswarar. After worshipping him and receiving his grace, and enjoying the artistic skills, one can then worship Varuneswarar, Bhairavar, Parasurameswarar, Vasishteswarar, Arkaleswarar, Sabapatheeswarar, Agastheeswarar, Singanathar, Atharva Sarvaneswarar, Samavedeswarar, Vyaseswarar, Bhakteswarar, Rigvedeswarar, Yajurvedeswarar, Vilvatheeswarar, and then reach Siddheswaram. In Siddheswaram, there are separate idols of Pillaiyar, Bhairavar, and Ambikai.

The shrine faces west. A king named Amsan attained Siddhi by chanting the Panchakshara mantra and ruled under the name Siddheeshan. Adjacent to this temple is a Dhakshinamurthy shrine. It was here that Sri La Sri Gnanasambandha Swamigal, the first Paramacharya of Dharmapuram Adheenam, approached Kamalai Gnana Prakasar, accepted him as his Guru, received initiation, and established the worship of Sokkalingamoorthy. After worshipping such a Siddheswaram, bowing to the Maha Yagasalai, seeing the Kattalai Araigal, and seeing the Vinayagar and Subramanyar Yagasalais and the Muvvar Moolasthanam, and seeing Pulastya Rishiswarar and Pulastya Brahmeswarar, and worshipping them, and then circumambulating the Indra Kodimaram and Rajanarayana Mandapam again, and passing the Ardha Jama Kattalai and other areas, one will come to a mandapam called Thattam Sutri Mandapam.

There, Vallabhai Ganapathi is enshrined. After worshipping him and proceeding slightly north, the Kodimaram (flagstaff) will appear. The sacred name of this Kodimaram Vinayagar is Porkambam Vinayagar. After worshipping him and turning west, the Vanmeeganathar shrine will appear. Vanmeeganathar, showing his magnetic grace, draws the iron-like hearts of devotees and blesses them by keeping them at his feet. To approach his shrine, one must pass through a Gopuram.

That Gopuram is called Alagiyan Gopuram. After worshipping the Dvarapalakas and entering, on the left side, facing north, one can see Adhikara Nandi enshrined. After worshipping him and entering further, and circumambulating, one can worship the Balipeedam and Nandi in the Narthana Mandapam of the first prakaram, and also Pradosha Nayagar with the Goddess in Kilikkurattu, and Chandrasekarar, Chola Maharaja, Manickavasagar, and Thirupura Sangaramurthy.

Then, entering the Garbhagriham through the left side entrance, one can worship Aingalakasu Vinayagar. He is also known as Alagiya Chola Vinayagar. After worshipping him and entering through the left side, one can reach the inner entrance of Vanmeeganathar. This part of the Thiru-temple is known as Thirumoolattanam. This Thiru-temple is also called Poonkoyil (Flower Temple). It is called Poonkoyil because it is the heart-lotus of Bhoomi Devi.

The Lord’s sacred name is Putridankonda Vanmeeganathar. To his right, in the Ardhamandapam, Priyavidai Amman is enshrined. She is known by the name Somakulambikai. After worshipping her and contemplating the great union of divine power with Shiva, and bowing with devotion, if one returns the same way to the first prakaram, one can worship Surya and Adithyalingam. Going further, the shrine of Thiyagaraja Peruman will appear. In front of this Thiyagaraja Peruman’s shrine, two great kings are enshrined.

One king is Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the earthly king. The other king is Sundaramoorthy Nayanar, the protector of poets. In front of this Thiyagaraja Peruman, Nandi Peruman stands. This is one of the unique features of this sthalam. After worshipping this Nandi Peruman and proceeding further, one can worship Mooladhara Ganapathi and Manickavasagar on the southern side of the first prakaram, Sambandar, Thiru-navukkarasar, and others.

Then, one can successively worship the Utsavamoorthy (processional deities) of the sixty-three Nayanars and the Moolavar (main deities) of the sixty-three Nayanars.

Then, through the Thennan Thiru-vaayil (southern sacred entrance), one can reach the shrine of Thiyagaraja Peruman. There, on a precious jeweled throne, with two auspicious weapons shining in front and a bouquet of flowers in the middle, He is enshrined and grants darshan. Next to Thiyagaraja Peruman, the Goddess is enshrined. That Goddess is called Kondi. Between them, the six-faced deity, the embodiment of their love and grace, is enshrined. To the right of Thiyagaraja Peruman, on a pedestal, a casket can be seen.

In it, a Shiva Lingam made of emerald is enshrined. His sacred name is Veethi Vidangar. For him, in the Kala Malai and second Kala, Abhishekam is performed. Devotees can always see the sacred face of Thiyagaraja Peruman and the sacred face of the Goddess. A part of the sacred hand can be seen during Abhishekam. During Margazhi Thiru-athirai, the left foot can be seen, and during Panguni Uthiram, the right foot can be seen.

The other sacred limbs are very secret. A devotional kirtanam, full of love, asks, “What is the secret mantra that reveals the face, O hand?” This proclaims it. Every object associated with Thiyagaraja Peruman has a name. The great staff for his swaying is Manithandu. His flag is Thiyaga Kodi. His throne is Rathina Singasanam.

His garland is Sengazhuneer Malai. His sword is Veera Kandayam. His dance is Ajapa Natanam. His elephant is Airavanam. His mountain is Arathanasungam. His drum is Panchamukavathiyam.

His horse is Vedam. His country is Chola Nadu. His town is Thiruvarur. His river is Kaveri. His melody is Pathinan Vagaippan. These are some of the many names of Thiyagaraja Peruman: Thiyagaraja Peruman, Veethi Vidangar, Devaragandar Peruman, Thiyaga Peruman, Adarava Kinkini Kaal Azhagar, Sengazhuneer Azhagar, Sevvanthi Thodazhagar, Kambik Kathazhagar, Thiyaga Vinodhar, Karunagara Thondaiman, Asainthadum Appar, one who bestowed a thousand gold coins for a step, Kamalesar, Sempon Thiyagar, Thirundhirai Kolathar, Deva Chinthamani, Thiyaga Chinthamani, Chinthamani, and over sixty other sacred names are in vogue.

Applying green camphor, wearing Sevvanthi earrings, wearing Sengazhuneer garlands, inscribing the name Thiyagar on the clothes offered to the Lord, plucking bitter gourds, and listening to songs sung through the Thennan Thiru-vaasal are the customs of Thiyagaraja Peruman. The garland dear to this Thiyagaraja Peruman is the Sengazhuneer garland. Nowhere else can one see decorations made with flowers from this sthalam like here. After worshipping this unique Thiyagaraja Peruman and coming through the southern entrance, one can see a beautiful painting depicting Thiyagaraja Peruman seated on the chest of Thirumal, near the Thennan Thiru-vaayil. After worshipping that and also bowing to Dhakshinamurthy, one can reach the western prakaram.

There, after worshipping deities like Annapoorani who granted darshan, Vathapi Vinayagar, known by the name Sthala Vinayagar, is enshrined. It is about this Vathapi Vinayagar that Muthuswami Dikshitar, one of the Musical Trinity, composed the song “Vathapi Ganapathim Bhajaeham,” which is sung as the first song in music concerts today. Next to him are the Chithira Peedam and Utsava Bhikshatanar. After having darshan of Mahalakshmi, Durga, Lakshmi, and Saraswati Utsavamoorthies located to the west of Vanmeeganathar, one can reach the Murugan shrine in the northwest corner. The Mantra Peedam is special to this sthalam. It was established by Agastya Muni, who invoked all the mantras into it.

By worshipping this, Agastya gained the power to drink up the ocean. In the northern prakaram, there are Navagrahas. Unlike the arrangement in other temples, the Navagrahas here are not arranged in a specific pattern but are in a straight line.

Then, one can have darshan of the Shiva Lingam named Ranavimochaneeswarar.

Then, after worshipping Durga, one can reach the Chandeswarar shrine. There are two Chandeswarar shrines. One is the Moolavar Chandar, who, with matted hair and beard, rests his face on his knee, supporting himself on one leg. He is Yama Chandar. Since those born in Thiruvarur are Shiva Gananathas, when Yama grieved that he had no work, the Lord blessed him, saying, “We have given you the position of Chandesan, which grants the benefit of worshipping us.” Therefore, Yama himself is Chandeswarar here. Standing next to him is Chandar, who comes in procession and grants darshan in a sacred form.

He is Adhi Chandar. He announces the arrival of the Panguni Uthiram festival to everyone by writing palm-leaf messages. All temple affairs are conducted in his name. In this same prakaram, The Chandrasekarar Temple houses the processional deity Chandrasekarar, who is enshrined with his consort, Tarunendu Sekari. Bhairavar is also present nearby. After worshipping within the inner circumambulatory path, one emerges to reach the temple of the divine Mother, the embodiment of grace.

Her sacred name is Alliyang Kodai, and in Sanskrit, Nilotpalambikai. Her left hand gently touches the head of Murugan, who is held by a companion. This depiction of touching Kandhan’s head is unique and not found elsewhere. The bedchamber is located here. After viewing and worshipping these, one should proceed to the northern side of the Rajanarayana Mandapam, facing the Sthaneswarar Dakshinamurthy shrine, chant the Panchaksharam, and then exit.

Thus, we have contemplated the glories of the Thiruvarur Temple.

Now, we will continue to reflect on its puranic history.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 25 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
We are contemplating the glories of Thiruvarur.

Let us consider the unique attributes of Thiyagaraja Peruman of Thiruvarur. Thiyagaraja Peruman possesses many distinctive features, whose glories are as follows: Thiyagaraja Peruman’s seat is the Ratna Simhasanam (gem-studded throne). The Simhasanam, upon which Thiyagaraja is enshrined beneath a pearl canopy, is known as Sri Peetam, Ratna Simhasanam, and Mantra Simhasanam. Yogis also refer to it as the Dwadasanta Mantra Simhasanam. This throne, made of sandalwood overlaid with gold plates, displays exquisite artistic carvings. It is called Simhasanam because it is supported by four lions.

During Thiyagaraja’s floral worship (Pushpavrishti), the Devas sound their celestial drums and shower Kalpaka flowers like rain. This floral shower is known as Malar M or Pushpavrishti. This signifies that Thiyagaraja, the Kalpa, descended from the celestial world to the earthly realm. Pushpanjali is the offering of flowers to the Lord after the dance known as Suddha Niruttam. It is believed that celestial maidens and Apsaras perform Pushpanjali daily. In ancient times, the Rudra Ganikas known as Pathiyilar regularly performed this Pushpanjali.

The royal emblem: Therefore, it is customary to fan Thiyagaraja, the King of Kings worshipped by the immortals, with white whisks (venchamarams). During the evening puja and processions, white whisks are waved.

Next, the valorous sword (Veera Kandayam) of Thiyagaraja Peruman. Two swords are placed leaning before the Lord. These are the Veera Kadgam Since the Lord is enshrined as a great emperor and Kshatriya, the Veera Val (sword of valor) signifies His prowess. The Gnana Val (sword of wisdom) severs the three impurities of devotees and bestows the supreme state of Sayujyam. Next is the Thiyaga Parivattam (sacred cloth) adorned by Thiyagaraja Peruman. The long cloth draped upon Thiyagaraja Peruman is called Parivattam.

Twenty-one Parivattams are used for the inner adornment. The Parivattam is a white cloth with five stripes. These are changed after the secret anointing on every Monday. Thiyagaraja is seated in the center of the throne. To His left is Kondiyammai, and to His right, a silk cloth embroidered with a lingam design in zari on blue silk, symbolizing the element of earth, is draped. At one time, the Lord was adorned with red silk garments.

These were called Kusumapattu. During winter, fine deer-skin garments were also adorned by the Lord.

Next, Krishnagandham, a Thiyaga Vinodham. The black paste applied by the Lord in the evening is called Krishnagandham. It is prepared from eighteen herbs, sandalwood, and ghee. This paste is applied to the Lord’s Chrysanthemum earrings. At the conclusion of Thiruvandhik Kaappu, that is, after the Sayarakshai, it is mixed with sacred ash (Vibhuti) and distributed to devotees. This is called Thiyaga Vinodham.

On the evening before the Aazhi Therottam (chariot festival), a special puja is performed, and it is applied. It is called Thiruchaanthu. For the Lord, Narunthilagam. Before the Thiruvandhik Kaappu, known as Deva Sayarakshai, a reddish paste made of green camphor, saffron, and sandalwood is applied as a tilak to the Lord. During the anointing ceremony, Narunthilagam is also applied to the Lord. Next is Thalai Seera.

This is a type of ornament that adorns the Lord’s sacred head. It is an intricately crafted, square-shaped ornament embedded with precious gems. It is also known as Choolamani.

Hence, Navukkarasar Peruman praises the Lord as Choolamani Ser Mudiyan (He whose crown is adorned with Choolamani). Next are the Ganga and the Crescent Moon. On either side of the Thalai Seera, two crescent-moon-shaped ornaments are seen. One represents the Ganga flowing in the Lord’s matted locks, and the other, the waxing young crescent moon. Elders interpret this to mean that the mind of the seeker, converging at the Dwadasanta, forms the Somavattam (lunar circle) around his head, which is one crescent, and the Kundalini, shining in the Yogi’s head, is the Ganga, the other crescent. Next are the Lord’s Chrysanthemum earrings.

Below the crescent moons, these earrings, made of flowers, are adorned. When chrysanthemums are unavailable, jasmine, champak, and rosewater leaves are used to fashion them. Krishnagandham is applied to these earrings and adorned by the Lord.

Therefore, the Lord is praised as Sevvanthi Thodazhagar (He with beautiful Chrysanthemum earrings). Next is Thiruppani. The garlands adorned by the Lord are called Thiruppani. They are made of fragrant flowers like jasmine and mullai. The Sevvanthi Thodu and Thiruppani together are known as Poothodu Karavaram. Thiyagesar bestows grace by adorning garlands called Indai Malai during the Thiruvandhik Kaappu.

Next is Thiyagaraja’s Angam Sira Malai. This garland adorns Thiyagaraja’s chest. This garland is adorned on both sides of the Kalpadakkams (medallions). It is believed that these heads are the heads of Brahma. After Brahma’s lifespan ends, the Lord adorns his head.

Thus, countless heads of Brahmas are found in the Lord’s Sira Malai (garland of heads). This Brahma Sira Malai signifies that the Lord is not only beyond tattvas but also beyond time. Next is Aazhivalai Kaiyan. The rings and bracelets worn on the Lord’s sacred hands are called Aazhikal.

Therefore, the Thiruvarur Thevaram praises the Lord as Aazhivalai Kaiyanum Aroor Amarntha Ammaan (He with the Aazhivalai hands and the Lord residing in Aroor). Next are the Red Water Lilies. The red water lily flowers are sacred to Shiva Peruman. In Sanskrit, they are called Kalkara Pushpam. It is said that this rare flower came to Thiruvarur from the celestial world with the Lord specifically for His worship. An expanse of five velis of water, located east of the temple, is used for the Lord’s worship.

In one part of this pond, the Goddess’s namesake, the Nilotpala flower, and the Kalkara Pushpam, or red water lily, bloom. Next is Thiruppani Saathuthal and Niraippani Thirunaal. In all temples, it is customary to adorn the Lord’s form with flower garlands and floral chains.

However, a special practice for Thiyagaraja is Thiruppani Saathuthal.

Here, fragrant flowers like jasmine, mullai, chrysanthemum, and coral jasmine are not strung into garlands or chains, but rather showered upon Thiyagaraja Peruman as an adornment. This method is called Pani Saathuthal. A separate festival is even held here for Pani Saathuthal. This is called Niraippani Utsavam. On the Poorattathi day of the Purattasi month, red water lilies are plucked from the red water lily pond and placed in a palanquin. They are paraded through Thiruvarur and brought before Thiyagaraja Peruman, where pujas are performed and Pani Saathuthal takes place.

At that time, the traditional garland maker of this temple, an employee named Thirumaalaikkatti, is honored with a temple-sanctioned Parivattam. This is the Niraippani Utsavam. Next is Anthikkaappu. The unique and grand puja for Thiyagaraja Peruman is called Thiruvandhik Kaappu. Devas leave the celestial world and come to earth to witness the Lord in this puja. During Thiruvandhik Kaappu, the adornments and offerings are elaborate and magnificent.

Hence, the Lord is specially named Anthik Kaappazhagar. It is believed that during this Thiruvandhik Kaappu or Sayarakshai puja, all the Shiva Kalais (aspects of Shiva) from all Shiva temples merge into the Lord of Aroor. Next is Thiruchalagam. On either side of the sanctum where Thiyaga Thiyagesar is enshrined in His divine form, there are two windows facing each other, allowing the gentle breeze to enter. These are revered as Thiruchalagam or Thiruchalaram. Next is Devasirayam Thirukkaavanam.

Devas who come to worship Thiyagaraja stay outside this Devasirayam Mandapam until their turn arrives. Inside the Devasirayam Mandapam, Shiva devotees reside. It was the Shiva devotees in this Devasirayam Mandapam who inspired Sundaramurthy Swamigal to compose the Thiruthondathogai. Through that, we received the Periya Puranam. Next is Thiru Marunthu. After Thiyagaraja Peruman performs the Ajapa dance in the streets and enters the temple, a sacred medicine (Thiru Marunthu) prepared with dried ginger, pepper, long pepper, and jaggery is offered to Him to alleviate His fatigue.

Similarly, medicines like Thavalappuli and Ambarappuli are also offered. Next is Thirumanthiram.

After the completion of Thiruvandhik Kaappu for the Lord, the sacred ash (Thiruneeru) mixed with Krishnagandham that is distributed is called Thirumanthiram here. Next is Vasantha Vizha. Vasanthan, or Manmathan, the son born to Thirumal after worshipping Thiyagesar, is the one who revered and celebrated this Vasantha Vizha (Spring Festival).

Therefore, the Lord is known by the sacred name Vasantha Vaibhava Thiyagarajar. Rathi, Manmathan (Vasanthan), and his younger brother Saman worship Thiyagaraja on this day. Next is Pathigam Paaduthal. Generally, in Shiva temples, it is customary to say Thiruchitrambalam before and after reciting the Thirumurais.

However, in Thiruvarur alone, it is a special feature to say Aroora Thiyagesa twice, both when beginning and ending the recitation of the Pathigams. In this sacred place, two people sing the Thirumurais. One sings, and the other echoes it loudly; this is the ancient tradition of this place. Next is Aayiram Perudaiyan.

The Lord of Aroor has a thousand names. The word ‘thousand’ also signifies countless. Thiyagaraja Peruman has two Sahasranamas (thousand names). One is the Musukunda Sahasranama, praised and sung by Emperor Musukunda. The other is the Mukunda Sahasranama, praised and sung by Vishnu Murthy. Next is Manikkathandu.

The Manikkathandu, also known as Thiruvadu Thandu, is designed to facilitate Thiyagaraja Peruman’s Ajapa dance. It is also called Vaarai or Pillaithandu. Unlike placing the Simhasanam on bamboo poles or supports, there is no custom of tying it with ropes here. Instead, the Manikkathandu is secretly attached to the Vaarais with ropes by those of the Veliparamar tradition, unseen by others, connecting the Lord’s pedestal to the Vaarais. This is called the Pillaithandu Rahasyam (secret of the child-staff). There is a belief that this jeweled staff is the spinal cord of a Shiva Yogi.

Next is Vidangar. Since the Lord’s form is a secret, the anointings meant for Him are performed on His representative form, the Maragatha Lingam. He is known as Veethi Vidangar. He is enshrined to the right of Thiyagaraja Peruman. Anointing is performed for Him three times daily. The sandalwood paste applied to Him has medicinal properties.

Next is Nindra Idappam. In other temples, Nandi is seated, but here, in the shrine of Thiyagaraja Peruman, Nandi stands. Next is Kattiyam Kooruthal. Announcing the arrival of kings is called Kattiyam Kooruthal. In this sacred place, since Thiyaga Vinodhar is enthroned in royal splendor, when He processes, the custom of announcing Thiyagaraja’s arrival with Kattiyam has been prevalent in Aroor for many years. The next special feature is Suddhamathalam.

Behind the curtain, during Thiyagaraja Peruman’s festival, He is enshrined amidst the resounding of eighteen types of musical instruments. During certain festivals, artists who play the Suddhamathalam are honored with silk Parivattam.

Then, as they stand behind the Lord, a curtain is drawn. Immediately, the Suddhamathalam resounds. This rhythmic puja is a unique specialty of Thiyagaraja. Next is Bhootha Niruttam. During the Chandrasekarar festival, when the Lord’s form processes towards the northeast direction, the Muttukkarar (drummer) places the Suddhamathalam on the Parivattam tied to his head and plays the drum with both hands raised. This is called Bhootha Niruttam.

It is believed that while playing thus, the Bhootha Ganas perform a dance before Chandrasekarar. That is why the drummer plays the drum above his head. Next are Thiyagaraja’s Ganas. Since Thiyagaraja Peruman is the King of Kings, He never processes alone in the streets.

First, the celestial maidens called Apsaras dance, sing, and praise the Lord as they proceed. Others are the Lord’s Ganas. They are the Arulappadiyar and Urimai Thozhuthezhuvar, who are Adisaivars. Next are the Rudra Pal Ganathar, who are Shiva Ganas. Next are the Virichchadai Virathigal, who are ascetics, Brahmins, Saivites, Pasupathas, Kaapalikas, and other Ganas, with whom Thiyagaraja Peruman always processes. The next special feature of Thiyagaraja Peruman is that He is the Lord worshipped by the Devas.

The Lord also has sacred names like Devanayakan and Devaragandar. These names signify that the Devas constantly worship Thiyagaraja Peruman and rejoice. Next is Somakula Rahasyam. No one has ever seen the full form of Thiyagaraja, who is enshrined with such glories in Thiruvarur. Like the Thillai Rahasyam, the Thiruvarur Rahasyam is the sacred form of Thiyagaraja Swami. This is called Somakula Rahasyam.

We cannot behold the Swami’s full form; only the daily facial darshan is granted to us. In Thiruvarur, it is facial darshan, and in Thiruvalam, it is foot darshan. Only on the day of foot darshan do we have the good fortune to also behold Skanda. On Thiruvathirai, the Lord bestows the darshan of His left foot upon Vyagrapadar and Pathanjali. On that one day, from six in the morning until three in the afternoon, we can behold the left foot, adorned with heroic anklets, which Brahma and Vishnu could not see, placed upon Adiseshan.

Furthermore, we can joyfully behold the right foot of the Goddess Kondiyammai, adorned with anklets and bells, placed upon a lotus flower, as Kondiyammai is seated to the left of the Lord. Throughout that day, we also have the great fortune to see Skanda, covered in diamonds. Thiruvarur Thiyagaraja Peruman is a Somaskandamurtham.

Therefore, Thiyagaraja Peruman and Ambika Kondiyammai are on the left side, and Skanda is between them. However, we cannot always see Skanda Peruman. Only on this sacred day mentioned above do we have the good fortune to see Skanda Peruman, who is between the Mother and the Father.

Similarly, on Panguni Uthiram, during the Bhakta Jana Seva, one can receive darshan of the right foot. Next is Abhishekam. The auspicious days for the Lord’s Maha Abhishekam are the first day of Chithirai, the Aadi Ayana, Aippasi Vishu, Margazhi Thiruvathirai, the first day of Thai, and Panguni Uthiram. During festival periods, after Thiyagaraja descends from the chariot and enters the temple, a Prāyaśchitta Abhishekam is performed that very night in the Rajanarayana Mandapam, and a grand Maha Abhishekam is performed during the day for devotees to witness. In addition to this, Abhishekam is performed for the Lord during eclipses. Before the Somavara Alankaram, the Nayanars, including Urimai Thozhuthezhuvar, perform a secret Abhishekam.

Witnessing the Abhishekam performed for Thiyagaraja Peruman is an extraordinary experience, to say the least. Milk, curd, Panchamirtham, honey, tender coconut water, rosewater, sandalwood, sugarcane juice, one hundred and eight conch waters, Valampuri conch water, sacred water filled in a rhinoceros horn, and kumbha water are used for the grand anointing. Cardamom, cloves, and vetiver root are mixed into the anointing water. Since He is a secret deity, the forms of the Swami, Ambika, and Skanda Peruman between them are covered with cloth. An Indai (garland) made of rosewater leaves is adorned so that the crown on the head is not visible. The feet are also covered.

Therefore, even during the anointing, one cannot see Thiyagaraja Peruman, Kondiyammaiyar, or Skanda Peruman between them. We do not get a full darshan. Next is the Lord’s sacred foot darshan. On Margazhi Thiruvathirai, one can receive darshan of the left foot, and on Panguni Uthiram, darshan of the right sacred foot. One can also behold the foot of Umaiyammai, Kondiyammai. The Lord’s foot is adorned as if Adiseshan supports it, and the Mother’s foot as if a lotus supports it.

The lotus represents the Muladhara Padma, and the serpent, the Kundalini Shakti. Only on these days can Skanda be seen in a diamond armor. This is called Rudrapada Darshanam. The Lord’s back beauty. The Lord’s back beauty is three times more enchanting than His front appearance. He is adorned with beautiful golden armor.

His hair is beautifully braided, and this braided hair, embedded with jewels, resembles that of a woman.

Furthermore, one can behold the Lord’s elbows and wings. The wings signify that the Lord is in the form of the Hamsa Mantra. The Lord’s back beauty is supremely beautiful.

Therefore, He is praised as Munnilum Mummadangu Pin Azhagar (He whose back beauty is three times more than His front). Next is the Lord’s special feature, Kinkini Kaal Azhagar. The Lord’s feet are adorned with tiny bells (kinkinis).

Therefore, He is known as Kinkini Kaalagar. Next are the Lord’s sacred names. If the Lord’s form is thus, His names are countless: Aathiraiyan, Koothukandhan, Aadalvallaan, Asainthaadiya Peruman, Adikkum Aayiram Pon Azhippavar, Anthiyum Santhiyum Aaduvaar, Asainthaadum Appar, Rathina Singathipathi, Irunthaadum Azhagar, Thakkaarkku Thakkaan, Thevara Kanda Peruman, Kambik Kaathazhagar, Kamalesar, Karunagara Thondaiman, Kinkini Kaalagar, Thiyaga Peruman, Thiyaga Sinthamani, Thiyaga Vinodhar, Deva Sinthamani, Devanayakan, Devaraganda Peruman, Thiruvandhik Kaappazhagar, Munnilum Mummadangu Pin Azhagar, Sengazhuneer Azhagar, Sevvanthi Thodazhagar, Vallal Veethividangap Peruman, Thervurntha Selvan, Aazhither Viththagan, Pavanividangar, Vidangarai Veethi Ponthar, Kamala Vasanthath Thiyagar, Vasantha Vaibogath Thiyagar, Sempon Thiyagar, Kanagamani Thiyagar, Kamala Vasantha Nirai Selva Thiyagar. These are all descriptive names of Thiyagaraja Peruman. Next are the ornamental flowers sacred to Thiyagaraja Peruman. The red water lily is unique to the Lord.

Additionally, jasmine, mullai, chrysanthemum, champangi, marukozhundhu, and vetiver are used for the Lord’s adornment. The favored offerings for Thiyagaraja Peruman are Thoothuvalai greens and bitter gourd curry. Among the offerings, large, bamboo-tray-sized honey cakes made only with ghee, large vadai, and sweet suyams are particularly special. The musical instruments played in Thiyagesar’s shrine are Suddhamathalam, Karna, Conch, Ekkalam, Nagara, Namuki, Kidukitti or Kodukatti, Flute, Tharai, Parinayanam, Panchamukavathiyam, Thavandai, Perigai, Brahmadhalam, Vanga, Thiruchinnam, Thappattai, Mukaveenai. It is said that Indra presented Thimiri Nayanam, Parinayanam, Mukaveenai, Yazh, and Thavil to Emperor Musukunda along with the Thiyagaraja Murthis. Next is Archana.

The Mukundarchana, consisting of a thousand names of Thiyagaraja composed by Thirumal, and the Indrarchana, performed by Indra, were joyfully offered. After receiving Thiyagaraja from Indra, he returned to the sacred place of Thiruvarur. The Musukunda Chakravarti’s Archana and the Musukunda Sahasranamavali are three special features that add glory to the Peruman. Among these, the Musukunda Archana contains names related to the supremacy, characteristics, qualities, divine play, and profound devotion of Thyagaraja, the compassion shown to devotees, the Vedantic secrets of the word ‘Thyaga’ in the Lord’s name, the Ajapa dance, the greatness of the Thiruvarur Thirthalam, the Kamalalaya Thirkulam, Thyagaraja’s gem-studded throne, the Gnana Kadgam, Veera Kadgam, Thirusalagam, and the Aazhi Ther.

Next, there are five-foot tall and stout Thivattis (torches) in Thiruvarur. Ghee is used in these Thivattis. Four people carry these Thivattis to the right, left, front, and back of Thyagaraja. Behind the person pouring ghee to keep the torch burning brightly, another person will follow with a silver vessel resembling a rosewater sprinkler. Such are the unique Thiruvarur Thivattis. Another special feature in the worship of Thyagaraja Peruman is the use of mudras.

Many mudras are used in Thyagaraja worship. In ancient times, when the priest performed the puja, he would recite the mudras with mantras, and the temple dancers would perform the mudras through abhinaya. Mudras were revered as divine. Puja Paddhati texts state that the gods rejoice when mudras are shown. These women who showed mudras were specially called ‘Kai Kaattum Muraikkarigal’. The servants of Thyagaraja, those who have the hereditary right to touch and worship Thyagesan, are called ‘Urimaiyil Thozhudhezhuvar’.

Next are the Vilupparamars. These are members of a lineage who were blessed to support the Peruman when Thyagesar performed the Ajapa dance.

Thus, Thyagaraja Peruman, possessing many special attributes befitting an emperor, presents a wondrous sight on a magnificent gem-studded throne, adorned with an Aragachai, jingling anklets, a five-lined Parivattam, a sword, an Udara Bandhanam, a Veera Kandayam, a Gnana Kandayam, a head-dress, and a Tilakam called Thyaga Vinodam, with Pakampiriyal (Kondiyammal) on His left side, and Sri Skanda Peruman in the center. When we contemplate Thiruvarur in the Periya Puranam, why do we contemplate Thyagaraja Peruman in such detail? Because this Thyagaraja Peruman Himself initiated Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with the words ‘Adiyen’ (I am a servant) to ‘Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum’ and blessed him to sing the Thiruthondathogai, a Thiru-Padigam that praises the glory of the devotees. This Thiruthondathogai is the Thiru-Padigam that led to the creation of the Periya Puranam. That is why we have had to contemplate Thyagaraja Peruman in such detail. This Thyagaraja Peruman is the original creator and the cause of the Periya Puranam.

Let us pursue this contemplation further. Aroora, Thyagesa, Thiruchitrambalam.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 26 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
We have been contemplating the glories of the Thiruvarur Thiru-Kovil.

Let us press on. The Thirthams of this Thalam are all Shiva Thirthams. The Kamalalaya Thirtham is prominent; it is a Deva Thirtham, located on the west side of the Thiru-Kovil, spanning five acres.

Then there are Sangam Thirtham, Gaya Thirtham, Vaani Thirtham, and a Sengalineer stream that flows on the eastern side of this Thiru-Kovil. The Thiru-Thondathogai, a Padigam glorifying devotees by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, originated in this Thalam. All those born in this Thalam are considered Shiva Gana Nathars. It is a Sthalam that grants liberation upon birth. Thirumagal (Mahalakshmi) performed penance here and received blessings. Kalaimagal (Saraswati) performed penance here.

Kamalai Parasakthi, the Para Sakthi, performs penance here. It is the birthplace of Paravai Nachiyar. It was ruled by Musukunda Chozhan and Manu Needhi Chozhan. This Thalam is also known as Kshethravarapuram. When Brahma, to manifest his creative power, weighed all the places he created on one scale and Thiruvarur on another, he found that all other places combined were not equal to Thiruvarur. Hence this name.

Since the Lord, with the sacred name Adagesar, along with Gnanasundari Ammai, has been eternally present here, it is called Adageswarapuram due to His possession of Adagesam.

Similarly, because Brahma performed a Yagam with many Devas, it is called Devayaga Puram. And because Murugan blesses and protects His devotees, it is called Kandapuram. Musukunda Chozhan, having received Thyagaraja from Indra, brought Him here, installed Him, and conducted festivals and other rituals properly, thereby receiving blessings.

Hence, it is called Musukundapuram. When Sanatkumara and other sages grieved, fearing the advent of Kali Yuga, Brahma told them that if they went to Thiruvarur and worshipped the Lord, the miseries of Kali would not occur.

Accordingly, they came, and since Kali, fearing to enter, stood outside, it was named Kali Sela Nagaram. Since the Lord, with the Goddess, has been eternally seated beneath the earth as a divine form unknown to anyone, it is called Andarageswarapuram. And since the Peruman took abode in the anthill created by Indra, it is called Vanmeeganathapuram. Since the Devas always worship Vanmeeganathar, it is called Devasirayapuram. And since King Samarkaram built and protected the city, it is called Samarkara Puram. As the Mooladhara for Virata Purusha, it is called Mooladharapuram.

And since Thirumagal and Nilamagal worshipped here and received blessings, it is known as Thiruvarur and Kamalalayapuram. Regarding the history of Vanmeeganathar: In ancient times, Thirumal, Indra, and other Devas decided to perform a Yagam together. They determined Kurukshetra to be the suitable place. They vowed that whoever completed the Yagam without fail would be superior to all others. In that Yagam, Indra and others failed; Vishnu won. He became arrogant.

To subdue him, by the grace of Shiva Peruman, a bow appeared in the Homa Kundam. Vishnu took it, bent it, and intending to subdue all the Devas, pursued them relentlessly. When Vishnu Murthy reached this Thalam, called Parasakthipuram, by the divine grace of the Lord and Goddess, all his strength diminished.

Therefore, with the bow in his hand, he propped it on the ground, rested his head upon it, and sat down exhausted. Considering this the opportune moment, the Devas sought advice from their Guru, Brihaspati. As per his instructions, all the Devas went beneath the bow, transformed into ants, and settled in an anthill. They then gnawed and broke the string of the bow that Vishnu was holding, which was supporting his head as he rested. The Devas were in the form of ants.

Thus, as they gnawed and broke the string, the bow straightened, and Vishnu’s head was severed. The Devas were terrified. The Paramam, who had manifested in the netherworld with the sacred name Adagesa, took abode in the anthill and appeared as a Shiva Lingam. Indra and Brahma worshipped and praised Him. From the Shiva Linga form of the Lord, the Devas emerged and worshipped. They pleaded for protection.

The Lord instructed them to reattach Thirumal’s head to his body. They did so and thus received Thirumal back.

Thereafter, Thirumal and the other Devas performed puja and worship according to the Agamic rites and attained all joys. This is said to be the history of the manifestation of the Peruman who took abode in the anthill. Mahalakshmi grew up with Varuna, the king of the ocean. Hearing the story of Thirumal defeating the Asuras Madhu and Kaitabha, she desired to marry him. She came to this Thalam, worshipped Thirumoolattaneswarar, and performed penance.

The Lord appeared and granted Mahalakshmi her wish. Mahalakshmi requested that this city be known by her name, Thirumagal.

The Lord granted this wish, and this Thalam is still called Kamalalayam. Dasaratha came here, worshipped, and received the boon of having four sons, including Rama.

Now, the history of Thyagaraja Peruman. Thirumal desired a beautiful son.

Therefore, Vishnu Murthy worshipped the Lord and performed penance. To reward his penance, the Lord appeared with Uma Devi, His divine consort. Thirumal requested a son from the Lord who had appeared. The Peruman graciously agreed.

However, Thirumal did not worship Uma Devi, who was on the left side of the Lord.

Therefore, Uma Devi, slightly angered, cursed him, saying that even if he received a son by the grace of the Lord, he would perish by fate.

Then, as Vishnu stood, not knowing what to do, a thought arose, inspired by divine grace. According to that thought, he installed Shiva Peruman, Uma Devi, and Kumarakkadavul on a single seat, and performed elaborate worship according to Agamic rites. Pleased by this worship, the Lord and Goddess appeared on a Rishabha Vahanam, with Muruga Peruman in between. Thirumal worshipped all three. Delighted by this, Maheswari said, “My curse will not be thwarted, but your son will be consumed by the Lord’s fiery eye and then revived,” thus providing a remedy for the curse.

Then Vishnu requested that the Lord, in this Somaskanda form, always remain here and bestow blessings.

The Lord granted this wish. Thereafter, Thirumal installed that Murthy in his heart-temple and worshipped Him. The Thyagaraja Peruman, the Somaskanda Murthy residing in Thirumal’s heart, dances with joy due to the movement of Thirumal’s breath, that is, the inhalation and exhalation of air. This dance is called Ajapa Natanam.

Later, when the Devas were tormented by Asuras, Indra approached Thirumal in the Milk Ocean and recounted what had happened. Thirumal, moved by compassion, said, “I will give you a Supreme Being who is dearer than my life. If you keep Him and worship Him with unwavering love, you will achieve victory by the grace of the Peruman.” He then gave him the Somaskanda Peruman. By worshipping this Somaskanda, Indra was immensely pleased and defeated the Asuras.

Later, at another time, an Asura named Valan came to wage war against Indra. Indra was defeated and fled. At this same time, in the Chola kingdom, in Thiruvarur, a king named Musukundan ruled. In his previous birth, he was a monkey in the sacred Kailasam, roaming in the flower garden. When the Lord and Goddess were present in that garden, the monkey dropped Bilva leaves on them. Pleased by this act of Shiva Punya, the Lord made him a king in his next birth to rule the world.

He was Musukundan, the Chola king, who ruled in Thiruvarur. This Musukundan was a friend of Indra.
