Therefore, with the help of Musukundan, Indra defeated the Asura named Valan.

After the war, Musukunda Chakravarti came from Indraloka.

At that time, Musukunda Chakravarti saw the Thyagaraja Peruman, the Somaskanda Murthy worshipped by Indra, and remembering the worship he had performed in his previous birth in sacred Kailasam, his heart melted with immense love. That Lord, Thyagaraja Peruman, the Somaskanda, appeared only to the eyes of Musukunda Chakravarti and commanded, “Take me to Thiruvarur and worship me there.” Afterwards, Musukunda Chakravarti wished to take leave after partaking of the divine nectar with Indra.

At that time, Indra said, “Ask me what you want, and I will give it to you, as you helped me defeat the Asura Valan.” Musukunda Chakravarti then requested, “If that is so, give me the Thyagaraja Peruman whom you worship.” Indra replied, “This Murthy is not mine; it was worshipped by Thirumal. His permission must be obtained.” Therefore, Musukunda Chakravarti insisted, “Even if you have to ask, you must give Him to me. I will not leave without Him.” Indra then went to the Milk Ocean, and obtained the permission of Vishnu Murthy.

However, Indra was unwilling to part with this divine Somaskanda Murthy. So, he created six identical Murthys and showed all seven Murthys to Musukunda Chakravarti, saying, “If you can identify the true Murthy among these, you may take it to earth.” Shiva Peruman, from within, inspired Musukunda Chakravarti, who then pointed out the true Murthy. Indra was astonished and, not knowing what else to do, granted permission for that Murthy to be brought to Thiruvarur. Musukunda Chakravarti then installed that Thyagaraja Peruman in Thiruvarur.

Thus, Thyagaraja Peruman, who performed the Ajapa dance in the heart of Thirumal in the sacred Milk Ocean, and who showed compassion to Indra in Indraloka, came to this sacred Thiruvarur through Musukunda Chakravarti.

Similarly, there are many other sacred histories associated with this Thiru-Kovil. This Lord Himself blessed Somasi Mara Nayanar. This Lord Himself blessed Manu Needhi Chozhan. This Lord Himself blessed Naminandi Adigal.

Similarly, the histories of Viranminda Nayanar and Kalarcinga Nayanar are also connected with the divine grace of this Thyagaraja Peruman. Six daily pujas are performed in this Thiru-Kovil. The Thiruvananthal takes place at six in the morning. Sri Sokkaperuman is brought from the shrine of Sri Neeloorpalaambal to the shrine of Sri Thyagaraja Swamy, and milk offering is made.

At that time, Deeparadhana takes place in the Vanmeeganathar shrine.

Then, Kalasandhi takes place, followed by Uchikkala Puja for the Maragathalividangar in the Sri Thyagarajar shrine. Abhishekam is performed in front of Thyagaraja in the mandapam. Sayarakshai is performed here, known as Thiruvandhikappu, which is special to this Thalam. It is believed that all the Shiva Kalas from all Shiva Thalas unite and merge in Thiruvarur during Thiruvandhikappu. During Sayarakshai, elaborate worship is performed for Thyagaraja Peruman with Thiruppani Saathi, Thiruchanthi, pure mridangam, and grand Nadaswaram. The second period involves Abhishekam for Vanmeeganathar, and Abhishekam and Naivedyam in the surrounding temples.

Abhishekam is also performed for Neelothpalaambal. Abhishekam is performed for Maragathavidangar before Thyagaraja. Only then are the Alankara Deeparadhanas performed for Thyagaraja.

Afterwards, Sribalithayakar is brought out and Bali is offered in the central mandapam of Devasiriyam.

Then, the sixth period, the Ardha Jama Puja, is offered to Vanmeeganathar. After performing Aradhanas, Sri Sokkaperuman from the Thyagaraja Swamy shrine is brought out in a palanquin with torches, chamaras, and other honors, circumambulated, and then placed in the Palliyarai (bedchamber) in the Neelothpalaambal shrine for the swing ceremony.

Afterwards, Abhishekam and Nivedanam are performed for Bhairavar. On every full moon day of the month, Abhishekam and special Naivedyams are performed for Kamalambal, Neelothpalaambal, Vanmeeganathar, and the Panchalingams. For Thyagaraja Peruman, during the auspicious periods of Adi, Aippasi Vishu, Margazhi Thiruvathirai, Panguni Uthiram, and Thai Sankranti, after He is brought out for the chariot festival and returns, a Prayaschitta Abhishekam is performed. Maha Abhishekam is performed during Chithirai Vishu and during eclipses. In Chithirai, the Vasanthotsavam is celebrated for ten days. In Vaikasi, on Visakha Thirunal, the Pal Mangai Erupadi and Siralan Kari Erupadi are performed.

Furthermore, the Aavani month’s Surya Puja, Aavani Moolam, Kedara Puja, Purattasi Pournami’s Niraippani, Navaratri, Ashtadurga Utsavam, Kanda Sashti, Aippasi Bharani, Aippasi Pooram, Karthigai Somavarams, Thirukkarthigai, Margazhi Thiruppalli Ezhuchi, Manikkavasagar Thiruvambavai Utsavam, Oonjal Utsavam, Arudra Darisanam, Kanu Mandapa Utsavam, Thaipusam, Maha Shivaratri, and other festivals are celebrated in a very elaborate manner. Among these, the Niraippani Utsavam is very special. It is believed that Indra comes here to worship Thyagaraja Peruman.

Then, the Panguni Utsavam, which is the Brahmotsavam, begins with flag hoisting on the Hastha star in the month of Masi and concludes with Theertham on the Uthira star in the month of Panguni. The festival, known as Chandrasekarapattom, is celebrated grandly until the first Theertham. Thyagaraja will sway and come during the three festivals: Vasanthan, Thiruther, and Bhaktha Kaatchi. The Margazhi Thiruvathirai festival received a Thiruppadigam from the mouth of Appar Swamigal. When Thirugnanasambandha Peruman was residing in Thiruppukalur, Appar Swamigal, who had come after witnessing the Thiruvarur Thiruvathirai festival, met Thirugnanasambandha Peruman.

At that time, Thirugnanasambandha Peruman asked Appar Swamigal, “From where do you come?” Appar Swamigal then explained the glory of the Thiruvarur Thiruvathirai festival with a Padigam beginning with “Muthu Vidhanam.” Upon hearing this, Sri Thirugnanasambandha Peruman immediately came to Thiruvarur. Maha Abhishekam is performed for Thyagaraja Peruman on Thiruvathirai day. Aadi Pooram is a grand chariot festival for Kamalambal, lasting fifteen days, with a chariot procession. On Aadi Pooram, the Goddess offers Theertham, and that night, adorned in white garments, white sandalwood paste, and white flowers, She proceeds to the Parvatheeswarar temple to unite with the Lord, as per tradition. During these festivals, Patanjali and Vyagrapadar from the nearby Thiruvalamar Thalam come to receive Darshan for Uthirapada Darisanam and Margazhi Thiruvathirai Darisanam. Special Archana is performed for Thyagaraja Peruman on Somavaram and Sukravaram.

Two grand Archanas, Musukundarchanai and Mukundarchanai, are performed. These are unique and special Archanas to this Thalam.

Thus, no matter how much is said, the glories of Thiruvarur will continue to grow. Let us now move forward in our contemplation.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 27 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam called Thaduthatkondapuranam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrives at Thiruvarur. As Shiva Peruman had already instructed, all the devotees living in Thiruvarur welcome Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and lead him through the streets of Thiruvarur to the Thiruvarur Thiru-Kovil. Upon seeing the Gopuram and the sacred entrance of the Thiruvarur Thiru-Kovil, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal immediately prostrates himself. It is a rule of Shiva temple darshan to prostrate upon seeing the Gopuram, which is a Sthoola Lingam. This is because the Gopuram is one of the seven Lingams found in Shiva temples: Gopure Sikare Dware Prakare Balipitake Archake Moola Lingeccha Sapta Lingasiya Dar.

This is the Sivagama. In this verse, the seven lingams of the Shiva temples are enumerated. These seven lingams are the gopuram, the vimana called shikhara, the entrance of the temple, the prakaram, the balipeetam, the archaka, and the Moolalingam. These are the seven lingams of the Thiru-koyil.

Therefore, it is the rule of Shiva temple darshan that one should worship all seven upon seeing them.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal also follows this. Upon seeing the gopuram, which is a sthoola-lingam, he worships it. He also worships the entrance, which serves as the face of the Thiru-koyil located beneath the sthoola-lingam gopuram, as soon as he sees it. Sekkizhar Swamigal also describes how he worships.

First, he worships with anjali mudra, and then, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that he worships with ashtanga and panchanga namaskaram. That is, a namaskaram is a combination of ashtanga and panchanga. These two together are considered one namaskaram.

Similarly, the method of worship is to first perform ashtanga namaskaram and then panchanga namaskaram. Performing both in this manner constitutes one namaskaram.

Likewise, one should perform namaskaram three times, five times, and so on, according to the rule of Shiva temple darshan. The method of worshipping with ashtanga namaskaram is to prostrate with eight limbs touching the ground: one head, two hands, two ears, one forehead, and two feet. Panchanga namaskaram is when five limbs—one head, two hands, and two feet— touch the ground. It is also a rule that women should not perform ashtanga namaskaram. It is also a rule that one should not worship anyone other than the Lord in the Thiru-koyil. One must walk without stepping on the shadows of the stoopi and other structures.

Sekkizhar mentions that Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships here according to these and many other rules.

Thus, worshipping in the manner prescribed by the Sivagamas for temple worship is the proper way. Sekkizhar Swamigal illustrates throughout the Periya Puranam the history of devotees who worshipped without the slightest deviation from this method.

Now, after worshipping the Thiru-vayil and Thiru-gopuram in this manner, Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds to the Devasiriya Mandapam, where he prays and worships with devotion, his hands raised in reverence, He worships with folded hands. After worshipping the Devasiriya Mandapam, he enters the Thiru-koyil and reaches the prakaram surrounding the temple where Thiru-moolattananathar is enshrined in Thiru-varur. With his heart overflowing with love for the Peruman, tears welling up in his eyes, and his flesh and soul melting with devotion, he reaches the Thiru-maligai Pathi, the entrance to the Thiru-maligai, which is the mandapam surrounding Thiru-moolattanam, where Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, is enshrined. Thiru-moolattanam is the place where the Lord who resides in the anthill is enshrined. Thiru-moolattanam is a special sthalam found in Thiru-thillai and Thiru-varur. In Thillai, Thiru-moolanathar is enshrined to the north of the Sabha where Natarajar is enshrined.

The place where Thiru-moolattanam is located is different from the place where Thiyagesar is enshrined here, and also different from the Thiru-moolattanam where the Lord who resides in the anthill is enshrined.

Then, he goes inside and worships the Lord who resides in the anthill. Do you know who this Lord who resides in the anthill is? He is the ancient Lord who made the anthill His abode, the Peruman enshrined in the Poongoyil, the primordial God who is the support for all beings, and the one who holds Parvati in His half. Sekkizhar Swamigal praises Him in this manner. He is the ancient one who resides in the anthill, meaning He is so ancient that it is impossible to know when He first manifested in the anthill. He is the Supreme Being who is the support for everyone, meaning He is the great entity without whom all beings—humans, devas, and infernal beings—have no other support.

He is the great Being who holds Parvati Devi, who bestows grace upon all living beings, in His left half.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, melting with devotion, worships this Lord who resides in the anthill, who possesses such great glories. He melts, thinking that this Lord who resides in the anthill has bestowed upon him the great blessing of granting him the grace of His sacred feet.

Thus melting, he falls to the ground, and the Lord of Thiru-navaloor, who is skilled in good Tamil, truly feels that he has attained the good fruit that should be achieved by taking this body, and offers his salutations. This Lord who resides in the anthill is the same one who, in Kailash, previously granted Sundaramurthi Swamigal the grace to offer flowers and sacred ash as a close devotee, and to worship him.

Then, He blessed Sundaramurthi Swamigal to be born in the Adisaiva lineage, which serves Him generation after generation. Since the most excellent worship, overflowing with love, is song, He graciously commanded, “Sing Tamil songs for Us on earth,” and gave him the opening phrase “Piththa” (Madman), blessing Sundaramurthi Swamigal to sing the pathigam.

Later, in Thiru-thuraiyoor, He bestowed upon him the path of penance and the blessing of worshipping Lord Shiva. Following this path, Sundaramurthi Swamigal continued his sacred service, and the Lord also became the reason for him to sing the pathigam known as Thiru-thondathogai.

Therefore, the Lord who resides in the anthill is the Peruman who is the reason for the fulfillment of the purpose of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s sacred incarnation.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, realizing that this body’s The purpose of taking this body has been fulfilled today, he melts and sings and worships. Why did Sundaramurthi Swamigal incarnate here? “For the southern land, having performed great penance, to prosper, and to bestow the flawless Thiru-thondathogai.” This purpose of incarnation is now about to unfold in Thiru-varur.

Therefore, we have attained its nature, meaning we have attained the fruit of having this body. Thinking thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with his heart, remembers the Lord, with his words, praises the Lord, and with his body, worships the Lord, and stands here overflowing with grace.

Furthermore, generally, the nature of the body is to experience prarabdha and to perform Shiva dharmas to prevent further births. After experiencing the prarabdha we have brought, further akami should not accumulate.

Therefore, all efforts of this body should be directed towards Shiva dharma.

Thus, the nature of this body, the purpose of taking this body, is to remember Lord Shiva with the heart, praise Him with words, and worship Him with the body.

Therefore, generally, the purpose for which living beings take a body is fulfilled when they go to Thiru-varur and see Thiyagaraja Peruman, the Lord who resides in the anthill. This is the insight given to us by Sivakavimani.

Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman describes Sundaramurthi Swamigal standing with overflowing love, his heart melting and agitated, having first worshipped with ashtanga and panchanga namaskaram according to the prescribed method, and finally, as boundless love matured and intensified, his five pure senses united, He attained the sacred feet of the Lord, and immersed in that flood of bliss, he sang the sweet and powerful Tamil garland of songs. The meaning of this is that as love overflowed in his heart, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s heart melted and became agitated. He first worshipped Thiru-moolattananathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, with eight limbs, and then with five limbs, that is, first with ashtanga and then with panchanga, according to the rules. As his boundless desire to worship the Lord grew ever stronger, and his five most pure senses united, he attained the sacred feet of the Lord. Immersed in the flood of bliss that arose from this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, remaining steadfast in it, sang the sweet and melodious Thevara Thiru-pathigam, which is rich in meaning. When describing Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s state here, the Swamigal says that love first arose in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s mind, and then it grew and grew, maturing into boundless love.

Love is the affection between a hero and a heroine. Although it is generally called love, when this affection exists between a hero and a heroine, it is called ‘kadal’ (romantic love).

Similarly, it is called ‘natpu’ (friendship) between friends, and ‘vatsalyam’ (affection) between a mother and child, and so on, with different names.

Therefore, here Sundaramurthi Swamigal stands with boundless love matured. This love is the affection that arises between a hero and a heroine. In the previous verse, he worshipped with the general love for the Lord, referring to Him as Purana Pran, Param-porul, Bagan, Annal.

Now, as that love overflows, and his heart becomes entangled and agitated, and matures further, the nayaki-nayaka bhava (the sentiment of the beloved and the lover) emerges, where all souls are the nayakis and the Lord is their nayaka. As this nayaki-nayaka bhava matures in Sundaramurthi Swamigal, his five pure senses unite and experience bliss. According to the Thiru-kural, “The five senses—seeing, hearing, tasting, smelling, and touching—are united in the eye,” the bliss experienced when the five senses are united The bliss experienced when the five senses are united is this nayaki-nayaka bliss. This fivefold bliss, experienced by seeing with the eyes, hearing with the ears, tasting with the tongue, smelling with the nose, and touching with the body, here appears as a single bliss due to the nayaki-nayaka bhava that Sundaramurthi Swamigal has for Lord Shiva, bestowing upon him the bliss of Suddha-advaita.

Here, the object experienced by the five senses uniting as one is the Shiva Param-porul. It is eternal, immensely great, always benevolent, and imperishable.

Therefore, the bliss of experiencing this Shiva Param-porul is also of that nature. When the five senses follow the worldly path, they cause harm, but when they follow the path of the Lord, they bring good. This experience creates a flood of bliss for Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Unlike other lesser pleasures, this bliss is immeasurable in its greatness, and therefore Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to it as a flood. Experiencing this is beyond the reach of the karmendriyas, jnanendriyas, antahkaranas, and even the atma-bodha. It is only by singing from this state of pure advaitic union with the Lord that all the compositions of the revered saints like Sundaramurthi Swamigal become the very words of the Lord.

From this flood of bliss, Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings the Thevara Thiru-pathigam here.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, immersed in the flood of Sivananda, sings the Thevaram. While singing, so that living beings may worship Him and attain salvation, Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, who is the embodiment of the great Vedas and has taken the anthill as His abode, bestowed His grace upon Sundaramurthi Swamigal and addressed him, saying, “Sundara, We have given Ourself to you as a friend. On the day of the wedding when We previously intervened and took you as Our own, may you always retain the wedding attire you wore then, and may you play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled. May you live long.” Thus, Vanmeeganathar spoke these words. In this Thiru-varur, Vanmeeganathar thus bestowed His grace upon Sundaramurthi Swamigal by giving Himself as a friend.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal also, in many pathigams, sings, implying that he is the Lord’s He sings in many pathigams, implying that he is the Lord’s friend, saying, “My friend also.” Furthermore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal came to explain the path of friendship among the four paths of Saivism. That is, Saivism speaks of four paths to attain the Lord: Dasa Marga, Satputra Marga, Sakha Marga, and San Marga.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal came to explain the Sakha Marga, the path of friendship.

Similarly, Thiru-navukkarasar Swamigal explained the Dasa Marga, Thiru-gnanasambandha Peruman explained the Satputra Marga, and Manikkavasagar Swamigal explained the San Marga.

The Lord does not become accessible to living beings unless He gives Himself.

Therefore, Sekkizhar composes this, portraying the Lord as saying, “We have given Ourself.” The Lord, The Lord dwells with all beings as a friend, but beings do not perceive Him as such. However, here, Sundaramurthi Swamigal made this known, meaning that the Lord’s being a friend was made known for us to understand the greatness of Sundaramurthi Peruman and attain salvation.

Thus, since the Lord Himself became a friend, in the later events of Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s life, such as the marriage of Paravaiyar, the marriage of Sangiliyar, and resolving the quarrel with Paravaiyar, the Peruman acts as a friend and helps Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Thus, since the Lord Himself gave Himself as a friend to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, from that day onwards, all devotees called Sundaramurthi Swamigal “Thambiran Thozhar” (Friend of the Lord). Our reflection now resumes. 
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 28 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thaduthatkonda Puranam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with a melting heart, immersed in the flood of Sivananda, sings a pathigam before Thiru-varur Puththidam Konda Nathar. As he sings, Lord Shiva, known as Vanmeeganathar, speaks to Sundaramurthi Swamigal in the form of sound.

Lord Shiva graciously states that He has given Himself as a friend to Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Then, did not the Lord come as an old Brahmin and intervene to take Sundaramurthi Swamigal as His own? That is, at the moment when Sundaramurthi Swamigal was about to marry the daughter of Sadangavi Sivachariyar, during that wedding ritual, Sundaramurthi Swamigal was in his wedding attire. Did not the Lord intervene and take him as His own? He now blesses him, saying that he should always remain in that wedding attire.

Then, in this He also says, “Live and play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled.” In this, we reflected on the news that Lord Shiva, considering Sundaramurthi Swamigal as a friend, gave Himself as a friend to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Because of this, all devotees called Sundaramurthi Swamigal “Thambiran Thozhar” and celebrated him.

Then, at the moment when Lord Shiva came as an old Brahmin and intervened to take him as His own, He commands that Sundaramurthi Swamigal should henceforth live on this earth in the same wedding attire he wore then.

Thus, He says, “Remain forever in your wedding attire and play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled.” What does it mean to play until desires are fulfilled? Sivakavimani explains it thus: Sivakavimani explains the meaning thus: “Vettkai theera” (until desires are fulfilled) means to experience all the desires one has for Lord Shiva and then come to Kailash. How so? By going to many places suitable for Shiva worship, that is, by visiting many sthalams where Lord Shiva is enshrined on this earth, and fulfilling the insatiable desire to worship Lord Shiva in this earthly world, and by completing the desire to save all living beings in this world, He says, “Come.” This is what Sivakavimani says. This is what Lord Shiva says: “Come and play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled.” This desire, called “vettkai,” is the same desire that Manikkavasagar spoke of in his “Asai Paththu.” Furthermore, He says, “Come and play until your desires are fulfilled.” We have seen the meaning of ‘vettkai’ as explained by Sivakavimani.

Now, the Peruman says, ‘vilaiyadi vaa’ (come and play). ‘Vilaiyaduthal’ means to enjoy with delight. ‘Vilaiyaduthal’ itself implies joy, does it not?

Therefore, Sivakavimani explains that the Peruman says, ‘Do not suffer, thinking that birth on this earth is evil, but understand it as bliss, and enjoy and play, saying, “Even human birth is desirable on this great earth.”’ Thus, Lord Shiva bestows Himself as a friend to Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Secondly, He says that Sundaramurthi should always retain the same wedding attire that He, as an old Brahmin, had previously intervened in, in Sundarar’s wedding attire.

Thirdly, He says, ‘Come and live and play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled.’ He also says, “Come and live and play on this earth until your desires are fulfilled.” The Lord Shiva who said this is the Shiva who resides as Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, having taken the anthill as His abode, which is the great Veda. What is an anthill? It is the nature of an anthill to conceal and cover what is within it. Within this anthill, Lord Shiva resides here as Vanmeeganathar. Sekkizhar describes this anthill as the Vedas. Just as an anthill conceals and covers what is within it, so too do the Vedas contain Lord Shiva within them.

Furthermore, for those who do not understand the Vedas as the abode of the Lord, that is, for those who do not realize that the Supreme Being spoken of by the Vedas is Lord Shiva, Because it conceals and reveals that true entity, Lord Shiva, to them, the Vedas are called ‘Marai’ (concealment).

Therefore, Sekkizhar calls this anthill ‘Marai-puttru’ (concealed anthill). Thiru-navukkarasar Peruman also states this in the Pavanasam Kurunthogai Pathigam: “What if one recites the Vedas? What if one listens to the Shastras? What if one constantly practices many books of ethics? What if one studies the six Angas? There is no one without the Lord.” Furthermore, it is natural for a cobra’s gem to be inside an anthill.

Similarly, what resides here, taking the anthill as its abode, is the blue-throated gem (Lord Shiva).

Thus, Sekkizhar explains this anthill as the Vedas, comparing it to the Vedas and calling it the ‘Vedas as an anthill of concealment.’ Thus, Sekkizhar’s verse, which contains the Lord’s words, The following is Sekkizhar’s verse: “By the grace of the King who resides in the great Veda-anthill, a word arose: ‘We have given Ourself to you as a friend. The attire you wore on the day of the sacrifice when We previously took you as Our own, wear it always, and live long on earth, playing until your desires are fulfilled.’” Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings in verse. Having heard the sacred words of the Lord, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with great desire, worshipped the imperishable Lord who resides in the anthill and spoke thus: “O Peruman, the Brahmin who on that day prevented me from becoming a slave to the world and made me a slave to You, today, in the same way, has come to take me as His own.” O rare jewel enthroned in Thiruvarur! O Lord who holds Uma, whose eyes are like swords and fish, in one part of your being! Is it not by your grace that you, valuing even a dog-like me as something worthy, bestowed your lotus-like feet upon me then, and do so even now?

Thus he worshipped.

Now, the commentary by Sivakavimani on this hymn: Sundaramurthi Swamigal refers to the Lord as ‘He who is never diminished’.

The Lord is eternal, imperishable. However, according to Siddhanta, even souls are imperishable, meaning they are indestructible. Just as the Lord has no beginning or end, so too do souls have no beginning or end. Nevertheless, souls are embodied, and these bodies are perishable.

Therefore, it is metaphorically said that souls perish due to their association with the bodies they assume. But the Lord is imperishable, undiminished.

Hence, Sundaramurthi Swamigal refers to Him as ‘He who is never diminished’.

Now, Sundaramurthi Swamigal addresses the Lord as ‘rare jewel’, ‘precious gem’. It is natural for a snake’s gem to be in an anthill.

Therefore, he calls Lord Shiva, who resides in the anthill, a rare and great gem.

Now, in this verse, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to Uma as ‘the maiden with fish-like eyes’. This means that the eyes of Uma Devi are like swords and fish. Uma Devi’s eyes are first compared to a sword. The nature of a sword is to cut or sever. The sacred eyes of the Goddess Uma Devi sever the bonds that bind souls.

They separate and cut souls from their bonds.

Therefore, he compares Uma Devi’s eyes to a sword.

Now, the second simile is ‘fish-like eyes’. This means Uma Devi has eyes like fish. A fish incubates its eggs with its gaze, bringing the young within the eggs to maturity and releasing them.

Similarly, Uma Devi, with her gracious gaze, removes the fetters of souls and bestows upon them the bliss of liberation, and by causing them to roam in this bliss, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Uma Devi’s eyes as being like fish-eyes.

Thus, by possessing both the nature of a sword and the nature of fish, Sundaramurthi Swamigal refers to Uma Devi’s eyes as ‘sword-fish eyes’. Through the nature of the sword, the removal of bonds, and through the nature of the fish, the bestowal of Shiva’s grace, are the functions of Uma Devi’s eyes. That is, the two sacred eyes of the Goddess bestow the removal of bonds and the bliss of Shiva upon souls.

Therefore, the sword and the fish are used as similes. The Lord’s eyes, and previously, the Lord’s intervention at Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s wedding, which prevented it and took him into service, was like the sword’s nature of removing bonds.

Afterwards, the Lord gave Himself to Sundaramurthi Swamigal to experience, which was the fish’s nature of bestowing Shiva’s grace.

Thus, Sivakavimani writes that Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings in this manner.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal thus melts in remembrance of the great compassion of the Lord who bestowed grace upon him. This great compassion is the grace by which the Lord gave Himself as an inseparable friend to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. It is by contemplating our unworthiness and insignificance, and the greatness of the Lord’s compassion, that our love for Him overflows, leading us to salvation, writes Sivakavimani.

Now, Sundaramurthi Swamigal addresses the Lord, saying, ‘You considered even me, a dog, as worthy.’ That is, he says, ‘You valued even me, who is like a dog, as something worthy and bestowed your grace upon me.’ Similarly, in Thiruvachagam, Manikkavachagar Swamigal in many places says, ‘me, a dog.’ In the Kirthi Thiru Agaval, Manikkavachagar says, ‘me, a dog, who came to the beautiful common hall in Thillai, filled with goodness.’ In Thiru Poovalli, he says, ‘Our Peruman, who is more compassionate than a mother, made even us, who are lower than a dog, into something worthy.’ In Kuzhaitha Pathu, he says, ‘You, who are lower than a dog, graciously took me into your service.’ In Achcho Pathu, he says, ‘The Mother who made me ride in a palanquin.’ Thus, in many places in Thiruvachagam, Manikkavachagar Swamigal refers to himself as a dog and sings in wonder, remembering the Lord’s grace.

Similarly, Sundaramurthi Swamigal here sings to the Lord, saying, ‘You considered me, a dog, as worthy and took me into your service.’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings to the Lord. The nature of a dog is to prefer and eat inferior things even when many good things are available.

Similarly, souls, even with the knowledge given by the Lord, and the good scriptures like Vedas and Agamas given by the Lord, and good company, will continue to pursue worldly pleasures, which are evil associations. This is the nature of souls. It is the principle of the wise to compare this nature to a dog.

Now, secondly, a good quality of a dog is loyalty to its master. That is, recognizing its master and being loyal to him.

But souls do not recognize their master, the Lord. If they were to recognize the Lord as their master, they are not loyal to Him.

Therefore, the ability to recognize the master and the quality of being loyal to the master, which a dog possesses, are not found in souls. Hence, it is the tradition of the great ones to sing of souls as being lower than dogs.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal says, ‘You bestowed your sacred feet even upon me, who is like a dog.’ The sacred feet of the Lord are the embodiment of grace. The Lord’s power of grace is His sacred feet.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having praised the Lord many times, and having worshipped Vanmikanathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, many times, then proceeds with a majestic gait, like a victorious bull, filled with joy. This gait of the bull is the gait of the Rishabha, on which Lord Shiva is seated. The Rishabha on which Lord Shiva is seated is Dharma Nandi. This Rishabha, which is Dharma, fights and conquers those who do not approach it, and protects those who do, proceeding with a majestic, victorious gait. This Rishabha is called Dharma Nandi of Lord Shiva.

Similarly, Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds here with a victorious gait. This victorious and proud gait of Sundaramurthi Swamigal is an external manifestation born of the pride of having Lord Shiva as his eternal and inseparable close friend.

Afterwards, Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds from the shrine of Putridam Kondanathar to the shrine where Veethi Vidanga Peruman is enshrined. In Thiruvarur, it is customary to first worship the formless-form of the Lord residing in Thirumoolattanam, Putridam Konda Peruman, and then to worship Thyagaraja Peruman, who embodies a manifest form.

Furthermore, Siddhanta philosophy states that form emerges from the formless-form. This form of Thyagaraja Peruman is the Somaskanda Murtham, which is specially celebrated among the twenty-five forms of Lord Shiva. This Thyagaraja Peruman, as Somaskanda Peruman, will process through the streets and bestow grace upon devotees. When He processes through the streets, He will be mounted on the Rishabha, as the Lord who rides the bull.

Therefore, just as the Rishabha carries the Lord and walks, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who always carries the Lord within his heart, also walks with that Rishabha gait when he goes to worship this Peruman. Just as the Rishabha carries the Lord upon itself to do good for the living beings of the world, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, carrying the Lord within himself, walks to bestow grace upon us, the living beings. This is the explanation of the Rishabha gait adopted by Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

We shall continue with our reflection.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 29 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam’, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having worshipped Vanmikanathar and Thyagaraja Peruman in Thiruvarur, and by the divine command of Lord Shiva, always assumes the form of a bridegroom and worships the Lord by singing in ‘Van Tamil’. The Tamil sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is ‘Van Tamil’, meaning powerful Tamil. It is also called ‘Van Tamil’, which means generous Tamil.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s Tamil is generous, giving abundantly whatever is desired by those who desire it. In the later history of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, he regains his eyesight by singing the Thiru Pathigam ‘Meela Adimai’.

Similarly, he obtains gold by singing the Pathigam ‘Thammaiye Pugazhndhu’. He brings back to life a child swallowed by a crocodile.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s Tamil, known as Thevaram, is a generous ‘Van Tamil’ that bestows desired boons upon those who desire them.

Now, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, by the command of the Lord, appears in the guise of a bridegroom. That bridal guise is the sacred form that combines the glory of a king and the glory of a Vedic Brahmin. That is, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having grown up in a king’s palace, possessed the royal attire befitting a king, and having come from the Adisaiva lineage, possessed the Vedic sacred attire. He appeared in this form in the wedding pandal before, and the Lord commanded him to always maintain that form.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal always appears in the royal attire and the Adisaiva attire. Sacred ash, rudraksha, makara-kulai (earrings), sacred thread, etc., constitute the Adisaiva attire. Sandalwood paste, flower garlands, bead necklaces, golden silk, etc., constitute the royal attire.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal always appears in the bridal attire, combining these two forms, as commanded by the Lord. This form assumed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is a ‘Mei Valar Kolam’, meaning a form that fosters truth. This sacred form of Sundaramurthi Swamigal helps to cultivate devotion to Lord Shiva, the true essence, in the hearts of those who behold it. It is not like the bodily adornments made by others. The bodily adornments made by others all cast those who behold them into worldly pleasures.

But this royal form and Vedic form assumed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is a sacred form that reveals Lord Shiva, the true essence, to those who behold it.

Therefore, it is a true sacred form, a form that fosters truth. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who shines in this manner, as a ‘Tava Vendhar’ (King of Ascetics). He has the attire of an ascetic, meaning the Vedic attire, and the glory of a king.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal calls him ‘Tava Vendhar’. Asceticism is Shiva Puja itself.

Therefore, this ascetic form assumed by Sundarar, who came from the Adisaiva lineage that performs Shiva Puja, is an ascetic form.

Now, Sivakavimani explains the second meaning of the word ‘Tava Vendhar’ as follows: ‘Tava Vendhar’ If ‘Vendhar’ means king, then he is the king of all ascetics. Ascetics are those who always possess the consciousness of Lord Shiva, who bestows endless bliss.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal is the king among all these ascetics. He is the leader whom all ascetics revere as their family deity.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal praises Sundaramurthi Swamigal as ‘Tava Vendhar’. As an example, Sivakavimani writes that Perumizhalai Kurumbar, a great yogi, attained the Lord solely by worshipping Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Therefore, through such histories, the name ‘Tava Vendhar’ becomes clear to us, writes Sivakavimani. In this glorious city of Thiruvarur, Kamaliniyar, who was formerly one of the female companions of Uma Devi in Adi Kailash, has already incarnated and is living there. We have previously seen the history that Aninditai and Kamalini, two female companions of Uma Devi in Kailash, came to the Nandavanam, and that Sundaramurthi Swamigal, when he was Alala Sundarar in Thiru Kailash, set his mind on them.

Therefore, the Lord commanded all three of them to be born on earth. Among the two female companions of Uma Devi, Aninditai and Kamalini, Kamalini Devi has already incarnated here in Thiruvarur and is living there. It is very fitting that Kamaliniyar incarnates in Thiruvarur, which is Kamalalayam. The history of Aninditai Ammaiyar, the other of the two, living as Sangili Nachiyar in Thiru Otriyur, can be found in the Puranam of Eyar Kon Kalikkama Nayanar. Kamalini Nachiyar, one of the female companions of the Goddess Uma Devi, has incarnated here in Thiruvarur in the lineage of the Pathiyilalar, who are called Rudrakanikas, and is living with the sacred name Paravai Nachiyar.

Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal begins to narrate the history of Kamalini Nachiyar, who lived in Kailash, incarnating as Paravai Nachiyar in Thiruvarur and living there. Kamaliniyar, one of the female companions of Parvati Devi, incarnates in this glorious city of Thiruvarur, in the lineage of the Pathiyilalar, who are called the Rudrakanikas. Great Kamalini Nachiyar incarnates here like a rare gem that came unbidden, due to the great penance performed by this lineage of Rudrakanikas, who were blessed with great ascetic merit. Sekkizhar refers to this Rudrakanika lineage as the Pathiyilalar lineage. The Pathiyilalar lineage is a name found in inscriptions. This tradition of Rudrakanikas is different from other traditions of courtesans.

Rudrakanikas are a lineage blessed with the great fortune of performing sacred service in Shiva temples. The sacred service performed by Rudrakanikas in Shiva temples is found in the Kamika Agama, Acharya Lakshana Padalam, from verses two hundred and twenty-five to three hundred and fifty. The three Shivasaktis of the Lord are Iccha Shakti, Gnana Shakti, and Kriya Shakti. The Kamika Agama states that Rudrakanikas were born from the sound of Iccha Shakti’s anklets, Rudra Kannikas from the sound of Gnana Shakti’s anklets, and Rudra Dasis from the sound of Kriya Shakti’s anklets. Their characteristics, conduct, and the sacred services they are to perform in temples are described in detail in various places. Rudrakanikas are those who perform temple services such as sweeping the temple from the Maha Mandapam to the Garbhavarana Prakaram, anointing with sacred oil, decorating with five powders, remaining pure, holding lamps during puja times, singing, and dancing.

Among the three types of dance—Suddha, Misra, and Kevalam—only Suddha Nirtanam (pure dance) was prescribed for Rudrakanikas. It was also customary for them to be marked with a dot by the Acharya and to consider him as their lord.

Furthermore, it is stated that Rajakanikas, Gramakanikas, and Dasis were born from the sound of the anklets of Durga, Lakshmi, and Saraswati respectively, and their characteristics, duties, way of life, and lineage development are described in detail in various places. In Thiruvachagam, it is said, ‘We, your humble servants, who have you as our Lord, will bow to your devotees’ feet. We will be their companions. They are our husbands. We will serve them as slaves in whatever way they desire.’ This can be seen. Rudrakanikas are those who perform the service they owe to Paramasivan and live without a marital life.

One who has no lord of their own, that is, no master, is Rudramurthi.

Similarly, the Rudrakanika lineage is one that does not take a lord for themselves, but has Rudra Nayaki as their lord. In this glorious Rudrakanika lineage, Kamalini Nachiyar is born. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that she is born in this lineage when he describes her incarnation. By saying that Kamalini Nachiyar ‘appeared’ when describing her incarnation, it should be understood that she was not conceived in a mother’s womb and born, but rather incarnated as an Ayonija (one not born from a womb). Previously, when describing the incarnation of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, Sekkizhar Swamigal similarly states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal ‘appeared’ in the Adisaiva lineage.

Thus, for Kamalini Nachiyar, who incarnated in this way, many elders gathered and performed the sacred naming ceremony on the auspicious day of Thiruvathirai, giving her the name Paravai Nachiyar. Afterwards, all the relatives gathered and placed various protective charms on this small child to prevent harm from minor deities. They applied various tilakams on her forehead. Those who know the truth apply the sacred ash, which is a great protection superior to all these, on her forehead. ‘They desired no other protection, but placed the sacred ash on her long forehead and completed it,’ This is from the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. The name ‘Ratchai’ (protection) for sacred ash also explains this. In this stage, it is the tradition of Pillaitamil texts to compose a ‘Kappu Paruvam’ (protection chapter) where the mother and nurses invoke deities like Thirumal to protect the child.

Thus, at each stage of the child’s growth, festivals are held and beautifully celebrated. Those with abundant wisdom sing, invoking various deities to protect the child from the first to the third month. Up to the fifth month, the child lifts one leg, folds the other, places both hands on the ground, lifts its face upwards, and sways gently. As the child reaches this stage, relatives offer auspicious words, wishing for the child to utter sweet babble. This is known as the Sengkeerai stage. From the fifth month to the seventh month, it is the stage of lulling the child to sleep with lullabies, called the Thaalap Paruvam.

This later became commonly known as Thalaattu. The stage from the seventh month to the ninth month is the Sappani Paruvam. Paani refers to clapping with the hands, hence it is the Sappani Paruvam. In the ninth month, after the child begins to sit up, it naturally engages in playful activities like clapping and waving its hands.

Therefore, parents and others will clap their hands, beckoning the child to play. The child, in response, will clap its hands, making a ‘sap’ sound, hence it is the Sappani Paruvam. The fifth stage is the Mutthap Paruvam, occurring in the eleventh month of the child’s life. It is the stage of delighting in receiving kisses from the child’s flower-soft lips, after praising, cherishing, and holding the child on one’s lap. The sixth stage is the Varugai Paruvam, occurring in the thirteenth month of the child’s life. The Varugai Paruvam describes a beautiful event in Tamil culture, which is the act of welcoming.

It is the stage of joyfully and lovingly calling out ‘Come, come’ to the child who slowly toddles and stumbles towards one. The stage of calling out ‘Come, come’ with a joyful heart and a sweet voice to the child who slowly toddles and stumbles towards one is the Vaaraanai Paruvam, or Varugai Paruvam. The next, seventh stage is the Ambuli Paruvam, occurring in the fifteenth month. Ambuli refers to the moon. It is the stage of inviting the moon in the sky to come and play with the child. It is customary to invite the moon to play with the child during this stage, as the child, having learned to walk, greatly desires to go everywhere and see sights.

Until now, these stages are common to both male and female children.

Now, the stages exclusive to female children are Neeraadal Paruvam, Ammaanai Paruvam, and Oosal Paruvam. The Neeraadal Paruvam is the eighth stage for female children. This stage occurs in the seventeenth month of the child’s life. It is the stage of singing to the child, asking her to bathe in water bodies. The ninth stage is the Ammaanai Paruvam. This is the ninth stage for female children.

It is also called Kazhangu. The Ammaanai Paruvam is the stage of playing with small balls made for this purpose.

Furthermore, when three women gather, one asks a question, another explains it, and a third answers both, while playing with Ammaanai balls.

Thus, Ammaanai is a type of game played by women. The tenth stage for female children is the Oosal Paruvam. Oosal means swinging. It is the stage of asking the child to swing and enjoy. This is the Oosal Paruvam. Among the finest works of female Pillai Tamil literature is the Meenakshiyammai Pillai Tamil, composed by Kumarakuruparar Swamigal.

Similarly, Paravai Nachiyar’s parents and relatives have been celebrating and rejoicing in the appropriate festivals for each stage, from the time Paravai Nachiyar incarnated as a child.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 30 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthaatkonda Puranam,’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal resides in Thiru-varur, daily worshipping Vanmeeganathar and Thiyagaraja Peruman. At this same time, many years ago, in this very Thiru-varur, Kamalini Nachiyar, one of the two women with whom Sundarar’s mind had been captivated in Kailash, is born as Paravai Nachiyar and is growing up. Sekkizhar describes the beauty of Paravai Nachiyar’s growth after her sacred birth. Upon Nachiyar’s birth, elders gather and perform the naming ceremony.

Then, they perform the protective rituals. Afterwards, festivals and ceremonies are performed according to each stage of the child’s growth. Kamalini Nachiyar, who was born as a child, reaches the toddling stage.

Then, gradually, She grows. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes her growth through several verses.

Paravai Nachiyar’s growth is such that she is like a young female deer, or a young bud of a divine flower about to bloom, or an unripe young drop of fragrant honey, or a beautiful young coral creeper grown on the young coral branches in the ocean waves, or a young sprout first emerging from the moon’s rays, or the incomparable young bow with which Manmathan learns his art of war in his youth.

Furthermore, her sweet and beautiful nature grows and flourishes day by day, so much so that relatives and others seek to know her. She plays with soft Kalarcchi seeds.

The child then plays ball games, the game called Ammaanai, and games like Oonjal (swing).

Similarly, she plays musical games and grows to be skilled in music.

Thus, when Paravai Nachiyar sings about Uma Devi, all who hear it are filled with love, their bodies and souls uniting, as she sweetly sings songs about her beloved Uma Devi. Having grown through the stages of childhood up to five years, the lady then passes through the Paedhai stage and continues to grow. During this time of Paravai Nachiyar’s growth, she daily visits the Vanmeeganathar Thiru-kovil and regularly performs temple worship.

Similarly, one day, surrounded by her companions, with the fragrance of her hair filling all directions, she walks along the Thiru-varur street, her anklets jingling, to worship Putridam Konda Nathar, who is enshrined in the Thiru-varur Poongkoil. At this same time, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who has already worshipped Putridam Konda Nathar and is returning surrounded by his retinue, sees Paravaiyar face to face.

Thus, Paravai Nachiyar, who is going to worship Putridam Konda Nathar, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who has already worshipped Putridam Konda Nathar and is returning, see each other on the Thiru-varur street. Upon seeing Paravai Nachiyar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal wonders if this is the grace of Lord Shiva.

Thus, Paravai Nachiyar and Sundaramurthi Swamigal look at each other with their eyes. As stated in the Thirukkural, ‘When eyes meet eyes, what use are spoken words?’ Sekkizhar Swamigal, explaining this event, states that it occurred due to a great auspicious destiny. The divine grace of the Lord, as destiny, brings Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar together here. At the moment of their meeting, their minds become engaged.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who was always absorbed in divine contemplation and had never before directed his gaze of love towards anyone else, Upon seeing Paravai Nachiyar here, his mind was drawn to her. The reason for this is the divine grace of the Lord. Just as they had looked at each other by the grace of the Lord when they were previously picking flowers in the Nandavanam in Thiru-kailayam, that same divine grace brings them together again here. This meeting of the hero and heroine is a department of Akapporul (love poetry) called ‘Kaatchi’ in Tamil literature. Elders refer to this department as ‘Gnanartham Sarkuru Darisanam.’ Thus, seeing the smiling face of Paravai Nachiyar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal stopped his onward journey and stood there.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal stands. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes that between him, who stood thus, and the maiden Paravai Nachiyar, whom he saw, stood Kama (the god of love). By the divine grace of Lord Shiva, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s mind, which until then knew nothing but Him, was now, due to the Lord’s command in Thiru-kailayam to unite the two, also seen by Paravai Nachiyar. When Paravai Nachiyar thus looked directly at Sundaramurthi Swamigal, a new desire, which she had never experienced in this world, arose in the gentle-natured Paravai Nachiyar’s mind. This desire suppressed the four feminine qualities of modesty, fear, innocence, and aversion, which Paravai Nachiyar had hitherto upheld, And rose slightly above them.

Thus, Paravai Nachiyar, upon seeing Sundaramurthi Swamigal, looked at him and wondered, ‘Is this person standing before me Lord Murugan, who appears as a great effulgence? Is he the incomparable Manmathan? Is he one of the celestial beings adorned with unfading garlands? Is he one who has received the true grace of Lord Shiva with the reddish matted locks? Who is this person who has stirred my heart?’ Paravai Nachiyar had previously lived in Mount Kailash as an intimate companion of Goddess Parvati, singing only of her love.

Therefore, if someone standing before her could thus amaze and captivate her, who possessed such inner truth, then he must be one who has received the divine grace of Lord Shiva.

Paravai Nachiyar becomes certain that he must be one who has received the divine grace of Lord Shiva.

Thus, as the love Paravai Nachiyar felt for Sundaramurthi Swamigal filled her heart, the four feminine qualities of modesty and the like receded to one side. However, the divine devotion to worship Putridam Konda Nathar by going to Lord Shiva’s Thiru-kovil held her steadfast and guided her, and Paravai Nachiyar entered the Thiru-kovil. As Paravai Nachiyar entered the Thiru-kovil, the thought, ‘Who is this woman?’ arose in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s mind. Those nearby, sensing his thought, said, ‘Her name is Paravaiyar. She is difficult for even divine beings to approach.’ Having heard these details, Sundaramurthi Swamigal admired Paravai Nachiyar in his mind and also entered the Thiru-kovil. As Sundaramurthi Swamigal entered the Thiru-kovil, Paravai Nachiyar, who had already completed her worship inside the Thiru-kovil, exited through another entrance.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal entered the Thiru-kovil and searched for Paravai Nachiyar, who had stolen his heart. Unable to find Paravai Nachiyar, he was greatly distressed and returned to his sacred mansion. By then, evening had arrived. In this evening, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar, thinking of each other, are tormented by love. When composing an epic, it is a tradition of Tamil epics to use the hero and heroine to compose the epic and explain the concepts of Akapporul (love poetry).

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal, who began to compose the Periya Puranam as an epic, makes Sundaramurthi Swamigal the hero of this epic, and Paravai Nachiyar and Sangili Nachiyar the heroines, in writing this epic. According to Tamil epic grammar, matters related to Akapporul that occur between the hero and heroine must be written.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal, utilizing this situation where Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar are tormented by love, thinking of each other, constructs the epic grammar and sings.

That night, Paravai Nachiyar, in her mansion, asks her companions, ‘Who was that devotee I saw in front of the Thiyagaraja Peruman Thiru-kovil?’ Then the companions narrate the history of Sundaramurthi Swamigal and also explain his nature as the Thambiran Thozhar (friend of the Lord) of our Lord.

Thus, knowing that the one she saw was Thambiran Thozhar, a sacred devotee of the Lord, Paravai Nachiyar melts further in love and requests Thiyagaraja Peruman to alleviate her suffering.

Similarly, Sundaramurthi Swamigal requests the Lord to unite him with Paravai.

Therefore, to bestow grace upon both of them, Lord Shiva appears in the dreams of the devotees living in Thiru-varur and gives a divine command to arrange the marriage of Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar.

Similarly, Lord Shiva appears in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s dream and says, ‘Sundara, to make you happy, I have given Paravai to you as your wife.’ He further states that he has already informed all the devotees in Thiru-varur about this matter.

Similarly, Lord Shiva appears in Paravai Nachiyar’s dream and graciously states that her marriage with Sundaramurthi will soon take place.

Thus, Lord Shiva, by appearing in the dreams of the devotees living in Thiru-varur, issued a divine command to arrange the marriage of Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar.

Similarly, he speaks in Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s dream and in Paravai Nachiyar’s dream.

Therefore, all the devotees living in Thiru-varur unite and arrange the marriage of Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar.

After this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar live, meditating in their hearts on the lotus feet of Lord Shiva, who is their master.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal bestows many Tamil word-garlands, which are the Thevaram Thiru-pathigams.

Similarly, Paravai Nachiyar also performs sacred service. Both of them, together, engage in the sacred service of the Lord and live their lives.

Let us go on reflecting.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 31 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthaatkonda Puranam,’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal lives in Thiru-varur, having married Paravai Nachiyar. The sacred mansion where Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar live is a very beautiful mansion. It is surrounded by groves. It is surrounded by ponds filled with water. Flowers bloom in these ponds.

Nearby, there is a small platform-like structure. There, a cool pearl canopy, that is, a pearl pandal, is situated. While Sundaramurthi Swamigal lives with Paravai Nachiyar in such a magnificent mansion, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal practices Yoga. Daily, they reside in the Poongkoil in Thiru-varur. He goes to praise, worship, and sing Thevara Pathigams to Vanmeeganathar and Thiyagaraja Peruman, who are enshrined there.

Thus, even though all objects that cause worldly pleasures surround them, due to the continuity of their past austerities, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar live a life of profound yogic austerity.

Thus, even though they lived amidst worldly pleasures such as groves, ponds, small hills, platforms, and pearl canopies, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that the desire to go and worship Vanmeeganathar arose strongly in the Nambis (Sundarar).

Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that even when in a mansion that offers worldly enjoyments, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s mind was not attached to it, and the desire to worship the Lord was paramount. He writes that all living beings possess three powers: desire, knowledge, and action, known as Iccha Shakti, Gnana Shakti, and Kriya Shakti. While in this world, the actions of Shiva-gnanis, which combine desire, knowledge, and action, do not incline towards worldly things but towards Shiva. Shiva-gnanis do not perceive the world; their eyes remain open, yet they do not see the world but gaze inward. Bodies for living beings come as a result of their karma, as a consequence of their past actions.

Thus, this body we have obtained is a result of our previously accumulated karma. However, these bodies, once obtained, can further contribute to our karma.

Therefore, for other worldly beings, this body, which came as a result of Prarabdha (destiny), not only serves to experience Prarabdha but also becomes a means to accumulate further Agamiya (future karma). However, for Shiva-gnanis, this body serves only to exhaust the Prarabdha karma; this body is useful for that purpose. For Shiva-gnanis, this body will not be a tool to accumulate further Agamiya. At night, we need light to perform any action, so we light small lamps and perform actions with their light.

But during the day, if we light the same lamp in the sunlight and perform any actions, that sunlight Before the light, the glow of this small lamp is useless, and the light of that small lamp is not needed when the sun’s light fills the day.

Similarly, for the Shiva-gnanis, because they stand in the path of grace, which is Shiva’s command, their prarabdha experience, performed in the great effulgence of the Lord’s supreme grace, becomes inactive.

Here, the state of Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the same. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is also one who has been taken over by the Lord and revels in the Lord’s grace.

Therefore, even though he is amidst worldly things, he has no need for them and always lives with the memory of Thiru-Vanmeeganathar uppermost in his mind, practicing yoga. Shiva-gnanis are like cows, souls that are like cows, who truly understand the nature of the bonds of anava, karma, and maya, and do not fall under their sway, but rather see everything that happens as actions occurring through the grace of the Lord. Those who thus see everything as the Lord’s grace are the devotees who possess the quality of servitude to the Lord.

Therefore, these devotees and Shiva-gnanis, no matter what body they inhabit or what actions they perform, those actions do not bind them. Prarabdha karma and the primal being, Shiva, who brings about that prarabdha karma, in the presence of the Lord, in the sanctum of Shiva, will be exhausted as bodily experience for Shiva’s devotees, unlike for other worldly people. Unlike for others, this prarabdha will not accumulate further agamiya for Shiva’s devotees and will itself be exhausted.

Thus, the prarabdha karma brought by Shiva-gnanis will be like a vessel that contained asafoetida. That is, if we put asafoetida in a vessel and then remove it, the faint smell of asafoetida will subtly linger in that vessel for some time.

Similarly, for Shiva-gnanis who perform every action with the consciousness of ‘Shivoham,’ prarabdha karma and its results, such as the body and other mayeya, will come and affect them only as a subtle fragrance.

Thus, prarabdha will subtly come, affect, and then depart.

Therefore, further agamiya will not accrue to Shiva-gnanis. Light just as light dispels darkness, this ‘Shivoham’ consciousness dispels their agamiya.

Therefore, when we read the news that Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Paravai Nachiyar married and lived together, we should understand it to mean that they did not experience worldly things like other beings, but always worshipped the Lord, keeping Him in their minds, thoughts, and on their heads, engaging in the practice of yoga.

Similarly, later in the story of Sangili Nachiyar, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal falling in love with Sangili Nachiyar and marrying her should also be understood in the same sense and meaning, explains Sivakavimani.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who practiced yoga, blossoms the lotus feet of Shiva, his Lord, in his head and mind, and sings Thiru-Pathigams. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal places the lotus feet of the Lord, the Saranaravinda, on his head and makes them blossom in his mind, and sings Thiru-Pathigams.

Here, placing them on the head signifies the act of placing them on the head and contemplating. The Agamas prescribe this as ‘Shirasi Sam Chintayaga.’ Making them blossom in the mind, that is, the second act of making them blossom in the mind, means making that lotus fully blossom in the mind and benefiting from it.

Thus, making them blossom in the head and mind is an action, and as a result of that action of contemplating the Lord’s feet on the head and making them blossom in the mind, the Thevara Thiru-Pathigam, the Tamil garland, was bestowed upon us. The purpose of a flower blossoming is to become a garland, isn’t it?

Similarly, the purpose of Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal making the Lord’s feet, the red lotus, blossom in his head and mind, is to offer a garland of Thevara Pathigams made from those flowers.

Thus, by making the Saranaravinda flowers blossom in his mind and head, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s desire to worship the Lord arose. This worship occurs in a waking state, that is, in the Jagrata Avastha. Making them blossom occurs in dream states, that is, in Svapna and other Avasthas. The head, called Chenni, is said by the wise to be like a thousand-petaled lotus blooming upwards, and the mind is like a closed lotus.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who always visited the Thiru-Koyil and worshipped the Lord, prepared to set out to worship the Lord on this day as well. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes his appearance as he goes to worship the Lord. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, adorned in beautiful garments praised by the celestials, wearing a paste of kumkum and sandalwood, with beautiful coiled hair, and sparkling jeweled ornaments, shines with the supreme beauty of Indra’s wealth.

Thus, describing the appearance of Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who possessed wealth superior to Indra’s, subtly conveyed that he was to be praised by the celestials. All the Devas, including Indra, are always worthy of praise by devotees. Indra, Thirumal, Brahma, and other Devas are always waiting to praise devotees. We will later see the story of the white elephant, where they came with a white elephant, greeted Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and took him to Thiru-Kailash.

Similarly, the Devas who are always waiting to worship Shiva’s devotees still praise Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal today. This is because the radiance and beauty of Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as he goes to the Thiru-Koyil now is the reason for him to bestow the faultless Thiru-Thonda Thogai, which is his incarnate purpose.

Therefore, the Devas praise and extol him. The Devas waiting outside the Devasiriyan Mandapam, where Shiva’s devotees are assembled, are praising Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal because he is now singing for the benefit of these worldly beings and thereby for their own repose. In this song, Sekkizhar mentions ‘Suliyam’ as one of the ornaments worn by Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Suliyam is a special crown worn by men.

Similarly, the special crown worn by women is called Choodamani.

Thus, in this first verse, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes and sings about Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s ‘Indra Thiruvai,’ that is, his kingly splendor.

Next, he describes and sings about Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s Vedic appearance. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal holds a beautiful golden staff in his hands, and Kavacha Kundalas sparkle in his ears. He wears a sacred thread that drapes and sways on his holy chest. The holy ash that shines on his holy forehead also illuminates him. This Vedic appearance is more beautiful than his previous kingly appearance.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes it as the ‘true holy appearance.’ This Vedic holy appearance is imperishable and is associated with Shiva.

Therefore, it became the form of true prosperity. However, why does Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also wear the kingly appearance? It is because, by the Lord’s command, he must always appear in a wedding attire, and by that gracious command, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal appears in the kingly attire and form.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, adorned in both kingly and Vedic appearances, walks through the streets of Thiru-Arur. He walks through the Thiru-Veethis to reach the Poongoyil and worship the Lord who resides in the anthill and Thiyagaraja Peruman.

At that time, on both sides, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s retinue and crowds of women gather and praise Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. They praise Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as an elephant-like figure who appeared in Thiru-Navalur as the good fruit of Saiva religion’s penance. The Saiva religion attained Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as an Acharya through penance. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is one of the four Samayacharyas of the Saiva religion.

Furthermore, Saivism is not just a religion; it shines beyond religions. Thayumanavar states this as, “Having established Saivism as the foremost religion, and then the silent, harmonious equality that transcends religions.” This Thus, that great principle, that great doctrine which transcends religions and cannot be directly perceived by anyone, is praised as having taken a form for everyone to see, in the person of Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. An elephant is something that has nothing greater than itself. Siddhanta is also like that, having no doctrine superior to itself.

Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as ‘Navalur Vantha Saiva Nattrvakkaliru,’ meaning, ‘the male elephant that appeared in Thiru-Navalur as the good fruit of Saiva religion’s penance.’ Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, walking through the streets to the praise of all, reached the Thiru-Koyil of Thiyagaraja Peruman with the Vedic sages.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 32 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkonda Puranam,’ Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, residing in Thiru-Arur, one day walks through the streets of Thiru-Arur to go to the Thiru-Koyil where Thiru-Vanmeeganathar and Thiyagaraja Peruman are enshrined.

At that time, as his retinue and other men and women praise him, he reaches the Thiru-Koyil with a host of sages. He approaches the Devasiriyan Mandapam, which is in the sacred courtyard of the Thiru-Arur Thiru-Koyil, in front of the sacred entrance. In this Devasiriyan Mandapam, all the Devas stand outside. Inside this Devasiriyan Mandapam are assembled the most special Shiva devotees, great souls who have incarnated on this earth. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having had their darshan, proceeds, praising the Lord’s feet, with the thought, “When will be the day I become a servant to these great devotees?” Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had already become a devotee, a servant of Shiva Peruman. Just as he became a servant of Shiva Peruman, he enters, thinking in his mind, “When will be the day I become a servant to Shiva’s devotees?” and in this yearning for the Lord to grant him the grace of becoming a servant to Shiva’s devotees, he praises and worships those devotees.

Thus, with the love of “I will be a servant to these devotees” overflowing in his sacred heart, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bows and worships at the inner gopuram entrance, joining his hands above his head, and enters the Thiru-Koyil. Nambigal entered.

At that moment, Thiyagaraja Peruman, adorned with a Konrai garland, appeared before Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Seeing Thiyagaraja Peruman appear before him, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed to His sacred feet, placed them on his head, and sang songs. When the Lord appeared before him, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praised the Lord’s sacred feet. The Lord’s feet are those who are filled with this pervasiveness, the devotees. Because they always reside at these sacred feet of the Lord, the name ‘devotees’ came to be for them. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, seeing the Shiva devotees inside the Devasiriyan Mandapam, thought, “When will be the day I become a devotee to them?”

Having entered the Thiru-Koyil with this thought, the Lord showed him His sacred feet. That is, He showed Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal the sacred feet where the great devotees reside. Showing His feet in this way is to make known the nature of the devotees and the blessings they have received.

The Lord is one who grants what is desired to those who desire it.

Therefore, to Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who entered the Thiru-Koyil with the thought, “When will be the day I become a devotee to the devotees?” the Lord graciously showed His sacred feet.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having had the darshan of the Lord’s sacred feet, addressed the Lord as Bhoothanatha and praised the lotus feet of the Lord, the Bhoothanatha, eleven times, enumerating eleven special qualities of the Lord. He calls the Lord the Lord of the Bhoothas, that is, the chief of the Shiva Bhootha Ganas. ‘Bhootha Pathaye Namaha’ is a Shiva Ashtothram. Being surrounded by Bhoothas and performing the dance is unique to Shiva Peruman. Sekkizhar Swamigal described in the section ‘Thirumalai Sirappu’ that the Lord resides on Mount Kailash, surrounded by crores of small Bhoothas. These are the Bhoothas, Shiva Ganas. Their number is crores, their forms are crores, and their strength and bliss are crores.

These Bhoothas perform wonders by the Lord’s gracious command. These Bhoothas branded the Rishabha Mudra and Shoola Mudra on Thiru-Navukkarasu Swamigal’s shoulder. These Bhoothas gave Thiru-Gnanasambandha Peruman the measuring bag containing a thousand gold coins. These Bhoothas are thus capable of performing wonders by the Lord’s will. Although they appear as small Bhoothas in sight, they are very great in their power. On Mount Kailash, from small Bhoothas to great Bhoothas, they stand surrounding the Lord and worshipping Him.

These Bhoothas are capable of creation and destruction. This world is subject to creation by the combination of the five elements and destruction by the excess or deficiency of each of them.

Therefore, worldly people, deluded by these five elements, worship them and perform rituals. But Sekkizhar Swamigal says that these Bhoothas are capable of creating and destroying even these five elements.

Thus, Shiva Peruman is surrounded by the glorious Shiva Ganas, the Shiva Bhoothas, and is their Lord.

Therefore, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal calls Him Bhoothanayaka, Bhoothanatha.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal addresses the Lord as Bhoothanatha and praises His lotus feet eleven times, enumerating eleven special qualities. The Shiva Agama tradition is to praise the Lord with eleven mantras in total, consisting of the Pancha Brahma Mantras and the Shadanga Mantras. Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord’s sacred feet, that is, the sacred feet where the devotees reside, eleven times, using these eleven mantras as symbols. As an explanation of these eleven mantras, the Thiru-Thonda The eleven songs of the Thondakai unfolded.

Therefore, because Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praised the Lord’s sacred feet, the sacred feet where the Lord’s devotees reside, eleven times, the Lord granted him the grace to sing the Thiru-Thondathogai, a Pathigam consisting of eleven songs, praising the devotees.

Now, let us contemplate those eleven praises, those eleven aspects with which Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord’s sacred feet. The first special quality is that the Lord’s sacred feet become the good, great, supreme bliss, like honey, that flows in the red lotus hearts of those who remember Him with love.

Thus, he first praises that this supreme bliss, like honey, appears before even me, who is utterly unworthy of receiving it. The second praise is that these sacred feet perform the great dance of the five actions in the sacred hall for the salvation of this world. This is the second praise. The third special quality is that it was these sacred feet of the Lord that became angry and took away Yama’s life. This is the third special quality that Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings. The fourth special quality is that these sacred feet, which are so glorious, become red when Umaiyammaiyar, adorned with flowers, caresses them with her red hands.

This is the fourth special quality he mentions.

Now, the fifth special quality is that these sacred feet shine brightly in the minds of those who practice penance and stand in righteousness. The sixth is that these sacred feet enlighten even those who do not know them and correct them. The seventh is that these sacred feet are the light of all lights, giving light to them and rising above them to shine as the supreme light. The eighth is that these are the sacred feet that Thirumal could not see. The ninth special quality is that these sacred feet stand on the elephant of the Vedas. The tenth special quality is that these sacred feet forgave the mistakes I, who am ignorant, made and took me as their own.

The eleventh special quality is that these sacred feet stand ready to grant grace to remove all the faults I may commit in the future.

Thus, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord’s sacred feet eleven times. For these, a wonderful Sivakavimani Ayyavargal provides a pure commentary. Let us take up these eleven special qualities through his commentary and benefit from them. This is because these eleven praises of the Lord’s sacred feet by Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal later became the reason for the composition of the Thiru-Thondai Thogai, which has eleven songs. In that Thiru-Thondathogai, Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings about the stories of sixty-three Shiva devotees and glorifies nine collective devotees. Sekkizhar sings the Periya Puranam as an elaborate work based on that Thiru-Thondathogai.

Therefore, let us systematically contemplate these eleven praises of the Lord’s sacred feet by Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and attain Shiva’s blessings. Among these, the first special quality praised by Thiru-Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is that the Lord’s sacred feet blossom as a red lotus in the minds of devotees. He states that the good, great honey of supreme bliss, which is steeped in the lotus, is surrounded by the bees of the Vedas. The Thiru-feet of the Lord are that supreme bliss-honey, and this supreme bliss-honey resides in the lotus-like minds of the devotees.

Thus, the bees of the Vedas surround this lotus, the minds of the devotees, to partake of the supreme bliss-honey, which are the Thiru-feet of the Lord. This honey of supreme bliss is the Thiru-feet of the Lord. Supreme bliss is that joy which has no equal, no higher, and no superior.

Furthermore, supreme bliss is eternal joy.

Thus, That which has no equal or superior is supreme bliss; it is the eternal manifestation of Shivathuvam. To deeply understand this manifestation of Shivathuvam is called experience. Those who possess such experience are the devotees of Shiva.

Moreover, in the Vedas, words like Vishnu-anandam, Agni-anandam, and Indra-anandam, associating the names of other deities, are not heard. However, Shiv-anandam is heard.

Therefore, this great honey of Shiv-anandam, which is supreme bliss, resides in the red lotus-like minds of the devotees. That honey, which is the Thiru-feet of the Lord, that supreme bliss, which is the Thiru-feet of the Lord, shines in the red lotus-like minds of the devotees. As devotees always keep the Lord’s Thiru-feet in their minds, that Thiru-foot, mingled in the mind, overflows with bliss as devotion matures. When the Lord reveals such Thiru-feet, Nambigal praises and worships them.

Now, the bees of the Vedas surround the Chinthamarai, the minds of the devotees, where this honey, that is, the honey of the Lord’s Thiru-feet, shines. What is the specialty of these Vedas? Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Vedas as “Man Perum Thiru Mamari” (great sacred Veda of the earth). Mamari means a great Veda, a profound Veda, because it encompasses all things within itself. Thiru Mamari, that is, such great Vedas, Mamari, The Vedas are Thiru Mamari. Because they embody Thiru, the ultimate reality of Shiva, these Vedas are Mamari, Thiru Mamari.

Since these Vedas are eternal, they become Man Perum Thiru Mamari. The meaning of Thirumari is the great Veda that contains Shiva Peruman, the Lord, as its ultimate reality.

Furthermore, the word Thiru also means exalted wealth. Other forms of wealth mentioned in other scriptures are impermanent and therefore insignificant.

However, the Thiru mentioned in the Mamari, the Vedas, which is Shiva Peruman, is eternal and glorious.

Therefore, this Veda is Thiru Mamari. It is natural for bees to surround a flower, Therefore, these bees, which are the Vedas, also surround the lotus-like minds of the devotees. Within that lotus, the lotus-like minds of the devotees, the supreme bliss-honey of Shivathuvam shines.

Thus, with the Shivathuvam-honey of supreme bliss residing within the red lotus of the devotees, the bees of the Vedas hum around that red lotus.

Furthermore, the two-syllable word ‘Shiva’ shines in the heart-place of the Yajur Veda.

Therefore, the bees of the Vedas, encompassing this Shivathuvam within themselves, shine around it.

Thus, the Thiru-feet of the Lord, which are the honey of supreme bliss shining within the red lotus-like minds of the devotees, always surrounded by the bees of the Vedas, now grant a direct vision to Sundaramurthy Swamigal.

Therefore, he first praises the Thiru-feet, Thus he speaks and worships.

Secondly, he describes the Thiru-feet as those that danced for the salvation of the world. We shall go on reflecting.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 33 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the Periya Puranam, in the section called Thaduthatkonda Puranam, Sundaramurthy Swamigal stands before the Devasiriya Mandapam in Thiruvarur. With the thought that he too would become a devotee of the Shiva devotees enshrined within that Devasiriya Mandapam, he enters the Thiru-koyil.

There, Thiyagaraja Peruman grants a divine vision to Sundaramurthy Swamigal. Sundaramurthy Swamigal then offers eleven praises to the sacred feet of the Lord who granted this vision.
