In the first praise, he extols the Lord’s Thiru-foot as the honey within the Chinthamarai flower, the minds of the devotees, which is surrounded by the bees of the Vedas.

Next, in the second praise, he states that the Lord’s Thiru-foot is the dancing Thiru-foot that moves for the salvation of this world.

The Lord performs five great functions—creation, preservation, destruction, concealment, and grace—so that all beings in the world may attain salvation. These five functions performed by the Lord are referred to by the wise as the Lord’s dance of grace. The world, or Gnanam, is of two types: sentient and insentient. Sentient beings are living, conscious entities, while insentient beings are unconscious, inert objects. Among these, the inert objects are for the experience of the sentient beings, which are conscious entities.

Therefore, even when it is said that the world attains salvation, it should be understood that the Lord performs His sacred dance for the salvation of the sentient beings, the souls in the world. Such souls are bound by impurities from time immemorial, namely the three impurities of Anavam, Kanmam, and Mayai. They are bound by these impurities.

The elders refer to this obscuration by impurities as darkness. This darkness is very powerful. These impurities, which are the bonds of darkness, do not leave the souls on their own. The souls bound by these impurities are not capable of removing them on their own.

Therefore, there must be one who is different from these two, free from impurities, and all-powerful. But He does not need a form or action for Himself.

Therefore, if He takes on a form and action, it must be for the salvation of beings.

Thus, Shiva Peruman, who is eternally free from impurities, assumes a form and performs actions to save beings. For the souls thus bound by bonds and lying dormant, To remove the bonds that cling to them, they need instruments and faculties. For these, a body must be given. For that body to exist, worlds must be provided, and for experiencing pleasures within those worlds, enjoyments must be given. The creation and provision of these from the primordial causal substance, Moolamayai, is called Srishti. Causing them to remain stable is Sthithi, or preservation, which is a functional name.

Thus, when beings repeatedly experience sorrows and joys, when they experience enjoyments, the third function, Samharam, is to withdraw them for their rest. To act according to the three impurities and perform their functions is the power of concealment, the fourth function, Thirodhanam. To unite souls freed from impurities with His feet is called Arul, or Anugraham, This is the fifth function.

Thus, the Lord performs these five functions—creation (Srishti), preservation (Sthithi), destruction (Samharam), concealment (Thirodhanam), and grace (Anugraham)—merely by His will, by a mere thought. This is referred to as the dance of grace or the play of grace. Sundaramurthy Swamigal refers to this as the Thiru-foot that dances for the salvation of the world. Just as lotuses in ponds bloom merely by the presence of the sun, remain bloomed, and then close, these five functions occur merely by the Lord’s presence.

Therefore, he speaks of the ‘danced Thiru-foot.’ Dancing is an action, the Lord’s action.

Thus, dancing is an action. The feet are necessary for the act of dancing.

Therefore, Sundaramurthy Swamigal specifically refers to the feet as the ‘danced feet.’ Now, the Lord’s feet are Shiva-Shakti. Although Shiva-Shakti are one, due to the functional differences of knowledge and action, they are explained as Gnana-Shakti and Kriya-Shakti, which are the Lord’s feet. Sundaramurthy Swamigal describes these feet as the ‘danced feet.’ Thus, Sundaramurthy Swamigal states that the second greatness of the Thiru-foot is that it is the Thiru-foot that bestows the five great functions as dances for the salvation of all beings in the world. The third greatness of the Lord’s Thiru-foot that Sundaramurthy Swamigal praises is that it angered Yama to the point of taking his life, meaning he praises the Thiru-foot that performed the act of Kala-Samharam. Why is Yama called the ‘Lord of Time’ (Kalan)? He is the one who, according to the Lord’s command, calculates the time of each individual and approaches them accordingly.

Hence, Yama is called Kalan. This Kalan was kicked by the Lord’s Thiru-foot. We can see this in the history of Markandeya. The thirty-six Tattvas are categorized as five Shiva Tattvas, seven Vidya Tattvas, and twenty-four Atma Tattvas. The corresponding worlds are also arranged accordingly. Among these, the Tattva of Time is the first of the seven Vidya Tattvas.

However, the devotees of the Lord transcend this Tattva of Time. Markandeya Muni, a devotee of the Lord, who had transcended the Tattva of Time, approached him, exceeding his authority. He exceeded his authority and approached him.

Therefore, he was kicked by the Lord’s feet.

Thus, the Lord’s Thiru-feet, by performing Kala-Samharam, angered Yama to the point of taking his life. The Lord’s Thiru-feet are two, thus signifying the two qualities of grace: anger and blessing.

Now, Sundaramurthy Swamigal, beginning to describe the fourth greatness of the Lord’s Thiru-foot, states that the Lord’s Thiru-feet are reddened because Umaiyamaiyar lovingly caressed them with her red hands. Umaiyamaiyar gently strokes the Lord’s Thiru-feet. To stroke gently means to stroke in the proper manner, according to custom. The wise say that this is also a form of worship. The Nayagan’s (Lord’s) Caressing the Thiru-feet is an essential act of the Nayagi (consort).

Therefore, Umaiyamaiyar touches the Lord’s Thiru-feet, and as a result, those Thiru-feet become red. Sundaramurthy Swamigal thus sings of such Thiru-feet as the fourth greatness. As the fifth greatness, Sundaramurthy Swamigal describes these Thiru-feet as those that shine brightly in the minds of ascetics who abide in righteousness.

Here, righteousness refers to the principles of the Agamas, and asceticism refers to Shiva Puja.

Therefore, the meaning is that the Thiru-feet of Shiva Peruman shine brightly in the minds of those who perform worship according to the Shiva Agamas, which are the sacred words of Shiva Peruman. Although the Lord is omnipresent, this indicates that He resides distinctly in the minds of Shiva Gnaniyar.

Now, as the sixth praise of the Lord’s Thiru-foot, Sundaramurthy Swamigal sings that it transforms those who are ignorant. To be ignorant means to be unaware of the Lord. ‘Vith’ means to know.

Therefore, ‘Vedhiyathavar’ means those who do not know the Lord. These are ignorant people, and therefore, they lack the words and actions to attain the Lord, unlike those who know Him.

Therefore, it is the Lord’s gracious act to make them know. Sekkizhar refers to this gracious act as ‘Vedhippana’ (that which transforms). ‘Vedhiyathavar’ refers to the ignorant, and ‘Vedhippana’ refers to bestowing grace.

Therefore, describing the Thiru-foot as ‘Vedhiyathavar Thamai Vedhippana’ signifies the gracious act of making the ignorant know the Lord.

Furthermore, Another meaning here is that the Lord is the one who refines and elevates those who are deeply immersed in impurity in the Kevala state, bringing them into the Sakala state, and then further refining them to the Suddha state.

The Lord shines forth to those who know and worship Him. To those who live without knowing the Lord, He grants grace by making them aware from within.

Therefore, these two functions are attributed to the two sacred feet. We can understand this in the section where the Lord speaks to the Mother in the chapter on the removal of great sins in the Lord’s sacred play.

Thus, Sundaramurthy Swamigal praises the Lord’s Thiru-feet as the sixth greatness, stating that they perform the gracious act of making the ignorant know. The Thiru-feet are the light of lights, rising as a flame. Generally, the sun, moon, and fire are luminous objects, sources of light. The sun and moon are mentioned, and all other stars should be understood as being included within fire.

Thus, there are generally three lights: the sun, moon, and fire. This fire includes all other stars and all other luminous objects.

However, these do not give light by themselves.

The Lord is the one who stands within them and gives light. But the Lord Himself is self-luminous; He does not need anything else to shine.

Therefore, He is called Swayam Jyothi, Paranjyothi.

Thus, for all luminous objects, The Lord is the one who stands within all things and gives light to those things. It is the conviction of the Shiva Agamas to conceive of Him as luminous, as having a form of light. In the Shiva Puja ritual, the method of worshipping Sadashiva Murthy, who is the form of light shining in the center of the solar orb during Surya Puja, should be learned from Shaiva Acharyas.

Furthermore, the three impurities are inert.

Therefore, having been freed from them, the soul becomes one with the light of Atma Gnana.

The Lord is the one who directs the light of the soul from within this light of the soul.

Therefore, He shines as the light of lights. And it is the Lord’s Thiru-feet that shine in this way.

Thus, Sundaramurthy Swamigal praises the Lord as the light that rises within lights, as the seventh greatness.

Now, as the eighth, As the eighth greatness, he praises the Lord’s Thiru-foot as the Thiru-foot that even Thirumal could not find. Thirumal, in the form of a boar, went in search of the Lord’s Thiru-foot, but it remained elusive even to him.

Next, as the ninth greatness, Sundaramurthy Swamigal states that the Lord’s Thiru-feet stand upon the elephant of the Vedas. The Lord’s Thiru-feet stand upon the elephant of the Vedas. Sekkizhar Swamigal mentions this in the hymn as “Vedhavaranam Yer Kondirundhana.” He says “Vedhavaranam,” which can be split as “Veda as elephant” or “Veda as horse,” since “Varanam” can mean both elephant and horse.

Therefore, by splitting “Vedhavaranam” as “Veda as elephant” and “Veda as horse,” it can be understood that the Lord’s Thiru-feet stand upon them. The Lord’s Thiru-feet stand upon them.

Furthermore, this “Vedhavaranam” can also be split as “Veda Aranam” and interpreted.

Thus, Sivakavimani offers three interpretations. Let’s examine the first interpretation, where “Vedhavaranam” means the elephant of the Vedas. The Vedas, like an elephant, bestow greatness and clarity.

Therefore, the Vedas are called an elephant.

Moreover, an elephant is the embodiment of Pranava, and the Vedas are an expansion of Pranava.

Therefore, it is customary to compare the Vedas to an elephant.

Furthermore, the Vedas are practiced in the Nishada note, and the sound of this Nishada note is the sound of an elephant, that is, the trumpeting sound made by an elephant.

Therefore, comparing the Vedas to an elephant, he speaks of the Thiru-foot that stands upon the elephant of the Vedas.

Now, for the second meaning, “Vedhavaranam” can be split as “Veda Aranam,” meaning the Aranyaka portions, which are the knowledge section of the Vedas. The Aranyakas of the Vedas are its knowledge section, the Upanishads. In the Vedas, there is first a section called Samhita. This Samhita section contains mantras.

Next, there is a section called Brahmana. This Brahmana section prescribes which Vedic rituals are to be performed and explains how they should be performed. When the mantras from the Veda Samhita are used in a Yajna, it is further explained in the Brahmana how certain words should be interpreted. It can be said that these serve as a guide for performing Yajnas and other rituals.

Thirdly, there is a section called Aranyaka. The essential meaning, or inner meaning, or Siddhantas, of what is in the mantras of the first part of the Veda, the Samhita, and in the rituals of the second part, the Brahmana, are explained in this third part, the Aranyaka. We can understand what is said in a hidden and allegorical way in the first two parts of the Vedas only through the Aranyaka. The Aranyakas emphasize investigating the inner meaning of performing a Yaga rather than just performing it. It is at the end of these Aranyakas that the Upanishads appear. If the Samhitas are the tree, then the Brahmanas are the flower, the Aranyakas are the unripe fruit, and the Upanishads are the final ripe fruit.

The Upanishads are what teach the path of knowledge. The Samhitas, Brahmanas, and Aranyakas exist to lead us on this path of knowledge. Based on these, the elders have divided the Vedas into two sections: Karma Kanda and Gnana Kanda.

Thus, the Thiru-feet of Shiva Peruman are praised by the Upanishads, which are the ultimate essence of the Vedas, the conclusion of the Vedas, the end of the Vedas, found in the final parts of the Aranyakas.

Therefore, for “Veda Aranam,” Sundarar praises the Thiru-feet that stand upon the Veda Aranas, implying that the Vedantic meaning described in the Upanishads, the knowledge section of the Vedas, refers to the Lord’s Thiru-feet. We understood the meaning of Vedavaranam as the elephant of the Vedas. We separated it as Veda Aranyam and understood it to mean the ultimate conclusion of the Upanishads found in the forest sections of the Vedas.

Now, we can interpret Vedavaranam as the horse of the Vedas, separating it as Veda Varanam, with Varanam meaning horse, and thus understanding it as the Lord who comes upon the horse of the Vedas. The history of the Lord coming as a horse-groom on the Vedic horse for Manikkavasagar is narrated in the Tiruvilaiyadal Puranam. For Manikkavasagar, Lord Shiva transformed all the foxes in the forest into horses and rode upon a chief horse himself. This horse he rode upon is the Vedic horse, as described in the Tiruvilaiyadal Puranam. This is found in the chapter where foxes were turned into horses. In it, the author of the Tiruvilaiyadal Puranam, Paranjothi Munivar, explains the Vedic horse.

A horse has four legs. The four legs of the Vedic horse are the four purusharthas: Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha. A horse has two ears. The Jnana Kanda and Karma Kanda of the Vedas are the two ears of the Vedic horse. Parajnana and Aparajnana shine as the two eyes of the Vedic horse. The face of the Vedic horse is the injunction (vidhi) that the Vedas declare, stating what should be done.

The tail of the Vedic horse is the prohibition (nishetha), which is the exception to the rule. This Vedic horse is adorned with ornaments made of Agamas. This Vedic horse is adorned with a garland of jingling bells (satangai malai) and a garland of small bells (kinkini malai) made of expanded mantras, and anklets (silambu) set with gems. The bridle of this Vedic horse is Pranava. All the cosmic eggs (anda kotigal) are stacked as a single mass (pindam) on the back of this Vedic horse. The seat upon which the Lord sits on the Vedic horse is the Upanishad.

The saddles of the horse are also the Upanishads. This Vedic horse has the power to fight against and defeat the external religions that stand against the Vaidika religions. When the Lord sits on this Vedic horse, the sun and moon serve as the two sides that support His holy feet.

Thus, the Lord, riding upon this pure Vedic horse, comes to untie the strong knot of our bondage (pasam). The Lord, as the supreme light (Paranjothi), rides on the horse to cast down our bonds. The practice of the Lord riding on a horse vehicle during temple festivals also signifies this meaning.

Thus, what Sundaramurthi Swamigal said, ‘holy feet standing on the Vedavaranam,’ is interpreted by Sivakavimani as ‘holy feet standing on the elephants of the Vedas,’ and ‘holy feet standing at the conclusion of the Upanishads in the Aranyaka sections of the Vedas,’ and ‘holy feet riding upon the Vedic horse.’ We shall continue our reflection.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 34 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkondapuranam,’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the Thiruvarur temple.

There, he meditates on the devotees of Shiva gathered in the Devasiriya Mandapam, contemplating, ‘When will be the day I become a devotee to them?’ With this thought, he enters the temple. At that moment, Thyagaraja Peruman appears before him and reveals His holy feet.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal praises those holy feet with eleven hymns. We have seen nine of these praises.

Now, as the tenth praise, Sundaramurthi Swamigal extols and worships the holy feet that pardoned his mistakes and took him as their own.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that these are the holy feet that pardoned his mistakes and took him into service. The detailed explanation of this can be found earlier in the history of Thirumalai Sirappu and the history of Thaduthatkondapuranam. As the eleventh hymn, he praises, “They consented to remove what is appropriate.” That is, Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that these are the holy feet that stood ready to remove all the faults he might commit in the future and to rectify those errors.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal says that those holy feet have already expressed their consent to forgive and absorb all future mistakes.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal states here, implying that not only did they forgive his mistakes, but they also consented to remove the mistakes of all beings.

Thus, the Lord’s holy feet are described as the supreme bliss-honey that flows in the red lotus hearts of devotees, surrounded by the bees of the Vedas; as the holy feet that dance for the salvation of the world; as the holy feet that angrily caused Yama’s life to depart; and as the holy feet that are gently caressed by Uma Devi, who comes with virtue. They are the holy feet that shine brightly in the minds of ascetics who stand firm in righteousness; the holy feet that enlighten and correct even the ignorant; the holy feet that are the light of all lights and transcend them all; the holy feet that even Thirumal could not see; the holy feet that stand upon the elephant of the Vedas; the holy feet that pardon one’s mistakes; and the holy feet that stand ready to remove all future transgressions.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal praises and sings.

In this way, the Lord’s devotees always sing eleven hymns to the ever-manifest holy feet of the Lord. These eleven hymns later expand into the eleven songs of the Thiruthondathogai. That, in turn, later expands into the Periya Puranam. To Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who thus praised, the Lord with the bull banner, who appeared before him, Lord Shiva bestows upon Sundaramurthi Swamigal the ability to become a servant to the devotees, just as he yearned and sang, ‘When will be the day I become a servant to the devotees?’ Lord Shiva now begins to narrate the glories of His devotees, granting the wisdom to understand the traditions of sacred service passed down through generations of steadfast and glorious devotees. The Lord’s devotees are ‘Mannu Seer Adiyavargal,’ meaning devotees of eternal fame. All other glories are not eternal, but the glories of the devotees are everlasting.

In the book ‘Sivaragasiyam,’ the Lord instructed Uma Devi, revealing that such devotees would appear. Lord Muruga then bestowed the same upon the sage Saikishavya. Pollapillaiyar bestowed their glories upon Nambiyandar Nambi. Upamanyu Munivar and all other sages, along with Nandi Devar and other Gananathas, have praised these devotees. Even today, many worship and live by these devotees. Such are the glorious devotees.

While Sundaramurthi Swamigal thus praises the Lord’s holy feet, Sekkizhar arranges for the Lord to speak of the devotees’ greatness in the following verse. That verse, which speaks of the devotees’ greatness, is as follows: ‘Perumaiyal Thammai Oppar Penalal Emmai Petraar Orumaiyal Ulagai Velvar Unam Mel Ondrum Illar Arumaiya Nilaiyil Nindrar Anbinal Inbam Maruvar Irumaiyum Kadandhu Nindrar Ivarai Nee Adaivayendru.’ This song is arranged for the Lord to speak of the greatness of His devotees.

The Lord says, ‘What kind of devotees are these? They are incomparable in their greatness. By their devotion, they have made Me their own. By their oneness with Me, they will conquer the world.

Therefore, they are free from any future imperfections. They stand in a state difficult for others to attain. Filled with love, they experience abundant joy. They have transcended both worlds.’ The Lord thus speaks of the devotees’ greatness to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, saying, ‘You shall join these devotees who possess such qualities.’ Sivakavimani explains the greatness of the devotees as spoken by the Lord, elucidating each line.

First, the Lord describes the devotees’ greatness as: “Perumaiyal Thammai Oppar,” meaning they are incomparable in their greatness. What is their greatness? What is their glory? They are capable of performing many deeds that are difficult for others to accomplish.

Therefore, according to the maxim of the Kural, “Periyar seyarariya seivar” (the great will do what is difficult to do), and according to the scripture Thirukalittrupadiyar, “Seytharkariya seyal palavum seythu silar eydarkariyathanai eydinargal” (some achieved the unachievable by performing many difficult deeds), these devotees perform many deeds that are difficult for others to do.

Therefore, they are incomparable in their greatness.

The Lord states that there is no one greater than them. We will see in great detail the extraordinary deeds performed by these devotees in the Periya Puranam. Not only that, but Sekkizhar has already described the extraordinary deeds of these devotees, starting with “Bhootham ainthum nilaiyil kalanga, Mathoru Bagar malarthal marappilar” (the five elements were disturbed, but the Lord with the female half did not forget His lotus feet), in the section on the glory of the holy assembly that we have already seen.

Later, he elaborates on this in the history of each devotee. These devotees are so great that there is no one else comparable to them. In their greatness, they are comparable only to themselves. The line ‘Perumaiyal Thammai Oppar’ means that there is no one equal to these devotees, as the Lord states.

The Lord alone is incomparable. The Thirukkural states, ‘Thanukku umay illathan thal serntharkallal manakkavalai maatral arithu’ (It is difficult to remove mental anguish except by joining the feet of Him who has no equal). Just as the Lord is incomparable, so too are the devotees who have attained the Lord. They are incomparable, being equal to themselves, and there is no one equal to or greater than them. These are the devotees of the Lord. Sivakavimani explains this as the meaning of the phrase ‘Perumaiyal Thammai Oppar.’ Next, the Lord says that the devotees ‘Penalal Emmai Petraar,’ meaning that by their devotion, these devotees have made the Lord their own. ‘Penuthal’ means to cherish, to care for, and to protect, just as a mother cherishes, praises, and protects her child.

Just as a mother constantly thinks of her child without interruption, so too do the devotees constantly think of the Lord without interruption. This is explained in the Karikkal Ammaiyar Puranam, where the Lord says of Karikkal Ammaiyar, We can understand this through the Lord’s statement, ‘This mother cherishes Us.’ We can see this in detail in the Karikkal Ammaiyar Puranam.

Thus, by cherishing the Lord, these devotees possess the Lord as their own rightful property.

The Lord also becomes subject to their love. Not only that, but those who wish to attain the Lord will first attain the Lord’s devotees. The Lord’s devotees guide those who approach them, and through this, all beings attain the Lord. We can see in detail in later histories the blessings attained by Nayanmars such as Siruthonda Nayanar, Appoodiyadigal, and Kulachiraiyar by worshipping and serving the Lord’s devotees.

Thirdly, the Lord says about the devotees that ‘Orumaiyal Ulagai Velvar,’ meaning they will conquer the world through their oneness. These devotees are united with the Lord through a sense of oneness.

Thus, by being united with the Lord in an Advaitic manner, they conquer the world. Since the devotees are always united with the Lord, this world does not affect them; it remains subservient to their grace.

Furthermore, this is the only way to be freed from the bonds of worldly attachments.

Therefore, ‘orumaiyal’ can also mean oneness.

Thus, these devotees will conquer the world through their oneness.

Next, the Lord states about the greatness of the devotees, ‘Unam Mel Ondrum Illar,’ meaning the devotees are without any blemish. It is common usage to refer to any defect in our body as a blemish. This is a bodily blemish.

But what is the blemish of the soul? The blemish of the soul is the body it has taken. That is, it is the birth it has taken. All other blemishes are confined to the body, but taking a body, taking birth, is the cause and origin of all blemishes.

Therefore, the blemish that comes to the soul is this birth of taking a body. But the Lord’s devotees are without any blemish. They have ensured that they do not take birth by always cherishing the Lord.

Therefore, the Lord’s devotees are without any blemish.

Next, they have attained a rare state, meaning they have attained the state of liberation (mukti) which is difficult for others to achieve.

Next, the Lord says that they ‘Anbinal Inbam Arvar,’ meaning they experience joy through love and remain steadfast in it. The love that these devotees have for the Lord is an imperishable, blissful love. Karikkal Ammaiyar also sings, desiring this imperishable, blissful love.

Next, the Lord describes the special quality of the devotees as having transcended both worlds (‘Irumaiyum Kadandhu Nindrar’).

Here, ‘irumai’ refers to this world (immai) and the next world (marumai). ‘Immai’ is birth in this world, and ‘marumai’ is the state of birth in heaven. In the state of being attached to the Lord, devotees do not experience the state of this world, and the pleasures of heaven are despised and rejected by them.

Therefore, they have transcended both worlds, meaning they have transcended the three categories of dharma, artha, and kama, and the heavens, and have attained liberation (apavarga).

Therefore, they stand transcending the two states of this world and the next. Manikkavasagar in Thiruvachagam says, ‘Kollen purantharan mal ayan vazhvu’ (I do not desire the life of Indra, Vishnu, or Brahma). He also says, ‘Uttrarai yan vendan ur vendan per vendan’ (I desire no relatives, no village, no name).

Thus, the Lord tells Sundaramurthi Swamigal about the greatness of the devotees: ‘They are incomparable in their greatness, they have obtained Me through their devotion, they will conquer the world through their oneness, they are without any blemish, they stand in a rare state, they experience joy through love, and they have transcended both worlds. You shall go and join such devotees.’ Thus, the Lord speaks to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, describing the greatness of the devotees and asking him to join them.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal then bows to the Lord, saying, ‘O Lord, I have attained and rested in the faultless path here.’ At that moment, the Lord graciously commands, ‘You shall worship them according to the rules and sing of these faultless and truthful devotees with a garland of perfect words.’ Then Sundaramurthi Swamigal, hearing the command of the Lord who had taken him into service, to sing the praises of the devotees, bows before the Lord and says, ‘O Lord, my Master who took me into service! As you have commanded, how shall I know how to sing and what qualities to praise in a garland of perfect words for the greatness of the devotees? What qualification do I possess for that sacred service? You yourself must bestow upon me the ability to sing a garland of perfect words for them.’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal pleads, ‘The Lord commanded me to sing of the devotees, but how can I sing of them? I am not qualified for that. You must bestow that ability upon me.’ The Lord continues to answer.

He said.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 35 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the section of Periya Puranam titled ‘Thaduthatkondapuranam,’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal, upon seeing the devotees of Shiva gathered in the Devasiriya Mandapam in Thiruvarur, yearns, ‘When will be the day I become a devotee to them?’ As he enters the temple, the Lord appears before him.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal praises the Lord’s holy feet eleven times. Afterwards, the Lord Himself speaks of the greatness of the devotees and graciously commands Sundaramurthi Swamigal, ‘Go and join them, and praise them with a garland of your words.’ Hearing this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal bows to the Lord and asks, ‘How can I sing of the greatness of these glorious devotees? What qualities of theirs shall I sing? I am not at all qualified to sing in such a way.

Therefore, you must bestow upon me the ability to sing of the devotees.’ Sundaramurthi Swamigal makes this plea. The Lord, who holds Uma Devi in one part of His body, the Lord who, to relieve the suffering of this world and save it, graciously revealed the Vedas through His holy words, addresses Sundaramurthi Swamigal and initiates him by saying, ‘Thillaival Andhanartham Adiyarkkum Adiyen’ (I am a servant to the devotees of the Brahmins of Thillai), and commands him to sing a garland of praise extolling the greatness of the devotees. In describing the Lord who thus initiated Sundaramurthi Swamigal, Sekkizhar here states that He is the Lord who has Uma, the pure one born in the Himalayas, as one part of His body. The implication here is that whenever the Lord comes to bestow grace upon beings, He appears with Uma Devi. Uma Devi, who is the embodiment of purity, is the gracious form of the Lord.

Therefore, the meaning of this verse is that the Lord, with compassion, bestowed grace.

Furthermore, this Thiruthondathogai, which is a patikam (decad of hymns), is a song that is bestowed as a mantra, as a light of gracious sound.

Therefore, the Lord, accompanied by Uma Devi, the power of grace, initiates that song.

Here, the Lord, who revealed the Vedas for the salvation of beings, now initiates Sundaramurthi Swamigal by saying, ‘Thillaival Andhanartham Adiyarkkum Adiyen.’ The fact that the Lord, with His holy words, the same words that spoke the Vedas, thus initiates, is to show that this Thiruthondathogai is also a Veda. Not only did the Lord, who revealed the Vedas, say this, but He also explains its greatness, showing that it is superior to the Vedas, praising the devotees, and directly stating and clarifying the characteristics of the Lord’s devotees. Just as the Lord saved the world by speaking the Vedas, so too does He speak this faultless Thiruthondathogai.

The Lord redeems souls. How do souls attain salvation by singing this Thiru-Thondath Thogai, which praises the Lord’s devotees? Souls will first receive the benefits of the good disposition of the devotees by singing and reciting this faultless Thiru-Thondath Thogai and joining their company. This Thiru-Thondath Thogai hymn will further protect souls from harm and prevent the defilement of latent impressions from affecting them.

Thus, the Lord performs this act of grace to bestow the Thiru-Thondath Thogai for the salvation of souls.

In this manner, the Lord gives the opening line, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen” (I am a servant to the servants of the Brahmins of Thillai). Thiyagaraja Peruman gives the opening line. Previously, when the Lord took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal into His service in Thiruvennainallur, He gave the opening line “Pithan” (Madman) to sing His glory. That is, He gave the opening line by uttering only the first word.

But here, the same Lord, to sing the glory of the devotees, graciously gives the first half of the line, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. When praising the Lord, He graciously gave only a single word as the opening.

But when singing about the devotees, He graciously gave a complete half-line. Through this, the Lord makes us understand the greatness of the devotees and the proper way to praise them.

Now, the Lord does not say, “I am a servant to the Brahmins of Thillai,” but repeatedly emphasizes, “I am a servant to the servants of the Brahmins of Thillai.” The Brahmins of Thillai are the three thousand sages of Thillai, who have Lord Nataraja as their chief. Their greatness can be found in the Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Puranam.

Thus, Thiyagaraja Peruman gave the opening line, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and instructed him to praise the Lord’s devotees. The inner meaning of this instruction is that the Lord revealed to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal the sacred names, histories, and glories of all the devotees. As the Lord thus revealed the sacred names and glories of the Thiru-devotees to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed and rose, and then Lord Shiva disappeared.

Afterwards, with the same grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approached the Devaasiriya Mandapam and joined the devotees gathered there. He repeatedly bowed and worshipped those devotees from a distance. Out of abundant love, he repeatedly prostrated and rose, then worshipped.

Then, as the Lord had already commanded, he began with “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen” and sang, naming each of the Lord’s sacred devotees individually, saying, “I am a servant to these, I am a servant to him, I am a servant to him.” In this way, he not only sang the sacred names of each individual devotee but also sang about the collective devotees. Collective devotees are those who belong to a group, a sacred assembly, in various categories. For example, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, “I am a servant to all those born in Thiruvarur.” This belongs to the category of collective devotees. For instance, the Lord’s giving the opening line, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” refers to collective devotees. That is, the collective devotees known as Thillai Vaazh Andhanar. The Thillai Vaazh Andhanars are the three thousand Brahmins residing in Thillai.

Therefore, they are a collective group, and the Lord gives the opening line, “I am a servant to them.” Similarly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, “I am a servant to all those born in Thiruvarur.” Here, he collectively sings about all the devotees born in Thiruvarur in this line.

Likewise, another example for collective devotees is, “I am a servant to all those who have set their minds on Shiva.” There are many devotees who have set their minds on Shiva.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal collectively refers to them as, “I am a servant to those who have set their minds on Shiva.” In this manner, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings about nine collective devotees in the Thiru-Thondath Thogai. We can see the details of these in the later parts of this Periya Puranam.

Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, taking the line “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” which the Lord gave as the opening, composed the sacred hymn known as Thiru-Thondath Thogai, a garland of Tamil. Since Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed this sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, based on the opening line given by Lord Thiyagaraja Peruman, this entire hymn is the sacred word of Lord Shiva, Thiyagaraja Peruman, sung with divine wisdom as a recorded utterance. It is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s intention that this Thiru-Thondath Thogai should be bestowed by him, and through it, this Thiru-Thondath Thogai should be available to all of us, living beings, and that all of us, living beings, should sing, learn, and understand this Thiru-Thondath Thogai sacred hymn and attain liberation. This is the sacred purpose and objective of his divine incarnation.

Thus, our leader, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in accordance with the sacred verse “Adiyen Un Adiyaar Naduval Irukkum Arulai Puriyai” (Grant me the grace to be among Your devotees), prayed and, by the grace of the Lord, understood the nature of the devotees. He then sang and worshipped this faultless Thiru-Thondath Thogai, and by the Lord’s grace, he joined the company of those devotees and became one of them. Saiva Siddhanta states that joining the company of devotees is the ultimate supreme bliss that souls can attain. The ultimate state of Shiva-attainment, as described by Saiva Siddhanta, is indeed the blessing of joining the company of devotees. The details of this can be found in scriptures such as Sivagnana Siddhiyar. This sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, is a sacred hymn jointly uttered by Lord Shiva, the Thambiran, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Thambiran Thozhar (friend of the Lord).

In this hymn, Lord Shiva, the Thambiran, gave the opening line, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” and the inspiration to sing it was bestowed upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal by Lord Shiva.

Thus, the Tamil of this sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, was uttered in a state where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and the Lord were merged in non-duality. This uniqueness is not found in any other language. Taking this highly esteemed sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, as his primary source, Sekkizhar Swamigal composed the Periya Puranam. This sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, contains a total of eleven verses. Sekkizhar Swamigal expanded these eleven sacred verses into eleven sections of the Periya Puranam. He expands and sings.

Thus, as a mark of singing the eleven sacred verses of this Thiru-Thondath Thogai sacred hymn as eleven cantos, Sekkizhar Swamigal names each of the eleven cantos after the first phrase of the eleven sacred verses of this Thiru-Thondath Thogai sacred hymn. For example, “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen” is the first phrase of the first sacred verse. Sekkizhar Swamigal expands this into a canto called “Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam.” Similarly, he expands the eleven verses of this hymn into eleven cantos, from “Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam” to “Manniyasir Sarukkam.” Along with these eleven cantos, he composes the “Thirumalai Sarukkam” as the first canto, containing all the events that led to the creation of this Puranam. He sings it as “Thirumalai Sarukkam.” Then, at the end, he sings the Puranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaching Thiru-Kailash as the thirteenth canto, “Vellaanai Sarukkam.” Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal composed this Periya Puranam with thirteen cantos: eleven cantos corresponding to the eleven verses of this sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, with the Thirumalai Sarukkam as the first canto and the Vellaanai Sarukkam as the final canto. Not only that, Sekkizhar Swamigal has arranged the histories of the devotees in the same order as they are mentioned in the sacred verses of this Thiru-Thondath Thogai.

In this way, Sekkizhar Swamigal explains the sacred history of the origin of this sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, in the first canto, the Thirumalai Sarukkam. Having explained this sacred history, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that he attempts to praise the greatness of the devotees mentioned by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in this sacred hymn, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Thambiran Thozhar, by elaborately describing the history of each devotee’s sacred service and their individual characteristics in the same order. Sekkizhar Swamigal then proceeds to elaborately sing the history of each devotee in the Periya Puranam. We too, with the grace of the Lord and the grace of the devotees, can continue to contemplate the sacred history and greatness of each devotee in Sekkizhar Swamigal’s Periya Puranam.

Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal concludes the Thaduthaatkonda Puranam, which is the concluding part of the Thirumalai Sarukkam, the first canto of the Periya Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal concludes. We too, now, by concluding this section, the Thaduthaatkonda Puranam, which is the concluding part of the Thirumalai Sarukkam, the first canto of the Periya Puranam, have completed the Thirumalai Sarukkam, the first canto of the Periya Puranam. In accordance with Manikkavasagar’s saying, “Avan Arulaale Avan Thazh Vanangi” (By His grace alone, I worship His feet), such a great sacred task cannot be accomplished without the Lord’s grace.

Therefore, remembering and rejoicing in the Lord’s grace and the compassion shown to us by the Lord’s devotees, we offer our salutations to them and begin the second canto of the Periya Puranam, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam. We pray for the Lord’s grace and the grace of the sacred devotees to further contemplate the histories of the devotees in detail, and thus we will continue this sacred act of Shiva.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 36 — Sundarar

Thiruchitrambalam.
In the second canto of the Periya Puranam, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam, we have contemplated up to the Puranam of Amarneethi Nayanar. We have already seen that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in the sacred hymn called Thiru-Thondath Thogai, mentions each Nayanar and sings “Adiyen” (I am a servant) to them, thereby mentioning the sacred names of sixty-three Nayanars and nine collective devotees, and stating that he is a servant to them. We also saw earlier that this Thiru-Thondath Thogai, this sacred hymn, consists of eleven verses. These eleven verses in the Thiru-Thondath Thogai are Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed the Periya Puranam, also known as Thiru-Thondar Puranam, with eleven cantos, each canto corresponding to one of these verses. Sekkizhar Swamigal has arranged the Puranam of each Nayanar, as mentioned in the respective verses of the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, within its corresponding canto.

Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed the Periya Puranam centered around these eleven cantos. As an introduction to this, he composed the Thirumalai Sarukkam as the first canto, and then finally, he included the Vellaanai Sarukkam.

Therefore, we already know that the Periya Puranam consists of thirteen cantos. In that sequence, in the first verse of the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, that is, the one beginning with “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen,” there is We have so far contemplated the history of the collective devotee known as Thillai Vaazh Andhanar and six Nayanars, whose Puranams were sung by Sekkizhar Peruman in the second canto, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam. Sekkizhar Peruman has named each canto after the first line of the corresponding verse in the Thiru-Thondath Thogai. In that way, the first verse of the Thiru-Thondath Thogai begins with “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham,” doesn’t it? That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal named the second canto “Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam.” In that first verse of the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, it goes: “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen, Thiru-Neelakandaththu Kuyavanaarkku Adiyen, Illaiye Ennaadha Iyarpagaikkum Adiyen, Ilaiyaandhan Kudimaaran Adiyaarkkum Adiyen.” It continues: “Yen Vellumaa Migavalla Meypporulukku Adiyen, Viri Pozhil Soozh Kundraiyaar Viranmindarkku Adiyen, Alli Menmullaiyanthaar Amarneethikku Adiyen, Aarooran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale.” This is how the song goes, isn’t it?

Accordingly, in this Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam, we have seen the Puranam of Thillai Vaazh Andhanar, followed by the Puranams of Thiru-Neelakanda Kuyavanar, Iyarpagai Nayanar, Ilaiyankudi Maaran Nayanar, Meypporul Nayanar, Viranmindar Nayanar, and Amarneethi Nayanar, in that order, in accordance with the phrase “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen.” Among these, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanars are collective devotees, and the other six Nayanars are individual Nayanars.

Therefore, we have so far We have been contemplating the Puranam of one collective devotee and the sacred Puranams of six Nayanars. In this first verse, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after singing “Alli Menmullaiyanthaar Amarneethikku Adiyen” (I am a servant to Amarneethi, who wears a garland of soft jasmine) for the sixth Nayanar, Amarneethi Nayanar, concludes the verse with “Aaruran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale.” The meaning of this is, “I, Nambi Aaruran, Sundaramoorthy, am a servant to the Lord who resides in Thiruvarur.” Sekkizhar Swamigal, who arranged the Puranams of the Nayanars in the same order as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, has composed a verse of praise for Sundaramoorthy Nayanar as the concluding verse of this canto, in accordance with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s concluding line, “Aaruran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale,” in this collective work. He has composed a hymn of praise.

Similarly, at the end of every verse in the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal would have concluded with “Aaruran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale.” Likewise, at the end of each canto, Sekkizhar Swamigal would have concluded by composing a hymn of praise for Sundaramoorthy Nayanar, appropriate to that canto.

Therefore, if we now contemplate the hymn of praise for Sundaramoorthy Nayanar, which comes as the concluding verse of this second canto, the Thillai Vaazh Andhanar Sarukkam, this canto will be completed, and it will be a cause for us to begin the next canto.

First, let us contemplate the meaning of a collective work. According to tradition, we contemplate the lines of the collective work, the Thiru-Thondath Thogai, and then the verses pertaining to each Nayanar in the categorized work, the Thiru-Thondar Thiru- Thiru-Andhaadhi, composed by Nambi Aandaar Nambigal, and then we contemplate the Puranams found in Sekkizhar’s extensive work, the Periya Puranam. Following that tradition, let us first contemplate this line from the Thiru-Thondath Thogai. That line is “Aaruran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale.” Its meaning is, “I, Nambi Aaruran, am a servant to the Lord who resides in Thiruvarur.” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after saying “Thillai Vaazh Andhanartham Adiyaarkkum Adiyen” and then stating that he is a servant to six Nayanars, finally concludes with “Aaruran Aaruril Ammaanukku Aale.” We also know that this Thiru-Thondath Thogai is a song given by the Lord to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as an opening line to sing the greatness of the Lord’s devotees.

Furthermore, we know that the very purpose of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s divine incarnation from Thiru-Kailash to this earthly world, our South India, was to bestow upon us this Thiru-Thondath Thogai.

Therefore, when interpreting this line of the song, how should we interpret it? We should interpret it as: “I, who am a servant to the Lord residing in Thiruvarur, am a servant to the servants of the Brahmins of Thillai; I am a servant to Thiru-Neelakanda Kuyavanar; I am a servant to Iyarpagai, who never said ‘no’; I am a servant to Ilaiyandan Kudimaaran; I am a servant to Meypporul, who is mighty in victory; I am a servant to Viranmindar of Kundraiyur, surrounded by vast groves; I am a servant to Amarneethi, who wears a garland of soft jasmine.” Now, Nambi Aandaar Nambigal has composed a verse in the categorized work, Thiru-Thondar Thiru-Andhaadhi, that is appropriate for this line.

Let us now ponder it. “Thozhudhum Vanangiyum Maal Ayan Theda Arunjothi Sendru Aangu Ezhudhum Tamil Pazha Aavanam Kaatti Enakku Un Kudi Muzhudhum Adimai Vandhu Aatsai Ena Pettravan Mural Then Ozhugu Malarin Nal Thaar Empiraan Nambi Aarurane.” This is the verse from Thiru-Thondar Thiru-Andhaadhi, composed as a hymn of praise for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

Now, let us contemplate the hymn of praise for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, which is the concluding verse of the second canto in Sekkizhar’s Periya Puranam. This is the five hundred and fiftieth verse in the Periya Puranam. That verse is “Malar Misai. Ithai Ayanum Maalum Kaanutharku Ariya Vallal Palar Pugazh Vennai Nallur Aavana Pazhamai Kaatti Ulaku Oiya Aandu Kolla Pettravar Paadhamunni Thalai Misai Vaiththu Vaazhum Thalaimai Nam Thalaimai Aagum.” This is that verse. Its meaning is that Lord Shiva, the benevolent one, who is difficult for Brahma, who resides on the lotus, and Vishnu to see, showed the ancient document in Thiruvennainallur and took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal into His service to redeem the world. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that the leadership of meditating on the sacred feet of such Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and living with them placed upon one’s head is our true leadership.

Sekkizhar Swamigal states that this is our leadership. We have extensively contemplated the Puranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal being taken into service by showing the document in Thiruvennainallur in the first canto, the Thirumalai Sarukkam. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings about that very event as a hymn of praise here. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that we should worship the sacred feet of such Sundaramoorthy Swamigal by placing them on our heads. Sekkizhar here says to meditate on the sacred feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Meditating is an act of speech.

He says to celebrate by placing the sacred feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on our heads. This is an act of the body.

Thus, he says to worship the sacred feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with all three of our faculties. Kilar Swamigal states that placing the feet of devotees upon our heads is a Shaivite tradition. The head is the highest part of our body; there is no part higher than the head.

Therefore, we should joyfully place the sacred feet of devotees upon our heads. It is a Shaivite tradition to place the feet of the Lord and the feet of devotees upon one’s head. This is a scriptural meaning, where the feet of the Lord are permeated by the fullness of the sacred feet.

Now, how does Sundaramurthi Swamigal sing? “Adiyen to the Thillai Vazh Andanar and their devotees,” he sings, “I am a devotee” to each of the Nayanmars. Such are the sacred feet of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Sekizhar Swamigal sings, “We shall place his feet upon our heads and serve him.” Therefore, we shall become servants to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. We shall also become servants to the Nayanmars whom Sundaramurthi Swamigal has already sung about. Through this, we shall forever be servants to the Lord.

Thus, the second canto of the Periya Puranam, known as the Thillai Vazh Andanar Charukkam, concludes here. It is only due to the compassion that the all-powerful Shiva Paramapoorul has bestowed upon us, and the sacred grace He has placed upon us, that we have been granted the opportunity to hear the history of the devotees, which is such a great Shiva Punya.

In the same way, may we be able to contemplate the histories of the upcoming Nayanmars, according to the sacred will of Peruman Sekizhar. With the prayer that the grace of the Guru and the grace of the Lord may stand by us and enlighten us from within, we conclude this second canto. Again, we shall conclude by contemplating the sacred names of the Nayanmars whom we have considered in this canto, following the first verse of the Thiru-Thondar Thogai: “Adiyen to the Thillai Vazh Andanar and their devotees; Adiyen to Thiru-Neelakandar the potter; Adiyen to Iyarpagai, who never thought ‘I have nothing’; Adiyen to Ilaiyanthan Kudi Maran and his devotees; Adiyen to Meipporul, who is very skilled in victory; Adiyen to Viranmindar of Kunrattur, surrounded by vast groves; Adiyen to Amarneethi, who wears a garland of soft lilies; Aaruran is a servant to the Lord of Aarur.” Praise be to the sacred feet of Thiruvarur Thiyagaraja Peruman. Sundaramurthi Praise be to the sacred feet of Swamigal. Praise be to the sacred feet of Nambi Andar Nambigal. Praise be to the sacred feet of Sekizhar Peruman.

Praise be to the golden sacred feet of Sivakavimani Aiyya, who composed the commentary for this Periya Puranam and bestowed it upon us.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 37 — Vellanai Charukkam 1

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, Part 1023, composed by the divine Sekizhar Peruman: Vellanai Charukkam. In the Periya Puranam composed by the divine Sekizhar Peruman, the Thiru-Malai Charukkam was the first canto, recounting the history of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the hero of this Puranam, residing in Thiru-Kailash. Subsequently, in the canto known as Thaduthatkonda Puranam, we saw the section of sacred history that formed the basis for the composition of the Thiru-Thondar Thogai Thiru-Pathigam.

Following that, we also observed that the divine Sekizhar Peruman arranged eleven cantos corresponding to the eleven sacred verses of the Thiru-Thondar Thogai composed by our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal.

Thus, we have so far considered twelve cantos.

Now, the thirteenth canto, the Vellanai Charukkam, is the concluding canto in the Thiru-Thondar Puranam, the Periya Puranam. The sacred history of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, along with our Lord Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, reaching Thiru-Kailash, has been graciously narrated by the divine Sekizhar Peruman in fifty-three sacred verses. The divine Sekizhar Peruman states in the first sacred verse, “We shall narrate, as we have learned, how Nambi Thambiran Thozhar, who is the foremost of the original Thiru-Thondar Thogai and who rose to this knowledge, along with Kalaritrarivar, whose eyes are like red lotuses blooming in the morning, reached Thiru-Kailash, the abode of the one who drank poison.” The Thiru-Thondar Thogai Thiru-Pathigam, composed by our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, is the primary text that narrates the histories of the Thiru-Thondar.

Therefore, the Thiru-Thondar Thogai Thiru-Pathigam is the source text, the primary text, for this Periya Puranam, the Thiru-Thondar Puranam. The divine Sekizhar Peruman states in this sacred verse that Nambigal, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who came to this world for the salvation of this earthly realm as the foremost one entitled to compose the sacred work, along with Kalaritrarivar Nayanar, whose eyes are like red lotuses, reached the sacred Kailash of the Lord. We shall narrate this history as we have learned it. The word “moolam” means “that which created.” Therefore, we previously saw in detail within the Thiru-Malai Charukkam that this Periya Puranam, the Thiru-Thondar Puranam, originated as a derivative work, taking the Thiru-Thondar Thogai Thiru-Pathigam as its primary text and the Thiru-Thondar Thiru-Andathi as its secondary text. We previously saw in detail within the Thiru-Malai Charukkam that this Puranam is not merely a translation. It is also clearly explained that it is not a translation.

Such is the foremost of the original Thiru-Thondar Thogai. “Foremost” means the author, the creator. And it also implies that he is equal to the foremost. We previously saw this as the statement of Upamanyu Munivar. Such a foremost one, he is the Nambi who rises. Swamigal says that if this world were to rise from this earth, the South, which performed great penance, would live, and the Thiru-Thondar Thogai would be given.

We saw this at the beginning. The divine Sekizhar Peruman has thus graciously stated this, recalling it at the end of this Puranam and keeping it in mind. Such is Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Kalaritrarivar Nayanar, who is Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. His eyes are like red lotuses blooming in the morning. Redness in the eyes, with red streaks, signifies great compassion. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar received this great boon of compassionate sight.

Moreover, the term “red lotus eyes” generally refers to Thirumal. If he himself has red lotus eyes, then according to the saying, “I saw Thirumal in the form of Thiru,” it is appropriate to say that Cheraman Perumal Nayanar has red lotus eyes, like an aspect of Thirumal.

Therefore, the divine Sekizhar Peruman graciously states that they are about to narrate the sacred history of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaching Thiru-Kailash with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, as they have learned it. The divine Sekizhar Peruman graciously states that they are about to narrate the sacred history of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaching Thiru-Kailash with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, as they have learned it. The second sacred verse: “In this world, the prolonged devotion, nurtured and grown by the water of love, took the beautiful form of Nambiyarurar’s crimson body. The harsh, bitter two karmas were weeded out, and rising, spreading their rays, the endless Shiva-bhoga ripened and matured.” The meaning of this sacred verse is that in this world, prolonged devotion, nurtured and grown by the water of love, took the beautiful form of Nambiyarurar’s crimson body. The harsh, bitter two karmas, like weeds, were plucked out and cast away. Rising above, spreading the rays of knowledge, the fruit of endless Shiva-bhoga ripened and matured greatly.

This sacred verse is a pun. It describes the period after Nambiyarurar Sundaramurthi Swamigal first saw Kalaritrarivar, separated from him, and returned to Thiruvarur, until he again thought of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and went to Kodungolur, marking the end of his earthly life. This is a beautiful sacred verse, structured as a pun. It describes the yield of paddy, how paddy grows, and also the state of Shiva-bhoga ripening due to Nambiyarurar’s incarnation in this world. It uses a metaphorical description for both. In the field, rows of crops, starting from seedlings, grow well when sufficient water flows to them.

At that time, the weeds that are harmful to the crop must be identified and removed. If they are removed, ears of grain will emerge from the stalks and spread. The grains will ripen and become dense, yielding a paddy harvest.

Similarly, in this world, according to the grace of the Lord, the love that Shiva has for Shiva’s devotees is the root of devotion. This devotion spreads and thrives with the quality of love, taking the form of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s sacred body. The two karmas, evil karma and good karma, which harm the Shiva-crop, were plucked out like weeds and cast away by Sundaramurthi Swamigal. He plucked out and cast away the two karmas, which are like weeds hindering souls from experiencing Shiva-bhoga.

Thus, the light of Shiva-gnana shone everywhere, and it became abundant with maturity and density. Just as evil karma, good karma also leads to rebirth.

Therefore, both karmas are weeds.

Thus, as a result of the incarnation of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, Shiva-bhakti flourished in this world with the help of love, removing the two karmas, good and evil, and increasing gnana, leading to Shiva-ananda. This is the meaning of the sacred verse. These imperceptible qualities are abstract entities.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s sacred body became a form, a manifestation, and all these became visible. What is invisible to women, all these intellectual entities, became visible, taking the form of our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s sacred body. The third sacred verse: “The Lord who wears snakes as garlands, His beloved, the intimate devotee Vanthondar, in the beautiful Thiruvarur, surrounded by groves filled with cool, honeyed flowers, during his stay, thought of Cherar Perumal, worshipped the divine feet of the Lord, and with an eager desire to reach the mountainous country, departed.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the intimate devotee, the one who serves with intimacy. The intimate services performed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal include offering water and garlands to the Lord, performing sacred ablutions, and other services related to the sacred form.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal belongs to the Adisaiva tradition, which touches the sacred form three times a day.

Therefore, it is stated thus.

Moreover, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s previous state was in Thiru-Kailash, where he performed the sacred service of holding the sacred ash for the Lord and offering sacred garlands.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal is an intimate devotee of the Lord, having the right of friendship close to the Lord’s grace. During his stay in the beautiful Thiruvarur, surrounded by groves filled with cool, honeyed flowers, Sundaramurthi Swamigal is said to have departed with a great desire to reach the mountainous country, having thought of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, worshipped the divine feet of Thiyagesa Peruman in Thiruvarur, and received gracious leave.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal is an intimate devotee, one who has the right to serve intimately. The intimate services performed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal include offering water and garlands to the Lord, performing sacred ablutions, and other services related to the sacred form.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal belongs to the Adisaiva tradition, which touches the sacred form three times a day.

Therefore, it is stated thus.

Moreover, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s previous state was in Thiru-Kailash, where he performed the sacred service of holding the sacred ash for the Lord and offering sacred garlands.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal is an intimate devotee of the Lord, having the right of friendship close to the Lord’s grace. During his stay in the beautiful Thiruvarur, surrounded by groves filled with cool, honeyed flowers, the days he spent in the beautiful Thiruvarur, surrounded by groves filled with cool, honeyed flowers. All these epithets highlight the qualities of Thiruvarur and the fact that those who reside there are reluctant to leave it; it is rare for them to even think of leaving Thiruvarur. Previously, we saw that Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having been honored by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and residing with him in Kodungolur, intensely remembered the Lord who rules Thiruvarur and sang, returning to Thiruvarur.

Similarly, here, during his stay in Thiruvarur, he thought of Cheraman Perumal and desired to go to Thiru-Anjaikkalam. All these indicate the ways in which our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal acts according to divine grace.

Therefore, worshipping the feet of the divine Perumal, that divine Perumal is Thiyagaraja Perumal, who resides in Thiruvarur.

The Lord Himself bestowed friendship, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who was Thambiran Thozhar, made himself a friend of Cheraman.

Therefore, the friendship of Cheran came from the divine.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s love and enthusiasm were equally directed towards both the divine Perumal, Thiyagesa Peruman, and Cheraman Perumal, Kalaritrarivar Nayanar. The divine Sekizhar Peruman here portrays Thiyagesa Peruman as “divine Perumal” and Kalaritrarivar Nayanar as “Cheraman Perumal,” indicating that his affection was for both. We see the usage of the words “Cherar Perumal” and “divine Perumal” here. We see the magical word usage of the divine Sekizhar Peruman.

Therefore, he worships Thiruvarur, Thiyagesa Peruman, and the Lord who resides in the ant-hill, and departs. Our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal departs after worshipping Thiyagesa Peruman and the Lord who resides in the ant-hill. This is the complete way of worshipping Thiyagesa Peruman and the Lord who resides in the ant-hill. In the Puranam of Gnanasambandha Peruman, we saw that when Aludaiya Pillaiyar was going for his wedding, he worshipped the foremost Lord through his friend and then departed.

Here, as a concluding act, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships Thiruvarur Thiyagesa Peruman and Vanmeeganathar and departs with the desire to reach the mountainous country, where the rain showers and the resulting vegetation are found. The sacred verse: “Having worshipped and departed from the sacred places where the Lord of the fertile country rejoices, he reached Kudakongu, where deer leap in the groves, and arrived at the sacred Thiru-Puliyur, where the pure moon is placed upon the head of the Lord.” The Kaveri Thiru-Nadu, which has good water. If the Kaveri’s water is good, it means that not a single drop of water is wasted, but is used for crops and living beings. We see this in the Kaveri in the Chola country. He worships and departs from all the sacred places on both banks of such a Kaveri. This is the state where all living beings worship Shiva and attain salvation.

He worships directly the sacred places he encounters on the way and remembers other sacred places as he goes. Leaving the Chola country surrounded by the Kaveri, he crosses the Kaveri, leaves that country, and reaches the western Kongu country. Since these routes were described in great detail when he went with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the divine Sekizhar Peruman now describes them very briefly. Our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal departs after worshipping Thiyagesa Peruman and the Lord who resides in the ant-hill. This is the complete way of worshipping Thiyagesa Peruman and the Lord who resides in the ant-hill. In the Puranam of Gnanasambandha Peruman, we saw that when Aludaiya Pillaiyar was going for his wedding, he worshipped the foremost Lord through his friend and then departed.

Here, as a concluding act, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships Thiruvarur Thiyagesa Peruman and Vanmeeganathar and departs with the desire to reach the mountainous country, where the rain showers and the resulting vegetation are found. The sacred verse: “Having worshipped and departed from the sacred places where the Lord of the fertile country rejoices, he reached Kudakongu, where deer leap in the groves, and arrived at the sacred Thiru-Puliyur, where the pure moon is placed upon the head of the Lord.” The Kaveri Thiru-Nadu, which has good water. If the Kaveri’s water is good, it means that not a single drop of water is wasted, but is used for crops and living beings. We see this in the Kaveri in the Chola country. He worships and departs from all the sacred places on both banks of such a Kaveri. This is the state where all living beings worship Shiva and attain salvation.

He worships directly the sacred places he encounters on the way and remembers other sacred places as he goes. Leaving the Chola country surrounded by the Kaveri, he crosses the Kaveri, leaves that country, and reaches the western Kongu country. Since these routes were described in great detail when he went with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the divine Sekizhar Peruman now describes them very briefly. The Mullai Padappai is a Mullai forest land, a forested land area before reaching the mountainous country. These are places where Kurumbars, who live in hills and mountains, reside. We have already seen that he crossed many hills, mounds, thorny bushes, and famous forests.

He crossed those Mullai forests and approached the villages of that land. These are areas that combine the characteristics of both country and city. We can still see them today. Having crossed them, he reaches what is called Kudakongu. In Tamil Nadu, Kongu Nadu is divided into three parts: Melkongu (Western Kongu), Kizhkongu (Eastern Kongu), and Vadakongu (Northern Kongu). Among these, the Melkongu country is on the way from the Chola country, and after crossing this Melkongu country, it becomes the mountainous country.

He must reach and attain that. Having crossed it, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the sacred place of Thiru-Pukkoliyur, which is Avinasi.

There, the Lord who wears the pure moon resides. The pure moon is the moon that attained purity by reaching the Lord. This is called Aprakrita Chandra, or Gnana Chandra Kalanvitam. Thiru-Pukkoliyur is now popularly known as Avinasi. Its sacred name is Karunambikai.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the sacred Thiru-Pukkoliyur, which is rich in grace. The fifth sacred verse: “Approaching the mansion-lined street of that town where Brahmins reside, in two opposing houses filled with wealth, a joyous sound arose, and in another, the sound of weeping came forth, and the Brahmins residing there asked what caused these two events to occur simultaneously.”

Sundaramurthi Swamigal reaches the side of the mansion-lined street where Vedic scholars reside in Avinasi, also known as Enbukkoliyur. We have seen many sacred places like Thiru-Chengalur, all of which are entirely inhabited by Vedic scholars.

But here, many people are mixed.

Therefore, we see that there is a separate street called “Mariyore Vazhum Madaveethi” (Street where Vedic scholars reside). This area, as we see it, might have been located south of the present temple in Avinasi, on the way from Vanjipalayam to Avinasi. The town of Pukkoliyur was located south of the Avinasi temple, south of the irrigation tank. It is known through historical records and government land survey records that the town, which was located on that land, was later destroyed over time. Even now, we can see the signs of the destroyed town in this land. There was also an old charity house called Pukkoliyur Madam, and that tradition serves as evidence for this.

Therefore, it is believed that when Nambigal came this way, he would see and hear the events described in this verse. It is known that in earlier days, there was an old road for those traveling from the Chola country to the mountainous country, which crossed the Kaveri near Thiru-Ingoy Malai, came via Thiru-Pandi Kodumudi, and then went west through Thiru-Murugan Poondi and Thiru-Pukkoliyur, which is Avinasi. Regarding this, we previously saw that he crossed the mountainous country of Karur, and also the Kacchuram, and the water-filled forests and rivers, and many beautiful groves, and went through Thiru-Murugan Poondi. The present town of Avinasi is located north of the temple. If one crosses the mansion-lined street where the Brahmins mentioned here reside, there is a lake called the “pond where the crocodile swallowed the child,” and beyond that, there is the sacred temple.

Therefore, keeping all these in mind, we can read these historical events. When he reaches the mansion-lined street, he hears a sound from two opposing houses filled with wealth, and from another house, the sound of weeping.

Therefore, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal asks the Brahmins there, “What is the reason for these two different sounds occurring at the same time?” He graciously asked them. The houses, though equally prosperous, experienced contrasting events during that very period.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, therefore, inquired about the reasons for these occurrences. Let us resume our reflection upon this.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 38 — Vellanai Charukkam 2

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, Part 1024, by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman, Vellanai Charukkam. In Avinasi, on a street where Brahmins resided, Sundaramurthi Swamigal observed two houses, facing each other, experiencing contrasting events simultaneously. The occurrence of such divergent events caused Sundaramurthi Swamigal to take notice. One might ask if great souls, who live in the bliss of Shiva and are immersed in Shiva-yoga, would pay attention to such worldly happenings. The answer is no.

It is due to the impending consequences that divine grace, residing within their hearts, directs their attention in this manner.

Therefore, in one house ...auspicious musical instruments resounded, while in the house opposite, sounds of weeping were heard.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal inquired of the Brahmins near the house where the auspicious music played. The sixth Thiru-paadal (sacred verse): “The Brahmin, bowing, said, ‘Two precious sons, both five years old, went to bathe in a pond. A crocodile swallowed one child first. The one who escaped is now having his sacred thread ceremony.’” Sundaramurthi Swamigal listened.

The Brahmins, bowing to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, replied: “There were two precious sons, both five years old. They went to bathe in a pond. In that pond where they bathed, a crocodile came and swallowed one child.

“The child who escaped is now having his sacred thread ceremony, the Upanayanam, in this house. The weeping for the other child is happening in the opposite house,” they said. It is mentioned here that a crocodile does not chew its food but swallows it whole. The boy who escaped the crocodile’s mouth and survived is now having his Upanayanam, the sacred thread ceremony, in this house. This signifies the imparting of the sacred knowledge of Brahmopadesam. The house of those who lost their child to the crocodile is on the opposite side, the Brahmins explained.

“The seventh Thiru-paadal: “Hearing this, the compassionate heart of the Thiru-thondar ...the Vantondar, seeing them before him, bowed.

“The Brahmin and his wife, who had lost their child, came quickly, their sorrow forgotten, and prostrated at his holy feet.” Hearing this account, the Vantondar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, felt compassion in his heart. This compassion arose in the heart of the Vantondar. It is the nature of universal grace that extends to all beings. That grace arose in the heart of Nambigal.

“The Brahmin who had lost his child and his wife, desiring to worship Sundaramurthi Swamigal who had arrived there, came very quickly and prostrated at his holy feet. For a very long time, they had desired to have a darshan of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. They were Brahmins with great devotion, filled with love for Lord Shiva, love for His devotees, and devotion to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Forgetting the sorrow of losing their son, they came and worshipped at Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s holy feet. These individuals, who had been weeping in the sorrow of losing a child until then, upon hearing that Sundaramurthi Swamigal had arrived, their long-held desire to see and worship him, which had been deeply ingrained in their hearts, now manifested. Their devotion to Shiva’s feet overshadowed their sorrow.

“Not only was their sorrow forgotten, but their faces also brightened. This is explained in the next Thiru-paadal, the eighth Thiru-paadal. Peruman explains: “Their sorrow gone, their faces brightened, they worshipped. Looking at them, he asked, ‘Are you the one who raised the joyful son?’ They replied, ‘Our love has not been in vain; we have been blessed by your presence.’” The Brahmin and his wife, their sorrow of losing a child gone, their faces brightened, worshipped Sundaramurthi Swamigal. He addressed them as “joyful son,” referring to a son who brings joy and is a blessing to a household. Although this son had died long ago, he is referred to as “joyful son” here.

“This is a divine premonition that this son will rise again. This is a divine premonition that the son will be resurrected and bring joy. When our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal inquired, the two, bowing to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, replied: “That event happened many days ago, it is a past event, do not worry about it. We have been trying to worship you for a very long time. Our love has not been in vain; we have been blessed by your presence here.” Thus, they expressed their long-held love for Sundaramurthi Swamigal. They requested, “The strength of our love has not been in vain; without our effort, you have blessed us with your presence here.”

“They stood bowing, saying, “Today we have received this blessing as a result of the penance of love performed for many days.” The ninth Thiru-paadal: “Forgetting the sorrow of losing his son, the Brahmin and his wife rejoiced at my arrival. I will call forth the boy from the mouth of that crocodile and restore him.

“Then I will worship the feet of my Lord, the Father of Avinasi.” This is the Thiru-paadal that says, “He who removes obstacles went.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal understood. For their sake, his wife also rejoiced. “Only after I call forth that boy from the mouth of the crocodile that swallowed him, and restore him to them, by the grace of the Lord, will I go and worship the holy feet of the Lord of Avinasi,” he said. Only after I call forth that boy and restore him to them, will I go and worship the holy feet of the Lord of Avinasi, said Nambigal, who removes the distress of those who come with love. Of all sorrows, the sorrow of losing a child is the most profound and unforgettable, possessing great power. Yet, they have come with joyful faces, having forgotten even that.

“He said, “I, who am the object of their long-held desire to see and worship, have come here unexpectedly. This shows the greatness of their devotion, which has overcome even the sorrow of losing a child.” Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal resolved, “I will find a way to alleviate the sorrow of these devotees.” Here, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s resolve was, “I will pray to the Lord and, by His grace, bring him back.” He asks, “Why?” The sacred verse of Thevaram says, “Tell the crocodile to give the child, O Yama.” Interpreting this as Nambigal himself bringing back the child would be inappropriate here. If Nambigal were to say, “I myself will bring back the child,” it would imply that he, like the Lord, performs the five divine functions, and that this power also exists in other beings, which would contradict the conclusions of the Shivagamas and lead to error. The Gnana Shastram explains this in the Sankalpa Nirakaranam, Shivasamavadi Nirakaranam, from the thirtieth to the thirty-sixth Thiru-paadal.

“Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal states that only after bringing the boy from the crocodile to his parents will he go to the temple and worship Avinasi Appar. Even in bringing back the child, he does so by praying to and bowing before the Lord.

“Therefore, saying “I bowed, I will bow” here refers to the worship performed by going to the temple. We have previously seen that the path Sundaramurthi Swamigal took to the temple involved crossing the streets of Pukkoliyur and the crocodile pond, then proceeding north to reach the temple.

“Thus, “Avinasi Enthai” refers to the Lord of Avinasi. The Lord’s holy name there is Avinasi because, even though the crocodile and the child it swallowed appeared to be destroyed, the Lord here is capable of restoring the child without destruction.

“Therefore, His holy name is Avinasi. “Pukkoliyur Avinasiye” is praised in the Pathigam. The tenth Thiru-paadal: Having thus spoken, he asked, “Where is the pond where the cruel, fierce-mouthed crocodile swallowed their son?” He inquired, asking where the pond was where the cruel, sharp-toothed crocodile had swallowed their son.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having thus spoken as mentioned earlier, asked the Brahmins present there, “Where is the pond where the cruel, sharp-toothed crocodile swallowed their son?” He asked this question of the other Brahmins standing nearby, not of the child’s parents, in accordance with the worldly custom of not reminding parents of the loss of their child. A pond is a deep body of water, a suitable habitat for crocodiles.

“However, at this moment, the pond was so dry that its location could not even be discerned, as indicated by the hint here.

“Thus, in this sacred history, we see the mention that he brought back the water to the pond, the crocodile into the water, the child from the crocodile’s belly, and the years that had passed for the child. “The dried-up pond filled again, the crocodile that lived there appeared, and the son who had died grew up and attained his years. We praise the feet of Sundaran, who sang this song.” This is followed by the Thiru-Avinasi Sthala Puranam, which describes the pond being dry without water, and other details. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman did not elaborate on all these details. These are local legends and special accounts that are told from time to time.

“However, we see here the way in which the divine Sekkizhar Peruman hints at and conveys these things.

“Therefore, from that holy street, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeded to the bank of the pond. It is referred to as a “living pond.” Although their son had died before, it is called a pond that gives life, and it also implies that this world will gain life by knowing this history. A pond is a body of water not created by humans.

“Therefore, the meaning of Sundaramurthi Swamigal saying that he would bring the boy back from the crocodile’s mouth is thus explained with the truth of Saiva Siddhanta.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having arrived at the bank of that pond, sang a sacred hymn to the Lord, requesting the crocodile, which had swallowed the boy on a previous day and possessed sharp, sword-like teeth, to bring him forth. We shall continue our reflection upon this.”
Thiruchitrambalam.
