Chapter 39 — Vellanai Charukkam 3

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, Part 1025, by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman, Vellanai Charukkam. To bring the child back from the crocodile’s mouth, our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal sang a sacred hymn, praying to the Lord. The eleventh Thiru-paadal: “Before the Thiru-paattu, which begins ‘Uraippar Urai,’ was completed, Yama brought back the life of that son. The crocodile, from its belly in the wavy waters, brought forth the child, as if he had grown there, with the two years that had passed, his body fully developed.” Our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal sang the sacred hymn. The Thiru-paadigam begins with “Ettraan Marakken.” The fourth Thiru-paadal begins with “Uraippar Urai Ugandhu.”

Therefore, before the Thiru-paattu, which began with “Uraippar Urai” in that sacred hymn, was completed, Yama brought back the life of that son. The crocodile, from its belly in the wavy waters, brought forth the child, as if he had grown there, with the two years that had passed, his body fully developed. “Urai” is the beginning of this Thiru-paattu. This Thiru-paattu is dedicated to this Thiru-paadigam, he submits. We also see here that “Thiru-paattu” is a name specifically used for Nambigal’s songs. Before this Thiru-paadal was completed, the life that had passed two years ago Thus, it is understood that the child returned with a body that had grown for two years.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s Thiru-paadigam for the Avinasi Sthalam is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. “How can I forget? For seven births, You are my Lord. I meditate on You, my only refuge, with a knowing heart. O serpent dancing on the anthill, O Avinasi of Pukkoliyur, I live by holding on to You, O Pasupathi, O Parameshti. O young Brahmin who joined the travelers, is it right for you to leave?

“Tell me, O Lord with the grace-filled matted locks. In the groves of Pukkoliyur, the young Brahmin who bathed in the pond and was swallowed, what a wonder he performed! Wherever I go, if I remember my Lord, even if I am filled with anger, there is no one to scold me. O Avinasi of Pukkoliyur, O Lord of the cremation ground, You are the beginning and the end. O Avinasi of Pukkoliyur, with its groves of words. Tell Yama to make the crocodile on the bank give the child.

“O good path, O nine-fold path. O Lord of righteousness, our Namban! You do not desire the white skin, but you desired the tiger’s skin. In the groves of Pukkoliyur, the young Brahmin who went to bathe in the pond, what a wonder he performed!

“The Lord with the rising water, the Lord with the flowing matted locks, the Lord of the lightning, the Lord of the great bull, the Lord of the lake, the Lord of Avinasi in Pukkoliyur, the Lord with the dark throat, those who can sing the glorious songs composed by Aaruran will have no sorrow.

“The twelfth Thiru-paadal: “The mother, her heart melting with love, ran and took the child that the great-mouthed crocodile had brought to the bank and vomited. She fell at the holy feet of the holy one who had restored his life, along with the virtuous Brahmin. The celestials in the sky showered flowers from the wish-granting tree.” The child whom the crocodile brought to the bank and vomited from its large mouth The mother, her heart melting with overflowing love, ran and took the child, and fell at the holy feet of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the holy one who had restored his life, along with the virtuous Brahmin. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman, who in the previous verse stated that Yama brought back the life with the body’s growth from the crocodile’s mouth, here implies the act of the crocodile vomiting him out by simply saying “the vomited child,” without explicitly stating it as a separate action.

“Thus, the divine poetic skill of the author is shown here in quickly conveying the parents’ sorrow through the phrase “the vomited child.” The mother’s love is greater than the father’s.

“Therefore, the mother, knowing that her son had returned, with great joy, brought the son and joined her husband, the Brahmin. She fell at the holy feet of Sundaramurthi Swamigal in a state of helplessness. She bowed down. This child would not know this history. He would not understand.

“Therefore, the mother makes him worship as well. The mother herself took him and fell at the holy feet of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. She fell at the holy feet of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the holy one who restored life, and worshipped. “Thiruvalan” means one who has received divine grace. “Thiruvalan Thiru Neeru” means one who has the sacred ash.

“Lord Shiva alone is called Thiruvalar.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal is like Lord Shiva.

“Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal is also called Thiruvalan here.

“Moreover, restoring life is one of the five divine functions performed by the Lord.

“Here, Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s actions are also by the grace of the Lord.

“Here, we also consider the state in which the Lord was placed. The thirteenth Thiru-paadal: “The people on earth were amazed; all the Brahmins threw their upper garments into the sky and cheered; the sound of the Vedas rose mightily. The great Lord also brought the son, who was the apple of the eye of the holy Brahmin, to Thiru-Avinasi and worshipped the Lord.” The people of this earthly world were amazed to see this. All the Brahmins threw their upper garments into the sky and cheered. This is one of the acts that shows joy in the sky. This is a custom prevalent among Brahmins and other high-ranking individuals.

“The sound of the Vedas itself rose mightily. The great Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also took that child and worshipped Lord Shiva, who is Thiru-Avinasi. He worshipped. The note about the son being brought back to life by Shiva’s grace. He made the son worship.

“There, all the Brahmins performed their duties. This event was the act of the Lord, so Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped the Lord concerning it. The fourteenth Thiru-paadal: “Having sung the Thiru-padigam of spreading glory, and having worshipped and departed, the beloved son of the Brahmin, who was filled with love, was adorned with the sacred thread in Vellur. The great Lord, having caused the drums to sound, completed the ceremony and proceeded to the Chera kings.” We saw that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Thiru-padigams. We saw that the Thiru-paadal itself was the child.

“Therefore, the Thiru-padigam was continuously concluded with the Thiru-kadaikkaappu and worshipped in the Thiru-koyil.

“Therefore, in this padigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the first four Thiru-paattukkal were sung by the son standing by the pond. It is understood here that the remaining six verses were composed after he came back to life, and after taking him, in front of the Lord in Thiru-Avinasi Thiru-koyil.

“Therefore, he concluded that Thiru-padigam with Thiru-kadaikkaappu and worshipped in the Thiru-koyil.

“Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had previously said, “I will worship my father in Thiru-Avinasi, having brought the son.” This is fulfilled here.

“In addition, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was like a servant to the Thiru-vadi, was the father of that Brahmin boy.

“Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal himself took the lead in performing the Upanayana Mangalam for this beloved son of the Brahmin, adorning him with the sacred thread and sounding the drums. That is, he himself performed the auspicious ceremony of Upanayana. The sacred thread marriage is a tradition.

“Therefore, the house, which until a short while ago was filled with the sound of weeping, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal caused auspicious musical instruments to be played in that house. In the house opposite, this event occurred due to the Upanayana of the son who had previously lived there. Having completed that auspicious event, our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus blesses. We recall here the history that Thiru-Gnanasambandhar, the child-saint of Thiru-Marugal, cured the merchant of poison and himself arranged their marriage, granting them a great life. Having completed such auspicious ceremonies, our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds to meet the crowned kings.

“Thus, Thiru-Avinasi is a Thalam where divine miracles occurred. This is one of the seven Thalam-s in Kongu Nadu sung in the Thiru-Devaram. During Panguni Uthiram, the traditional festival of our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bringing the child back from the crocodile’s mouth is celebrated. These images are found on the temple flagstaff and on the large flagstaff in the front courtyard. The Thalam history states that the Goddess performed penance in this Thalam, married the Lord, and obtained the right side of His body. The Thiru-uruvam of the Goddess in a state of penance is in a separate temple outside, on the western bank of the Thiru-kulam.

“The Goddess is enshrined here under a Patiri tree and is known as Patiri Marathu Amman. The Patiri tree is not the Thalam tree. The Thalam tree of this place and the tree under which the Goddess performed penance is the Mango tree, according to the Thalam Puranam. The proverb “Kasiyil Vasi Avinasi” is prevalent in this region. This Thalam is also known as Dakshina Kasi. The Thiru-tha Kinaru (sacred well) in the Swami temple is famously known as Kasi Kinaru or Kasi Gangai.

“Beautiful copper idols of the sixty-three Nayanmars and the crocodile-mouth child are present as Utsava Moorthigal (processional deities). Pukkoliyur is the name of the Thalam. Avinasi is the name of the Lord enshrined in this Thalam.

“Now, the name of the village, Pukkoliyur, has disappeared, and Avinasi is used as the village name.

“The Lord performed the Agni Thandavam, and the terrified Devas, unable to witness it, entered and hid there. Hence, this Thalam is called Pukku Oliyur (the place where they entered and hid). Thiru-Gnanasambandhar Peruman has visited this Thalam. Since it is mentioned, “Having gone to all sides in the nearby pond and worshipped Thiru-Nannavinai,” it is believed that Pillaiyar might have visited this Thalam. It is believed that both Pillaiyar and Arasugal (Thiru-Navukkarasar) have visited and worshipped many parts of the upper Kongu Nadu in this Thalam through their Thiru-padigams. The Thiru-vasagam Keerthi Thiru-kavali mentions, “The body full of grace that bestowed the three fruits, the antiquity that showed the wonder.”

“Lines thirty-three and thirty-four appear. The seventh song of Ananda Malai, “Oh Father Avinasi, the rare substance, the Pandya’s wealth,” appears.

“Therefore, it is believed that Thiru-Vadavuradigal also visited this country and this Thalam through such sacred utterances. Approximately two hundred years ago, the Thalam Puranam for Thiru-Avinasi was written by the great poet Ilaiyan Kavirayar, of the Seerkarunigar lineage, who lived near this place. This was examined by Vidwan Kandasamy Mudaliar of Coimbatore and Vidwan Muthunagappa Mudaliar of Negamam and published in print a century ago. The Thalam Puranam has thirteen chapters, from the preface chapter to the Sundara chapter, and one thousand and twenty verses.

“In addition, Karunambigai Sathagam composed by Mahavidwan Vasudeva Mudaliar of Chettipalayam, and Perum Karunaiyam composed by Sivakavimani C.K. Subramania Mudaliar, the commentator of Periya Puranam, and other literary works, and the Kirtanams composed by Karunadasar, belong to this Thalam. The Lord’s name is Avinasi Appar, and the Mother’s name is Perungarunai Ammai. This Thalam is near Thiru-pur. One can also reach this Thalam from Coimbatore. These are the Thalam Puranam notes about Thiru-Avinasi.

“The sixteenth Thiru-paadal: “The son who had entered the crocodile’s mouth before, was brought back as before. Thiru-Arurar is arriving,” thus the Chera king’s devotees ran ahead and informed the Chera king. “They showered gold, jeweled ornaments, coins, and clothes.” The son who had fallen into the crocodile’s mouth was brought back to life. Thiru-Arurar Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is arriving, thus the devotees of Shiva in Chera Nadu went and informed Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. The arrival of Nambigal Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was a great and auspicious Shiva news.

“Therefore, the devotees ran ahead of the king and announced it very quickly. This shows us the nature of kings and devotees of that time, and the discipline of love. Delighted by the greatness and auspiciousness of the news, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar showered gold coins, jeweled ornaments, coins, and clothes like rain upon all those who announced this news. The seventeenth Thiru-paadal: “Not knowing what to do, with a joyful heart, overflowing with delight, my Lord has arrived.

“The Lord who rules me has arrived. The Shaivite from Thiru-Arur has arrived. My companion, my leader, has arrived. He has arrived to save the whole world.” Thus, he caused the drum of joy to be sounded. Overwhelmed with joy, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, Not knowing what to do, with a heart overflowing with joy, he exclaimed, “My Lord Nambigal has arrived! The great one who takes me as his own has arrived!

“The great Shaivite who delights in Thiru-Arur has arrived! My companion, my leader, has arrived! Nambigal, who incarnated in the world to save the entire world, has arrived!” He thus spoke in many ways and caused the drum of joy to be sounded throughout the city.

“Let us persist in our reflection.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 40 — Vellanai Charukkam 4

Thiruchitrambalam.
Periya Puranam, Part One Thousand Twenty, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Eighteenth and nineteenth Thiru-paadal-s of Vellanai Charukkam. “Having summoned the ministers skilled in abundant counsel, and having adorned the city for the arrival of the great one, and having prepared for the journey, the king, mounted on a majestic elephant, came forth to meet him on the great streets. The Chera king, whose lineage was destined to flourish, came to meet the valiant Thiru-Thondar who had entered the borders of the mountain country, bearing a victorious banner with a bow inscribed on the mountain. The Thiru-Thondar, whose feet were like freshly bloomed lotuses, worshipped him.

“Thiru-Arurar, the nectar of many arts, also worshipped and joined him.” Cheraman Perumal Nayanar summoned his ministers, who were skilled in abundant counsel, He summoned them and adorned the city for the arrival of the great Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. He also made them set out, and he himself set out. He set out riding on an elephant. He went a long distance, past the great street where kings live, to the border of the country, to meet Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, worship him, seat him on the elephant, and then follow behind him to serve him. It is a special feature of the government that the king and his ministers go to meet someone. Cheraman Peruman, who had his bow-banner inscribed on the peak of Mount Meru and had victorious armies, went to meet Nambigal, who had arrived at the border of the mountain country.

“He worshipped the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, which were like freshly bloomed lotus flowers.

“Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar went to the border of the mountain country to meet and invite him.

“Nambi Arurar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who established many arts that are like nectar and remove suffering, also worshipped Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and joined him. The twentieth Thiru-paadal: “Cheralan, whose heart rejoiced, and Thiru-Arurar, though their beautiful forms were different, were of one nature. With overflowing love, they worshipped and embraced each other. The foremost king of the Uthiya lineage, our Lord, inquired about the wealth of Thiru-Arur and rejoiced.” As mentioned before, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, whose heart rejoiced, and Nambigal of Thiru-Arur, these two great individuals, They embraced each other. Although their beautiful forms were different, they were of one nature. With great eagerness, they worshipped and embraced each other.

“Then, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the foremost king of the Chera lineage, rejoiced upon hearing about the special blessings of our Lord’s Thiru-Arur. If it is the wealth of Thiru-Arur, he rejoiced upon hearing the divine powers of the Lord of Thiru-Arur, as narrated by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The twenty-first Thiru-paadal: “The people of both sides, seeing the act of friendship of the two, who were united with each other and had no shortcomings, and whose love was high, rejoiced greatly and cheered. Perumal, the Perumal of Tamil, was seated on the elephant, and he himself followed behind.” Cheraman Perumal Nayanar held the white parasol over him.

“Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, their hearts united with each other, without any shortcomings, the people from both sides, who witnessed the act of friendship of these two, whose love was high—these were the attendants who came with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and the ministers who came with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar—these two great groups rejoiced and cheered. Cheraman Perumal seated Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Perumal of Tamil, on the elephant he had ridden, and he himself sat behind him and held the white parasol. Holding a parasol means holding it so that it casts a shadow over the head. The first time Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited the mountain country, in Thiru-Anjaikkalam, in Kodungolur, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar seated Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on an elephant, He sat behind and fanned him with a chamara. This time, since he came to the border of the mountain country to welcome him, and there was a long journey ahead, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar held the white parasol, sitting behind. The twenty-second and twenty-third Thiru-paadal-s.

“The twenty-second Thiru-paadal describes the glory from the moment both started riding on the elephant until they reached the city gate. The twenty-third Thiru-paadal describes the glory from the city gate, through many streets of the city, until they reached the king’s street. The next Thiru-paadal describes the glories until they came through the king’s street and reached the entrance of the Thiru-maligai. “The great army of the Uthiya Perumal roared like the surging ocean.

“The devotees adorned with radiant white sacred ash roared like the surging Ganges. The ministers who came to worship, their horses’ hooves raised dust. The moon, The two great ones reached the beautiful gate of Vanchi city, whose ramparts were touched by clouds. This is the twenty-second Thiru-paadal. “As the sounds of Vedic chants resounded, the Brahmins danced the Kunalai. The elephants shed ichor like rain.

“The horses’ hooves sparkled with light. Full pots were everywhere. Auspicious women showered flowers. The decorated arch entered the street. The elephants ridden by the companions entered.” The great army of the Chera king roared like the surging ocean. The crowds of devotees adorned with radiant white sacred ash roared like the surging Ganges.

“The dust raised by the hooves of the horses ridden by the ministers who came to worship spread everywhere. The moon, The two great ones reached the beautiful gate of Vanchi city, whose ramparts were touched by clouds. As the sounds of Vedic chants resounded, the Brahmins danced the Kunalai. Kunalai is a type of dance performed by clapping hands and cheering. This comes from an excess of joy. Elephants shed ichor like rain.

“Horses sparkled with light. Full pots were everywhere. Auspicious women showered flowers. The decorated arch entered the street. The elephants ridden by the companions entered. The fourth Thiru-paadal: “In the street of the wise women, as they danced, the sounds of anklets on their tender feet resounded.

“The sounds of large drums spread. The conch shells with spirals resounded in rows. The two companions, having arrived, descended from the mighty elephant they had ridden at the long gate of the beautiful palace.” In the street, the sounds of anklets on the tender feet of the dancing women resounded. The sounds of large drums spread. The conch shells with spirals resounded in rows.

“The two companions, having arrived, descended from the mighty elephant they had ridden at the long gate of the beautiful palace. The previous time Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived, it was the first time, so he was taken directly to Thiru-Anjaikkalam and then to the palace. This time, he came directly to the palace gate. The twenty-fifth Thiru-paadal: “As they scattered fresh flowers, pearls, and grains, and as the Brahmins, skilled in the four Vedas, uttered auspicious words of blessing on both sides, and as the white parasol cast its full shadow above, As the chamaras were fanned on both sides, the Lord of the Devas and his friend entered the palace. They scattered fresh flowers, pearls, and grains. Scattering these together and blessing is a traditional auspicious welcome.

“They scattered pearls, grains, and flowers and blessed them. Pearls are the most special among the abundant jewels in the mountain country. It was a situation where pearls were found in abundance in that country.

“Therefore, the custom of scattering pearls and flowers when welcoming great people is mentioned here. Even today, the scattering of golden flowers mixed with the rising of Thiyagesa Peruman of Thiru-Arur is a visible event. In front, on both sides, the sages skilled in the four Vedas uttered auspicious words of blessing. Above, the white parasol cast its full shadow, and chamaras were fanned continuously. Cheranar and his friend Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered the middle of the palace. The twenty-sixth Thiru-paadal: “As Nambigal, like a powerful young bull, shone on the royal throne, and as the mountain kings worshipped him, seated on the flower-strewn couch, they then performed good deeds of service, which are endless, and scattered many precious jewels to gladden the hearts of the attendants.” Cheraman Perumal Nayanar seats Nambigal Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on the throne, on the flower-strewn couch.

“Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the king of the mountain people, worships and requests Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to be seated there like a powerful bull on that throne. Valor, victory, and youth, These indicate a very exalted appearance, and Sekkizhar Peruman here praises him as a bull. Bull means Rishabam.

“The Lord resides on the Rishabam.

“Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the one who provides a place for the Lord, our Lord, to reside.

“Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the one who made Shiva manifest by making his body a place through Shivaraja Yoga. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performs various acts of worship for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, such as washing his feet and holding lamps. He serves the attendants on both sides to gladden their hearts. The twenty-seventh Thiru-paadal, the four thousand two hundred and fifty-fifth Thiru-paadal in Periya Puranam: “In this manner, the leader of the Uthiyas, having rejoiced and conversed sweetly with the king of Munaippadi, whose troubles were removed, During these days, the valiant Thiru-Thondar visited and praised many Thiru-pathigal (sacred places) of Paramartham, adorned with snakes, throughout the long mountain country, and entered the ancient city of Mahodai.

“Thus, during these days, Cheralan, the king of the Cheras, rejoiced and conversed sweetly with Nambigal, the king of Munaippadi, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with his troubles removed.

“Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, accompanied by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, visited and praised many Thalam-s (sacred places) where Lord Shiva resided, throughout the long mountain country. As these two great individuals visited and praised the Thiru-thalam-s of the mountain country, this world was saved from troubles.

“Thus, the Thalam-s in the mountain country visited by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar were Thiru-Chivapperur, The Thiru-thalam known as Vidaikkundrunatheswaram, which is now referred to as Thiru-chur. The Thalam worshipped by Vaikkam Karan. Thiru-chenkundrur, the Thalam of Viranminda Nayanar. Suchindram, a Thalam located in the Thiru-vitankur region. Having visited all these Shiva Thalam-s, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal returns to Mahodai, which is Thiru-Anjaikkalam and Kodungolur, with Chera Perumal.

“Let us resume our reflection.””””
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 41 — Vellanai Charukkam 5

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, authored by the Peruman, Devasekhilar, Part 1027, Vellanai Charukkam.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having visited all the Thiru-sthalams in the mountain country with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, is residing in the palace at Thiru-Anjaikkalam, Kodungolur. The twenty-eighth Thiru-padal: After many days of such activity, as the kings, beginning with Cherar, commenced their sacred bathing ritual, their companion, Van Thondar, recalled the distant, good path from which he had separated from the Lord, whose long matted locks are surrounded by the flowing Ganga, and approached Thiru-Anjaikkalam.

Thus, on a propitious day, while Sundaramurthi Swamigal was residing for many days in the palace of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, early in the morning, Cherar Peruman Nayanar set out to perform the sacred bathing ritual. The term ‘Manjana Thozhil’ refers to the act of bathing. It is customary to refer to this act as ‘Manjanam Aduthal’ when speaking of elders. Even today, in large monasteries, this practice is known as ‘Manjana Thozhil.’ Similarly, it is customary to refer to the sacred ablution or anointing of the Lord as ‘Thiru-Manjanam’ and the water used for it as ‘Thiru-Manjana Neer.’ Therefore, when speaking of elders, it is customary to equate them with the Lord, a complimentary usage, to refer to it as ‘Manjana Thozhil.’ Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar sets out for the Manjana Thozhil.

At that moment, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the inseparable companion of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, reflects. He considers that the good path, the distance he traveled after separating from Lord Shiva, whose long matted locks are adorned with the widespread Ganga, having come from Thiru-Kailash to this world, is now reaching its completion. With this thought, Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds to the Thiru-Anjaikkalam temple. We observe that wherever Sundaramurthi Swamigal went, he was always accompanied by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar.

Now, as Cheraman Perumal Nayanar begins his sacred bathing ritual, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, separating from him, has this thought, this sacred idea, and goes alone to Thiru-Anjaikkalam. He graciously proceeded. The reason for this is stated as: “The distant good path from which he had separated from the Lord is now drawing near.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal realized that the state of his separation from Lord Shiva, having been an intimate devotee in Thiru-Kailash, was now nearing its end. Previously, in Thiru-Kailash, under the name Alala Sundarar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal served as an intimate devotee, carrying the sacred garlands and the sacred ash casket for the Lord.

One day, he went to the sacred garden to pluck fresh flowers for the Lord in Kailash. Before Alala Sundarar arrived at that garden, two female companions of the Mother, who rules over all beings, came to pluck flowers for her tresses. The two companions of the Ambika, named Anindithai and Kamalini, who possessed immeasurable excellence, were selecting and plucking freshly bloomed flowers in the sacred garden. Due to the divine grace of Lord Shiva, the God of gods, and for the flourishing of the South, which had performed great penance, and for the bestowal of the flawless Thiru-Thondar Thogai, and for the prosperity of this Southern land, which had performed great penance, and for the giving of the great work called Thiru-Thondar Thogai, Alala Sundarar, by the grace of Lord Shiva, set his mind upon those maidens.

Thus, those two women, having received love, engaged in a scene of affection. Afterwards, the maidens went from the sacred garden to where Ambika was, and Alala Sundarar went to the Lord. He takes all the plucked flowers and proceeds.

At that time, the Lord, seeing Alala Sundarar’s action, graciously declared, “You have set your heart on women.

“Therefore, be born in the South, be born with those women, and then return here.” Then Alala Sundarar, with folded hands raised above his head, his mind agitated, pleaded, “O Lord, if I, being of small stature, am separated from your sacred, reddish feet and am born into a deluded human life, when I am deluded, you must come and prevent that delusion and graciously take me as your own.” The greatly compassionate Lord Shiva graciously said, “So be it.” Accordingly, Alala Sundarar and Anindithai, and Kamalini, the two companions of Ambika, were born in the South, as we saw in the Purana of Thaduthatkonda.

“Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal was born, and his purpose of incarnation was for this South to flourish and for the world’s beings to attain salvation through the Thiru-Thondar Thogai, a sacred hymn narrating the history of the Nayanmars. By the grace of Lord Shiva, this happened in Thiru-Kailash, and he was born here.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal also sang the Thiru-Thondar Thogai.

“After that, he visited many places on earth and sang hymns.

“Now, the time has come for him to return to the sacred feet of Lord Shiva in Thiru-Kailash. With this thought, he alone proceeds to Thiru-Anjaikkalam. He proceeds to the sacred temple. The twenty-ninth Thiru-padal: “Having circumambulated the temple of the dark-throated Lord, with overflowing love and compassion in his heart, he praised the Lord’s sacred feet. He began the Tamil garland, ‘Thalaikku Thala Malai,’ realizing that the difficult, turbulent worldly life was receding.” He circumambulates the Thiru-Anjaikkalam temple of the blue-throated Lord and stands before the Lord. How did he circumambulate?

“Nambigal, whose mind was completely filled with abundant and overflowing love, circumambulated the temple in that manner. His mind was entirely absorbed in the Lord through continuous contemplation. He praised the Lord in various ways, extolling His glories through hymns and other means, and worshipped His sacred feet. Standing in a state of profound devotion, he prostrated himself on the ground and rose, realizing that the turbulent worldly life was receding from him. He then began the Tamil garland, the Thiru-padikam, with the refrain “Thalaikku Thala Malai.” Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who was greatly beloved in this worldly life, enjoying the happiness of his two consorts, the royal wealth and life of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the crowned king, and the joy intertwined with the divine, the life intertwined with joy, and the abundant grace of the Lord, now comes to seek liberation, rejecting such worldly domestic life. He seeks liberation.

“Therefore, this is praised by the Peruman, Deivasekkilar, as a rare deed. The Thirukkural states that those who perform rare deeds are great. This worldly life is a turbulent life. Turbulence is the cause of joys and sorrows, and the desires and aversions that arise from them, and the repeated births that result from them. Deivasekkilar Peruman states that this worldly life should recede. ‘Saridhal’ means to fall off by itself. The scriptures state that it is like a sleeping person’s hand becoming empty, meaning detachment.

“If a person holds an object in their hand while sleeping, after they sleep soundly, the object in their hand will automatically fall off.

“Similarly, this worldly life should fall off by itself.

“Sundaramurthi Swamigal, The inner meaning of the thirtieth Thiru-padal, which he composed, is that the devotee, with love, sings that the life entangled in worldly attachments should be severed. The Lord, whose throat is dark from consuming poison, is in Thiru-Kailash with His divine retinue. The act of preventing him from being there and sending him to this world is now completed, and the Lord grants him His proximity. The inner meaning of this Thiru-padal is that Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the devotee, sings with great love that the life entangled in worldly attachments should be severed. This is the sacred intention of Sundaramurthi Swamigal when he sings this Thiru-padal. This Thiru-padikam is a supplication to the Lord’s sacred feet, praying that this worldly life, which is bound by the attachment that causes one to come into this world, should be severed.

“This Thiru-padikam, composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal, belongs to the Thiru-Anjaikkalam Thiru-sthalam. It is the ninety-ninth Thiru-padikam in the Seventh Thiru-murai, set to the Indalappann tune. In the eighth verse of this Thiru-padikam, Sundaramurthi Swamigal has sung, “I have renounced worldly life, I have abandoned it.” Based on this, Deivasekkilar Peruman states that Sundaramurthi Swamigal composed this Thiru-padikam, supplicating that the life entangled in worldly attachments should be severed. In this Thiru-padikam, the questions that appear in the first verse, “Enne,” and in the second, fifth, and sixth verses, are structured as if the speaker, addressing the Lord and describing His attributes, asks as if he does not know. The Thiru-padikam is structured as if the speaker, addressing the Lord and describing His attributes, asks as if he does not know. The Thiru-padikam is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.

“What is it that you wear a garland of skulls? What is it that you bear the flood of Ganga on your matted locks? What is it that you wear a tiger skin as a loincloth? What is it that a furious serpent coils around it? O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where waves, like mountains, crash and roar, bringing conch shells, what is it that you wear a serpent, making it dance? What is it that you wear the crescent moon on your shining matted locks?

“What is it that you smear your entire body with sacred ash? What is it that you delight in riding a spotted bull? What is it that the strong waves crash and roar, bringing conch shells, and pearls from the ocean, O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where the waves crash and roar, bringing conch shells, you are like a gooseberry to those who meditate and arise. If the small and the great enter your mind, those who worship you first will not die, will not be born. O Lord of ascetics, O Lord of gods, you are like the red sky at dusk, O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where the waves, like vast mountains, constantly churn. You are the life of the letters that are formed.

“You are the leaf. You are the existence. You are the cloud for the sprouting crops. You are the refuge for your devotees. O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where the waves, like mountains, crash and roar, bringing conch shells. O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where the waves, like mountains, crash and roar, bringing conch shells, what is the purpose of liberation?

“What is the purpose of birth? What is the purpose of riding a bull? What is it that you wear Ganga on your matted locks, with a mountain maiden beside you, while a mad elephant stands? What is the meaning of your grace to singing poets? What is the meaning of the many treasures and the chaotic expenses? O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the dancing ocean, what is it that you dance in the fire of the cremation ground at night?

“What is it that you collect alms in the skulls of the dead? What is the great treasure of those who worship you? O Supreme Lord, O Parameshti, graciously command! O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where conches and pearls are brought by the waves, crashing and roaring, what is it that you are the creation and destruction, I say? I say that you are the words they speak. I say that you are the tongue, the ears, and the eyes.

“I say that you are goodness. Now I have understood you well. O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where many treasures are cast into the throat and churned, I have renounced worldly life, I have abandoned it. O Brahmin with shining earrings, you are the one who caused the ten heads and many shoulders of the king of Lanka to fall. You are the one who drank the poison from the ocean and have a dark throat. You are the one who cut off one of the five heads of Brahma.

“O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean, you are the male elephant for the female elephant, my Lord. You are the Lord of Brahma and other great beings. You are the one who shot the bow and burned the three cities in an instant. I will not forget you. O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the ocean where the strong waves, like those being carved, crash and roar, bringing conch shells. Our Lord, the Lord of the celestials, is the one with the jewel-like throat that always destroys me.

“O Lord of Anjaikkalam, with beautiful groves, on the shore of the cool ocean, Nambi Urar, the king of Navalar, sang these sweet Tamil garlands with the sound of drums and flutes. Those who can fall at His feet with these garlands will not falter.

“Thus, as Nambigal, the devotee, made his sacred supplication, the Lord, whose throat shone with the poison, graciously commanded him to be with His divine retinue in Thiru-Kailash, as He had previously prevented him and commanded him to be born in this world. The extent of that act, which previously prevented Alala Sundarar from being with the Shiva Ganas and sent him to this earthly world for the flourishing of the South, which had performed great penance, and for the bestowal of the flawless Thiru-Thondar Thogai, is now fulfilled.

“The Lord grants him His sacred feet. Let us go on contemplating this.”
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 42 — Vellanai Charukkam 6

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, authored by the Peruman, Devasekkilar, Part 1028, Vellanai Charukkam. When our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal made his supplication for liberation, Lord Shiva granted him the proximity of His sacred feet, as the period of his being prevented from being with the Shiva Ganas in Thiru-Kailash and being commanded to be born in this world had come to an end. The thirty-first Thiru-padal: “When the Lord, the rider of the victorious white bull, was seated with the mountain goddess in the fragrant, flower-filled Kailash, He commanded Brahma and other gods, ‘Bring our Nambi Ooran, whose mind is one with Us, on the divine white elephant.’” Thiru-Kailash is a beautiful, fragrant Kailash. In the beautiful Kailash, where fragrant trees are dense, the Lord is seated on the Rishabha with the Mountain Goddess, His consort.

At that time, He commands Brahma and other gods, saying, “Go and bring our Nambi Aruran, whose thoughts are united with Ours, on the white elephant.” Lord Shiva refers to Sundaramurthi Swamigal as “Nambi Ooran, whose mind is one with Us” through His divine utterance. “One mind” means a state of being completely absorbed in the Lord, without duality, through Shiva Raja Yoga. The Devaram states, “Be united and remember.” Thus, He commands them to bring Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who is in such a state, on the white elephant to Thiru-Kailash. He commands them to bring him to Thiru-Kailash. This white elephant is the white elephant in Thiru-Kailash, Airavatham, the vehicle of the Lord.

Thus, He commands. The thirty-second Thiru-padal: “The celestial beings, led by Hari and Ayan, bowed and received permission. They brought the pure, auspicious, luminous white elephant for Van Thondar and arrived at Thiru-Anjaikkalam in Mahodai, surrounded by honey-laden groves. Seeing him there, they circumambulated the strong walled sacred gate.” The gods, Vishnu, Brahma, and others, having heard the divine command of Lord Shiva, bowed and received permission. They brought the pure, auspicious, luminous white elephant for Van Thondar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, and arrived at Thiru-Anjaikkalam in Mahodai. Thiru-Anjaikkalam in Mahodai.

Mahodai is the capital of the Cheras, where Thiru-Kodungolur, Anjaikkalam, and other places are located. All the gods arrive there. The gods are beings whose feet do not touch the ground. Such gods, to fulfill the divine command of the Lord, come to Thiru-Anjaikkalam, their feet touching the ground, accompanied by the white elephant.

At the same time, Sundaramurthi Swamigal circumambulates the Thiru-Anjaikkalam temple and reaches the walled sacred gate. The thirty-third Thiru-padal: “At the gate crowded with the host of gods, Thiru-Navalurar, the guardian king, came forth. The gods, praising it as the command of the Lord, whose matted locks bear the flower-laden waters of Kailash, and considering it a command, humbly rose and received him, having nothing else to do.” As mentioned before, through the sacred gate of the temple, where the host of gods stood crowded, Thiru-Navalurar, the guardian king, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, emerged after completing his worship. This sacred gate is considered to be the gate facing the Thiru-Anjaikkalam temple. The signs of the elephant arriving and Nambigal departing are inscribed here, which we can still see today.

As our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal emerged, all the gods came forward to meet him and praised him, saying, “This is the divine command of the Lord.” The Lord, who is seated in Thiru-Kailash, whose matted locks bear the cool waters of the Ganga, overflowing with flowers such as the Konrai, gave this divine command. They say that “Arulipaadu” means command. It is the traditional way of conveying the Lord’s command, and it is called the Lord’s Arulipaadu. This is also a summons. All the Devas, bowing to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, praise and say, “It is the Lord’s command that you be brought on an elephant.” They elaborate on the Lord’s command. Hearing this, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, having no other course of action, bows and rises.

Generally, in the world, when the command of Yama and others comes, it also comes according to the will of Lord Shiva. When the command of Yama and others, which comes according to the will of Lord Shiva, arrives, the beings of this world depart without any other action. In such a state, This is the Lord’s command. When it came directly, what else could one do but comply and proceed?

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal accepts and stands by the command of Lord Shiva. The thirty-fourth Thiru-paadal: “Etra thondarai andar vellanaayin ethir valam kondu etra naatradangadal muzhakku ena aivagai naatham meethu ezhundhu aarppotri vaanavar poo mazhai pozhindhida podhuvaar uyiriyellam saatrum maatrangal unar perum thunaivarai manathinil kodu saarndhaar.” The Devas convey the command of Lord Shiva, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal accepts it and gives his consent. All the Devas circumambulate and worship Sundaramurthi Swamigal, then lift him onto the white elephant they brought. The great seas in the four directions The five types of divine drums rose and sounded like the roar of the seas. It was the roar of the sea surrounding Thiru-Anjaikkalam. We saw how Sundaramurthi Swamigal, in the Thalaikku Thalaimalai Thiru-padigam, glorified the roar of the Thiru-Anjaikkalam sea in all the verses.

Therefore, the sea roared greatly.

Similarly, the five types of divine drums rose and roared, suppressing the roar of the sea and rising above it. The sounding of the divine drums and the showering of flowers by the celestials are among the manifestations of Lord Shiva’s grace that we have been witnessing throughout the Puranas. This is happening everywhere now.

Here, the manifestation of the Lord’s grace is Sundaramurthi Swamigal severing his worldly attachments in this world and It is Sundaramurthi Swamigal going to Shiva’s world on the white elephant with the same body. As he goes, Nambigal Sundaramurthi Swamigal ascends the white elephant, remembering Cheraman Perumal Nayanar in his mind. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, by the grace of Lord Shiva, had the ability to understand the words spoken by all beings.

Therefore, he also has the name Kalarittrarivar. He is a great companion, a friend and great helper to Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Other companions in this world would remain bound by worldly connections.

But here, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, unlike them, accompanies Sundaramurthi Swamigal even as he goes to Thiru-Kailash.

Moreover, even after reaching Thiru-Kailash, he will always be there. He is also destined to remain an inseparable companion, seated permanently.

Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal remembers Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, his great companion and friend, in his mind. Having received the command of Lord Shiva’s grace, he is in a state where he must immediately accept and depart. Our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal had arrived at Thiru-Anjaikkalam without being able to inform and bid farewell to his friend Cheraman Perumal Nayanar when the latter was engaged in the bathing ritual at the palace in Kodungolur.

Therefore, now, in a state where he must immediately accept the Lord’s command and depart, he remembers his friend Cheraman Perumal Nayanar in his mind and ascends the white elephant. To sever the worldly attachments, he had sung in the Thiru-padigam, “I detested worldly life, leaving my wife and home, I, your servant.” If we consider that Sundaramurthi Swamigal had sung this, then why does Sundaramurthi Swamigal remember only this companion, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, who is praised as a companion, and proceed? If this is not worldly attachment, then it is not. His connection is not a connection of worldly attachment, but a connection related to Shiva. Their friendship occurred by the Lord’s bringing together.

Therefore, their friendship arose from love related to the Lord.

Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, remembering no one else, ascends the white elephant, thinking only of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. Let us go on contemplating.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 43 — Vellanai Charukkam 7

Thiruchitrambalam.
Periya Puranam, Part One Thousand Twenty-Nine, Vellanai Charukkam, Thirty-Fifth Thiru-padal, composed by Theivasekkizhar Peruman. “Cherar thambiraan thambiraan thozhatham seyal arindhu appodhe saara nindradhor pariyinai misai kondu thiruvanjaikkalam saarvaar veera venkaliru ugaithu vinmel sellum meyththondar thamai kandaar paaril nindrilar sendra tham manathodu pariyum mun selavittaar.” Cheraman Perumal Nayanar immediately understands the actions of Thambiran Thozhar, Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Cherar Thambiran, meaning king or elder, is a traditional term still used in the mountainous regions. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar understands the action that Sundaramurthi Swamigal held in his mind. He understands.

Cheraman Perumal Nayanar understands everything: Sundaramurthi Swamigal leaving him, coming to Thiru-Anjaikkalam, making his plea for liberation, the Devas coming with the white elephant and conveying the Lord’s command, Sundaramurthi Swamigal accepting it, ascending the white elephant, and ascending to the heavens while remembering him in his mind. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar understood all this while he was in the palace at Kodungolur. His understanding was due to the powerful mental communication of Nambigal Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who intended to inform him. The fact that one’s thoughts can enter the mind of someone with a similar mental resonance and make those thoughts known to their consciousness is an experiential truth that even psychologists cannot deny. The reason this happened here is The deep mental connection and supportive bond between our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar was a connection centered on Lord Shiva. The friendship between these two was a pure, imperishable, divine connection of souls, born of their love for the Lord.

In contrast, all other worldly connections are perishable bonds of attachment.

Similarly, we see the power of the mental feeling of Apputhi Adigal Nayanar, who was devoted to love, service, and servitude, which, without his own knowledge, led him to the home of Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman. We see the same here. Having understood this, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar mounts a horse there and goes to Thiru-Anjaikkalam.

Then he saw Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the true devotee, riding the valiant white elephant and ascending into the sky. This was a sight seen by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar as he was about to depart for Thiru-Anjaikkalam. When Cheraman Perumal Nayanar looked at the sky, he saw Sundaramurthi Swamigal traveling in the sky on an elephant. After Sundaramurthi Swamigal mounted the elephant on the ground, the elephant traveled on land, then through the sea, after the king of the sea came forward with flowers and made way, and then ascended into the sky. These were the stages that had occurred until then.

Sundaramurthi Swamigal will describe this in the Thiru-padigam itself. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar saw Sundaramurthi Swamigal thus ascending into the sky. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, unable to bear being separated from Sundaramurthi Swamigal, did not wish to remain on the ground for even a moment. His mind was directed towards Sundaramurthi Swamigal. With a speed matching that mental velocity, he urged his horse forward. The thirty-sixth Thiru-padal: “Vitta vem pari seviyinil puvi muthal vendhar thaam vidhiyaalai ittamaam siva mandiram othalin iru visumbu ezhappaayndhu mattalarndha paintheriyal van thondar mel konda maathangathai mutta eydhi valam kondu sendradhu matra adhan munnaaga.” As mentioned before, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar urges his horse forward.

Then, into the ear of his beloved horse, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar recites the Shiva mantra, which is his chosen mantra, according to the prescribed rules. He is the first king of the earth, born into the Chera lineage, considered foremost among the three Tamil kings. Such a Such a Chera lineage is the only one that, even today, in some way, continues to rule and exist. Such a first king of the earth, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, issues a command according to the rules of the Shiva Agamas. He recited the Shiva mantra, which was his chosen, readily available, and cherished mantra, into the ear of the horse. It is a Shiva mantra that fulfills desires.

This Shiva mantra is something to be learned from experienced Desikas who are well-versed in the Shiva Agamas. As soon as the Shiva mantra was recited, the horse, which was running on the ground, rose into the sky and galloped. This is the Sri Panchaksharam that makes the horse move swiftly. It is referred to here as the Shiva mantra. Among the variations of the Ashtakarma Panchaksharam, it is a division of the Ucchadana Karma, It is a mantra belonging to the Sri Panchaksharam. It is the Ashvamanogati Panchaksharam, which falls under the Mriga Ucchadana Panchaksharam, as noted by Sri Sivaprakasa Gurukkal Aiyya.

Therefore, such a Sri Panchaksharam, the Ashvamanogati Panchaksharam, which makes the horse swiftly ascend into the sky, is referred to here as the Shiva mantra by Theivasekkizhar Peruman. As soon as that Shiva mantra was recited into the horse’s ear, the horse, which was running on the ground, rose and galloped in the sky. One might ask if a horse running on the ground can fly. Today, we see even inanimate objects like machines rising from the ground and flying into the sky due to the force of an inanimate power. If an inanimate object can fly in such a way, then it is easy for a horse, a living and conscious being, to fly in the sky through the power of a spiritual mantra. It is easy.

The horse that thus rose went and joined the side of the white elephant on which the valiant devotee, adorned with a fresh garland of blooming flowers, was riding in the sky. And that horse circumambulated the white elephant and went ahead of it.

Now, the thirty-seventh Thiru-padal: “Udiya mannavar tham perum senaiyin udan sendra padaiveerar kadhikkul vaasiyil selbavar pulappadum ellai ethir visumbinil kandu pin kandilar aadhaliin ellaarum mudhirum anbinil uruviya surigaiyaal muraimurai udal veezhndhaar.” The soldiers who went with the great army of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar saw their king, who was moving at great speed in the sky, on a horse in the sky, up to the limit of their vision.

After that, they could not see him.

Therefore, all those soldiers, who had a mature love for Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, cast off their bodies and followed their king in their subtle bodies. This is the action of the Chera king’s soldiers, born of the maturity of their love for their king and Nayanar.

Thus far, these were victorious soldiers who had never known defeat. They cast off their bodies with their daggers out of the maturity of their love for the Chera king, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, unable to bear separation from him.

Then, They attained the heroic body, the Veera Yaakkai. Having attained that heroic body, they did this to always serve their king. The thirty-eighth Thiru-padal: “Veera yaakkaiyai mel kondu sendru poy villavar perumaanai saara mun sendru sevagam yetranar thani thondar mel konda vaazhum mummathathu aruvi vellanaikku vayappari mun vaithu serar veerarum sendranar mandravar thirumalai thisai nokki.” Thus, those soldiers who had cast off their bodies, having attained the subtle body of a hero, went ahead of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and continued to serve him. Soldiers who sacrifice their bodies in battle for their country attain the subtle body of a hero, known as Veera Yaakkai, and are said to attain Veerasvargam (hero’s heaven).

Therefore, these soldiers, They attained the heroic body, the Veera Yaakkai. Having attained that heroic body, they did this to always serve their king. The thirty-eighth Thiru-padal: “Veera yaakkaiyai mel kondu sendru poy villavar perumaanai saara mun sendru sevagam yetranar thani thondar mel konda vaazhum mummathathu aruvi vellanaikku vayappari mun vaithu serar veerarum sendranar mandravar thirumalai thisai nokki.” Thus, those soldiers who had cast off their bodies, having attained the subtle body of a hero, went ahead of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and continued to serve him. Soldiers who sacrifice their bodies in battle for their country attain the subtle body of a hero, known as Veera Yaakkai, and are said to attain Veerasvargam (hero’s heaven).

Therefore, these soldiers, With subtle bodies, they went to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and served him. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar is praised here as Villavar Peruman (Lord of the Archers). This name comes from the Cheras having a bow as their emblem.

Moreover, even today, we see the image of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar holding a bow at Thiru-Anjaikkalam. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, having accepted the service of his warriors, arranges for his victorious horse to go ahead of the peerless great devotee Sundaramurthi Swamigal’s white elephant.

Thus, in the celestial path, the white elephant proceeds towards Thiru-Kailash, the sacred mountain, and in front of that white elephant, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar’s horse goes. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar rides on it. Cheranar, the heroic leader of the Cheras, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceed towards the direction of Ambalavanar’s Kailash, the sacred mountain, in the celestial path.

We shall continue our contemplation.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 44 — Vellanai Charukkam 8

Thiruchitrambalam.
Periya Puranam, Part One Thousand Thirty, Vellanai Charukkam, Thirty-Ninth Thiru-padal, composed by Theivasekkizhar Peruman. “Yaanai melkondu selgindra pozhudhinil imaiyavar kuzhaam ennum thaanai mun sela thaan ena munpadaithaan enum thamizh maalai maana vanthondar paadi mun anaindhaar madhi nadhi podhi veni thenalambu than kondraiyaar thirumalai thendhisai thiruvaayil.” As our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal rides on the white elephant, the army of celestials proceeds before him.

Then, the glorious valiant devotee Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings the Tamil garland beginning with “Thaan ena munpadaithaan” and approaches. He sings “Thaan enai munpadaithaan.” This is the first line of the padigam sung by Sundaramurthi Swamigal on his way to Mount Kailash. This Thiru-padigam, composed by our Lord Sundaramurthi Swamigal, is dedicated to Thiru-Nodithanmalai, which is Mount Kailash, and is set in the musical mode of Panchamam. It is the hundredth Thiru-padigam in the seventh Thiru-murai composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal. As Sundaramurthi Swamigal rides on the white elephant towards Thiru-Kailash, he sings this padigam, his heart melting with thoughts of the Lord’s grace. This Thiru-padigam expresses profound admiration for the Lord’s immense grace.

These Thiru-padigams In the voice of our revered Oduvar Murthigal, Thiru-Kazhukundram Mahadevan Aiyya, Thiru-Nodithanmalai, Thiru-Kailash, praise to the sacred feet of Swami Thiru-Paramasivan, praise to the sacred feet of Devi Thiru-Parvati Ammai.

As he rides on the elephant, as the army of celestials proceeds before him, “Thaan enai munpadaithaan” (He created me first). The Tamil garland, “Thaan enai munpadaithaan,” the Tamil garland, “Thaan enai munpadaithaan,” the glorious valiant devotee sang and approached the southern sacred gate of the sacred mountain of the Lord with the moon and river in his braided hair, adorned with fragrant konrai flowers. The southern sacred gate of the sacred mountain, “Thaan enai munpadaithaan,” “Thaan enai munpadaithaan,” knowing that, at his golden feet. Knowing that He created me first at His golden feet, what can I, a dog, sing? What can I, a dog, sing, considering me?

What can I, a dog, sing, considering me? The sky came to me, the fierce elephant, granting grace, the sky came to me, the fierce elephant. Maṟṟa yāṉai aruḷ purintu mattayāṉai aruḷ purintu ūṉuyir vēṟu ceytāḷ ēṉuyir vēṟu ceytā noṭittāṉ malaivutta maṉē ūṉuyir vēṟu ceytā noṭittāṉ malaivutta maṉi āṉai poṟitta pakai aṭiya noṭu nīḷa kolō Uyir veruṭṭi oḷi yāṉai iṇaintiruntē vāṉai matittamara palam ceyteṉṉai ēṟa vaikka yāṉai aruḷ purinta noṭittāṉ malaivuttamaṉē mantiram oṉṟaṟiyēṉ maṉai vāḻkkai makiḻntatiyē cuntara vēṭaṅkaḷāl turicē ceyyum toṇṭaṉ eṉṉai antaramāḷ vicuṅkil aḻakāṉai Aruḷ purintatu uṉ taramō neñcamē noṭittāṉ malai uttamaṉē vāḻvai pukanta neñcē maṭavā taṅkaḷ valviṉaippaṭṭu āḻa pukanta eṉṉai atu māṟṟi amararella cūḻa aruḷ purintu toṇṭaṉēṉ param allatoru vēḻam aruḷ purinta noṭittāṉ malai uttamaṇi maṇṇulakil piṟantu Nummai vāḻttum vaḻiyaṭiyā poṇṇulakam peṟutal toṇṭaṉēṉ eṉṟu kaṇṭoḻintē peṇṇulaka taparkaḷ virumpa veḷḷai yāṉaiyiṉ mēṭal kāṭṭuvittāṉ noṭittāṉ malai uttamaṉē añciṉai oṉṟi niṉṟu alaṟ koṇṭaṭi cēvariyā vañcaṉai eṉ maṉamē vakivāṉa naṉ nāṭar muṉṉē Tuñcutal māṟṟu vittu toṇṭaṉ ēṉ param mallatōru piṉciṉa āṇai tanta noṭittāṉ malai uttamaṇi nilai kaṭa veṇ natīra nilam eṅkum atirnta ceyya malaiyaṭai yāṉai ēṟi vaḻiyē varuvēṇṭiyatirē alai kaṭalla aṟaiyaṉ alaṟkoṇṭu muṉvantirañca pulaiyaṉaiyātavaṇṇō noṭittāṉ Malai uttamaṇi aṟaoli yāka maṅkaḷ aṟivārari tōttiraṅkaḷ tiraviya vēdavoli piṉṉellām vantu etirntucaiparamaḷivāṉaṉ vantu vaḻi tantu eṉakkēṟuvatō ciramaḷiyāṉai tantā noṭittāṉ malai uttamaṇi intiraṉ mantiramaṉ eḻil ārmiku tēvarellā vantetīrkoṇṭu Mattayāṉai aruḷ purintu mantiramā muṉivar ivaṉ ñāyiṉaiyam veṟumā nam tamaṟuvūraṉ eṉṟāṉ noṭittāṉ malaivutta maṉē kūḻitōr ūḻi muṟṟum kuyarpoṉ noṭittāṉ malaiyai kūḻitōr ūḻi muṟṟum kuyarpoṉ noṭittāṉ malaiyai cūḻicaiyiṉ karumpil cuvai nāvala ūraṉ coṉṉa Ēḻicaiyiṉ tamiḻā icaintēttiya pattinaiyum ēḻicaiyiṉ tamiḻā icaintēttiya pattinaiyum āḻi kaṭalaiyā añcai appaṟku aṟivippatē āḻi kaṭalaiyā añcai appaṟku aṟivippatē añcai apparkkaṟivippatē añcai appaṟku aṟivippatē ā Then, prostrating before the Lord with the eye on His forehead, he arose, and like a calf that finds its mother cow after being separated, he swiftly approached with love. We shall ever contemplate the unique and great devotee who stood and praised, whom the world declared to be one with the Lord, who is united with the beautiful-waisted Goddess.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 45 — Vellanai Charukkam 9

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Thiru-Periya Puranam, composed by Thiru-Devasekkizhar Peruman, Part 1031, Vellanai Charukkam. We saw that our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was proceeding towards Thiru-Kailasa Mountain, having sung the Thiru-Pathikam beginning with “Than ena munpaṭaittāṉ” (He created me first). The commentator of Thiru-Periya Puranam, Sivakavimani Sambandha Saraṇalaya Swamigal, has provided a commentary for this Thiru-Pathikam. The first Thiru-Padal, “Than ena munpaṭaittāṉ,” means that the Lord, Siva-Peruman, commanded me to appear in the southern direction and created me to be born in Thiru-Navalur. This also signifies a divine creation.

He created me, His eternal servant, to manifest His grace in this world. I was unaware of this divine grace before. During that time of ignorance, the Lord Himself came here, engaged in a dispute, and made me His servant.

After that, I realized that the Lord had commanded me to appear in the southern direction and thus caused my birth. Having understood this, and having fully comprehended the significance of that divine grace, alas, what songs did I sing for the Lord’s golden feet? Not even a little. I did not.

Moreover, my songs are very humble. Yet, considering me, a dog-like being, and hearing the Thiru-Anjaikkalam Thiru-Pathikam, which was a plea for liberation, He considered even me, a lowly being like a dog, as significant. He caused Brahma and all the celestial beings to come and receive me, and bestowed the white elephant. Siva-Peruman, for my sake, graciously commanded Airavatham, the white elephant that serves as His vehicle, to carry me. It is said that He sent the devas to receive and escort me. This serves as internal evidence for the life story of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

We see this state of mind, where he repeatedly remembers and becomes one with the greatness of divine grace, in all the subsequent verses. We see the state of being. “He gave the elephant to carry me,” “He bestowed the beautiful elephant in the vast sky,” “He bestowed the elephant, though I, His servant, was unworthy, surrounded by all the celestials,” “He caused my body to be seen upon the white elephant,” “He gave the fierce elephant,” “He rode the elephant,” and “He gave the elephant,” and “He bestowed the other elephant”—all these are found as internal evidence in the second, third, fourth, fifth, and ninth verses respectively.

Thus, by causing him to ride the white elephant, He separated the soul from the flesh. “Flesh” refers to the body with its instruments and faculties, and “soul” refers to the Atman. This separation of flesh and soul means that even while the flesh is in this world, He transformed it. This means transforming the pasu-karanas (instruments of the soul) into pati-karanas (instruments of the Lord), and making this body, like a lamp in daylight, merge into the light of grace. Separating the soul means making it understand comprehensively, not partially through illusory instruments, by merging it into the Lord’s light of grace.

The Lord who did this is the supreme God residing on Thiru-Kailasa Mountain. Such a Peruman is the Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai. Thiru-Nodittanmalai is Thiru-Kailasa Mountain. “Nodittal” means destruction. “Nodittal” means sarva-samharam (total destruction), maha-samharam (great destruction).

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, “O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, the mountain of such a destroyer.” The second verse is “Ana uritta.” O enemy who flayed the elephant, will I return? I, who, having been terrified by the flesh and soul, meditated on the radiant One, the Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, bestowed the elephant to carry me, after the celestials, valuing the heavens, circumambulated me. The enmity of flaying the elephant was a past act of anger, an act of grace. Was it to remove that enmity that He now bestowed the elephant upon me, His servant, as a blessing, an act of grace? Was it so that this enmity might be removed?

Thus, having been terrified by the flesh and soul, and desiring the liberation of the soul, I despised the body and meditated day and night, without forgetting, on the radiant One, the all-knowing, the effulgent light of wisdom. “Olliyan” refers to the Lord, who is the effulgence of wisdom. Having meditated on such a radiant One, the celestials, who greatly valued the pleasures of the celestial world, circumambulated me, and Siva-Peruman, who resides on Thiru-Kailasa, the mountain of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, bestowed the Airavatha elephant to carry me. The third verse: “I know no mantra, O mind, I, a servant who delights in domestic life and commits only faults with beautiful guises, is it proper for You, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, to bestow the beautiful elephant in the vast sky upon me?” “I know no mantra,” O mind, I have never chanted a mantra according to the rules, not even for a moment. I know nothing of mantra recitation. “I, a servant who delights in domestic life and commits only faults with beautiful guises,” I, a staunch devotee who delights in living with my wife in domestic life, and adorned with beautiful guises, even divine guises, commits only faults.

The Lord bestowed the Airavatha elephant upon me. This verse expresses his lament as he speaks to his own mind. “With beautiful guises” refers to the Lord’s divine guises, such as the kavacha kundalas (earrings), sacred ash, rudraksha beads, and other such adornments. “Turisu” means fault. We have seen in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s Thiru-Pathikams, “I am complicit in the faults I commit.” Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings that even though he committed faults while adorned in beautiful guises and delighting in domestic life, the Lord bestowed His grace upon him. It is the nature of great souls to thus lament and become one with the mind.

We shall see this same nature in the other verses. We shall see. The fourth verse: “O mind that loved life, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, who rescued me, immersed in the strong karmas of women, and graciously bestowed upon me, Your servant, that peerless elephant, though I was utterly unworthy, surrounded by all the celestials.” O mind that desired this worldly life, Siva-Peruman, who resides on Thiru-Kailasa Mountain, rescued me, who was deeply immersed in the powerful karmas of women, from that state, and graciously bestowed upon me, His servant, that peerless Airavatha elephant, though I was utterly unworthy, surrounded by all the celestials. “Life” refers to worldly life.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus sings, “me, who was deeply immersed in that worldly life.” The fifth verse: “O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, who caused my body to appear upon the white elephant, desired by the inhabitants of the celestial world, I, Your servant, have realized that devotees who are born in this earthly world and praise You will attain the golden world.” O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, Siva-Peruman, who resides on Thiru-Kailasa Mountain, who graciously caused my body to appear upon the Airavatha elephant, desired by all the celestials! I have seen something with my own eyes. What is it? I, Your servant, have now seen with my own eyes the meaning of the ancient saying that true devotees, who are born in this earthly world and praise You, will attain Siva-Loka, the golden world. It is said, “He caused my body to appear.” This implies that only the body was on the white elephant, while the soul was united with Siva.

The sixth verse: “My deceitful mind, which, clinging to the five senses, knows not how to offer flowers and attain Your feet, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, who, before the good people of the heavens, removed my slumber and bestowed upon me, Your servant, that peerless, fierce elephant, though I was unworthy.” My deceitful mind, which, clinging to the five senses and dwelling in them, knows not how to offer flowers and attain the Lord’s feet! He, Siva-Peruman, removed this cycle of birth and death, this suffering of being born and dying in this world, before all the great celestials, and bestowed upon me, His servant, that peerless elephant, though I was unworthy. He sings that Siva-Peruman bestowed the peerless elephant, though I was unworthy. The seventh verse: “As the sky trembled and the earth everywhere shook, I came riding the elephant through the mountains, and before me, Varuna, the king of the ocean, came with flowers to worship, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, so that I would not be troubled.” As the stability of this world was disturbed, as the celestial world trembled, and as the entire earth shook, I came riding the white elephant. This white elephant was so magnificent that even the greatness of mountains would be defeated by it. Such was the glorious elephant.

Riding upon it, the Lord first proceeded through the mountain paths on His way to Thiru-Kailasa, then through the ocean, and then through the sky. He graciously proceeded.

Then, He graciously caused Varuna, the king of the ocean, to come before Him with flowers, with his waves as hands, and worship Him.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this. We saw in the Puranam of Thiru-Navukkarasu Peruman, “Varuna also performed great penance in the past, bearing the compassionate King of Tongues on his head with his wave-hands, in the manner of clear wisdom.” Here let us ponder this. This illustrates the special status of devotees. It is understood that Varuna, offering flowers, made way through the ocean, and Nambigal (Sundarar) rode upon the elephant through that path. The eighth verse: “As the sounds of Dharma, the Agamas, the praises known to the wise, and the Vedic chants resounded throughout the heavens, the benevolent Vāṇan came, made way, and gave me an elephant to ride, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai.” The sounds of Dharma, the names of Siva, the chanting of Siva’s names, “Hara Hara,” resounded.

The Lord proceeds on the elephant. The celestials who accompanied Nambigal and rode with him on the elephant chanted “Hara Hara.” The celestials are superior in penance and knowledge.

Therefore, among them were those skilled in the Vedas, Agamas, and stotras. The Vedic chants, mixed with the praises known to the great ones who understood the Agamas, resounded there. All the celestials in the celestial world came, stood before Him, and praised Him. Such was the chanting.

Moreover, the people on earth, as far as their eyes could see, The chanting that resounded as they saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal ascending to the heavens made the sky tremble. The glorious Vāṇasura came and guided the way, and Siva-Peruman, the Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, gave me an excellent elephant to ride. “Vāṇan” refers to Vāṇasura, a leader of Siva’s ganas. The ninth verse: “As Indra, Vishnu, Brahma, and all the beautiful celestials came to receive me, He bestowed the fierce elephant upon me, and when the great sages asked, ‘Who is this?’ our Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai said, ‘He is our own Uraṇ.’” As Indra, Vishnu, Brahma, and all the beautiful celestials came to receive me, a humble one, the Lord bestowed the fierce elephant, Airavatham. And when the great sages, who are well-versed in all the Vedas and Agamas, asked, “Who is this?” Vedic chants resounded throughout the heavens, and the benevolent Vāṇan came, made way, and gave me an elephant to ride, O Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai.

The sounds of Dharma, the names of Hara Hara, the names of Siva, the chanting of Siva’s names.

The Lord proceeds on the elephant. The chanting of “Hara Hara” was uttered by the celestials who accompanied Nambigal and rode with him on the elephant. The celestials are superior in penance and knowledge.

Therefore, among them were those skilled in the Vedas, Agamas, and stotras. The Vedic chants, mixed with the praises known to the great ones who understood the Agamas, resounded there. All the celestials in the celestial world came, stood before Him, and praised Him. Such was the chanting.

Moreover, the people on earth, as far as their eyes could see, The chanting that resounded as they saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal ascending to the heavens made the sky tremble. The glorious Vāṇasura came and guided the way, and Siva-Peruman, the Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai, gave me an excellent elephant to ride. “Vāṇan” refers to Vāṇasura, a leader of Siva’s ganas. The ninth verse: “As Indra, Vishnu, Brahma, and all the beautiful celestials came to receive me, He bestowed the fierce elephant upon me, and when the great sages asked, ‘Who is this?’ our Lord of Thiru-Nodittanmalai said, ‘He is our own Uraṇ.’” As Indra, Vishnu, Brahma, and all the beautiful celestials came to receive me, a humble one, the Lord bestowed the fierce elephant, Airavatham. And when the great sages, who are well-versed in all the Vedas and Agamas, When the great Shaivite sages asked, “Who is this approaching?” Lord Shiva replied, “He is Our Own, Our Thiruvarooran.” Thus spoke the Peruman, who is enshrined on Thiru Nodi-thanmalai, also known as Thiru Kailaimalai. How immense is His great compassion!

This Thiru-padal mentions the ‘Mantra Maamunivar,’ referring to the eminent Shiva sages, such as Upamanyu Muni, who attained mastery of the great Shiva mantra. ‘Nam Thamar’ means ‘Our kin.’ Thus, the Peruman Himself declared Sundaramoorthy, who resides in Thiruvaroor, as ‘Our Own.’ Sundaramoorthy Swamigal understood what the Peruman conveyed to the Shiva sages. He understood because the Peruman made him understand.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, seated on the white elephant, seated on the white elephant, his thoughts entirely absorbed in Thiru Kailai.

Thus, when Upamanyu Muni meditated on the Lord there, and the Lord, from within, enlightened Upamanyu Muni and other sages, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, standing here, perceived it through his pervasive memory.

Therefore, because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal’s soul had attained Shivathuvam (the state of Shiva), he was able to know and sing in this manner by His grace. In the tenth Thiru-padal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, “To announce to Anjaiappar, who is praised through ages and ages, and whose ocean-like waves roar.” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who began singing this Thiru-pathikam on his way to Thiru Kailai, reached Thiru Nodi-thanmalai, also known as Thiru Kailaimalai, in the tenth Thiru-padal, which concludes this Thiru-pathikam.

Therefore, these ten Thiru-padals of the pathikam must be announced to Thiru Anjaikkalaththu Appar. So, he commands Varunan, “O Varuna, you shall carry these ten Thiru-padals of this Thiru-pathikam and announce them before Thiru Anjaikkalaththu Appan.” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus commanded. According to that command, Varunan came to Thiru Anjaikkalam on this earthly plane and made the announcement. This Thiru-pathikam, composed on the way to Thiru Kailai, thus came to be available on this earthly plane for all of us. Thiru Anjaikkalam is situated on the shore where the waves, like hands of the ocean king, strike. That is why Indra and other devas who were there, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal announces.

Varunan is the lord of the ocean.

Therefore, he carried out the command of that Peruman. ‘Kadal Arayaa’ means that Varuna Devar served as the medium to receive and manifest those sound waves.

Thus, Thiru Nodi-thanmalai, which rises golden through the ages of dissolution, as we have already seen, ‘Nodithan’ means ‘destroyer,’ one who performs the great dissolution, the universal dissolution. ‘Nodithal’ means ‘to destroy.’ Srimath Madhava Sivagnana Munivar Peruman has extensively explained its meaning in ‘Sivagnana Venba Nodithandre.’ Sundaramoorthy Swamigal made his supplication for liberation to Thiru Anjaikkalaththu Appar. The supplication Sundaramoorthy Swamigal made to Thiru Anjaikkalaththu Appar was in Thiru Kailai. Having heard, Kailayanathar sent the elephant, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal ascended it and went to Thiru Kailai.

Therefore, to inform Thiru Anjaikkalaththu Appar that he had reached his intended destination, he commanded Varunan thus, and Varunan came here and presented it to Anjaikkalaththu Appar.

Thus, this Thiru-padal and this Thiru-pathikam are structured such that Varunan, the king of the ocean, should announce these ten songs, sung and praised by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the leader of those in Thiru Navalur, which is like a sweet sugarcane surrounded by fame, to Paramapithavaagi Shivaperuman, who is enshrined in Thiru Anjaikkalam, regarding Thiru Nodi-thanmalai, which rises golden through the ages of dissolution. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang this Thiru-pathikam on his way to Thiru Kailai, the Lord, reaches the southern entrance of Thiru Kailai, the sacred mountain.

Let us go on contemplating.
Thiruchitrambalam.


Chapter 46 — Vellanai Charukkam 10

Thiruchitrambalam.
The Periya Puranam, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman, Part 1032. Vellanai Charukkam.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, singing the Thiru-pathikam ‘Thananaimunpadai-than,’ proceeds towards Thiru Kailayamalai and reaches its southern entrance. The fortieth Thiru-padal: “Maasil venmai ser peroli ulagelam malarnthida valar meymmei aasil anbardham sinthai pol vilangiya mani kizhar anivaayil thesu thangiya yanaiyum puraviyum izhinthu senilai selvaar eesar velli mamalaiththadam palakadanthu eythinar manivaayil.” The great light, like pure white sacred ash, without blemish, spreads throughout the world. Thiru Kailaimalai is Thiru Kailai, white, It is luminous, covered with snow, and removes impurities. Thiru Kailai mountain, which shines like the minds of faultless devotees, is a growing truth. Thiru Kailai mountain is like the minds of devotees because the Lord resides in the minds of devotees, and He also resides in Thiru Kailai. By its greatness, true light, and purity, it is like the mind of a devotee.

Its ornamental gate is the gate of the valley adjacent to the mountain. Upon reaching this Thiru-vaayil, our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal dismounts from the white elephant. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar dismounts from the horse. Both dismount and walk a long distance. They cross many places of the Lord’s great silver mountain and reach the beautiful jeweled gate. This jeweled gate is the jeweled gate of the temple where our Lord resides.

Therefore, it is the southern gate of Thiru Kailai. They dismount there, walk a long distance, and reach the jeweled gate of the temple where the Lord resides in Thiru Kailai. In this Thiru-padal, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman has sung, “Maasil venmai ser peruli ulagelam malarnthida,” and has placed the word ‘ulagelam’ and the mantra ‘ulagelam’ in this Thiru-padal. ‘Ulagelam’ is the first word that the Lord graciously bestowed upon this Purana, the Thiru-thondar Purana. This is the place where Nambigal, who came from Thiru Kailai, returns to Thiru Kailai, a rare and great state.

Thus, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman has placed the sacred mantra ‘ulagelam,’ bestowed by the Lord, in this Thiru-padal. The forty-first Thiru-padal: “Angan eythiya Thiru Anukkan Thiruva,” At the gate, the mighty Chera chieftains were stopped. By the grace of Thambiran, our Nanjalur chieftain, who came with the white elephant, pouring forth abundant ichor, praised by the celestials, reached the Lord’s presence. In terms of devotion, both were equal.

However, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar came uninvited. Our Lord Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, on the other hand, was invited by the Lord after making a supplication. This is the only difference between them.

Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar was stopped by Nandi Peruman, the lord of the temple, from entering.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal passed through Thiru Anukkan Thiru-vaayil and reached the Lord’s presence. The forty-second Thiru-padal: “Senru kannuthal thirumunbu thaazhnthu veezhnthu ezhunthu senidai vittak kanru kovinai kandu anainthathu ena kaathalin virainthu eythi ninru potriya thani perunthondarai nerizhai valappaakaththu onru meniya aroorane vanthanai enranar ulagu uyya.” Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went inside, bowed before the Lord, fell, then rose, and like a calf that, after a long separation, sees its mother cow and eagerly embraces it, he quickly went and stood before the Lord, praising Him.
